# Wild Bleak Bohemia — Master Source Analysis

Comprehensive working reference for the historical novel on Marcus Clarke, Adam Lindsay Gordon, and Henry Kendall — the three founders of Australian literature — and their convergence in 1860s–80s Melbourne. Source: Michael Wilding, *Wild Bleak Bohemia: A Documentary*, now worked through in full. It tells the whole documented life of the three men and their circle, in several registers; it is a reference to write *from* and verify *against*, not a draft of the novel.

## How to use this document

_Read it by section, not front to back. The same event often appears in three registers — as narrative, as a stageable scene, and as a structural theme — by design, so each is at hand when you need it._

**The apparatus — to verify against the source:**
- **Source Concordance & Coverage Map** — every topic block mapped; fill the Wilding page column once against your copy and every claim becomes traceable to a page.
- **1. Source Inventory** — what each newspaper report, letter, memoir, and court record is good for (scene, voice, fact, contradiction).
- **2. Timeline** — the whole chronology in strict date order, 1833–1922.
- **5. Supporting Figures** — dossiers on the secondary cast.

**The synthesis — to write from:**
- **3. Character Dossiers** — the narrated life of each man (Clarke · Gordon · Kendall), each closing on a death section.
- **4. Scene Candidates** — dramatizable moments, tagged `[KEY]`, `[colourful]`, `[colourful-dark]`, `[co-presence]`, `[death-arc]`, `[contradiction]`, `[dialogue]`, `[setting]`, `[interiority]`.
- **6. Thematic Notes** — the structural argument: the braid, the recurring laws (recognition-too-late, the death foreseen, dying-to-provide), and the patterns to build on.

_Conventions: primary quotation is public-domain (all three principals died before 1890); period antisemitic and racial idiom is paraphrased or contextualized; contradictions are preserved and flagged, not resolved._

## Orientation — structural spine (publisher framing)

**Gravitational centre:** the ~14-month Melbourne stretch when all three were Yorick Club members ("the Yorick years"), weighted alongside the two deaths.

**Four load-bearing beats:**
- Gordon shoots himself the day after *Bush Ballads and Galloping Rhymes* is published — too broke to pay the printer.
- Kendall returns to Sydney, forges a cheque, escapes gaol only by committal to a mental hospital.
- Clarke heads into the first of two bankruptcies.
- The braid itself: documented three-way co-presence in Melbourne.

**Span beyond Melbourne:** back to Gordon's early South Australia years; forward/across to Kendall's NSW life, including his disappearance on the Central Coast.

**Theme spine (recurrent backgrounds):** love & death; debt & madness; alcohol & opiates; horse-racing & theatre; newspapers & magazines; books & libraries.

**Discrete events flagged for capture as chunks arrive:** deaths of Gordon's and Kendall's infant daughters; Gordon's and Clarke's serious riding accidents; Kendall's alcoholism; Gordon's and Kendall's opiate addiction; Clarke's hashish experiments; Kendall's separation from his wife; Gordon's three steeplechase wins in a single day.

---

## Voice samples (primary quotation, via framing)

- **Clarke:** "The mornings were spent in scribbling, the afternoons in tobacco, the evenings in dinner, theatre and gaslight. I fear we did not lead virtuous lives."
- **Clarke (the critic, 1875):** "What is the dominant note of Australian scenery? That which is the dominant note of Edgar Allan Poe's poetry. Weird Melancholy... In Australia alone is to be found the grotesque, the weird, the strange scribblings of Nature learning how to write... the poet of our desolation begins to comprehend why free Esau loved his heritage of desert sand."
- **Kendall:** "Mention of the Yorick Club called up many memories of the darkest period of my life. I left Melbourne with the shadow of insanity on me... In that wild bleak Bohemia south of the Murray, I went through Gethsemane and I am only the grey shadow of the young man who commenced to write with so much enthusiasm in 1861."

---

## Supporting cast — roster (terse; from framing, to confirm/expand from the text)

- **Captain Standish** — fled England under an assumed name over gambling debts; became Chief Commissioner of Police.
- **Father Tenison Woods** — befriends Gordon; recruited Mary MacKillop.
- **Drummond** (Clarke's friend) — called a snake-bite antidote a fraud, had himself bitten to prove it, died.
- **Supple** (Kendall's friend) — tried to assassinate a politician, killed a bystander.
- **William Trainor** — circus performer; had himself buried in the grave next to Gordon's.
- **Walter Montgomery** — actor; shot his horse on leaving Australia, shot himself two days after marrying.
- **Gavan Duffy** — Irish nationalist politician gaoled by the English; advised Clarke on revising *His Natural Life*.
- **Henry Parkes** — "father of Federation"; lent Kendall money, appointed him Inspector of Forests.
- **James Murray** — Gordon's doctor; his blackbirding expedition murdered ~70 Solomon Islanders.
- **Castieau** — prison governor; took a Turkish bath with Clarke.
- **Sir Redmond Barry** — sentenced Ned Kelly to death; died twelve days after Kelly was hanged.

## Women's perspectives — sources flagged

- **Gordon circle:** wife Maggie; cousin Frances; first love Jane Bridges; SA friends Elizabeth Lauder and Mrs Lord.
- **Observers:** Clara Aspinall, Annie Baxter Dawbin, Georgiana McCrae, Margaret Whitworth, Geraldine Jewsbury, Lady Charlotte Jackson, Frances Cashel Hoey.
- **Letters:** Charlotte Kendall, Harriet Gordon, Rose Lewis.
- **Vignettes:** Marian Clarke, Polly Castieau, Mary MacKillop.

## 1. Source Inventory
_Per chunk: newspaper reports, court records, memoirs/recollections, letters, diaries — noting what each is good for (scene, voice, fact, contradiction)._

**Clarke — early years**
- Cyril Hopkins — biography of Clarke (written ~40 yrs on); reproduces Clarke's Australia letters. Primary voice source.
- Clarke's letters to Cyril Hopkins (English school friend) — his voice, candid.
- Hamilton Mackinnon — *Marcus Clarke Memorial Volume* (1884); knew Clarke 15 yrs in Australia.
- Gerard Manley Hopkins — notebook description of young Clarke; letter to Ernest Hartley Coleridge (22 Mar 1863).
- Death certificates — mother Amelia, father William Hislop Clarke.
- R. H. Vetch — *Life of Lieut.-General Sir Andrew Clarke* (the cousin).
- Ian McLaren — cites Melbourne Savage Club papers (1863 telegrams re Clarke's disappearance).
- Captain Standish — diary (records dining with Judge Clarke over the years).
- Henry Gyles Turner — banker-litterateur (chief accountant, Bank of Australasia, 1864), literary aspirant himself; on Clarke at the bank in *Melbourne Review* (1882) and Turner & Sutherland's *Development of Australian Literature* (1898). Recurs.
- *South Australian Register* correspondent (14 Jan 1885) — comic recollection of Clarke as correspondence clerk.
- Annie Baxter Dawbin — diary (sights Judge Clarke and nephew at the opera, 1864). A recurring woman-observer source.
- Clarke's own essays — "On Business Men" (his bank exit); "Speech Days and School Days" (*Australasian*, 28 Dec 1867).
- GMH — *Journals* (Clarke's bank address, 1864); letter to R. W. Dixon (5 Oct 1878) on disliking school.

**Clarke — jackarooing (Swinton/Ledcourt)**
- Arthur Patchett Martin — 'An Australian Novelist' (*Temple Bar*, 1884).
- John Wallace — Glenorchy postmaster/cobbler; told Mackinnon of Clarke's reading; later reminiscences in the *Stawell Times* (1889–90), reprinted in C. E. Sayers, *Shepherd's Gold*. Vivid eyewitness on Clarke's temper, drinking, finances, the move to Ledcourt.
- Donald Wallace — fellow station-hand; told Mackinnon that Clarke wrote nightly but kept it private.
- *Australian Monthly Magazine* (est. Sep 1865) — Clarke's first publications (own name + "Mark Scrivener").

**Standish — sources**
- Clara Aspinall — *Three Years in Melbourne* (woman-observer).
- Paul de Serville — *Pounds and Pedigrees* (Standish chapter).
- Robert Haldane — *The People's Force: A History of the Victoria Police* (1986).
- John Sadleir — *Recollections of a Victorian Police Officer*.
- James Smith — diary (Mitchell Library); via Butler Cole Aspinall, the daily brothel-report detail.
- John Pacini — VRC history (Standish's racing obituary).

**Clarke — Melbourne literary launch (1867–68)**
- Hamilton Mackinnon (again) — the Lewins/Argus discovery story; on Telo; on the Spring Meeting outcry.
- Dr Robert Lewins — materialist surgeon who recommended Clarke to Argus proprietor Lachlan Mackinnon.
- Frederick William Haddon — editor of *The Argus* (from 1 Jan 1867) and earlier of *The Australasian*.
- Alexander Sutherland — *Victoria and its Metropolis* (on *The Australasian* as chief literary authority).
- Clarke's articles — 'Balzac and Modern French Literature' (*Australasian*, 3 Aug 1867); 'Popular Art and Gustave Doré' (28 Sep 1867); 'The Melbourne Spring Meeting' (*Argus*, 4 Nov 1867); 'Cannabis Indica' (*Colonial Monthly*, Feb 1868).
- Catalogue of Clarke's library — documents his Balzac/Doré holdings.
- Clarke's obituary of Telo (*The Leader*, 11 Oct 1879); *Bulletin* "Fuimus" (27 Apr 1901) — both on Telo.

**Gordon — England / early years**
- Edith Humphris — biography of Gordon; and (with Douglas Sladen) *Adam Lindsay Gordon and his Friends in England and Australia*.
- Alexander Sutherland — Turner & Sutherland's *Development of Australian Literature*; *Melbourne Review* (1883, the steeplechase "compelled him to leave the country").
- Mrs Elizabeth Lauder (née Annie Bright) — knew Gordon in the 1860s; *The Record*, 25 Jun 1910 (the steeplechase as Gordon told it to the Brights). Recurring woman-witness.
- Douglas Sladen — lecture reported in *The Argus*, 2 Mar 1912; relays cousin **Frances Gordon**'s recollections (the "never in disgrace" counter-account).
- 1852 Steeplechase Calendar; *Berrow's Worcester Journal* (12 May 1852) — the deliberately obscured race record.
- Gordon's letters to **Charley Walker** (Worcester friend) — his candid early voice.

**Gordon — departure & South Australia**
- Jane Bridges — her memoir-letter to J. Howlett-Ross, in Vidler (ed.), *The Adam Lindsay Gordon Memorial Volume*; the farewell scene. Key women's-perspective source.
- Gordon's poems 'To My Sister' and 'An Exile's Farewell' (1853).
- Brian Elliott — 'The Friend of Charley Walker' and 'Adam Lindsay Gordon: A Small Discovery' (prints Gordon's Adelaide letters; the Tolmer recollection).
- A. C. Ashwin — letter (W. Park Low papers, SLSA); *The Advertiser* (21 Oct 1933) prints Capt. Gordon's 1854 thank-you to Ashwin.
- Alexander Tolmer — *Reminiscences...* (SA police chief; Gordon's inspector application and groom offer).
- Owen Meylett — *The Australasian* (14 Jul 1906), via Horace Tolmer (the "Mysterious Gordon" locked box).
- Edwin Gordon Blackmore — *The Age* (3 Jun 1899), via George Hamilton (the secret cross-country exercising).

**Gordon — Penola police years & resignation**
- Penola Police Journal (1855) — his duties.
- Humphris & Sladen — the Bright family and Trainor friendships.
- Edward "Ned" Bright — his poems (*Wattle Blossoms and Wild Flowers* by brother John; Ned's elegy 'In Affectionate Memory of Adam Lindsay Gordon'); Mrs E. A. Lauder's recollections.
- William Trainor — *Bendigo Advertiser* (Jun 1908, the circus meeting); *The Australasian* (27 Apr 1895, the disreputable-woman resignation). Cuthbert Fetherstonhaugh calls him Gordon's "most intimate friend."
- George C. Scott (fellow trooper) — *Adelaide Register* (30 Nov 1912): the rusty-stirrups exchange.
- Charles Mullaley (d. 1924, aged 105) — *The Argus* (2 Sep 1924): the tipsy-woman version of the resignation.
- Brian Elliott, 'The Friend of Charley Walker' — the official resignation correspondence (Archives, Public Library, Adelaide), incl. inspector George B. Scott's reply (1 Nov 1855).

**Kendall — origins & early years**
- Alexander Sutherland (Turner & Sutherland) — the grandfather Thomas Kendall; Leonard McNally.
- Bishop Reed — doctoral thesis on Kendall (the mother's unstable name).
- A. G. Stephens — 'Kendall's Name and Age' (*Bulletin*, 21 Aug 1929); 'Kendalliana' (*Bulletin*, 9 Jul 1930, the "Mad Harry" story via Louisa Lawson).
- Thomas Bawden — lecture, Grafton School of Arts (5 Aug 1886): the family at Gordon Brook.
- *Sydney Morning Herald* — Basil Kendall's forgery indictment, sentence, and death (1847–52).
- Kendall's own essays — 'A Fight with a Devil Fish' (*Australasian*, 5 Jun 1869); 'A Cruise amongst the South Sea Islands' (7 May 1870).
- Donovan Clarke — edition of Kendall's letters; maritime records pinning the *Waterwitch*.
- Marcus Clarke — *The Leader* supplement (19 Mar 1881), romancing Kendall's South Seas (a braid cross-link: Clarke writing on Kendall).
- Kendall's letters — to Joseph Sheridan Moore (29 Jun 1877; 9 Sep 1864 on hating *The Empire*).
- Michael Ackland — *Henry Kendall: The Man and the Myths*; A. W. Martin — *Henry Parkes: A Biography*.

**Kendall — Sydney literary circle (1860–61)**
- J. Sheridan Moore — Kendall's first mentor (Dublin lawyer's son, ex-Benedictine, schoolmaster); superintended *Poems and Songs*.
- The Nicol Stenhouse circle (Balmain bibliophile, De Quincey's friend) — Daniel Deniehy, James Michael, Dr John Woolley (University Library access).
- *The Empire* / *SMH* (Jan–Aug 1861) — the *Poems and Songs* subscription ads ("the boy-poet of Australia").
- Kendall, 'About Some Members of a Colonial Literary Dinner Party' (*Australasian*, 2 Apr 1870) — on Deniehy's *Southern Cross*.

**Kendall — Grafton & James Lionel Michael**
- Henry Halloran — letter to Sutherland (10 Nov 1891): finding Kendall in a Pitt St draper's shop.
- Kendall's letters — to Charles Harpur (25 Sep 1862, "I have cut them all"); to Mrs Selwyn (25 Mar 1865, on Michael).
- Charlotte Kendall (widow) — to Sutherland (22 Aug 1882): Michael's influence and library. Women's-perspective source.
- Mrs Hamilton-Grey — *Poet Kendall* (a description of Michael); Kendall, 'About Some Men of Letters in Australia' (*Australian Journal*, Oct 1869).

**Kendall — Poems and Songs, Grafton & the Lands Office (1862–64)**
- *Athenæum* (London, 27 Sep 1862) — three poems + Kendall's covering letter.
- *Sydney Morning Herald* (24 Oct 1862, review); G. B. Barton, *Literature in New South Wales* (1866).
- Annotated copies of *Poems and Songs* — Kendall's own ("the sincerest records of dead emotions"); the **Henry Lawson** provenance note (Fisher/Mitchell Libraries).
- Victor Daley — 'Pilgrimage' (*The Bulletin*, 9 Aug 1902): the Grafton lecture fiasco.
- Bishop Reed's dissertation — the debt letter to Moore (Mar 1863).
- James Tyrrell, *Postscript* — Kendall copying Michael's verse.
- Charles Harpur — letters to/from Kendall (1862–66); Kendall, 'Men of Letters in New South Wales' (on Harpur).
- Mrs Selwyn (Grafton vicar's wife, artist) — Kendall's correspondent; Conrad Wagner's painting behind 'Waiting on the Raft'.

**Gordon — horse-breaking years (recollections)**
- Melbourne *Leader* travelling correspondent — *The Press*, Christchurch (23 Sep 1891): the cabbage-tree-hat road meeting near Digby.
- Owen Meylett / Horace Tolmer — *The Australasian* (14 Jul 1906): breaking 173 ES colts; Gordon's dress and skill.
- **Harry Stockdale** — *The Argus* (17 May 1919): the major character memoir (Lake Hawdon, Dingley Dell, Blue Lake, the Tenison Woods night, 'From the Wreck'); plus his Gordon drawings (NLA, Mitchell).
- Harry Stockdale — 2nd reminiscence ('Adam Lindsay Gordon, Poet, scholar and horseman', W. Park Low papers): the 'Miss Kilmansegg' memory feat.
- Major-General Thomas Bland Strange — *Gunner Jingo's Jubilee*: Gordon's schoolboy memory.
- Julian Tenison Woods — 'Personal Reminiscence of Adam Lindsay Gordon' (*Melbourne Review*, Apr 1884): the meeting, the recitations, the dilapidated borrowed books.
- George O'Neill — Woods's biographer; *Australian Monthly Magazine* (May 1867, "R.G." on the Woods literary family); Margaret Press — on Woods's writings.

**Gordon — inheritance, marriage & Dingley Dell**
- Margaret "Maggie" Park Gordon — interview, *Adelaide Advertiser* (23 Mar 1912). Key women's-perspective source.
- Peter Low (Maggie's later husband) — *Adelaide Register* (5 Oct 1922); W. Park Low, *The Real Adam Lindsay Gordon* (typescript, SLSA); Katherine Hughes (Maggie's friend).
- Julian Tenison Woods — on the marriage and the class episodes (Melbourne Review reminiscence).
- Paul Gardiner — on Mary MacKillop (her own account of meeting Woods).
- Sir Frank Madden (*The Argus*, 11 Jun 1908) & E. G. Blackmore (*The Age*, 3 Jun 1899) — Gordon's tartan racing colours; Strange (*Adelaide Register*, 1 Dec 1894) — the dormitory Scott recitations.
- Lorraine Day — *Gordon of Dingley Dell* (the cottage/museum).
- Mrs N. A. Lord (the Mount Gambier vicar's daughter) — 'A. Lindsay Gordon and Dingley Dell' (*The Register*, 2 Nov 1886): the "lady by the fire" encounter. Women's-perspective source.
- Sir John Langdon Bonython — *Literary Opinion* (Sep 1891): the origin of *The Feud*.
- *Border Watch* (J. K. Moir's Gordon chronology, 5 May 1936) and *The Argus* (21 Oct 1915, John Watson) — Gordon's early anonymous publishing.

**Gordon — the famous leap & the horseman**
- Cuthbert Fetherstonhaugh — *After Many Days* (chapter on Gordon): the rider's portrait, and the leap.
- The leap's rival witnesses — Trainor (*Bendigo Advertiser*, 30 Jun 1908; *Register*, 20 Sep 1886), N. A. Lord (in Les R. Hill, *Mount Gambier: The City Around A Cave*), Maggie Gordon (*Advertiser*), Bradshaw Young. Hill dates it 27 Jul 1864.
- Gordon, 'Hippodramia' ('The Fields of Coleraine', *Hamilton Spectator*, 19 Apr 1865) — on the rival Bob Learmonth.

**Gordon — Parliament (1865–66)**
- Tenison Woods — on persuading Gordon to stand, and on his melancholy (Melbourne Review reminiscence).
- George Riddoch; Rev. John Blacket (*Adelaide Register*, 28 Apr 1923; *Register*, 17 Mar 1923) — the speeches, the result, the Riddochs.
- Harry Stockdale (*The Argus*, 17 May 1919) — Gordon's poor oratory but his popularity.
- *The Wayfarer* (2011) — the riotous campaign; the Riddoch obituary.
- Sutherland (Turner & Sutherland) — the Glenelg house and library habits; Gordon D. Combe, *Responsible Government in South Australia* — the library-borrowings list.
- E. G. Blackmore (*The Age*, 3 Jun 1899) — the library/Horace meeting.
- Gordon's satirical verse — 'Exodus Parthenidae – the Lay of the Last Squatter'; 'Hippodramia'.
- Sir Langdon Bonython (*The Argus*, 23 Apr 1915) & *South Australian Hansard* (6 Jun 1865) — Gordon's famous parliamentary speech.
- Cuthbert Fetherstonhaugh, *After Many Days* — the 'Banker's Dream' anecdote; Gordon's will (Park Low papers); John Riddoch (*The Advertiser*, 19 Aug 1895) — Gordon as member and the WA venture.

**Kendall — Parkes's patronage & the Colonial Secretary's office (1866)**
- A. W. Martin, *Henry Parkes: A Biography*; Kendall's letters to Parkes (25 Jan, 19 Feb 1866); Parkes's replies.
- Charlotte Kendall (to Sutherland, 22 Aug 1882) — Parkes taking Kendall to his country seat.
- G. B. Barton, *The Poets and Prose Writers of New South Wales* (1866); *Athenæum* (27 Feb 1866); William Bede Dalley (*SMH*, 12 Jan 1881).
- *Sydney Punch* (26 Jan 1867) — the "three literary Henrys" satire.
- Kendall, 'Government Clerks in Gotham' (*Freeman's Journal*, 5 Jan 1878) — the ungrateful recollection; his Deniehy elegy and letters (in Cyril Pearl, *Brilliant Dan Deniehy*).
- A. G. Stephens, 'Kendalliana – III' (*The Bulletin*, 9 Jul 1930) — the Rose parasol parting (via Louisa Lawson).
- Kendall's *Sydney Punch* work — the 'Dyspeptic Lectures' (Apr–Jun 1867); *The Bronze Trumpet* (1866, attrib.); 'About Some Men of Letters in Australia' (the Punch "Bohemian brotherhood").
- Charlotte Kendall (née Rutter) — to Sutherland (22 Aug 1882): meeting Kendall after his "Love" lecture. Central women's-perspective source.
- George Oakley — *Australian Monthly Magazine* (Aug 1867): "Australia in him."

**Gordon — return, the daughter & the first books (1867)**
- Gordon's only known letter to Maggie (Ballarat, Apr 1867, Park Low papers) — with the 28-poem list scribbled on the back.
- Kenneth Binns (*The Argus*, 28 Nov 1923) — Copyright Office dating of Gordon's books.
- Ronald Campbell, *The First Ninety Years* (the printer Massina); John Holroyd, *George Robertson of Melbourne* (the publisher).
- Reviews — *Bell's Life* (5 Oct 1867); George Oakley, 'Book World by Evelyn' (*Colonial Monthly*, Sep 1867); the dismissive notice (Park Low papers); Richard Birnie's *Argus* review (which won Gordon's friendship).
- Robert Power (*The Australasian*, 14 Dec 1895) & Frank Madden — Gordon as strict gentleman-amateur rider.

**Gordon — to Victoria / Ballarat (1867–68)**
- Park Low papers — the 10 Sep 1867 Dingley Dell mortgage (and the "Marie" trust).
- Mark Twain, *Following the Equator* (on Ballarat's gold-rush birth); Fetherstonhaugh and E. G. Blackmore (*The Age*, 3 Jun 1899) — on Harry Mount.
- Gordon's letters to Trainor (Oct 1867, the Cadger race); the Coleraine Great Western memorial.
- Gordon's letters to John/George Riddoch (3 Mar 1868; 21 Jul 1869) — the Duke's second visit; the Hunt.
- William Brazenor (*The Argus*, 11 Oct 1913) — the Ballarat portrait ("a man who had lost himself").
- 'Aniseed' and Frank Madden (Humphris & Sladen) — the grim hunt-field jest to "Jones."
- Sutherland (Turner & Sutherland) & W. Davidson — the Light Horse troop (the flying-leap muster, the sergeant election).
- Harry Stockdale (*The Argus*, 17 May 1919) & Owen Meylett (*The Australasian*, 14 Jul 1906) — Gordon's boxing (Black Johnson); W. Park Low — the kennel and the loose-box sparring.
- W. Park Low, Hugh Anderson, Hutton / *Ballarat Star* — Gordon's near-fatal riding fall (21 Mar 1868; rival horse-names).
- Edith Humphris / Rev. H. W. Adeney; Maggie (*The Advertiser*, 1912) — Annie's death and burial.
- 'Racing Ethics by the Turf-Cutter' (*The Australasian*, 18 & 25 Apr 1868) — Gordon on the lethal dangers of Australian steeplechasing.
- *Clarence and Richmond Examiner* (5 May 1868) & *SMH* (11 May) inquest reports; Kendall, 'About Some Men of Letters in Australia'; J. Sheridan Moore, *Life and Genius of James Lionel Michael* — Michael's drowning.

**Clarke — Peripatetic Philosopher & the Melbourne portraits (1867–68)**
- H. G. Turner — *Melbourne Review*; *Once a Month* (1885); Turner & Sutherland — Clarke's journalism, manner, stammer.
- Mackinnon & Charles Bright (*Cosmos*, 30 Apr 1895) — the phantom-review sacking; Bright's Café de Paris first meeting.
- Curtis Candler (Melbourne coroner) — diary: the Free Banquet fiasco and the anonymous letter on Standish. (Recurring diarist.)
- Morris Brodzky (*The Bulletin*, 18 May 1904) — Clarke's stammer and the childhood horse-kick; the Batchelder photograph.
- Pacini (VRC history) — Gordon arranging the Duke's horses at Ballarat.

**Clarke — drugs & the city (1867–69)**
- 'Cannabis Indica (A Psychological Experiment)' (*Colonial Monthly*, Feb 1868) — the hashish experiment and the doctor's clinical notes (the suppressed "Dr —" probably J. E. Neild).
- 'Night Scenes in Melbourne' (*Argus*, 28 Feb–9 Mar 1868); 'Lower Bohemia' (*Australasian*, Jun 1869) — exposé journalism.
- *The Bulletin* anecdotes (13 Aug 1881; "Old Penjostler") — the "faces"/Cleopatra wandering; the sun-bath story (via Garnet Walch).
- Ken Oldis, *The Chinawoman* — the detective-guide Fook Shing.
- Geoffrey Blainey & Michael Cannon — opium's ready availability in 1860s Victoria (80 opium shops by 1868).

**Clarke — the Café de Paris / bohemian Melbourne**
- 'A Day in Melbourne' (to Cyril Hopkins, Jan 1865) — the Café de Paris / Theatre Royal / "Saddling Paddock" tour.
- Clarke's 'Café Lutetia' memoir (c.1880) — the bohemian round; the source of the book's epigraph.
- Wattle B. (*The Bulletin*, 9 Sep 1893) — the Mooney's IOU anecdote.
- Harold Love, *The Golden Age of Australian Opera* — prostitution in the theatres.

**Clarke — formation, family & temperament**
- Clarke's letters to Cyril Hopkins — the lost Foreign-Office expectations; the father's ruined estate; the "pit of vulgarity" self-analysis.
- Patrick Moloney (via Cyril) — Clarke's shortened left arm.
- Kendall to Harpur (8 Jan 1862) & Frederick Kendall to A. G. Stephens (1904) — Kendall's maimed right arm.
- Geoffrey Serle, *The Golden Age*; R. H. Vetch — cousin Andrew Clarke's career (Surveyor-General, railways, telegraph, Straits Settlements).

**Clarke — the Melbourne Club & the fast set (1868)**
- Clara Aspinall, *Three Years in Melbourne*; H. M. Hyndman, *Record of an Adventurous Life* — the Club.
- Mackinnon (*Memorial Volume*) vs Turner (*Once a Month*, 1885; Turner & Sutherland) — the dispute over whether the Club ruined Clarke.
- Harold Love, *Golden Age of Australian Opera*; David Blair, 'Three Melbourne Barristers' (*Centennial Magazine*, Jan 1889) — the rakish "fast set."
- Paul de Serville, *Pounds and Pedigrees*; Ronald McNicoll, *Number 36 Collins Street* — the Club world and library.

**Kendall marriage, the O'Farrell shooting & the Colonial Monthly circle (1868)**
- A. G. Stephens, 'The Truth about Henry Kendall' (*Windsor & Richmond Gazette*, 1928) — the marriage and the "Henry Clarence" name flourish.
- Clarke's *History* — O'Farrell's shooting of the Duke ("a madman").
- Clarke, 'Alfred Telo — A Reminiscence' (*The Leader*, 11 Oct 1879); Clarke's 1875 insolvency affidavit; *Argus* reviews (1 Nov 1867, 5 Feb 1868); Mackinnon, Turner, Brian Elliott (*Marcus Clarke*), Ronald Campbell (*The First Ninety Years... Massina*) — the *Colonial Monthly* and its murky partnership.
- George Gordon McCrae (obituaries/memoirs) & Hugh McCrae ('The Yorick' series, *Bulletin* 1929; *My Father and My Father's Friends*) — the magazine office and circle.
- Kendall, 'A Colonial Literary Club, by a Wandering Bohemian' (*Town and Country Journal*, 18 Feb 1871) — his affectionate portrait of Walstab.

**Clarke and the death of Drummond (May 1868)**
- *The Argus* (4 May 1868), 'Fatal Experiment with a Tiger Snake' — the full inquest narrative, with witness statements by Telo, **Marcus Clarke**, the ticket-taker Dailey, the publican Garton, and Professor Halford.
- Harold Love, *James Edward Neild* — the actor Charles Vincent's fatal fall the same day.
- Clarke, *Peripatetic Philosopher* (*The Australasian*, 16 May 1868) — "Death has been busy among us."

**The founding of the Yorick Club (mid-1868)**
- Joseph Johnson, *Laughter and the Love of Friends* (history of the Savage & Yorick clubs); *The Yorick Club* (1911 official history) / *Yorick Reminiscences*; Hamilton Mackinnon (*Memorial Volume*); Hugh McCrae, *My Father and My Father's Friends* — the founding, the naming, the first meeting.

**Clarke's near-fatal fall & the mid-1868 deaths**
- Clarke to Cyril Hopkins (15 Aug 1868); Mackinnon (*Memorial Volume*) vs Montgomery (*The Argus*, 18 May 1869) — how the accident happened; H. G. Turner (*Melbourne Review*) — the three weeks unconscious; Clarke's Telo obituary (*The Leader*, 11 Oct 1879) — Telo's nursing; Walstab (*Lone Hand*, Aug 1907) — writing *Long Odds* chapters.
- Neild ("Jacques") & *The Argus* (2 May 1868) — Marian Dunn's Ophelia (rave vs pan).
- Kendall to J. Sheridan Moore (31 May 1868) & to George Oakley (30 Jun 1868); Kendall, 'About Some Men of Letters in Australia' (*Australian Journal*, Oct 1869); *SMH* memorial (7 Jul 1868); Ann-Marie Jordens, *The Stenhouse Circle* — Harpur's death.

**Gordon's horsemanship & the Madden friendship; Clarke's recovery (mid–late 1868)**
- The Gordon / "Actæon" exchange (*The Australasian*, 4 Jul 1868); Frank Maldon Robb; Madden, Hammersley & McCrae portraits (Humphris & Sladen) — the feud-to-friendship and Gordon as rider.
- Gordon to John/George Riddoch (7 Jul 1868) — the hand fracture; the presentiment stories.
- Clarke, *Peripatetic Philosopher* (15 Aug 1868); Clarke to Cyril Hopkins (15 Aug 1868); Yorick minutes (via McLaren) — the comeback and the secretaryship resignation.

**Gordon to Melbourne; the Esslemont hope & the confession (Oct 1868)**
- Gordon to John Riddoch (6 Oct 1868) — the great letter (money, Esslemont, the sleeping-draught confession, Maggie's pluck, his warm word on Clarke).
- Fetherstonhaugh; de Serville (*Pounds and Pedigrees*); Pacini (VRC); John Adams, 'The Powers that Be' — Robert Power and Myrnong.
- Sladen (Humphris & Sladen) — the Esslemont entail; Frances Gordon on Gordon's mother; Hutton — the mother's 1857 letter.
- Clarke to Cyril Hopkins, with Cyril/Andrew Clarke's correction — Clarke's near-unknown mother.
- Agnes Hamilton-Grey, *Singer of the Dawn*; Sutherland (Turner & Sutherland) — the braid meeting at the Colonial Monthly office / Yorick.

**Gordon: appearance, the Babbler year & the Watson scene (1868–69)**
- McCrae (*The Argus*, 20 Mar 1912; Humphris & Sladen; *Southerly*) & Park Low — the beard contradiction and physical portrait; the skull-hollow gesture.
- Hugh McCrae, 'Gordon and his Friends' (*The Bulletin*, 30 Jan 1929) — the Watson reconciliation (Clarke present).
- The one surviving Gordon→Clarke letter (a Yorick supper-summons), kept "sacredly" by Clarke (Mackinnon).
- *The Press* (Christchurch, 23 Sep 1891); J. K. Moir; Frederick Vaughan (Humphris & Sladen) — Major Baker, Babbler, Major Robins.
- ADB; Michael Cannon, *Melbourne After the Gold Rush*; Pacini — George Watson; de Serville — women in the hunting field.

**Gordon's three-win day & Clarke's "Reckless" (Oct–Nov 1868)**

**Gordon: the 17 Nov letter & Lyttleton's likenesses (Nov 1868–69)**

- *The Argus* (19 Dec 1868); Lorraine Day, *Gordon of Dingley Dell*; de Serville, Sadleir, Annie Baxter Dawbin — Thomas Lyttleton and his Gordon paintings.

**The Yorick at play; Gordon's reticence; 'A Song of Autumn' (1868–69)**

**Gordon: the Brighton swim, the barracks & the 1868 poems (Nov–Dec 1868)**

**R. H. Horne; Kendall's prize, mother & second child (late 1868 – Jan 1869)**

**Gordon: Yallum, Brighton & 'The Swimmer' (early 1869)**
- C. D. Mackellar; *The Advertiser* (19 Aug 1895, John Riddoch); *Adelaide Register* (1 Jul 1911; 30 Nov 1912); Robb — Yallum Park and the composition method.

**Gordon's living-by-the-pen; Clarke the playwright (Dec 1868 – spring 1869)**

- Frederick Kendall's 'Memoir'; J. le Gay Brereton (17 Apr 1869); Sutherland; G. G. McCrae (*Australian Women's Magazine*, Feb 1883) — the move and the failed clerkship.

- John Holroyd, *George Robertson of Melbourne* — Clarke's 'The Peripatetic Philosopher' (Apr 1869); 'Long Odds' (Jun); the 'Lower Bohemia' series (*Australasian*, 12 Jun 1869).

**Hyndman's review; Gordon the racing-journalist; Kendall's awkward debt (Jul–Aug 1869)**
- H. M. Hyndman's full *Long Odds* review (*The Argus*, 2 Jul 1869) — derivative London-society novel, but "study men, study life, study nature."

**Gordon's death: the aftermath (June 1870)**

**Clarke's Bullocktown; Kendall leaves Melbourne (Jul–Sept 1870)**

- *The Australasian* (15 Oct 1870), Howlett-Ross & the later statues — Gordon's monument, the wattle, and the posthumous cult; Baker's letters & the Lawson/Beauchamp connection — the failed provision for Maggie.

**Clarke's marriage and daily life; Montgomery's suicide (Aug 1871–Jan 1872)**
- Clarke's Montgomery obituary (*Argus*, 25 Sep 1871); Castieau's 1871 diary — the friendship, the Turkish bath, *Peacock's Feathers*; Clarke's red-leather notebook (4 Nov 1871) — "she wished she had never married me."
- *The Argus* (22 Dec 1871) — *Old Tales of a Young Country*; Lurline Stuart & Castieau — James Smith's spiritualism and resignation.

**Trollope's visit; Kendall the critic, and the lost years (Dec 1871–1873)**
- Castieau & *The Argus* — Trollope at the Yorick and the Town Hall; Kendall's *Freeman's Journal* essays ('The Harp of Erin'; 'Notes Upon Men and Books,' reassessing Gordon and Clarke) & 'Men of Letters in New South Wales' (*Punch Staff Papers*).
- Parkes to Halloran (the bush rescue); A. G. Stephens (the "Rickeybockey" cell); Kendall to Holdsworth & Sheridan Moore (1876) — the unrecorded, "unhinged" lost years.

**The Cave of Adullam; Clarke's finances; Duffy reads HNL (1872)**
- Mackinnon, McCrae & Brodzky — the Cave of Adullam and Richard Birnie; Turner (the Café de Paris anecdote); Castieau & Clarke (*Australasian*, 4 Apr 1872) — the bounced cheque and the copyright grievance.
- Turner & Pearl — Duffy's premiership, fall, and the Cashel/Frances Hoey connection; Duffy's *My Life in Two Hemispheres* — his critique of *His Natural Life* and Clarke's revision.

**Making His Natural Life; 'Human Repetends'; Walstab arrested (1872)**
- Massina & Lawson — the serial completed and given away; Clarke to Duffy (22 Jul 1872), Shillinglaw & James Moloney (via Bedford) — the "mother's honour" motive and the killing of Rufus Dawes.
- Whitworth & Mackinnon — 'Human Repetends' as self-portrait; Castieau (21 Aug 1872) & the *Brisbane Courier* — Walstab's arrest and Basil Kendall's forgery.

**The Carl trial; Clarke's dark year; Kendall's second Gladesville (Dec 1872–Jul 1873)**
- Castieau & Harrisson — the Carl blackbirding trial (Mount and Murray); Elliott & the Mitchell correspondence — Clarke's debts and the Rose affair; 'Holiday Peak' (the Hopkins alternative-life fantasy); the *Argus* (24 Apr 1873) — the Commune interview.
- The *SMH* (23 Apr 1873) & the Gladesville records (Donovan Clarke) — Kendall's second committal and discharge.

**Holiday Peak; Trollope on Clarke and Gordon; the Black War (1873)**
- McCrae (the starving-artist caricature), the *Argus* & *Brisbane Courier* — *Holiday Peak* and 'Pretty Dick'; Holmes & Frances Cashel Hoey — Clarke's reach for English publication; Trollope's *Australia and New Zealand* — his notice of Clarke and of Gordon.
- Clarke's Port Arthur articles (*Argus*, Jul 1873) — Port Arthur, *His Natural Life*, and the "war of extermination."

**Kendall saved by the Fagans; Clarke breaks with the Argus (Jul 1873–1874)**
- Stephens, Sutherland, Fenton, Joseph Fagan & the Gosford plaques — Kendall, the Fagans, and the glen; the *Argus* (Sept, Nov 1873), Gowen Evans & Pacini — Clarke's Sub-librarianship, the *Herald* hoax, and the *Argus* break.
- 'The Wicked World' (*Weekly Times*, 1874) & Lukin's *Brisbane Courier* — Clarke's radicalization and new bases; Castieau — the fading Yorick.

**His Natural Life published; Clarke's insolvency (Apr–Aug 1874)**
- Robertson's volume & the Duffy dedication; Shillinglaw's annotations; C. T. Clarke (the sales) — *His Natural Life* in book form. The *Argus* (8 Jul 1874), Elliott & Clarke to Cyril Hopkins — the insolvency and Sir Andrew Clarke's rescue.
- McLaren (the library auction catalogue); the *Argus* (Aug 1874) & Castieau — the Harwood libel trial.

**The Harwood fallout; Kendall renounces Bohemia; Clarke blackballed (Aug–Sept 1874)**
- The *Argus* (26–27 Aug 1874) — the lie/anonymity debate; Charles & Joseph Fagan, and Kendall's letters to Holdsworth, Moore & Butler — the sober Gosford life and the renunciation of letters.
- Johnson — Clarke's Library resignation and the Yorick blackball; Turner — *Chidiock Tichbourne*.

**The Bentley edition; Clarke survives; Kendall's Gosford verse (Dec 1874–1875)**
- The Bentley readers' reports & Clarke to Bentley (Sadleir, Stuart) — the English edition and the happy-ending pressure; the Library Minute Book & the *Argus* (Jun 1875) — Clarke keeping his job, the schools' *History*, and the parliamentary attack.
- 'Arcadia at our Gates' & Kendall's letters to Moore — the Gosford poems and the useful citizen.

**The English reception; John Dunn's death; Clarke's national-poetry manifesto (1875)**
- Clarke to Bentley & Frances Cashel Hoey (Sadleir, Edwards); the London reviews & Hyndman's memoir — the English edition of *His Natural Life* and its reception.
- The *Argus* (Aug 1875) — the death and funeral of John Dunn, Clarke's father-in-law; Clarke's 'Country Leisure' (*Brisbane Courier*, 4 Sept 1875) — "Weird Melancholy," "the Land of the Dawning," and the ranking of Kendall and Gordon.

**Kendall at Camden Haven; Clarke through Myers's eyes (1875–76)**
- Sutherland, Isaac, Frederick Kendall & Charlotte's 1882 letter — Camden Haven, the restored marriage, and the reluctant marksman; Levy-Lawson & Turner — the *Daily Telegraph* invitation declined.
- Frank Myers, 'Of Marcus Clarke (and Some Others)' (*Bulletin*, 1903) — the Birdcage dinner, and Clarke's contempt for the bush and for his own work.

**The Gordon copyright; Clarke's preface; His Natural Life's fame (1876)**
- Binns, Campbell, the Low papers & Bond — the Gordon copyright tangle and Maggie's loss; the 1876 reissue, Cyril Hopkins & Mackinnon — Clarke's preface to Gordon's poems.
- Patchett Martin & Mackinnon (Lytton, the Prince of Wales); McLaren — *His Natural Life*'s spreading fame and Clarke's unfinished projects of 1876.

**Clarke in 1877: the net withdrawn**
- Clarke to Cyril Hopkins (Jan 1877) & Brodzky — the Brighton self-portrait and the return of the moneylenders; Mackinnon & McLaren — the schools' *History* fiasco and the fatal business agency.
- The *Argus* & Clarke's *Telegraph* analysis — his civic work, *Four Stories High*, and the move to Syme's *Age*.

**Black Wednesday; Kendall the reluctant hack; the inverse poets (1877–78)**
- Mackinnon, Deakin & Myers — Clarke's red *Age* journalism and Black Wednesday; Turner & Patchett Martin — his contempt for business and the self-wounding Bohemianism.
- Kendall's 'Old Manuscripts,' the Holdsworth anecdotes & Bennett's "inspired blackfellow" tribute — the reluctant hack, the relapses, and the bush-poet who was Clarke's opposite.

**Clarke's gamble; Kendall crowned (1878–79)**
- Mackinnon, Barry & the Library minutes — Clarke declining the parliamentary post, *Alfred the Great*, and the trustees' reprimand; McLaren — the journalism-and-debt diary.
- The *SMH*, the *Argus* & the Exhibition reports — Kendall's opening cantata and hundred-guinea prize; the 'Gagging Bill' on Parkes; his "out of the running" letters to McCrae.

**'The Other Kendall'; Clarke's two controversies (1878–80)**
- Frank Myers, 'The Other Kendall' (*Bulletin*, 1903) — the defiant late Kendall, his scorching letter, his dislike of Clarke, and the "bitter loneliness" of colonial letters.
- Patchett Martin & the *Argus*/*Age* — Clarke's 'Civilisation without Delusion' and the Bishop clash; the banned burlesque *The Happy Land*.

**The title named; the legend begins; Marian returns (1880)**
- Kendall's letters to Swan & McCrae (Heseltine, Ackland) — "wild bleak Bohemia," the grey shadow, and his coolness to *His Natural Life*; Baillière, Barry & Kendall — the *Civilization Without Delusion* pamphlet and the establishment's rebuke.
- Archibald (the *Bulletin* and the Waxman anecdote); the *Argus* & Turner — the Blair feud and Marian's return to the stage in Clarke's plays.

**Kendall's late comeback; the vise closes on Clarke (1880)**
- Kendall's letters to Butler, Parkes, Swan & McCrae (Wilde, Ackland, Reed) — *Songs from the Mountains*, the forest-ranger plea, and the reconciliation with Clarke; the *Bulletin* & Brodzky — Clarke's ophthalmia, measles and bailiffs.
- The *Argus* (the *Moonstone*; the Baillière/Waxman bill); Clarke's Library application & Mackinnon — the gamble for the chief post and the deaths of Barry and Lyster.

**Clarke passed over; Kendall's laureate moment and first illness (1880–81)**
- Walstab (*Camperdown Chronicle*), the *Bulletin* & Patchett Martin — the librarianship lost, the "A.B.C." epigram, and Clarke's self-destroying pen; Reed, Tyrrell & Maddock — *Songs from the Mountains* and the 'Ninian Melville' recall.
- Dalley's review & the *Bulletin* feature ("uncrowned laureate"); Frederick Kendall & Kendall's letters — Cundletown, the Forests post, and the illness of 1881.

**Clarke's tribute and break-up; Kendall's forests post (1881)**
- Wallen/the Art Union (the illustrated *Orara*); Clarke's *Leader* article & Kendall's letters to McCrae — the generous tribute and the reconciliation by post; the *SMH* — the Inspectorship of State Forests.
- Cyril Hopkins (the "Uncle John" bailiff story) & Mackinnon — Clarke's charm and the onset of his "early break-up."

**The death of Marcus Clarke (1881)**
- The *Argus* (the Waxman insolvency; the obituary) & the Library minutes — the final sequestration and the demanded resignation; Mackinnon — the coin-toss and the deathbed.
- McCrae, Swan, Archibald & Moloney — the bare cottage, the medicine that came too late, and the grief of his friends.

**Clarke's funeral and afterlife; Kendall's elegy and decline (1881–82)**
- The *Argus* (the funeral; the destitute widow; the fund), McLaren & James Smith's *SMH* attack — the aftermath and the Waxman irony; Whitworth's preface & Bond — Clarke's defenders and the *His Natural Life* adaptations.
- Kendall's *Sydney Mail* tribute and *Bulletin* 'In Memoriam' & his last letters to McCrae — the elegy for Clarke, the death of his son Orara, and "before the year be out, I shall see Charles."

**The death of Henry Kendall (1882) — and after**
- Frederick Kendall & Charlotte's letters — the fatal Forests riding, the TB, and the deathbed ("so you will get the salary"); the *SMH*, Holdsworth & the *Evening News* — the funeral and its twenty mourners.
- The *SMH*, *Brisbane Courier* & *Argus* obituaries; Palgrave & Quiller-Couch (the anthologies); A. G. Stephens — Louisa Lawson's rescue of the grave and the naming of Henry Lawson.

## 2. Timeline
_Dated events, in strict chronological order (the reconciliation pass). Bare-year and bare-month entries sort to the head of their period; qualifier dates (early/late) are biased within the month. Weighted toward the Yorick window and the deaths._

- **19 Oct 1833** — Adam Lindsay Gordon born at Fayal, the Azores; only son (three of four sisters die young). The family's West Indian slave-plantation income is lost to abolition the same year.
- **18 Apr 1839** — Henry Kendall born (a twin, with Basil Edward) in a bush humpy at Kirmington, near present-day Milton, NSW. (In later life he liked to shave off a couple of years and claim 1841–42.)
- **1840** — the Gordons settle in Cheltenham; from 1846 the Captain teaches Hindustani at the new College.
- **1845** — the Kendalls move north to the Clarence/Orara (Gordon Brook station).
- **24 Apr 1846** — Marcus Clarke born, 11 Leonard Place, Kensington; only child.
- **1847–48** — Kendall's father Basil convicted of uttering a forged £5 order; two years' hard labour.
- **13 Mar 1850** — Clarke's mother Amelia (26) dies of phthisis (TB); Clarke aged 3.
- **May 1852** — Gordon, at the Royal Grammar School, Worcester, pulls off the *Lalla Rookh* steeplechase escapade — the event later said to have "compelled him to leave the country."
- **23 Sep 1852** — Basil Kendall dies of consumption, aged 42; Melinda keeps a day school near Wollongong.
- **7 Aug 1853** — Gordon (19) sails for South Australia on the barque *Julia*, packed off by his father with letters of introduction and money lodged in the Adelaide bank (the steeplechase affair quashed at heavy cost). Days earlier, the Jane Bridges farewell.
- **14 Nov 1853** — reaches Adelaide; received and housed by his father's friends the Ashwins in North Adelaide (not the legend of discarded introductions).
- **17–24 Nov 1853** — applies to the Mounted Police; rejected for an inspectorship, accepted as a trooper. Becomes "Mysterious Gordon" in barracks.
- **29 Dec 1853** — offered a mount in the hurdle race at the Summer Meeting; his colonial race-riding begins.
- **Nov 1854** — trooper at Penola: a settler's-hut station with no cells; boxes a horse-stealing prisoner into submission; romanticises the life as "moss trooper" adventure.
- **1855** — befriends the poetry-loving Bright family (Ned Bright → 'The Sick Stockrider') and circus rider **William Trainor**.
- **1855–62** — ~seven years buying, selling and breaking horses, roaming station to station; still half-intending to sail home for Jane.
- **1855–57** — Kendall, 16, spends ~18 months at sea as an apprentice in the South Sea Islands trade (the whaler *Waterwitch*, possibly also his uncle's brig).
- **4 Nov 1855** — resigns from the Mounted Police after ~18 months (a week short of two years; rival accounts of why); turns to droving and horse-dealing.
- **c.1857–59** — back in Sydney, a confectioner's errand-boy: "Mad Harry," carrying trays of tarts on his head and muttering verse through the streets.
- **17 Jun 1857 / 29 Apr 1859** — Gordon's father, then mother, die in England.
- **1859** — first poem, 'O, Tell Me, Ye Breezes', published (poignantly) in a temperance journal; more follow in *The Empire* and, from 1861, the *Herald*.
- **1860** — Mary MacKillop, governess at Penola, meets Tenison Woods; their schools-for-the-poor scheme begins (→ Sisters of St Joseph, 1866).
- **1860** — by 21, Kendall has 21 poems in print and a foothold in literary Sydney (Sheridan Moore; the Stenhouse circle).
- **c.1861–62** — first serious riding fall: his horse rolls on him, bruising his spine near the kidneys; lasting back pain (the body's decline begins).
- **1861** — subscription drive for Kendall's *Poems and Songs* (Moore superintending).
- **26 Oct 1861** — a legacy of ~£7,000 reaches Gordon's bank; he quits horse-breaking for the gentleman-rider life and is soon squandering it.
- **Sep 1862** — the London *Athenæum* prints three Kendall poems with his bold covering letter — his first overseas recognition.
- **Oct 1862** — *Poems and Songs* published; well reviewed (Barton; SMH) but a financial loss, leaving Kendall in debt for the first time.
- **20 Oct 1862** — marries Margaret "Maggie" Park (the day after his 29th birthday).
- **Dec 1862–Apr 1863** — Kendall in Grafton again: lectures on Shelley/Wordsworth/Burns; the lecture-platform fiasco; his tie to Charles Harpur deepens by letter.
- **1863** — clerkship in the Lands/Surveyor-General's office (~£150/yr, made permanent Jan 1864).
- **1863** — Kendall first meets Henry Parkes.
- **16 Mar 1863** — Clarke (17) leaves Plymouth, sent out at cousin Andrew Clarke's expense, carrying £300.
- **22 Mar 1863** — GMH writes that Clarke is sailing for Australia, his father struck by "paralysis of the brain."
- **7 Jun 1863** — arrives Melbourne; vanishes on landing.
- **10 Jun 1863** — uncle Judge James Langton Clarke telegraphs Chief Commissioner Standish; Clarke traced to the Criterion Hotel, already gone out to dine.
- **1 Dec 1863** — father William Hislop Clarke (56), barrister, dies in Northumberland House Lunatic Asylum, "softening of the brain."
- **1864** — Clarke placed as a probationary clerk at the Bank of Australasia (via David Macarthur); a hopeless bookkeeper but the office wit; eased out within months ("not a Business Man").
- **1864–67** — jackaroo on Judge Clarke's western Victoria stations: Swinton (mgr Joseph Holt), then Ledcourt as overseer (mgr John Holt); solitary Grampians rides behind "Pretty Dick"; broke, hot-tempered, drinking at Glenorchy.
- **26 Jun 1864** — Annie Baxter Dawbin sees Judge Clarke and his nephew at the opera (*Le Prophète*).
- **27 Jul 1864** — Gordon's celebrated Blue Lake leap at Mount Gambier (the day after the Border Handicap Steeplechase; finishing order Learmonth, Trainor, Gordon).
- **Aug 1864** — twin Basil's impersonation fraud forces Kendall's public CAUTION notice.
- **30 Aug 1864** — first poem, *The Feud* (anonymous, "A. Lindsay," keyed to Noel Paton's 'Dowie Dens o' Yarrow' plates), printed at Mount Gambier; stung by ridicule, Gordon destroys the copies.
- **22 Oct 1864** — 'Verses inspired by "My old black pipe"' (written ~1862, given to Trainor) appears in *Bell's Life in Victoria*.
- **11 Jan 1865** — a deputation asks Gordon to stand for the SA seat of Victoria against attorney-general Randolph Stow.
- **1 Mar 1865** — Gordon elected (378; Riddoch 370; Stow 367) after a riotous campaign.
- **23 May 1865** — takes his seat; moves Maggie to a house at Glenelg; spends his days in the parliamentary library more than the chamber.
- **6 Jun 1865** — delivers his famous Latin-strewn Hansard speech (the squatters as Sisyphus, as "the morituri").
- **3 Dec 1865** — makes his will, leaving everything to Maggie (witnessed by Craig and Moore of Ballarat).
- **1866** — publishing in the Melbourne *Australasian* and *Bell's Life* ('Ye Wearie Wayfarer'); resolves to quit politics for a literary life **in Melbourne**.
- **Mar 1866** — débuts in the *Australian Monthly Magazine* under his own name and as "Mark Scrivener" (a GMH coinage). Standish dines with the Clarkes (27 Dec 1865; 16 Mar 1866) and offers Marcus a mounted-police commission — weighed, declined.
- **10 Nov 1866** — resigns his seat; returns to Robe; a West Australian land-and-sheep venture soon loses him everything he puts in.
- **1867** — failed Queensland overlanding expedition (each man loses ~£300; horses die, cattle drown, fever; the Hungarian Max Kabat dies and is buried in the bush); Clarke reaches Adelaide in rags, then Melbourne.
- **1867** — joins *The Argus* as theatrical critic, £300/yr.
- **1867–68** — Clarke dropped from the *Argus* staff over a phantom theatre/concert review; freelance thereafter.
- **14 Feb 1867** — Clarke (21) writes to Cyril Hopkins from Ledcourt Station, Wimmera.
- **Mar 1867** — back from WA; gives up Glenelg, resettles at Mount Gambier; resumes racing and verse.
- **3 May 1867** — daughter **Annie Lindsay Gordon** born at Robe (Maggie's health precarious).
- **mid-1867** — Dr Lewins recommends Clarke to Argus proprietor Lachlan Mackinnon; Clarke back in Melbourne by June, on the *Argus* staff and the *Australasian*; shares Collins St chambers with **Alfred Telo** (starting at 30s/week as a reporter — Wilding notes this is ~¼ of the £300 Clarke had claimed).
- **10 & 19 Jun 1867** — *Ashtaroth* and *Sea Spray and Smoke Drift* published in Melbourne (anonymous, each "by the Author of" the other; £50 each).
- **3 Aug & 28 Sep 1867** — 'Balzac and Modern French Literature' and 'Popular Art and Gustave Doré' run in the *Australasian*.
- **10 Sep 1867** — mortgages Dingley Dell (£1,500); £1,000 settled in trust on a shadowy young woman known only as "Marie."
- **Oct 1867** — rides at Melbourne; his Melbourne ties deepen.
- **Nov 1867** — leases Craig's Hotel livery stables, Ballarat; moves there with Maggie and baby Annie; partners the horseman Harry Mount.
- **Nov 1867** — the Melbourne Free Banquet for the Duke collapses into a riot; Standish saves the Prince from the crush.
- **4 Nov 1867** — 'The Melbourne Spring Meeting' (*Argus*): anti-Semitic mockery of Jewish racegoers triggers an outcry; Clarke censured but kept ("invaluable as a writer").
- **23 Nov 1867** — Clarke launches 'The Peripatetic Philosopher' (as "Q") in *The Australasian*, timed to the Duke of Edinburgh's arrival; it runs 2½ years.
- **9 & 20 Dec 1867** — the Duke at Ballarat (Craig's Hotel); Gordon arranges his horses and rides in the steeplechase (a Clarke–Gordon near-convergence).
- **17 Dec 1867** — Clarke's supervised hashish experiment (the basis of 'Cannabis Indica').
- **1868** — consumed by the stables, Gordon publishes only two poems all year — both in Clarke's *Colonial Monthly*, his first appearance alongside Clarke.
- **Feb 1868** — 'Cannabis Indica (A Psychological Experiment)' (*Colonial Monthly*): the hashish write-up with the doctor's clinical notes, set in the death-haunted Telo chambers.
- **28 Feb & 9 Mar 1868** — 'Night Scenes in Melbourne' (*Argus*): the Chinese Quarter tour with detective Fook Shing.
- **Mar 1868** — Clarke, aged 22, becomes "proprietor and editor" of the *Colonial Monthly* (a squandered sheep-farming inheritance sunk into it); the debt spiral later confessed in his 1875 insolvency affidavit begins. All three principals are now in print in the one magazine.
- **7 Mar 1868** — Kendall marries Charlotte Rutter (Glebe, Sydney; banns dispensed with); he marries as "Henry Clarence Kendall," he 28, she 19. [Wilding says "29"; born 18 Apr 1839, Kendall would not turn 29 until six weeks after the wedding — correct age is 28.]
- **13 Mar 1868** — H. J. O'Farrell shoots the Duke of Edinburgh in Sydney (Duke recovers); Parkes pushes a discredited Fenian-conspiracy theory; O'Farrell hanged 21 Apr. Kendall contributes to the loyal volume *Prince Alfred's Wreath*.
- **21 Mar 1868** — Gordon's near-fatal riding fall at Ballarat (five ribs, collarbone, fractured skull); "arguably never fully recovered." Horse named variously Prince Rupert or Necromancer.
- **~Apr 1868** — the Ballarat stables burn (horses and vehicles lost); with the misfortunes mounting, Gordon resolves to leave Ballarat.
- **14 Apr 1868** — Gordon's infant daughter Annie Lindsay Gordon dies of enteritis (aged 10 months) while he convalesces; he designs her headstone, buried near Lake Wendouree under a slab not naming him as father.
- **18 & 25 Apr 1868** — 'Racing Ethics by the Turf-Cutter' (*Australasian*): Gordon's critique of the lethal dangers of Australian steeplechasing — four days after Annie's death.
- **28 Apr 1868** — James Lionel Michael, Kendall's mentor, drowns in the Clarence River (aged 44); accident, murder or suicide disputed — Kendall is certain it was suicide.
- **May 1868** — Clarke elected to the conservative Melbourne Club; living above his income, he slides toward the usurers (60% interest) and the first bankruptcy to come.
- **1–2 May 1868** — the snake-bite death of Clarke's friend **W. M. Drummond**: to "disprove" the showman Shires's antidote he has himself bitten by a tiger snake "for science" and dies the next evening. Clarke, a witness who tried to dissuade him, loses both a friend and the *Colonial Monthly*'s likely backer. Days later the actor Charles Vincent is thrown from his horse and dies. Clarke, 16 May: "Death has been busy among us."
- **9 May 1868** — first meeting of the **Yorick Club** (a bare room over the *Punch* office): Clarke argues all night for "Golgotha" (place of skulls); Carrington's "Alas, poor Yorick!" carries instead. Clarke's mahogany skull (from Moloney) sits on the mantel, pipe in its teeth.
- **30 May 1868** — Yorick committee formed (Clarke Secretary, Kane Treasurer, Neild Librarian); 64 foundation members, Gordon among them as "country" (still at Ballarat).
- **Jun–Jul 1868** — Gordon fractures a hand (a kick; the doctor threatens amputation); his newspaper feud with "Actæon" (Frank Madden) turns into a lifelong friendship.
- **early Jun 1868** — Clarke's near-fatal riding fall: a horse-kick to the head fractures his skull; ~3 weeks unconscious, out of action two months (Telo nurses him; Walstab writes the July–Aug episodes of *Long Odds*). A permanent skull-dent and memory lapses follow.
- **10 Jun 1868** — Charles Harpur dies of consumption — Kendall's second mentor gone in six weeks (after Michael, 28 Apr); his memorial poem follows (*SMH*, 7 Jul).
- **13 Jun – 2 Oct 1868** — Clarke serves as Yorick Secretary (the suggestion-book wars).
- **4 Jul 1868** — the rival **Athenæum Club** opens.
- *Founding-myth caveat:* the club's own histories place Gordon, Kendall and Shillinglaw beside Clarke at the naming — but **Kendall doesn't reach Melbourne until April 1869** (Clarke seconds his membership 29 Jul 1869) and **Gordon is at Ballarat**, active only after moving to Melbourne (Oct 1868; subscription paid 28 Feb 1869). The all-three-at-the-founding tableau is a backdating.
- **15 Aug 1868** — Clarke resumes the Peripatetic Philosopher (the "Two Months at Pentridge" joke prefiguring *His Natural Life*); ~£15/week and prospering.
- **25 Sep 1868** — Maggie leaves for South Australia; Gordon sells up Ballarat (£350 sunk in rent).
- **Oct 1868** — Gordon moves to Melbourne (staying with Robert Power at Toorak, training his horses); the **Esslemont** inheritance prospect surfaces (he's named nearest heir to an entailed Aberdeenshire estate, but doubts the entail).
- **Oct 1868 – Jan 1869** — Gordon's Babbler chases for Major Baker (his racing "living by proxy"): Hunt Club Cup (Flemington, Oct), Ballarat steeplechase (Dec), 3rd in the Grand National (Jan 1869).
- **Oct 1868** — Parkes resigns; the Martin ministry falls — Kendall's Sydney career hopes dim (he's in debt, £30 to Parkes among others).
- **2 Oct 1868** — Clarke resigns as Yorick secretary (the committee's regretful minute).
- **6 Oct 1868** — Gordon's confessional letter to Riddoch: drinking since the fall against constant head/back pain, "a strong sleeping-draught... that I should never awake from" — the clearest pre-1870 sign of the suicide to come. (Same letter: the Esslemont hope; Maggie's pluck; "Marcus Clarke... a very nice young fellow.")
- **10 Oct 1868** — Gordon's legendary three wins in a day (Babbler, Viking, Cadger) at the Melbourne Hunt Club meet — the peak of his riding fame; faithful old Cadger sold afterwards for £40.
- **late 1868** — at Myrnong (the Powers', Toorak), Gordon writes 'A Song of Autumn' — a spring-written autumn poem of his own mortality, prompted by little Maud Power's talk of dying flowers.
- **Nov–Dec 1868** — Gordon's two *Colonial Monthly* poems ('The Three Friends'; 'Doubtful Dreams') and an unsigned essay (likely Clarke's) championing him as "the most Australian" after Kendall; he moves to Baker's St Kilda Road barracks.
- **7 Nov 1868** — Gordon wins the VRC Steeplechase on Viking; the same day Clarke's 'Sketches in Pig-Skin' portrays him as "Reckless," with Clarke physically restraining him from a deadly jump.
- **17 Nov 1868** — Gordon's second long letter to Riddoch: off opiates now ("I never touch opiates in any shape"), but "I ride for money now," hiding in a horse-box on Cup day, "content... to leave this gay and festive scene"; still certain of the Esslemont claim yet sure he'll never get it.
- **late Nov 1868** — the **Brighton swim**: Gordon swims out to sea "determined not to return," then fights back to shore — a documented suicide attempt, survived.
- **end Nov 1868** — Maggie returns from South Australia; the couple take lodgings at North Brighton.
- **Dec 1868** — Clarke's first play, 'Foul Play,' staged (Duke of Edinburgh theatre) — his playwriting debut; Marian Dunn retires from the stage (their engagement?).
- **5 Dec 1868** — Gordon wins the Ballarat Handicap Steeplechase on Babbler (£100); resolves to pursue the **Esslemont** claim.
- **Jan 1869** — Kendall wins the *Australian Annual* poetry prize for 'A Death in the Bush' (as "Arakoon"), judged by Horne — who fails to spot his own *Orion* line embedded in it.
- **2 Jan 1869** — Charlotte bears a second daughter, Lizzie (Violet) Araluen, in Sydney.
- **early 1869** — Gordon rides ~200 miles to Yallum Park (Riddoch's, Penola); composes 'The Sick Stockrider' in a gum tree; dashes off the Mary Lord valentine on the journey.
- **early 1869** — the Gordons settle at Brighton (rooms from Higinbotham's gardener Kelly); Higinbotham lends Gordon the run of his library; daily sea-swimming; he writes the *Bush Ballads* poems.
- **Feb–Mar 1869** — Gordon refuses a staff post as *Australasian* racing reporter, distrusting himself among drink and opiates; tells Riddoch the public would not back his mounts "if they knew how often I hoped for a fall."
- **12 Feb 1869** — racing on Maud at Ballarat, Gordon runs down a miner's boy (broken leg); visits him in hospital and gives him money.
- **27 Mar 1869** — Gordon wins the VRC Steeplechase on Babbler.
- **Apr–May 1869** — the Clarke–Montgomery press war: a six-week public feud over Montgomery's bribery accusations against the Melbourne press, Clarke dismantling him letter by letter and threatening law (Montgomery in passing confirms their shared Goyder's riding accident).
- **Apr–Jun 1869** — Kendall's Melbourne publishing splash ('Ogyges' beside Clarke's 'Pretty Dick'; the famous 'Bell Birds'; *Australasian* poems).
- **1 Apr 1869** — Kendall lands in Melbourne ("an ominous first of April") with four shillings — **the braid completes** (all three now in the same city). A try at office work (a statistical "deaths sheet") collapses in three days.
- **18 Apr 1869** — Charlotte and baby Araluen arrive (SS *Dandenong*); the family takes a house by the Carlton Gardens.
- **late Apr 1869** — Clarke's first book, 'The Peripatetic Philosopher' (by "Q"), published by George Robertson.
- **20 May 1869** — Horne's library auctioned (£60); he sails for England.
- **Jun 1869** — Clarke's first novel, 'Long Odds,' published; his 'Lower Bohemia' series begins (12 Jun).
- **Jun–Aug 1869** — Gordon, "regularly on the staff" of *The Australasian* (deliberately dry racing reports; the essay 'The Ring and the Books,' 24 Jul), wins the Caulfield Maiden steeplechase (24 Jun), builds a new stable, and is made a Melbourne Hunt Club steward (21 Aug).
- **Jun 1869** — 'Lower Bohemia' (*Australasian*): upper vs lower Bohemia — the destitution "from which there was no return."
- **12 Jun 1869** — Horne sails for England (to a cold reception); the same Saturday, Gordon's 'How We Beat the Favourite' appears anonymously in *The Australasian* — the torch passes, and Gordon is famous overnight.
- **22 Jun 1869** — Kendall writes his secret confessional letter to Neild from the Yorick — the full story of his family's betrayal and his flight from Sydney.
- **Jul 1869** — Clarke's first novel, 'Long Odds,' published in book form (dedicated to Walstab); Hyndman's anonymous review urges Clarke to write about his own country — the seed of *His Natural Life*.
- **Jul 1869** — Kendall, having lampooned Montgomery in *Punch*, learns Montgomery is his arranged creditor and writes Neild a mortified apology.
- **21 Jul 1869** — Montgomery quits Melbourne for Adelaide, the day before Marian's wedding.
- **22 Jul 1869** — Clarke marries the actress Marian Dunn at St Peter's (and goes off to find lodgings once the knot is tied).
- **late Jul–Aug 1869** — Kendall's desperate secret loan-letters to Rusden; the "temporary insanity" letter ("Your letter has frightened me").
- **29 Jul 1869** — Clarke seconds Kendall's Yorick membership.
- **Aug 1869** — Clarke sells the *Colonial Monthly* to Shillinglaw after eighteen months; Kendall withdraws from the Yorick ballot, sleepless and starving.
- **5 Aug 1869** — Kendall, threatened with suit by Parkes, replies from the depths ("If you were to put me in gaol, I should probably be happier than I am now").
- **21 Aug 1869** — Kendall's 'The Hut by the Black Swamp' appears; Gordon praises it lavishly.
- **Sept 1869** — Kendall's second book, *Leaves from Australian Forests* (Robertson), published — dedicated to Charlotte; a commercial failure.
- **Sept–Oct 1869** — the satirical-press war: *Humbug* vs James Smith's *Touchstone* (Clarke branded 'The Literary Cad').
- **8 Sept 1869** — Clarke launches *Humbug*, the satirical weekly, co-edited with Kendall.
- **Oct 1869** — Gordon visits Adelaide (admitting he can no longer "be abstemious by myself"); Whyte-Melville praises *Sea Spray* (9 Oct) and is granted the *Bush Ballads* dedication; Gordon takes a severe fall at the Hunt Club (15 Oct).
- **Oct 1869** — Kendall's 'About Some Men of Letters in Australia' (a "catalogue of despair"); a Yorick testimonial helps him briefly.
- **8 Oct 1869** — *Leaves from Australian Forests* reviewed; Kendall hailed as "the first poet in whom Australia speaks."
- **Nov–Dec 1869** — Clarke runs drink-soaked *Humbug* parodies of Kendall ('Glycera') and Gordon; his friendship with Kendall sours.
- **1870** — Kendall sinks into poverty and squalor (Carlton → Fitzroy → Collingwood → Richmond); drinking, sleeping in lanes.
- **1870** — Charles Gavan Duffy appointed a Library trustee; he and Clarke become friends, and Clarke shows him the *His Natural Life* serial in manuscript for frank criticism.
- **1870–73** — Maggie returns to Robe (the trunks repacked; Gordon's box, perhaps with 'Penthesilea,' lost on the voyage); works as a seamstress; marries Peter Low, 1873.
- **Jan 1870** — both the *Colonial Monthly* and *Humbug* cease publication.
- **Jan 1870** — the *Colonial Monthly* publishes its last issue, carrying Gordon's 'The Sick Stockrider' (reprinted *Australasian*, 15 Jan) — the founding poem of Australian bush balladry.
- **Jan 1870** — Montgomery leaves Australia (shooting his horse Tudor on departure); Clarke and Haddon travel to Tasmania (Launceston, 21 Jan), visiting Port Arthur on Foundation Day (26 Jan) to "write up the criminal records."
- **5–12 Jan 1870** — *Humbug* dies; Clarke's valedictory 'On the Pleasures of Editorship' (the delirium-tremens joke, "nine deadly enemies"); Kendall writes its mock-obituary in *Touchstone* and denies it to Clarke.
- **Feb–Mar 1870** — the bitter Clarke–Kendall–Shillinglaw money dispute (Clarke's "brutal" letters while Araluen lay dead).
- **Feb 1870** — Clarke fails to win the National Gallery curatorship.
- **2 Feb 1870** — Kendall's daughter Araluen dies (dental fever aggravated by malnutrition) at Swan Street, Richmond; he cannot afford the funeral.
- **8 Feb 1870** — the Yorick suggestion-book solidarity: Gordon defends the broke Clarke (whoever mocked his name "ought to be kick'd"), and the grieving Kendall co-signs.
- **19 Feb 1870** — Clarke begins 'Old Stories Retold' (*Australasian*), his write-up of the convict records.
- **Mar 1870** — the *Australian Journal* launches the serial of *His Natural Life* (Clarke editor); it runs to twenty-seven episodes.
- **Mar 1870** — Kendall writes twice to the injured Gordon, thanking him and wounded by his silence; 4 Apr, Kendall's Town Hall cantata is commissioned (Horsley to set his poem).
- **9 Mar 1870** — Maggie sails to Robe (her father dying); Gordon "left alone."
- **12 Mar 1870** — Gordon's catastrophic last race on Prince Rupert: thrown twice, a very bad fall; friends had stationed themselves "in case he came to grief."
- **17–21 Mar 1870** — Gordon, bedridden and "hurt inside somewhere," writes Riddoch the bleakest letters of his life ("my wife would be better without me"); clings to the Esslemont hope but arranges its rents for Maggie.
- **Apr–Jul 1870** — Smith publishes seven of Kendall's prose pieces in *The Australasian* (incl. a review of Gordon's *Bush Ballads*); the Clarke rift partly mends.
- **Apr–Jun 1870** — Smith suppresses all mention of *His Natural Life* in *The Australasian* (his feud with Clarke); the *Argus* manager rebukes him.
- **Apr–May 1870** — Gordon readies *Bush Ballads* "with great effort... don't feel much interest"; English notice arrives (Baily's for Gordon, Swinburne for Kendall's *Leaves*); everything hangs on "if I can last out" for Esslemont.
- **7 May 1870** — Clarke, Gordon and Kendall all appear together in one issue of *The Australasian* — the braid in print.
- **17 May 1870** — the Supple shooting: a Yorick member fires on G. P. Smith in Spring Street, killing a bystander.
- **late May 1870** — Robertson declines *Bush Ballads*; Gordon resolves to self-publish it at his own cost.
- **Jun 1870** — Gordon drowning in debt (money-lenders at 90%); the Gordon–Kendall plagiarism dispute over 'Elijah' / 'The Road to Avernus' (the two dead-daughter elegies), resolved by Kendall's apology and public reparation.
- **4 Jun 1870** — Gordon learns the Esslemont claim has failed (the entail barred); the £2500-a-year hope dies — the travelling trunks already made for the trip home.
- **10–11 Jun 1870** — Clarke appointed Secretary to the Public Library trustees (beating "Rolf Boldrewood"); ends the 'Peripatetic Philosopher' — "sold my birthright of free speech for a mess of official pottage."
- **23 Jun 1870** — *Bush Ballads and Galloping Rhymes* published; Kendall's glowing *Australasian* review; Gordon's last day in town (Massina's, the Yorick smoking-room, a last drink with Kendall, Madden to St Kilda, the refused £100, the manuscript "present").
- **24 Jun 1870** — Gordon kisses the sleeping Maggie, takes his rifle, and shoots himself in the scrub by the Brighton beach. Found at nine, his hat beside him holding a knife, a pipe, tobacco, and one shilling. He was thirty-six.
- **25 Jun 1870** — Gordon's funeral at Brighton Cemetery (Maggie following against her friends' wishes; "the purest of white sand"); Kendall, shattered, stays away.
- **25 Jun 1870** — the inquest (held Saturday morning before the afternoon funeral): "unsound mind," the brain "much affected" by the steeplechase falls. [Wilding's date heading "27 Jun" is when The Argus and The Age reported it; the inquest itself was 25 June.]
- **28 Jun 1870** — *The Argus* reviews *Bush Ballads* enthusiastically, three days after the burial; the press laments a "mania for suicide." [Wilding's own text says "the day after the funeral" — an error: funeral was 25 June, review 28 June.]
- **Jul 1870** — Kendall resigns from the Yorick Club; his Melbourne period nears its end.
- **Jul–Oct 1870** — the Supple trial: Higinbotham pleads insanity; Supple, unrepentant, is sentenced to death, then reprieved.
- **2 Jul 1870** — Kendall's elegy 'In Memoriam' published; the racing world's tributes; *Bush Ballads* advertised "by the late A. L. Gordon."
- **6 Aug 1870 onward** — Clarke's Bullocktown stories begin (the "romance of reality"; his station years turned to realist fiction).
- **9 Aug 1870** — Kendall's 'Euterpe' performed at the opening of the new Melbourne Town Hall (he had beaten Gordon for the commission); the Sept dispute with Horsley follows.
- **Oct 1870** — a subscription raises Gordon's Brighton monument (the broken Doric column); *The Australasian* calls him "our only national poet." Major Baker finds the SA property mortgaged beyond its value; the family subscribes only £80 for Maggie.
- **(posthumous)** — the Gordon grave-cult grows (4,000 at the pilgrimage by 1921); a bust enters Poets' Corner, Westminster Abbey, 1934.
- **24 Oct 1870** — Kendall and Charlotte return to Sydney on the *Macedon*; he severs all ties with Melbourne.
- **Nov 1870** — Kendall arrested in Sydney for uttering a forged cheque; twopence-farthing in his pocket, "I am guilty."
- **Dec 1870** — tried; his family and doctors testify to his collapse (opium, drink, delusions, suicide attempts); found not guilty by reason of insanity and committed to the Darlinghurst receiving house.
- **24 Dec 1870** — a son, Frederick Clarence, born to the Kendalls; Charlotte takes the child and leaves Kendall.
- **1871–72** — Kendall's *Freeman's Journal* essays (reassessing Gordon and Clarke) and 'Men of Letters in New South Wales' — the colonial writer's doom in his own words.
- **26 Jan 1871** — Kendall's plight raised in the NSW Parliament ("punishing insanity as a crime").
- **13 Feb 1871** — Clarke attends a flogging at Melbourne gaol, research for *His Natural Life*.
- **Mar 1871** — Clarke's Bret Harte review: his realist-nationalist manifesto ("the material that lies ready to his hand").
- **5–29 Jul 1871** — Kendall committed to Gladesville asylum (melancholia, opium, threatened suicide), then discharged after three weeks, rational and writing again.
- **Aug–Sept 1871** — Walter Montgomery (Clarke's rival for Marian) shoots himself in London two days after marrying; Clarke writes a generous obituary.
- **Nov 1871** — Clarke's notebook: "Marian said she wished she had never married me"; *Old Tales of a Young Country* published for the Christmas market.
- **Dec 1871** — Trollope dines at the Yorick and lectures at the Town Hall (Barry in the chair).
- **1872** — Clarke's money troubles (a bounced cheque to Castieau; the copyright grievances); Duffy, premier since June 1871, voted out; Duffy reads the *His Natural Life* serial and gives Clarke a frank critique, and Clarke revises the plot.
- **1872** — the great revision: the "mother's honour" motive and the death of Rufus Dawes (the midnight walk with Moloney) reshape the book; Clarke publishes the autobiographical 'Human Repetends.'
- **1 Jan 1872** — Clarke resigns the Yorick; he and others form the short-lived Cave of Adullam (Birnie president).
- **16 Jan 1872** — James Smith resigns the *Australasian* editorship (his spiritualism); Clarke's contributions there resume.
- **Apr 1872 – Nov 1873** — Kendall's "lost years": near-unrecorded, sleeping rough, "my mind was unhinged"; Parkes finds him near death and takes him in.
- **Jun 1872** — *His Natural Life* serial completed; Massina hands Clarke the rights, and he sells them to Robertson for £25.
- **21 Aug 1872** — the novelist Walstab arrested at the Yorick for debt; Castieau receives his friend at the gaol.
- **Dec 1872** — the Carl blackbirding trial (Gordon's old partner Mount, and Dr Murray who did Gordon's post-mortem, implicated in a Pacific slaving atrocity).
- **1873** — Clarke's first collection, *Holiday Peak and Other Tales* (the title story his "Mount Might-ha'-been"); Trollope's *Australia and New Zealand* names Clarke and Gordon as the colony's two writers worth mention.
- **Jan 1873** — Clarke, deep in debt at Ledcourt, renounces an affair with his sister-in-law Rose ("stone instead of bread") and writes the alternative-life fantasy 'Holiday Peak.'
- **30 Apr – 7 Jul 1873** — Kendall readmitted to Gladesville (mania, "intemperance," starving, the murder-of-a-child delusion), then discharged "quite well."
- **Jul 1873** — Clarke's Port Arthur articles tell the truth of the Tasmanian "war of extermination," and draw angry denials.
- **Jul 1873** — Kendall, given a Grafton post, leaves the steamer at Newcastle and never reboards; walks toward Sydney, and at Gosford is taken in by the Fagans, who nurse him to recovery.
- **Sept 1873** — Clarke promoted to Sub-librarian (£400).
- **Nov 1873** — Clarke breaks with the *Argus* group; turns to the *Herald* and Lukin's *Brisbane Courier*, and leftward ('The Wicked World'); his "impending insolvency" noted.
- **1874–75** — Kendall at Gosford writes 'Mooni,' 'Names upon a Stone' and other late verse; the Yorick fades ("out at elbows").
- **1874–75** — Kendall, recovered at Gosford, renounces the Bohemian life ("rather turn bullock driver"; "let the Gods go hang").
- **Apr 1874** — *His Natural Life* published in book form (dedicated to Duffy; Dawes now drowns — "no return"); modest sales.
- **8 Jul 1874** — Clarke declared insolvent (liabilities £2,186); saved from eviction by his cousin Sir Andrew Clarke's power of attorney; moves to Brighton.
- **Aug 1874** — the Harwood-trial fallout: *The Argus* attacks Clarke for denying his authorship; he defends the journalist's anonymity as no lie.
- **8 Aug 1874** — Clarke's library (580 titles, the *His Natural Life* sources among them) auctioned for £250; the trustees request his resignation; the Harwood libel trial ends in farthing damages.
- **Sept 1874** — bankrupt, Clarke submits his Library resignation and is blackballed from the Yorick he founded; *Chidiock Tichbourne* begins serialization.
- **Dec 1874** — Bentley (London) demands a happy ending for *His Natural Life*; Clarke complies, but the tragic drowning is kept in print.
- **1875** — Kendall's Gosford poems ('Mooni,' 'Names upon a Stone') appear; he settles as a sober, useful citizen.
- **Feb 1875** — Clarke discharged from bankruptcy; the trustees return his resignation — he keeps his Library job.
- **Apr–Jun 1875** — Clarke writes the Eight Hour Day anniversary address; his schools' *History* commission is attacked in Parliament.
- **Aug 1875** — Clarke's father-in-law John Dunn, the veteran comic actor, dies in the street; his stone is cut with Hamlet's words for Yorick.
- **4 Sept 1875** — Clarke's 'Country Leisure' essay proclaims "a school of poetry peculiarly her own": "Weird Melancholy," "the Land of the Dawning," Kendall "the chief," Gordon "genuine," "the poet of our desolation."
- **16 Sept 1875** — Bentley's three-volume *His Natural Life* published in London to mixed but real notice (Hyndman: it "will live... long after his other work is forgotten").
- **Dec 1875** — the London *Daily Telegraph* invites Clarke to England; he takes the correspondent post but never returns home.
- **1876–81** — Kendall settles at Camden Haven as the Fagans' storekeeper, reunited with Charlotte and out of debt for the first time; the town is later renamed Kendall.
- **1876** — Clarke's preface to Gordon's *Sea-Spray and Smoke Drift* — the canonical bush-essay, "the beginnings of a national school of Australian poetry"; *His Natural Life* reaches Lord Lytton and the Prince of Wales.
- **1876** — Clarke scatters into unfinished work — the abandoned Rose novel *Felix and Felicitas*, a refused story collection, an unfinished play.
- **Jan 1876** — Frank Myers's portrait: Clarke in his clubland element at the Birdcage, and — out at Lyster's — revealed to loathe the very bush he immortalized.
- **1877** — Clarke's botched schools' *History* (eclipsed by the Sutherlands' 120,000-seller); *Four Stories High*; he joins Syme's liberal *Age*.
- **1877** — Sir Andrew does not renew the power of attorney; Clarke leaves Brighton for St Kilda, the usurers closing back in — the final decline begins.
- **Jan 1877** — Clarke's self-portrait to Cyril Hopkins: comfortable, indebted, declining the *Telegraph* and India, anxious about his masterpiece; "always in debt and contrive to live pleasantly."
- **17 Nov 1877** — Kendall's 'Old Manuscripts' disowns literature ("one who has left the fields of literature forever").
- **1878** — Kendall's mother Melinda jailed; the Camden Haven relapses; Bennett calls Kendall "an inspired blackfellow."
- **c.1878** — Kendall's scorching letter to Myers warns him off the literary life and names Clarke a compromised debtor; he dislikes Clarke and dismisses the Gordon preface.
- **Jan 1878** — Black Wednesday; Clarke defends the dismissals from the left, and makes his will.
- **Jun 1878** — a market cart throws Clarke on his head on the Cheltenham road — a second skull-blow.
- **Dec 1878** — Clarke declines the Parliamentary Librarianship, gambling on a Chief Librarianship he will never get; his *Alfred the Great* becomes his greatest stage success.
- **Apr 1879** — the Library trustees reprimand Clarke over anonymous press writing; his diary tracks fees against mounting debts.
- **Jun 1879** — Kendall's 'Gagging Bill' savages his old patron Parkes.
- **17 Sept 1879** — Kendall's cantata opens the Sydney International Exhibition and he wins the hundred-guinea prize; hailed "premier bard of the southern hemisphere."
- **Nov–Dec 1879** — Clarke's agnostic 'Civilisation without Delusion' provokes the Bishop of Melbourne; the *Melbourne Review* number with his reply is suppressed.
- **1880** — *Poems of the Late Adam Lindsay Gordon* (with Clarke's preface) becomes a bestseller (20,000 in a decade); Maggie, having sold the copyright cheap, gets nothing.
- **1880** — Kendall prepares *Songs from the Mountains* (his first book in a decade) and pleads with Parkes for a forest-ranger post; "I will not last for ever."
- **1880** — Clarke collapses (ophthalmia, measles, eight stone, bailiffs); the *Moonstone* adaptation fails; *Felix and Felicitas* stalls.
- **Jan 1880** — Clarke's localized *The Happy Land* satirizes the Berry ministry; the government bans it, making him "powerful enemies."
- **Jan 1880** — Kendall, writing to Swan, names "that wild bleak Bohemia south of the Murray" where "I went through Gethsemane"; ranks *His Natural Life* below Swan's novel; "I have not written to Clarke for years."
- **Feb 1880** — *Civilization Without Delusion* sells 10,000 copies; Barry warns Clarke, with deadly courtesy, that he cannot afford such indiscretion.
- **Jun 1880** — *The Bulletin* launches and begins building the three men's legend.
