- **Jul 1880** — financial necessity returns Marian to the stage in Clarke's plays; *Forbidden Fruit* is his last completed play.
- **Oct 1880** — reconciled at last, Kendall sends Fagan with a warm note to Clarke — but Clarke, ill and hounded, dodges the meeting. The two survivors reach toward each other and miss.
- **Oct–Nov 1880** — Clarke applies for the Chief Librarianship as his patrons vanish: Duffy in Europe, Barry and Lyster both dead within days.
- **Nov 1880 – early 1881** — the trustees pass Clarke over for the librarianship (Archer recommended, then rejected by the Government); his chances "utterly destroyed" by his own pen.
- **Dec 1880** — Kendall's *Songs from the Mountains* recalled over the libellous 'Ninian Melville,' then reissued; the row feeds demand.
- **(Queen's Birthday) 1881** — the coin-toss near Yarra Bend: "the mad asylum or the pauper grave."
- **Jan 1881** — Kendall moves to Cundletown for his Inspectorship of Forests; *The Bulletin* names him "the uncrowned laureate of Australia."
- **Feb 1881** — Kendall ill, "out of my power to write" — six years of overwork telling.
- **Mar 1881** — Clarke's generous *Leader* tribute to Kendall; Kendall sends his book and Clarke replies with "a genial letter" — the estranged survivors reconcile by post.
- **Apr 1881** — Clarke's cantata *At the Dawning* is staged as his own decline sets in; Mackinnon sees "an early break-up."
- **12 Apr 1881** — the day Kendall is gazetted to the Forests, Clarke swears his second insolvency affidavit.
- **May 1881** — Kendall's Inspectorship of State Forests confirmed (£500); his health deteriorating under the riding.
- **19 Jun 1881** — Kendall's son Orara born; he now has six children, like Clarke.
- **20 Jun 1881** — Clarke's estate sequestrated (Waxman's claim); 6 Jul, Bride made Librarian over him; 21 Jul, his resignation demanded again.
- **Aug 1881** — a benefit fund for the widow reaches over £1,000 (three times the fatal Waxman debt; Waxman the largest single donor); James Smith attacks Clarke as a cautionary tale.
- **2 Aug 1881** — Marcus Clarke dies, aged 35, his hand moving over blank paper as if still writing.
- **4 Aug 1881** — Clarke's funeral; the Yorick that blackballed him joins the cortege; Marian and six children left destitute.
- **Sept 1881** — Kendall's 'In Memoriam – Marcus Clarke': the braid mourns its own.
- **1882 onward** — *His Natural Life*, retitled *For the Term of His Natural Life*, becomes the national classic (stage, film, endless reprints) — the fame arriving too late.
- **Jan 1882** — Kendall's son Orara dies; Kendall failing — "I can hardly move the pen."
- **Mar 1882** — Kendall foresees his death: "before the year be out, I shall see Charles."
- **Jun 1882** — Kendall, ruined by the riding, collapses at Wagga; carried to Sydney, he enters St Vincent's on the 14th.
- **1 Aug 1882** — Henry Kendall dies of consumption, aged 43, a day short of a year after Clarke — holding on to the 1st "so you will get the salary."
- **3 Aug 1882** — buried at Waverley above the Pacific; only twenty mourners.
- **1884–86** — Louisa Lawson rescues the neglected grave and campaigns the monument into being; she has named her son Henry after the poet.
- **1886–1939** — Kendall enshrined: the *Poems*, then the *Golden Treasury* and the *Oxford Book of English Verse*.
- **1914 / 1919 / 1924** — the widows die: Marian Clarke, Maggie Gordon, Charlotte Kendall — outlasting their poets by decades.
- **1922** — Henry Lawson buried in the plot beside Kendall: the torch laid down by the founder's grave.

## 3. Character Dossiers

### Marcus Clarke

**Born** 24 Apr 1846, Kensington; only child. Mother dead of TB when he was 3; father (a barrister) collapsed into brain disease and died committed in an asylum, 1863 — the family pattern of mental ruin shadows him from the start. Educated Highgate School alongside Cyril Hopkins and **Gerard Manley Hopkins**.

- Shipped to Australia 1863 (age 17) at cousin Andrew Clarke's expense after the father's breakdown; £300 in hand.
- Family ballast: uncle **James Langton Clarke** (Melbourne judge — the 1863 telegram episode); cousin **Andrew Clarke** (Royal Engineers, first Surveyor-General of Victoria); great-uncle **Sir Andrew Clarke** (Governor of WA, d. 1847).
- Early colonial life: bank clerk, gold-buyer, station hand (Holt's place), a ruinous Queensland overlanding expedition (1867), then theatrical critic on *The Argus*.

**Voice samples:**
- GMH on the young Clarke: "a kaleidoscopic, particoloured, harlequinesque, thaumatropic being."
- Self-portrait at 21: "What a life I have had! Bank clerk, gold buyer, squatter, overlander, play writer, author and man of means! ... Vive la bagatelle! If I had only saved the money I have made! Lord, what fools these mortals be!"
- On the Queensland ordeal: "People say that I look ten years older after this infernal Queensland business. I feel a hundred!"
- Literary affectation, quoting Poe (to Cyril, Feb 1867): "Tonight my heart is light, no dirge will I upraise."

**The bank (1864).** Uncle James parked him as a clerk in the Bank of Australasia. Useless at the ledgers but irrepressible — burlesque ballads and Horatian verse to amuse the other clerks, correspondence dashed off so fast and so cavalierly the bank was threatened with lawsuits — and eased out within months. He and GMH both loathed Highgate; Cyril was the one schoolfellow he kept up with.

**Voice samples (bank/school):**
- Turner: a clerk who could turn a joke into "excellent Horatian verse" shouldn't be set to writing pass-books — rather than see "Pegasus in harness," the office covered his work.
- Clarke on his dismissal ("On Business Men"): the manager "loved me as his own brother, and that I wore the neatest waistcoats he had ever seen, but that my genius was evidently fettered in a bank... in short, I was not a Business Man."
- On school ("Speech Days and School Days," 1867): "The happiest days of one's life one's school days? A thousand times no."

**Jackarooing in western Victoria (1864–67).** Judge Clarke sent him to two family-linked sheep stations — Swinton (manager Joseph Holt), then neighbouring Ledcourt, where John Holt made him overseer. The effete English boy hardened into a fine horseman; he read voraciously (English magazines, French novels by the bundle), wrote nearly every night but showed no one, and quarrelled, drank, and outran his creditors until his uncle's allowance squared the slate. The long solitary rides through the Grampians fed the bush feeling of "Pretty Dick." By March 1866 he was publishing in the *Australian Monthly Magazine* under his own name and as **Mark Scrivener** — a nickname GMH had coined for him at school. His one stated ambition: to "become favourably known as an author." Standish's standing offer of a mounted-police commission (weighed, then let go) also placed him among the police-literary set — poets Richard Horne and **Adam Lindsay Gordon**, novelist George Walstab — an early thread toward the braid.

**Voice samples (station years):**
- On being colonised: "One gets such an immense amount of humbug forced out of one by the force-pump of society here."
- Horsemanship: "I... can pick up my hat from the ground at full gallop... These things are considered nothing here."
- Weighing the police offer, on all he'd left: "What a change from all my old plans and hopes; the Foreign Office, jollity, good society, hunters, crack balls and diplomacy!"

**The Queensland expedition (1867).** Between the two Hopkins letters, Clarke left Holt's station and went overlanding to Queensland with five other men. It was a catastrophe: floods drowned the cattle, the horses died, ague-fever took the whole party, and each man lost about £300. One of them — the Hungarian, Max Kabat — died and was buried in the bush. Clarke spent three months up there, then limped into Adelaide in rags, waited on a remittance, and came up to Melbourne hollowed out. This is the ordeal behind the "I feel a hundred" letter — the bush as ruin, not romance.

**Melbourne, the literary launch (1867–68).** Befriended at Ledcourt by the freethinking surgeon Robert Lewins, Clarke is talked into a berth on *The Argus* and its weekly *The Australasian* — "the clubman's paper" and the chief literary authority in the colonies, the print common-ground he shares with Gordon and Kendall. He moves in with **Alfred Telo** over a Collins Street sewing-machine shop, starting at 30 shillings a week (Wilding flags the gap with the £300 Clarke had boasted of). His two showpiece essays — on Balzac and on Doré — announce his enthusiasms; Balzac is idol *and* cautionary tale, a life Clarke read as "one long struggle with debt and difficulty" and then proceeded to live. His first big splash, the 1867 Spring Meeting report, watches the crowd not the horses and lands him in an anti-Semitism row.

**Voice samples (Melbourne launch):**
- On Balzac as warning: "The struggle for fame and bread killed him. The pages of his finest works are written with his life-blood... one long struggle with debt and difficulty."
- On the Telo ménage: "I flung my 30 shillings a week into the ménage, and sent my modest trunk into the back attic."

**The Peripatetic Philosopher & freelance life (1867–68).** Clarke's breakthrough was 'The Peripatetic Philosopher', a weekly column of "humorous moralizing" in *The Australasian* signed "**Q**" (the cattle-brand of the station where he'd learned "colonial experience"). It launched on 23 Nov 1867 to coincide with Prince Alfred's arrival — "Though Bohemian I am loyal," Q announced, before mocking the celebrations — and ran, hugely popular, for two and a half years. His staff career ended around the same time, in character: deputed to review a show, he wrote it up without properly attending, only for the performer to have been indisposed (Mackinnon) or a song unfairly slated when the singer was off sick (Bright). The rival paper exposed the phantom notice and Clarke was dropped from the *Argus* reporting staff — "Clarke found himself suffering from a cold still more severe." He freelanced for the rest of his life.

**The man (Bright's and Turner's portraits, ~1868).** Charles Bright first met him at the Café de Paris, introduced by the actor **Walter Montgomery** ("Charley, my boy; you ought to know Marcus"): a slight, almost feminine dandy "below the middle height," beautiful clear-cut features and a soft moustache — who became "literally a giant" only with a pen, so readers always pictured a big man. He affected a cynical, blasé satiety (skin-deep, Bright decided) and was sipping absinthe as an experiment. He spoke with a slight Charles-Lamb stammer — which Brodzky says Clarke blamed on a childhood kick to the head from a horse — that "gave an added charm" and unexpected point to his jokes; among friends the first raconteur in the room ("his imagination too active to be limited by the stern necessities of facts"), but mute "as an oyster" among the uncongenial.

**Voice samples (Melbourne man):**
- The drug experiment (to Bright): "I'm experimenting with it. They say it'll drive a fellow mad in a month and I want to find out if that's a fact. I've tried opium-smoking, and rather like that."
- Q's mock-loyalty: "Though Bohemian I am loyal."

**Drugs as experiment (1867–68).** Clarke approached intoxicants as a self-experimenter. In December 1867 he sampled hashish under medical supervision and wrote it up as 'Cannabis Indica (A Psychological Experiment)' (*Colonial Monthly*, Feb 1868) — the suppressed "Dr —, of Collins Street" (probably **James Edward Neild**) supplying the dose and the clinical notes (pulse readings, the slide into stupor, "I am thinking. I shall be back directly"), after which Clarke dictated a story while drugged. "The drug seems to unlock the doors of thought," he wrote — eighty-five years before Huxley's *Doors of Perception*. He kept it up (a character "experiments upon myself" with hashish in 1872; a "Dr Cannabis" appears in 1873). The wider frame is the blurb's intoxication thread: opium was barely controlled (80 opium shops in Victoria by 1868), and **Gordon used opiates**, **Kendall laudanum and chlorodyne**.

**The city explorer (1868–69).** Clarke's other vein was nocturnal urban exploration — Mayhew-and-Dickens exposé. He wandered Melbourne hunting "faces," insisting a Collingwood bus driver was Julius Caesar and a Bourke Street barmaid "the positive presentment of Cleopatra." It bore fruit in 'Night Scenes in Melbourne' (*Argus*, Feb–Mar 1868) — a tour of the Chinese Quarter guided by the detective **Fook Shing** (an ex-mandarin opium-dealer turned policeman) through gambling house, opium den, eating house and brothel — and the six-part 'Lower Bohemia' (*Australasian*, 1869), which split the bohemian world into the glittering "upper" Bohemia of supper-parties and the "real," destitute lower Bohemia "from which there was no return." Wilding reads the recurring theme as Clarke half-warning himself of his own fate — the slide from the Bohemia of choice into the Bohemia of ruin.

**The Café de Paris and the bohemian round.** The hub of Clarke's Melbourne was the **Café de Paris**, the lavish upstairs restaurant of the Theatre Royal in Bourke Street — dining recesses, billiards, a Yankee cocktail-bar — and below it the vast hall of bars the wits called the **"Saddling Paddock,"** where barmaids in tights served two hundred smoking, drinking men and prostitutes worked the crowd. Clarke painted it in 'A Day in Melbourne' (1865): the actors (Barry Sullivan, Charles Young), the miserly ex-billsticker millionaire Ambrose Hawke (really Kyte) mopping his sickly face and guarding his shillings, the notorious "Sydney Pigeon," and — in a sudden compassionate turn — the silk-clad governesses "sent out here by the shipload" and "compelled to save themselves from a miserable death by a life of vice." A decade and a half later he elegised the café as the **"Café Lutetia,"** resort of Upper Bohemia, in the memoir that gives the book its epigraph: living in Fig Tree Court with "Savage" (Telo), writing for the "Peacock" and "Screechowl" (the *Argus* and *Age*), pawning a signet-ring "fifty times" to dine on venison and champagne.

**Voice samples (the bohemian round):**
- The epigraph (Café Lutetia memoir): "The mornings were spent in scribbling, the afternoons in tobacco, the evenings in dinner, theatre and gaslight. I fear we did not lead virtuous lives."
- The pawning life: a pawnbroker "has had an old signet-ring of mine there fifty times... each time that he 'advanced' upon it, Savage and I have dined upon venison and champagne."
- The elegiac note: "we lived every minute of our lives in those days!"

**The dispossessed gentleman (formation & temperament).** Clarke's bohemianism sat on a conservative, patrician core: he wrote for the Tory *Argus*, not the liberal *Age*, and never stopped mourning the life he'd lost. His plan had been the Foreign Office — attaché at Paris or Vienna, literary connections, perhaps a novel of his own — until his father's sudden collapse and death cancelled everything: "all is changed and I am cast out like a leper into the wilderness." The army (the family tradition) was closed to him anyway: a childhood bone disease had left his left arm several inches short, hidden in a pocketed sac coat — strikingly, **Kendall too was arm-maimed**, his right hand crushed by a slammed door, so that he wrote left-handed. The father's estate, expected at £70,000, was found in chaos — cheque-books showing large untraceable withdrawals, the Irish house sold, almost nothing left for Marcus. The dispossession bred the temperament he dissected to Cyril: a dread of sinking into colonial "vulgarity," a cultivated cynicism, recklessness with limb and money — but not, he insisted, with drink. His grand cousin **Andrew Clarke** (first Surveyor-General of Victoria, railway- and telegraph-builder, nearly premier, later Governor of the Straits Settlements — "Spicy Andrew," Clarke Quay's namesake) embodied the establishment success Marcus might have had.

**Voice samples (temperament):**
- The lost life: "all is changed and I am cast out like a leper into the wilderness."
- The dread of vulgarity: "I see daily before me a pit into which I dread to fall; the pit of vulgarity, ignorance, slovenliness and radicalism... I dread lest I become like others."
- Self-portrait: "I ride hard because I don't care about a broken limb. I play hard because I don't care for money. But thank God! I don't drink hard. Drinking is the curse of the place!"

**The mother he never knew.** Clarke's losses began at the root: his mother died when he was three, and he grew up knowing almost nothing of her — "I never saw my mother, nor a portrait of her; knew none of her relations, did not even know her maiden name." He half-built her into a romance (a convent-educated Catholic girl of seventeen, echoing Bulwer-Lytton's *A Strange Story*); the truer account — via Cyril Hopkins and cousin Andrew — has her a Kensington tradesman's daughter, Amelia Elizabeth Matthews, whom his father educated and married. The unknown mother is a deep blank at the centre of him — and a point of kinship with Gordon (see themes).

**The Melbourne Club and the road to debt (1868).** In May 1868 Clarke was elected to the **Melbourne Club** — the conservative, rich man's establishment venue his uncle and cousin already belonged to (40-guinea entrance, blackballing, "our lot"). Whether the Club ruined him is a documented dispute: Mackinnon blamed its "glitter" for drawing the impulsive bohemian into extravagance and the hands of usurers (a charge quietly cut from the 1890 reprint); Turner — a banker who knew exactly what 60% interest meant — called that "wholly indefensible," locating the ruin in Clarke's "own weak will and self-indulgence." Either way the mechanism was the same: living far above his income, he entangled himself with money-lenders at crushing rates and "worked for years to pay the interest to his bondmasters." The first bankruptcy (1874) was already taking shape.

**The Colonial Monthly: editor at 22 (Mar 1868).** In 1868 Clarke inherited "a sum of money which was to have proved my knowledge of the sheep-farming business" — and promptly sank it into a magazine instead. He took over the *Colonial Monthly* (founded 1865 as the *Australian Monthly Magazine*, bought from its printers Clarson, Massina) and from March 1868 was named "proprietor and editor," aged twenty-two; he slipped his own poem 'The Lady of Lynn' — the one Gerard Manley Hopkins had admired and illustrated — into his first issue. The venture was both his literary coming-of-age and a financial wound: his 1875 insolvency affidavit records spending "more than one thousand pounds" on it, his partners failing to pay their share, and his borrowing "at heavy interest to meet it" for no return. (Who those partners were stays murky — Walstab, Shillinglaw, the police-magistrate Drummond and possibly Telo are all named in different accounts.) The magazine's office in lower Elizabeth Street became a gathering-place for his circle — the editorial "den" frescoed by McCrae, a messenger nicknamed "Shrimp," and a standing arrangement for beer to be fetched from the Duke of Rothesay across the road: a working rehearsal for the Yorick Club to come. Crucially, the *Colonial Monthly* is where the braid first converges in print — Clarke's fiction, Kendall's verse and prose, and reviews of Gordon all sharing its pages through 1867–68. (His own first published story had appeared in the magazine back in March 1866 — 'The Mantuan Apothecary,' working the alchemical motifs that would later become the structural theme of *His Natural Life*; alchemy had fascinated Clarke since school, where Hopkins drew an 'Alchemist in the City,' possibly to illustrate it.)

**The death of Drummond (May 1868) — "Death has been busy among us."** Clarke was a witness and reluctant accomplice in the strangest of the season's deaths. At the Café de Paris one Friday evening the fearless police magistrate **William Murray Drummond** — Clarke's friend and the *Colonial Monthly*'s likely principal backer — declared he would expose the snake-showman Joseph Shires as a humbug by having himself bitten and sending the snake to Professor Halford. Telo refused to go ("Don't be a fool, Drummond"); Drummond turned to Clarke, who went along but spent the evening trying to talk him out of it ("It is madness to try and be bitten by a snake"), only to be laughed off ("All right old fellow, he will never let his snake kill me... Don't be frightened, my boy, I am not a fool"). Bound by a promise of secrecy, Clarke let it drop and slipped into the theatre; Drummond went off alone at nine, was bitten on the arm by a tiger snake at Garton's Hotel, swooned, roused Halford after midnight with the severed snake and a pencilled note, and died the next evening. Clarke lost both a friend and, it seems, his magazine's backer — amid a death-crowded fortnight (Gordon's fall and Annie, Michael's drowning, the actor Charles Vincent thrown from his horse). "Death has been busy among us," he wrote on 16 May; "men have almost begun to look in each other's faces and wonder who is to be the next."

**The Yorick Club: founder and first secretary (1868).** The Yorick was Clarke's club above all — the institution at the centre of his Melbourne and of this novel. It grew out of the late-night gatherings at the Café de Paris and editor F. W. Haddon's Saturday "symposiums," formalised in mid-1868 as a club for "literary men and those taking a special interest in literature, art or science." Clarke was its prime mover and Honorary Secretary, signing the invitations and chairing the famous naming debate: he argued all night for **"Golgotha"** (the place of skulls), having brought along a brown, mahogany-coloured skull given him by Dr Patrick Moloney and set it on the mantel with a churchwarden pipe between its teeth — but the artist Carrington's "Alas, poor Yorick!" won the room. The club room (over the *Punch* office, 74 Collins Street, £1/week) was furnished in studied bohemian poverty — cane chairs, a deal table, cocoanut matting, spittoons, newspaper-bundle seats — with Mueller's beefsteak suppers next door till 2 a.m. Confirmed secretary 13 June (to 2 October), Clarke waged a running comic war in the suggestion book — damning the "Abomination of Desolation" eating-room and "the worst" Yorick dinner, observing that "a club in which one can get nothing to eat on Sundays is a curious sort of place," and that some tempers were "not worth keeping" (one member dubbed him the "boy nuisance"). Thwarted on "Golgotha," he reportedly got huffy, took his skull and vanished for days — handing it to the actor Walter Montgomery, who used it for Hamlet's "Alas, poor Yorick" at the Theatre Royal, opposite the young **Marian Dunn** as Ophelia: the woman Clarke would marry fifteen months later.

**Voice samples (the Yorick):**
- On the blackball: "Why does a man go to a club — to meet those people he wishes to see, and to avoid those people he does not wish to see."
- The suggestion book: "a club in which one can get nothing to eat on Sundays is a curious sort of place"; some tempers were "not worth keeping."

**The near-fatal fall (June 1868).** Weeks after founding the Yorick, Clarke nearly died. Out riding — by Mackinnon's account dared to take a fence on the way back from a hounds meet, by Montgomery's simply schooling horses at Goyder's stables (Mackinnon later quietly dropped the meet detail) — he charged the timber, the horse failed it, and a kick to the head from the struggling animal fractured his skull. He lay unconscious for about three weeks ("I have been as nearly dead as it is possible for a man to be"), and carried a visible dent at the back of his skull and sudden memory-blanks for the rest of his life. **Telo** nursed him "like a woman," reading him Russian and German novels translated aloud on the spot; Drs Aubrey Bowen and Fitzgerald "literally" brought him back to life. The Peripatetic Philosopher column went dark for five weeks; **Walstab** kept the *Colonial Monthly* going and wrote the July–August episodes of Clarke's serial *Long Odds* (hence the book's dedication to him). The injury mirrors Gordon's of three months before — a horse-kick to a skull already "knocked about" — leaving a second of the three poets permanently marked by a horse.

**Bounce-back (Aug–Oct 1868).** Recovered, Clarke resumed the Peripatetic Philosopher on 15 August with a black joke — that he'd been away gathering material for "Two Months at Pentridge, by the author of A Summer at the Hulks" — an idle gag that came strangely true when he buried himself in the convict records for *His Natural Life* two years on. He told Cyril he was prolific and prospering: five leading articles a week across three dailies, theatrical criticism, editing the magazine, ~£15 a week. The workload may explain his resignation as Yorick secretary on 2 October — accepted with the committee's regret at losing "an able and efficient officer... thoroughly identified with the club since its formation."

**The race-writer, and his portrait of "Reckless" (late 1868).** Clarke's "P.P." byline extended to a horsey series, 'Sketches in Pig-Skin,' worldly satirical race-course pieces for *The Australasian*. One (7 Nov 1868) is invaluable: it portrays Gordon, under the name **"Reckless,"** with Clarke himself in the saddle alongside, struggling to hold him back. Reckless is "a tall thin man who looks like an ancient Viking and rides like an Assyrian of old," seized by the "madness" of the man who owns a jumping mare — risking his neck "twenty times a day," set to fly any impossible obstacle until "it was all Marcus Clarke could do to induce him not to make the attempt." Clarke's verdict is the death-drift seen plain by a friend: "I am certain Reckless would ride against Death on the pale horse if the grim old fellow would only give him six pounds and a bit of a start." Contemporary, affectionate, and clear-eyed about where Gordon was heading.

**Voice samples (the race-writer):**
- His Gordon ("Reckless"): "a tall thin man who looks like an ancient Viking and rides like an Assyrian of old."
- On Gordon's death-drive: "I am certain Reckless would ride against Death on the pale horse if the grim old fellow would only give him six pounds and a bit of a start."

**Seen from outside: "Marcellus Clodius" (1868–69).** A useful external portrait survives, from an enemy's pen. In a series of *Australasian* essays signed "the Hermit" (probably the departing R. H. Horne, whom Clarke had repeatedly mocked — "the feeble candle-glow of Mr Horne's intellect"), Clarke appears as "Marcellus Clodius," "that bright particular star of the evening": clever, full of "boyish blaséism," quoting "scripture and Voltaire with equal facility," Musset and Balzac "at his fingers' ends" — but armed with "a fatal fluency of pen," "a memory that supplies the place of profound research," an eye for "the joints in an adversary's harness," and a gift for "seizing on the ludicrous side of everyone" — "as he is strong he is not merciful." Hostile, but acute: the merciless, magpie-brilliant young raider his targets feared.

**The playwright's debut — and a rift over Marian (Dec 1868–69).** Clarke turned to the stage. After amateur turns at the Theatre Royal, his first play, **'Foul Play'** (an adaptation of Reade and Boucicault, a tale of financial chicanery part-set in Australia), ran for a week at the Duke of Edinburgh theatre in December 1868 — a modest success, warmly reviewed (the author too "modest" to take his curtain call; Neild defending even the improbable "carrier duck"). The same month **Marian Dunn** — the Ophelia to Montgomery's Hamlet, to whom Clarke had given the Yorick skull — took a benefit and retired from the stage; seven months later she married Clarke, and her withdrawal may mark their engagement. It may also explain the cooling between Clarke and the actor **Walter Montgomery**, once warm friends (Clarke's admiring notices had won Montgomery's regard), who "fell out afterwards, for some foolish reason" — possibly a rivalry over Marian.

**The Montgomery war (April–May 1869).** Whatever the private cause, it broke into open war that autumn. From the safe distance of Sydney, Montgomery accused the Melbourne press of venality; Clarke, who had been one of his first friends in the colony, answered in a relishing series of *Australasian* and *Argus* letters. Montgomery dragged in everything: that Clarke had read him a Jesuit-priest play, **'The Abbé's Plot'** (from Dumas), which he'd advised altering; that Clarke had once confided an overdraft of "some hundred and odd pounds" at the Union Bank (insinuating venal poverty); that a gold scarf-ring he'd given Clarke "as a remembrance" was really a bribe. Clarke skewered each point — the overdraft "secured to the satisfaction of the bank" and mentioned only in "a laughing conversation" at his own dinner table; the ring accepted "in perfect good faith" — and closed by declaring Montgomery "unfit for the society of gentlemen" and threatening law. (In the crossfire Montgomery confirms a useful fact: that he and Clarke "both received serious injuries whilst schooling our horses at Goyder's" — pinning the site of Clarke's near-fatal fall.) It is Clarke the polemicist in full: wounded honour, forensic wit, and no mercy — exactly the "as he is strong he is not merciful" man the Hermit had drawn.

**The loyal hub: inducting Kendall (1869).** For all his combativeness, Clarke was the braid's loyal centre. When Kendall reached Melbourne in 1869, it was Clarke who inducted him into the Yorick and "repeatedly and earnestly tried to help" him "when that erratic genius fell upon evil times"; he was "devotedly attached to Gordon," quoting his verse, prizing his friendship, and would be "quite overwhelmed with grief" by his death. Kendall repaid the kindness with an ambivalent portrait — Clarke as "**Perks**," "not a genius, but something more than a brilliant mime... good stuff too, only he himself did not appear to value it," forever hiding his real gifts under "the borrowed and theatrical garb" of the "blasé libertine." It is the gloomy native poet's-eye view of the dazzling English flâneur: admiring, exasperated, a little resentful.

**The published author, and a souring club (1869).** Clarke's books began to appear: in April 1869 his first, **'The Peripatetic Philosopher'** by "Q" (a selection from his column, from George Robertson, the pioneer Melbourne publisher); in June his first novel, **'Long Odds'**; and from 12 June a six-part **'Lower Bohemia'** series for *The Australasian*, deepening his earlier night-walks through Melbourne's low life (a book he planned but never finished). Meanwhile he was tiring of his own club — "that mysterious body... supposed by some who don't belong to it to be composed of the cleverest men on this side of the equator" — as the Yorick relaxed its rules and filled with doctors and lawyers (even the Duke of Edinburgh and, gloriously, the police chief Standish, who justified himself: "am I not the editor of the *Police Gazette*!"). His bohemian self-image he set down in album verses that June — "only brawling born of bitter beer, / And chorused with the clink and clash of glasses" — too good to waste, so he recycled them into *His Natural Life*.

**'Long Odds' and the seed of His Natural Life (July 1869).** Clarke's first novel, *Long Odds*, concluded its serial run and appeared in book form (Clarson, Massina) — dedicated "To G.A.W." (Walstab), who had ghost-written chapters fifteen to eighteen while Clarke lay concussed. The reviews were kind, but the sharpest came anonymously from the visiting English socialist **H. M. Hyndman**, who praised the book yet named its fatal flaw: it described "scenes in England which the writer had never looked upon," life known "only by hearsay," and urged Clarke to "turn his attention to the life and character of his own native country." Clarke never forgot it: years later, publishing *His Natural Life*, he sent Hyndman a copy. (Clarke himself dismissed the novel — "the greatest trash" — and relished its central irony: "They hate me all round, and the chapters they praise were the ones I never wrote" — Walstab's.)

**Voice sample:** "They hate me all round, and the chapters they praise were the ones I never wrote."

**Marriage to Marian Dunn (22 July 1869).** Clarke married the actress Marian O'Donoghue ("Dunn") at St Peter's — both twenty-three, the only witnesses her parents and best man B. F. Kane. How they met is unknown (the theatre, *Foul Play*, Walstab or Montgomery). The wedding was pure Clarke: the knot tied, he left his bride with her parents and went off to find them lodgings — "how characteristic of him." Marian had been a success opposite Montgomery, who had urged her to tour England and America with him and who, tellingly, quit Melbourne for Adelaide the day before her wedding ("after making an ass of himself," Gordon noted) — quiet confirmation of the rivalry behind the Clarke–Montgomery war. It was, like Gordon's, a "lost-caste" marriage, and a marriage *out*: as Gordon the Scot married a Scottish girl, Clarke — who "had Irish blood in my veins" — married an Irish one (daughter of the comedian John Dunn, whose memoirs Clarke was then revising). And there may have been a deeper pull: Clarke's own mother, dead when he was small, had come from a theatrical background and "had been on the stage herself" — "the loveliest girl I ever saw," a guest recalled. In marrying a lovely actress, was the motherless Clarke half-consciously following his father, and reaching back toward the mother he'd lost?

**Letting the Colonial Monthly go (August 1869).** The magazine that had been the braid's shared print-home never paid. After eighteen months as proprietor-editor — having made it, by the printers' own account, "the best 'high class' magazine in the southern hemisphere" but no commercial success — Clarke sold the *Colonial Monthly* to Shillinglaw, and turned to a short-lived new venture, *Humbug*. "We must both write like hell," he told Birnie just before the end. "I'm afraid we do," Birnie answered. The freelance treadmill, closing in.

**Humbug, and a collaboration with Kendall (Sept 1869–Jan 1870).** Having lost on the *Colonial Monthly*, Clarke promptly launched another — *Humbug: A Weekly Illustrated Journal of Satire* (first issue 8 September), out to denounce hypocrisy, cant and "the god of the guineas and dollars." Crucially, he ran it with Kendall: "In conjunction with Marcus Clarke I edited *Humbug*," Kendall recalled, "the most brilliant satirical paper ever published in Victoria" — a rare documented working partnership between two of the principals, each week building a theme around a Carrington cartoon, a Clarke essay and a Kendall poem. The targets were social: usury, the rich-poor gap ('Fashion and Famine'), spiritualism — and 'On Borrowing Money' ("It is no use borrowing, if you mean to pay," from a notorious debtor). The keenest irony of the venture is that *Humbug* attacked the very vices its makers embodied: Clarke (who would list a £150 wine-and-spirits debt) wrote 'The Curse of the Country' — "We are a nation of Drunkards... King Nobbler rules over us" — beside Kendall's 'The Demon of Drink,' even as Kendall's own drinking was starting to be noted in the press, "notably in *Humbug* itself." Like everything else, it lost money; it folded with the *Colonial Monthly* in January 1870.

**Another feud: Clarke vs James Smith (1869).** *Humbug* gave Clarke a fresh enemy. It sniped at the older, conservative critic James Smith (Neild's 'The Literary Humbug' mocked Smith's obscure drapery-or-schoolmaster origins), and Smith's rival weekly *Touchstone* hit back, branding Clarke 'The Literary Cad' and the Peripatetic Philosopher the "Perambulating Popinjay." Beneath the squibs lay the familiar class fault-line — the upper-middle-class metropolitan Clarke against the provincial petit-bourgeois Smith, both ex-*Argus* drama critics (Clarke sacked, Smith kept on). Another of the serial press-feuds in which Clarke's wit made him foes.

**The death of Humbug, and a farewell (January 1870).** *Humbug* died on 12 January 1870, and Clarke buried it in a valedictory that is both brilliant comedy and naked confession, 'On the Pleasures of Editorship': "editing as a business don't pay"; he had been chosen, he said, for "my habitual melancholy and an incurable tendency to spirituous liquors"; one contributor "went melancholy mad" (Kendall may have winced); and the killer line — "no man ever conducted a comic journal successfully unless he was either a capitalist or an habitual drunkard. Nobody would lend me the money to become the former, so I made an attempt to become the latter, and brought on five useless fits of delirium tremens." He reckoned he had made "at least nine deadly enemies." Turner agreed Clarke had "an unhappy talent for alienating friends... the caustic cynicism of his pen" — a talent on show that winter in the drink-soaked parodies he ran of Kendall ('Glycera': "give me whiskey over proof") and Gordon, which helped sour his friendship with the touchy Kendall even as they advertised both as celebrities worth parodying.

**Tasmania, Port Arthur, and the birth of His Natural Life (1870).** Out of the wreck of the magazines came Clarke's masterpiece. Overworked and ordered a holiday he could not afford, he turned the trip into an assignment — talking Massina into £100 to "write up the criminal records" and travelling to Tasmania with the *Argus* editor Haddon in January 1870. At Port Arthur (visited on Foundation Day, 26 January) he found his great subject. His account is steeped in horror: the leaden sky and the Island of the Dead, the conviction that "I should go mad were I condemned to such a life," the shame of an "unmanly curiosity" in staring at the chained men, and the unforgettable madman in the cell — a poacher transported at thirteen, now "a gibbering animal" poking a "nail-bitten hairy finger, like the toe of an ape" through the peep-hole, of whom the warder said "he'd best be dead." The published records sickened him further — they revealed "conditions of unspeakable horror... he dared not commit to print" (Turner). He began writing them up at once as 'Old Stories Retold' (*Australasian*, from February), and in March the *Australian Journal* launched the serial of ***His Natural Life*** — the convict novel Hyndman's review had all but commissioned ("write your own country"), planned for twelve parts and running to twenty-seven. The writing was chaos: chronically late, padded out in larger type, once replaced by a printed apology to readers, until Massina resorted to locking him in a room — "under the benign influences of a pipe, &c, &c., and a lock on the door" — to get the matter out of him.

**A harsher note: the dispute with Kendall (early 1870).** The loyal hub had a colder side. Clarke had genuinely helped Kendall — paying a *Colonial Monthly* debt, lending him £5 to work off in *Humbug* — but as both men sank, a tangle of debts, broken promises and Shillinglaw's mischief turned the friendship sour and, at the worst possible moment, cruel. Kendall told James Smith that when he wrote begging 30 shillings during his child's final illness, Clarke did not answer; and "on a day when he knew that our child was lying dead at home, he wrote me two letters that were brutal," demanding an account. Provoked partly by "that unprincipled blackguard Shillinglaw" and by Kendall's own double-pledging of the debt (orders written to both Hugh George and Brough Smyth on the same £5), it is still the ugliest moment in Clarke's record — the generous friend turned hard while a grieving father stood penniless beside a dead child. Hold it against the loyalty: Clarke was both.

**Security at last: the Public Library (June 1870).** Six months married and sick of freelance precariousness, Clarke went after a steady billet — failing for the National Gallery curatorship in February, then winning, on 10 June 1870, the post of Secretary to the Trustees of the Melbourne Public Library (£175–200 a year), with Sir Redmond Barry's backing. He beat, among others, T. A. Browne — the future "Rolf Boldrewood" of *Robbery Under Arms* — in what was effectively a contest between the colony's two coming novelists for a library clerkship. It was the making of him: he held it for the rest of his life, with light hours, "ample leisure" to write, and a great library's shelves at his elbow (the raw material that fed *His Natural Life* and his *History*). The cost was his column: ending the 'Peripatetic Philosopher' (a public servant could not write political journalism), he quipped that he had "sold my birthright of free speech for a mess of official pottage." But he kept writing prolifically around the job — the *His Natural Life* serial, 'Old Stories Retold,' the Bullocktown sketches.

**Life at the Library (1870s).** The post suited his double nature. The levity is well-attested: he parked his unfinished cigar in the mouth of a green metal lion on the library steps as a signal to friends that "Marcus was within," and once dashed Shillinglaw a note — "For God's sake come up to the Library and have a drink!... HOT COPPERS" (slang for a morning-after thirst). But he also took the work seriously: his briefcase was lettered MARCUS CLARKE PUBLIC LIBRARY MELBOURNE, he compiled the library's Bibliographical Catalogue in his own hand, collated every book for seven years, and gave his books' title-pages his official title. He used the antiquarian journal *Notes and Queries* both to display his scholarship and to promote his friends — drawing attention there to Gordon's 'Sick Stockrider.' His patron was the formidable **Sir Redmond Barry**, who had got him the post and treated him with a mix of "parental" interest and lofty correction — most memorably rebuking the casual librarian for the cabbage-tree hat (Pentridge-made) he liked to wear: "I scarcely think your hat... is exactly suited to the position you occupy." And through the trustees he came to know **Charles Gavan Duffy**, the Irish nationalist, who read the serial of *His Natural Life* in manuscript at Clarke's request and "wrote him frankly the impression it made."

**Grieving Gordon (1870 onward).** Clarke may have written one of the unsigned death notices; if he wrote nothing under his name, it was not for want of feeling. Mackinnon, who knew them both, thought Clarke and Gordon kindred beneath their surface differences — "both morbidly sensitive; both broodingly pathetic; both sarcastically humorous; both socially reckless... sons of genius and children of impulse." The grief lasted the rest of Clarke's life: in low spirits he would return to 'The Sick Stockrider,' and Mackinnon preserved the image of him "seated at the piano humming these lines to his own accompaniment, while the tears kept rolling down his cheeks" — "I should live the same life over, if I had to live again; / And the chances are I go where most men go." The warmest bond in the braid, mourned at the keyboard for a decade.

**Bullocktown: the romance of reality (Aug 1870 onward).** Appraising the newly dead Dickens that July, Clarke praised "the romance of reality" — men and manners "not as they should be, but as they are" — and promptly enacted his own. From 6 August he began the Bullocktown stories (drawn from his bush years at Swinton and Ledcourt): 'An Up Country Township,' 'How the Circus Came to Bullocktown,' 'A Mining Township' ('Grumbler's Gully'), a dozen in all over the years. They were a pioneering attempt to put the reality of Australian rural and goldfields life into literature — the failed-pastoralist apprenticeship he had hated now turned, safely behind him, into material he drew on to the end (a last Bullocktown idyll appeared four months after his death).

**The flogging (February 1871).** Mid-way through serialising *His Natural Life*, Clarke went to the Melbourne gaol to watch a man take fifty lashes — research, Castieau thought, and it struck close to home. Clarke "did not believe much in its efficacy." He had reason to brood on flogging: at Highgate School he had been birched "twice a week" by a notorious headmaster (Gerard Manley Hopkins, a schoolfellow, called Clarke "my co-victim" after one such beating), and he wore the memory with a boy's defiant pride — "I would have died rather than whimper." It fed the novel directly: Rufus Dawes's fellow-convict Kirkland, flogged to pieces, Clarke draws as "effeminate" (a word he used of himself) and a former bank clerk (his own first trade). The convict masterpiece was being quarried, in part, out of his own remembered pain.

**"His life is in his short stories" (1871–73).** Clarke kept pressing his realist creed. Reviewing Bret Harte's Californian diggings in 1871, he urged Australian writers to use "the material that lies ready to his hand" rather than "the English customs which are current 20,000 miles away," and foretold that "some author as unknown as Mr Harte" would make a masterpiece of it — opening the ground Henry Lawson would later till. But the stories were not only social documentary. Margaret Whitworth, who knew him, saw what the public missed: "He had troubles of which people at the time had no idea. His life is in his short stories. I am astonished to think how dull we were in not seeing it before." Unhappy marriage and thwarted fulfilment run through the tales of 1872–73. Castieau, dining at Clarke's house that May, glimpsed it from outside: Marian — the celebrated actress "settled down... into the domesticated wife and mother... very quiet" — appeared only "at dinner and at the end of the evening." The marriage was already a guarded, separate thing.

**"She wished she had never married me" (1871).** What the stories hid, the notebook stated. On 4 November 1871 Clarke wrote: "Marian said she wished she had never married me." The actress-wife and the dispossessed-gentleman husband — married barely two years — had already come to grief. Around it his outward life stayed full and convivial: in Castieau's diary he is forever walking home from the Yorick, buying a lobster so huge he needs a cab for it, standing the hard-up Cousins two pounds he can ill spare, initiating Castieau into the Russell Street Turkish baths "as an old hand." He kept the theatrical irons hot (the Molière adaptation *Peacock's Feathers*, *Fernande* from Sardou) and brought out *Old Tales of a Young Country*, the convict-history book mined from the Library's "musty pamphlets" — the *Argus* likening his method to a photographer developing "a vivid and faithful picture" from "a mere film." (When James Smith, his old enemy, was eased out of the *Australasian* over his spiritualism early in 1872, Clarke's contributions there resumed.) The public Clarke sparkled; the private one wrote "she wished she had never married me" in a red leather notebook and put it away.

**The Cave of Adullam, and the bounced cheques (1872).** Discontented with the changing Yorick, Clarke and a knot of others seceded early in 1872 to found a rowdier club, the Cave of Adullam — the name from the cave where "every one that was in distress, and every one that was in debt, and every one that was discontented" gathered to David. The qualification for membership was to be "happily impecunious," ideally "hunted by the myrmidons of the law"; the password was "Honour! No Frills!"; the rooms were up a guarded Flinders Lane alley; Clarke, "the Elf," led the move and installed the eccentric old ex-judge **Richard Birnie** as "Perpetual President with autocratic powers." It lasted only months, but it named the company Clarke kept — and increasingly resembled. His own finances were a muddle: he resigned the Yorick that January, bounced a £10 cheque on the gaol-governor Castieau (apologising, and paying cash), and railed in print at the copyright chaos that let his *Foul Play* be pirated interstate and his *Long Odds* staged in London, both without a penny to him — "It's a muddle!" The generosity didn't help: he would spend his last sovereign feeding a hungrier man (the asylum-barrister at the Café de Paris who ordered claret and a cigar on Clarke's half-crown intentions). He lived, like his Adullamites, one windfall ahead of the bailiff.

**Duffy reads His Natural Life (1872).** As the serial neared its end, Clarke sent the trustee and Irish patriot Charles Gavan Duffy the published numbers, with a clear statement of intent: the novel was "an attempt to expose the infamies that attended the old transportation system," the episodes "merely dramatized versions of the facts" — "I want to show that in many instances the law makes the criminal." Duffy answered with frankness. He thought it "singularly powerful and original" but "marred by serious faults": too unrelieved in its pain; its martyr-hero suffering "without any adequate or almost any intelligible motive," so the reader had no one to respect; the whole "unduly protracted" by the Ballarat-riots digression (the caricatured Peter Brawler), which "ought to be mercilessly expunged"; even a French-argot song that was not Clarke's own. Clarke "bore it manfully" and "set to work forthwith to amend the original plot." Much of what made the 1874 book greater than the serial — the tightening, the cuts — began in Duffy's douche-bath of criticism.

**Making the masterpiece: the mother's honour, and the death of Dawes (1872).** The serial finished in June 1872; Massina, sick of it ("Thank God!"), handed Clarke the rights for nothing, and Clarke sold them to Robertson for £25 — a pittance for what became the great Australian novel. The revision was where the book was made. Acting on Duffy's cuts, Clarke first proposed a noble Dawes sacrificing himself for a woman, but felt the flaw himself: "the great difficulty... is the motive. What motive would induce a young man to suffer himself to be transported for the life of another?" The answer — that Dawes endures it all to protect his mother's honour — gave the hero "a high and adequate motive" and became the spine of the novel (Shillinglaw claimed to have suggested it; "the skill of construction is all his own," he conceded). And the ending was settled on a midnight walk with the solicitor Jim Moloney, who argued Clarke out of a happy ending: "Marcus, Rufus Dawes must die." Clarke resisted — "I can't kill the poor chap... let him have a day or two of happiness" — until Moloney's logic (Norfolk Island "will go with him" wherever he is sent) won, and Clarke found the form of it: "Make him save Sylvia... and give his life for her in the end." They walked back near dawn and told Dr Pat Moloney that "Marcus and I have killed Rufus Dawes" — and "the three murderers sat down to beer." He dedicated the book to Duffy.

**'Human Repetends': the self in the story (September 1872).** That same year he published a story read ever since as self-portrait. Its narrator is "the only son of a rich widower" given over to "the society of men thrice my age," "suffered at sixteen to ape the vices of sixty," who learns "love meant money, friendship an endorsement on a bill," loses his evaporated inheritance when his father suddenly dies, and is shipped to Australia — "To walk down Collins Street was like pulling up the Styx. On either side I saw men who had vanished from the Upper World sooner than I." The dispossessed-gentleman's whole psychology, barely fictionalised: Melbourne as an underworld of the prematurely ruined, and Clarke one of its shades.

**Rose, the mountain, and the life not lived (1873).** The unhappy marriage curdled into something more painful. By 1873 Clarke had fallen into "some sort of liaison" with Marian's elder sister Rose, and took the blame for the marriage's ruin entirely on himself: "I found her a young and merry girl and I have made her a sad, petulant woman... mine the fault poor soul not hers." Up-country at Ledcourt over the New Year — borrowing heavily to stay afloat (£104 from the station manager, £50 from Swan) — he climbed alone to a mountain peak and "had it out with God and my soul," then wrote to Rose renouncing her: "I love you, passionately... [but] our love as it is must be always incomplete, fruitless, barren... I owe a duty to my wife, my children, my own honour." The despair beneath it was total: "My ambitions are defeated and weighed down by debt, by cares of family... I give in and take the usual stone instead of bread. I will make the world believe that I prefer granite to flour." Out of the same mountain stay came 'Holiday Peak,' a story of the life he might have led — the fortune never lost, Australia never reached, and his old schoolfellow Gerard Hopkins a celebrated painter instead of a hidden Jesuit. "A poor devil banished to Bush Land, tied neck and heels in debt," the narrator calls his real self, waking from the dream — which Clarke half-admitted was "a hashish fantasy, an opium dream."

**Holiday Peak, 'Pretty Dick,' and the genocide (1873).** His first story collection, *Holiday Peak and Other Tales*, appeared in 1873 — bush and country tales only, the title story his "Mount Might-ha'-been." McCrae, commissioned to design the cover and slow to be paid his guinea, drew himself as a starving pavement artist chalking "I AM STARVING & OTHER TALES BY MARCUS CLARKE." Reviewers were lukewarm on the "rehash" but singled out 'Pretty Dick,' a lost-child-in-the-bush story of such pathos that Oliver Wendell Holmes (to whom Clarke dedicated the book) wept over it as he had as a child over the Babes in the Wood. Clarke reached hard for England — Holmes and the Atlantic circle, and Frances Cashel Hoey, who placed 'Grumbler's Gully' in Dickens's old magazine *All the Year Round* (the only Clarke story published in England in his lifetime). And that July, prompted by Port Arthur's closure, he wrote three articles on the prison — feeding *His Natural Life* — that told the truth about the Tasmanian "war of extermination" of 1830: the settlers who "drew a cordon... drove the natives... and slaughtered them at their leisure," to "the complete destruction of the native race." The claim drew angry denials, and Mackinnon later cut the offending article from the collected edition. Clarke did not flinch from the darkness his convict country was built on.

**Breaking with the Argus, and turning left (1873–74).** September 1873 brought a step up — promotion to Sub-librarian (£400, "just a sufficient sum to keep one's pen idle") — but November brought a costly break. Over a press-pass quarrel, the *Herald* ran a hoax Melbourne Cup report "told by the camera," which Clarke had quietly lifted from the race-scene in his own *Long Odds*; the *Argus* manager, learning Clarke wrote for the rival paper, made him choose, and Clarke chose the *Herald*, ending his six-year association with the *Argus* group. He found new bases — the *Herald* and *Weekly Times*, and Gresley Lukin's *Brisbane Courier*, where the revised *His Natural Life* would run in 1875–76 — and he turned politically leftward: "the experience of researching the convict records for *Old Tales* and *His Natural Life* had radicalized him," and "his impending insolvency encouraged a cynical view." The result was 'The Wicked World,' a Balzacian exposure of Melbourne's acquisitive bankers, lawyers and merchants. The word "insolvency" had entered the record; the safe ground was beginning to tilt.

**His Natural Life, the book (April 1874).** At last the masterpiece was a book — Robertson's seven-and-six volume, Clarke's fifth, dedicated to Sir Charles Gavan Duffy, whose "advice and encouragement" had architected the revisions (and who, twice gaoled for Irish independence, knew the inside of a house of correction himself). The crucial change from the serial was the ending: where the magazine Dawes had finally sailed home to England, the book drowns him — "There was no return." Shillinglaw, reviewing, called it "one of the most horribly fascinating books" he had read, and scrawled in his copy that it had become "a Frankenstein instinct with a hideous life." Clarke sent copies to Reade, Holmes, Hyndman, around the world. And yet it did not sell: the great Australian novel made "no prodigious result," and only after Lord Rosebery praised it on his 1883–84 visit did demand for the Australian edition take hold. The masterpiece arrived, and the colony shrugged.

**Insolvency (July 1874).** Three months after the novel appeared, Clarke was declared insolvent — liabilities £2,186, assets £505 — undone by the old *Colonial Monthly* and *Humbug* losses, the interest on borrowed money, and mining speculations (the moneylender Waxman, the bank, Massina, Holt, Swan, and £350 to an insurance company among the creditors). He wore it lightly, to Cyril Hopkins: "insolvency is here a matter of no moment, especially to a fortunate man" — before drifting into Highgate-Woods nostalgia and a line of Swinburne ("Marriage and Death and Division make barren our lives"). And fortunate he was: his cousin Sir Andrew Clarke (now a colonial governor) gave him power of attorney over his Australian assets, so that instead of being sold up and evicted he moved his family to a pleasant Brighton house and ran up to town by train. The one real loss was his library — 580 titles, Shakespeare to Balzac's forty volumes, with the criminal trials and colonial pamphlets that had fed *His Natural Life* — auctioned for £250; the same day, the Library trustees asked for his resignation. The Cave of Adullam had been prophecy: the man on safe ground went down with the rest, and only a gentleman's safety-net kept him standing.

**Cast out (August–September 1874).** The bad season went on. The Harwood libel had ended in farthing damages, but Clarke had been caught denying authorship he'd privately admitted, and *The Argus* flayed him for it; he defended himself with elaborate casuistry — a denial of anonymous authorship "is not a lie," he argued, since a lie is "the saying of that which deceives another, when that other has a right to know the truth," and he had lied only to shield his employers, not himself ("I should have committed a far greater sin had I betrayed a trust"). The paper was unconvinced — he "doth protest too much" — and suspected a self-serving motive. Then, in September, the unkindest cut: bankrupt, he submitted his resignation to the Library; and when the cash-strapped Yorick balloted nine lapsed members for re-entry, "only Clarke was blackballed." The man who had named the club, served as its first secretary, and given it its skull was the one foundation member it would not take back. (He nonetheless kept his Library place; and a new serial, the weak *Chidiock Tichbourne* — its Jesuit-hunting agent named, tellingly, Gerard — ran from that September.)

**Surviving 1875.** The wreck did not finish him. Bentley of London took *His Natural Life* for an English edition — but their reader Geraldine Jewsbury demanded a happy ending: the hero "saved and returned home," the drowning softened. Clarke complied, writing an extra chapter in which Dawes rescues the dead woman's daughter and lives — yet protested the change, and when the Bentley edition appeared the tragic 1874 drowning had been kept after all. The masterpiece's "no return" survived the market. In February 1875, granted an unconditional insolvency certificate, Clarke saw the Library trustees return his resignation unaccepted — he had kept his job. He took a government commission to write a school *History of Australia* (partly to mend his finances), which dragged him through Parliament: a member denounced him as a scurrilous insolvent "kicked off the Melbourne press" who paid "not 1d. in the £," while the minister defended him as the best-qualified man in the colony. And he moved further left, writing the address for the 1875 Eight Hour Day anniversary — a striking step for a dispossessed gentleman.

**'Weird Melancholy': naming the national aesthetic (September 1875).** In his 'Country Leisure' column for Lukin's *Brisbane Courier*, 4 September 1875, Clarke wrote his fullest critical statement — his first proclamation that "Australia will have a school of poetry peculiarly her own," and the founding articulation of its character. Its keynote was Poe's: "What is the dominant note of Australian scenery? That which is the dominant note of Edgar Allan Poe's poetry. Weird Melancholy." The bush was "funereal, secret, stern," its solitude "desolation," its mountains named by hopeless explorers — Mount Misery, Mount Dreadful, Mount Despair. From this aesthetic he ranked his contemporaries, crowning the two of the braid above the rest. Kendall was "the chief of those who have attempted to climb the shining heights... he has caught clearly the wild and grotesque spirit of his native forest" ('Ghost Glen' quoted whole as "a true Australian ballad – wild, weird, and terrible"). Gordon, "now gone to his rest," was "genuine": of 'The Sick Stockrider' — "There is no 'feather-bed soldier' here. The writer has ridden his ride, as well as written of it." (McCrae, by contrast, was "Australian only by accident.") The essay closed on the phrase that would define the whole project: "the poet of our desolation." Wilding's question is the novel's: was it Kendall, Gordon, or himself Clarke had in mind? Much of the piece — the "Weird Melancholy" and "Land of the Dawning" passages — he would recycle the next year into his preface to Gordon's poems, the most famous criticism he ever wrote.

**Recognition, and a death (1875).** The London edition came out from Bentley that September — three volumes, modest sales (206 of 750 remaindered at ninepence), and mixed but real notice: the *Saturday Review* found the convict subject too sad, while the *Spectator*, *Graphic* and others praised its realistic power. Frances Cashel Hoey, who read the proofs for Duffy, caught it exactly in private — "What a horrid, powerful, clever, raw book his is!" — and Clarke's old acquaintance Hyndman, in his memoirs, made the prophecy that came true: "a masterpiece of horror... this novel of his will live by sheer force of its terror-inspiring delineations long after his other work is forgotten." That August, Clarke's father-in-law John Dunn — the veteran comic actor, born O'Donoghue — dropped dead in a Melbourne street; Clarke, who loved him, was a pall-bearer (so, reconciled, was Harwood), and the actor's stone was cut with Hamlet's words for Yorick: "He was a fellow of infinite jest." The skull that had named the club presided over the grave.

**The townsman who wrote the bush (1875–76).** Two glimpses from these years complicate the man who had just named the national aesthetic. In December 1875 the owner of the London *Daily Telegraph* — "the largest circulation in the world" — wrote inviting Clarke to England to write "journalism of the highest order." It was the road home, and a great career; Clarke took the Melbourne-correspondent post but never sailed, declining even a later war-correspondent offer (the young wife, the children, his frail physique). No return, again — by choice now. And then there is Frank Myers's 1876 portrait: Clarke "the most charming personality" in Australian letters, presiding over a sumptuous dinner at Butters's Birdcage club (Fijian waiters, palms and ferns, turtle and snow-chilled figs), his refrain a weary "If you want to prove that life is not worth living, persist." But out at Lyster's bush property Myers made a startling discovery: the author of "Weird Melancholy" and the coming Gordon preface — the man who wrote "the heritage of desolation" — *hated the actual bush*. He never walked into the forest willingly, had "not the faintest perception of... the glory of the forest," and "would rather have one smell of the fleshpots than the heritage of desolation whole and undivided." His celebrated bush was a townsman's gothic invention, written from distaste, not love. He dismissed his own masterpiece too — *His Natural Life* was "a chamber of horrors indifferently assorted, and confound it, it doesn't draw!" — and held "everything save the joy of life in utter contempt." The magpie on the dead bough, he said, "is typical of everything Australian. There is something in him, but it can't get out."

**The Gordon preface, and a scattering (1876).** When Clarson Massina reissued Gordon's *Sea-Spray and Smoke Drift* in 1876, Clarke supplied a preface — and it became the most famous thing he ever wrote. Two-thirds of it he recycled from 'Country Leisure' (and before that from his gallery notes): the "Weird Melancholy" and "Land of the Dawning" passages, "some of the best known writing about the Australian bush ever written." He used it to proclaim again "something very like the beginnings of a national school of Australian poetry," and to honour Gordon's "genuine poetic instinct." Bound into Gordon's soon-bestselling *Poems*, the essay became the founding text of Australian literary nationalism — endlessly reprinted, judged by Lord Lytton "the finest piece of work that ever came from the pen of Marcus Clarke." The irony Myers had exposed sat right inside it; Cyril Hopkins defended the preface as impression, not survey — Clarke reproducing "the mental impressions... of a wild nature which had awed and yet partly captivated his easily excited imagination," haunting him "long after he had left the scenes." Meanwhile his own book spread: *His Natural Life* reached Lord Lytton, who "could not lay it down," and the Prince of Wales, who sat up a night in India to finish it and wished to meet the author (who "ought to be in London"); Harper of New York reprinted it and "spontaneously" paid. But Clarke himself was scattering — a story collection no English house would take, an abandoned 'Two in the Bush,' an unfinished play, and above all *Felix and Felicitas*, the novel he tried to make of the Rose affair ("You will wrestle it down, work it out in a book," she had written; he set six chapters in type and never finished). The masterpiece was conquering the world; the man was leaving a trail of fragments.

**1877: the net withdrawn.** This was the year the safe ground gave way for good. In a long January letter to Cyril Hopkins, Clarke drew himself comfortable and doomed in one breath — the Brighton house with its boat and servants, the declined offers (he had asked the *Daily Telegraph* £1,000 a year and a free passage; he turned down a billet in cousin Andrew's India too), the confession "I am considered well off, am always in debt and contrive to live pleasantly." Cyril's gloss is grim: "He did not contrive to live pleasantly, for his debts and worries eventually killed him." He thought he might "never return home at all," and signed off with an anxious postscript about his masterpiece — "How did you like His Un-Natural Life? I mean *really* you know?" He stayed prolific (the story collection *Four Stories High*, the squib *The Future Australian Race*, work for the SPCA and a reformatory inquiry), but the year's two big enterprises went wrong. The schools' *History of Australia* — the commission he'd been savaged for in Parliament — he found tedious, handed to friends, and let turn into "a miserable fiasco," never used and eclipsed by the diligent Sutherlands' rival (which sold 120,000). And the cousin's safety-net was withdrawn: Sir Andrew, whose "fatal though amiable" trust in Marcus as his "sole and unchecked agent" had let a fortune be spent "in a most foolish way," did not renew the power of attorney in 1877. Clarke left Brighton for St Kilda; the usurers — Waxman and the rest — closed back in. Mackinnon's verdict is the death-knell sounding early: the business "led him deeper into the clutches of usurers, who eventually wrought him to death before his time." He moved his journalism to Syme's liberal *Age*, four times the *Argus*'s circulation, and wrote a brilliant class-anatomy of the two papers as "the political history of Victoria" — but the gentleman's reprieve of 1874 was over.

**Voice samples (1877):**
- "I am considered well off, am always in debt and contrive to live pleasantly."
- "How did you like His Un-Natural Life? I mean *really* you know?"

**Red radicalism, and a head on the road (1878).** At *The Age* Clarke found his politics. He threw himself into the Berry reform agitation, "a thorough hater of Shoddocracy," writing for Syme's paper and conferring with Duffy, now Speaker and "the moving spring" behind the Black Wednesday purge of January 1878. When respectable Melbourne raged at the dismissal of well-salaried conservatives, Clarke alone asked why no one had raged when "fettlers, railway porters, junior clerks or labourers" were sacked by the hundred, "with half a dozen children on the floor." His radicalism, Turner said, "was very red": he held business itself a polite theft — "a man of business is one who becomes possessed of other people's money without bringing himself within the purview of the law" — echoing his beloved Balzac, that a great fortune is "a crime forgotten." Yet the same observers saw the self-indictment in it: his keenest satire, Turner noted, was aimed at "that laissez faire Bohemianism which was the cause of all his troubles." That January, perhaps sobered, he made his will. In June a market cart smashed his buggy on the Cheltenham road and threw him on his head — left insensible and bleeding, the careless driver gone — a second skull-blow to set beside the fall of 1868. He charmed on regardless: he invited young Victor Daley to dinner, announced "I have no money!", borrowed a sovereign on "dire necessity," and spent it on champagne instead. His St Kilda house held no rare books now — they had gone with the bankruptcy — but he lived among the Library's thousands by day, growing, Patchett Martin saw, ever more aggrieved at a world where "the bank balance has too much become the barometer of human worth."

**The gamble, the triumph, the ledger (1878–79).** Two doors stood open at the end of 1878. Berry, through Duffy, offered Clarke the Parliamentary Librarianship — Barry urged him to take "an offer more valuable than that you occupy" — but Clarke declined, certain that the ageing Sheffield would soon retire and the Chief Librarianship of the Public Library fall to him. "In this hope he was doomed to disappointment." It was the fatal gamble: he turned down the bird in hand for a post he would never get. He could still triumph — that Christmas his *Alfred the Great*, written with Henry Keiley, became his greatest stage success, running thirty-five nights. But the Library was souring on him: in April 1879 the trustees hauled him in over an anonymous *Telegraph* letter "calumniating public men," reminding him he had pledged on appointment to write nothing unsigned, and reproving him for feeding *The Age* exclusive scraps. And the ledger was relentless — his 1879–81 diary records every paragraph and its fee, from seven-and-six to £4, set against his debts to Goldstein, Waxman, and the grocer. Six to twelve pieces a month, the hack's treadmill, the moneylenders always in the margin. The triumph and the trap, side by side.

**Two controversies, two sets of enemies (1879–80).** As the money tightened, Clarke set about making enemies on every side. In the *Victorian Review* he published 'Civilisation without Delusion,' a boldly agnostic essay; the Bishop of Melbourne replied gravely, and Clarke — delighted to see "those reverend gaiters actually getting over the 'ropes'" — fired back a 'Letter to his Lordship' so provoking that the *Melbourne Review* number carrying it was suppressed by its publisher under pressure from religious subscribers. (He protested he was no atheist — only that "the gods of the supernatural religion had no existence save in the imaginations of men" — but the row blew "far beyond the confines of the literary world.") Having offended the conservatives and the devout, he then alienated his own side: he localized the burlesque *The Happy Land* to caricature the radical Berry ministry — his political friends — and Berry's government censored it off the stage, the "Hush! prohibited" gaps drawing roars from packed houses while detectives took notes and the manager Aarons fled into hiding. Clarke's hand was disguised but suspected, and it "made him powerful enemies." The freethinker who needled the Church and the satirist who mocked his own party: in a year he had estranged nearly everyone who might have helped him.

**Marian back on the boards (1880).** The agnostic quarrel went on paying and costing at once: the pamphlet *Civilization Without Delusion* sold 10,000 copies — "I never saw so many black coats in my life" — even as Barry himself came quietly to the Library office to warn him, with deadly courtesy, that "I should require to have some thousands a year of a private income before I would venture upon writing such an article... among so punctilious a community." (Even Kendall thought he had blundered: "slightly 'off his onion'... a man of his unquestionable ability as utterly without judgment.") The enemies kept multiplying — the journalist David Blair printed a pamphlet branding Clarke "liar, coward, bully, thief, skunk," and Clarke wanted to sue but could not afford to. And the household was now leaning on Marian: financial necessity pushed her back onto the stage after a dozen years, as Mrs Marcus Clarke, in comedy-dramas Marcus wrote expressly for her — *A Daughter of Eve*, *Forbidden Fruit* — each running its modest five nights at the Bijou, the wife "leaving her character behind her," as Clarke wryly put it, to prop the family up. *Forbidden Fruit* was his last completed play; the opera-bouffe *Queen Venus* sat unfinished on his desk, two acts done, when death came.

**The vise closes (1880).** Through 1880 the body and the finances failed together. *The Bulletin* caught it cruelly: "A well-known Melbourne littérateur has been suffering from ophthalmia, measles and bailiffs. Pretty rough on an eight stone man." He went to the Library in goggles, half-blind; he moved house again, to a damp place he called "Sunnyside"; the bailiffs were real, though he could still wheedle £10 to "exorcize" them and spend the night shining at the Governor's ball. The work faltered — his *Moonstone* adaptation was "the biggest failure ever produced in any theatre," and *Felix and Felicitas*, the Rose novel that Bentley wanted and Frances Hoey thought "quite exceptional," he could not finish. The Waxman debt was closing in. And then the gamble he had staked everything on came due: old Sheffield retired, and Clarke applied for the Chief Librarianship he had refused the parliamentary post to wait for. But the timing was ruinous. His Bishop and *Happy Land* quarrels had made powerful enemies on every side; his old foe James Smith now sat on the selection committee; and his protectors were vanishing exactly when he needed them. Duffy had resigned and gone to Europe; and that November, within four days of each other, both Sir Redmond Barry — twelve days after sentencing Ned Kelly to hang — and the impresario Lyster died. Clarke, Mackinnon records, said he had "lost his best and most influential friend." The failing man, with the debts and the enemies and the dead patrons, had gambled his security on a post he would now almost certainly not get.

**Passed over (1880–81).** And he did not get it. Barry's last act had been to drag himself to the trustees' meeting to back Clarke — "looked upon by the whole literary world of Victoria as the legitimate successor" — but the new trustees overruled the dying judge and recommended Archer instead; the Government refused Archer; and the post hung in a limbo Clarke would not live to see resolved in his favour. He met it as he met everything, epigrammatically: at the French play when the news came, he "shrugged his shoulders, and smiled sweetly," and let a wit beside him circulate the "A.B.C." rhyme — Archer, Bride, and "C... a Clerk, with a quill dipped in gall, who could kill, if it pleased him, Bride, Archer, and all." His own pen had finished his chances: the jibes at the "good old earl" and his "noble balderdash," the contempt for "Superior Persons," had alienated exactly the people who decided such appointments, and Patchett Martin judged his hopes "utterly destroyed." Yet the same memoirist noted the thing that redeems him: "all the while he was penning these sneers, he would be taking an infinite deal of trouble to spread the fame of Adam Lindsay Gordon or Henry Kendall." Ruining himself, he never stopped serving the braid.

**A genial letter, and the break-up (1881).** The clearest proof of that loyalty came now. In March 1881 Clarke devoted a *Leader* article to Kendall — half journalistic puff, half tribute — recalling him in Melbourne as "a slight figure" in clothes "worn so unconventionally that they appeared almost ludicrous," welcomed by every literary man but unable to live on welcome; comparing him to Duffy's doomed Clarence Mangan, but adding, gladly, that Kendall "unlike Mangan... lived to see the force of his talent triumph." His verdict was generous and exact: Kendall's note was "regret," his beauty celebrated "with no joyous note," no match for Gordon's "berserker" gallop — "but for melody and tenderness, for gloom and sadness, some of his poems have not been excelled by any writer of English be he who he may." Kendall, who had sent Clarke his book, read the piece and was moved; Clarke wrote him "a genial letter" in return. The two survivors who had never corresponded, the poet who had despised the novelist, exchanged warm words at last — by post, in their final months, though they never met. And then the body gave way. Clarke's old cantata *Proi: or, At the Dawning* was staged that April, but "life was no longer dawning" for him: Mackinnon saw "ominous signs of an early break-up," the "once-active brain" grown "lethargic," the work that came so easily now "a task too vast to be undertaken without great effort." He could still summon the old charm — the family told of a bailiff sent to seize the furniture whom Clarke greeted as long-lost "Uncle John from the East," fed and wined and so disarmed that the man chopped wood for days and swore he would "go to prison himself" sooner than see Clarke suffer (a good story, possibly borrowed). But the charm was performing now over a failing man. The end was months away.

**The end (2 August 1881).** The months ran out fast. On 12 April — the very day Kendall was gazetted Inspector of Forests — Clarke swore the affidavit that began his second insolvency; Waxman pressed his tangled claim, Clarke could not even obtain a clear accounting, and on 20 June his estate was sequestrated. In July, Bride was made Librarian over him, and the trustees demanded his resignation once more. He knew what was coming. On the Queen's Birthday, walking with a friend near the Yarra Bend asylum, he framed it as one last grim wager: "Which shall it be – the mad asylum or the pauper grave? Let a toss of the coin decide – head, grave; tail, asylum." The florin fell tail. "Not if I know it, my festive coin. No gibbering idiot shall I e'er be; rather the gleeful gallows tree." A week of illness took him — pleurisy, then the worn-out liver, then erysipelas — with Marian nursing him night and day and Walstab helping; the doctors, called late, "held out little hope," the constitution "utterly worn out" and "the mental worry of the latter weeks" finishing the work.
