He did not believe he was dying: "When I get up I will be a different man, with a new liver," he told Marian a few hours before the end, and asked for his coat. Then his speech failed, and he beckoned for pencil and paper, and moved his hand across the sheets as if writing — faster and faster, the mind wandering, the tongue unable to say what the fevered brain wanted to set down — until the arms tired, and he fell back, and "the large, beautiful eyes, with a far off gaze in them, opened widely, for a second – then closed – and all was over." He was thirty-five. McCrae had no doubt what the dying hand was doing: "the poor fellow imagined he was turning out something that was to bring a little coin for his wife and children." The writer died writing, trying to earn. The cottage was nearly bare, everything sold; at first there was not even money for the medicines — "everything came in time," McCrae wrote, "but came too late."

**Voice samples (the end):**
- "Which shall it be – the mad asylum or the pauper grave? ... head, grave; tail, asylum." / "No gibbering idiot shall I e'er be; rather the gleeful gallows tree."
- "When I get up I will be a different man, with a new liver."

**Afterward.** The Yorick that had blackballed him turned out for his funeral; the cortège swelled through Swanston Street with the club and the Library officers, and he was buried at the Melbourne General Cemetery in the wet. Marian and the six children were "left utterly destitute" — and the city that had not paid him alive paid now, in benefit matinees and concerts and a subscription fund that climbed past £1,000, three times the Waxman debt that had broken him. (The single largest donation, in a last black irony, was £10 from Waxman himself.) Friends defended him in print — Whitworth's preface to the posthumous *Major Molineux* insisting that "beneath all there beat a heart of gold," that he was "facile princeps amongst the litterateurs of Australia" — while James Smith and others used his ruin to lecture young writers against the "Bohemianism of letters." And the masterpiece did exactly what Hyndman had foretold. Retitled *For the Term of His Natural Life* in 1882, it became the national classic — endlessly adapted for the stage from 1886, filmed three times before 1930 (the last with Clarke's own daughter playing Lady Devine), reprinted by Macmillan and Penguin and Oxford, the fame and the money pouring in at last to a man four years in the ground, and to the widow who took a stand at the 1888 Exhibition to sell his books. Everything came; everything came too late.

### Adam Lindsay Gordon

**Birth & blood (1833).** Born at Fayal in the Azores, 19 Oct 1833 — only son; three of his four sisters died young. Father Capt. Adam Durnford Gordon (b. Worcester 1796), army officer (Barbados, Bengal Cavalry), who in 1829 married his own first cousin Harriet, daughter of the Governor of Berbice. Grand pedigree on both sides — descent claimed back to the murderous "Edom o' Gordon" of the old ballad — but the wealth was West Indian slave-plantation money that dried up with abolition (1833). The family settled in Cheltenham (1840), where the Captain taught Hindustani at the new College, schooling officers' sons for India.

**The family shadow.** Harriet Gordon suffered a "mild religious" melancholia; on doctors' advice she spent the rest of her life drifting around Europe. Mental affliction shadows Gordon's line as it does Clarke's.

**Cheltenham, steeplechasing & Jane Bridges.** Cheltenham was the cradle of steeplechasing and Gordon caught the fever for life. He also fell, unrequited, for **Jane Bridges**, a Worcestershire "farmer's daughter" (his father's contemptuous phrase) — the cause of bitter rows at home.

**The steeplechase scandal & exile (1852–53).** Removed summarily from a string of schools, Gordon was at the Royal Grammar School, Worcester, when the escapade that "compelled him to leave the country" occurred — the *Lalla Rookh* affair (see scene candidates). His father paid to quash the matter and packed him off to South Australia in 1853, armed with letters of introduction, money in the Adelaide bank, and a place arranged in the Mounted Police.

**Voice samples (leaving England, to Charley Walker):**
- On the Mounted Police berth: "devilish good pay, a horse, three suits of regimentals yearly and lots of grub" — but first "I must do something... to make my friends remember me, rob somebody or something equally notorious."
- Leaving home: "that choking sensation of sorrow... as if the air one breathed in was like liquid lead, but I swallowed it somehow, and... gave vent to a long whistle and lit my pipe."
- The father's despair, a dark prefiguring: "He said once he'd sooner see me in my grave, and I don't know but what I felt much the same thing myself sometimes."

**South Australia & the Mounted Police (1853–54).** He sailed on the barque *Julia* (7 Aug 1853, aged 19) and reached Adelaide 14 November. The romantic legend — that he threw his letters of introduction overboard and enlisted as a common trooper to repudiate his birth — is false: he used them, was received and housed by his father's friends the Ashwins in North Adelaide, and only then applied to the Mounted Police (17 Nov), accepted as a trooper on 24 November. He'd hoped for an inspectorship; a rejected application and the rules put him in the ranks. In barracks he became "**Mysterious Gordon**" — dark, reserved, unsociable, hardly speaking — until a locked wooden box was found half-full of racing tackle (top-boots, colours, whips) and the secret was out: a superb cross-country rider who took the officers' horses "straight as the crow flies over five or six fences" and jumped the picket gate in and out of the yard.

**Voice samples (departure & SA):**
- 'To My Sister' (4 Aug 1853): "They say I have belied my blood, / And stained my pedigree."
- 'An Exile's Farewell' (in a lady's album aboard ship): "I shed no tears at quitting home, / Nor will I shed them now!"
- Father's letter to Ashwin: Lindsay is "one of the most careless and helpless of God's creatures," forever saying "I feel myself as lively as a kitten."
- On trooper life, to Charley: "We get good pay and live like fighting cocks... I feel now like a new man and am... turned as steady as a judge comparatively."

**Trooper at Penola (1854–55), then the bush (1855–62).** Stationed mostly at Penola near the Victorian border, Gordon policed a rough frontier from a cell-less settler's hut — once boxing a horse-stealing prisoner into submission with an old pair of gloves when the handcuffs wouldn't fit. He romanticised it all as moss-trooper adventure. Here he made his deepest South Australian friendships: the poetry-loving **Bright** family (Ned Bright the model for 'The Sick Stockrider') and the circus rider **William Trainor**. After about eighteen months he resigned (4 Nov 1855) over some clash of duty and pride — the accounts disagree (see scene candidates) — and spent the next seven years droving and dealing horseflesh from station to station, still, by his own admission, half-hoping to sail home and marry Jane.

**Voice samples (Penola):**
- Boxing the prisoner: "my handcuffs would not go on his wrists... I showed him an old pair of boxing gloves and he put them on. We set to... I took him... a right-hander with all my strength and floored him."
- The romance of the work: "you have only to fancy yourself a moss trooper of the olden time and your situation is quite romantic."
- Still pining (Oct 1855): "I would marry her to-morrow if I had the chance and she would have me."

**The horse-breaking years & Dingley Dell (1856–62).** For seven years Gordon roamed the south-east breaking and dealing horses — most steadily at Edward Stockdale's Lake Hawdon (the "ES" brand; he once broke 173 colts there), later settling on his own small farm, **Dingley Dell**. Harry Stockdale, who worked beside him for years, left the fullest portrait: a remarkably handsome man when animated, dark-grey eyes sparkling, head thrown back — but careless in dress, brooding, "a little superstitious," writing in snatches on old envelopes and bills. He was acutely, sensitively short-sighted: the standing station joke was "Gordon's grey one," because told "There they are" he'd instantly answer "there's a grey amongst them" (there nearly always was). Stockdale calls him "the soul of truth," holding a liar "lower than a thief," and "especially respectful to all women." A compulsive cold-water bather, he'd take the steepest descent to the **Blue Lake** at Mount Gambier and plunge into near-freezing water till his teeth chattered, insisting it was "glorious"; at Robe he swam out till he vanished in the waves. A bad sunstroke crossing the Ninety-Mile Desert nearly killed him — one of many blows to a head that, Stockdale notes, "had too much knocking about" (a thread to carry: the riding accidents and the decline are coming). Stockdale also gives yet another steeplechase-origin — he and **Tom Oliver** went halves on a horse seized by the bailiff for Oliver's debt, which Gordon "took possession of by force." And the *Admella* wreck of 1865 near Cape Northumberland lies behind 'From the Wreck' — Gordon's own ride with the news, per Stockdale.

**Voice / character (horse-breaking years):**
- The short-sight joke: "Yes. I see them. Yes, there's a grey amongst them."
- To Tenison Woods, late at night: "Look here Father, what does it matter. Old or young it all comes to eat, drink and be merry for tomorrow you die."
- Stockdale's summative note on Gordon and Woods alike: "in each life the past held a shadow."

**The legacy, the marriage & the first bad fall (1857–64).** Both parents died (father June 1857, mother April 1859), and in October 1861 a legacy of about £7,000 reached Gordon's bank — instantly inflating his local legend into "millionaire." He gave up horse-breaking for the life of a gentleman steeplechase rider, and squandered the money with reckless open-handedness (lending £600 to a man who'd just stiffed him £6; sinking £800 trying to save a Penola storekeeper) — the start of the spiral. On 20 Oct 1862, the day after his 29th birthday, he married **Margaret "Maggie" Park** — Glasgow-born, barely five foot, perhaps only seventeen to his six-foot, ten-and-a-half-stone — at the house of the Rev. J. Don, giving her an 18-carat gold watch and muff-chain. By his own telling there was "no romance" in it (see scene candidates); he habitually called her "girl." They shared a love of horses (the pair rode 300 miles, Adelaide to Mount Gambier, ~50 a day). And — first of the body-blows the blurb promises — more than a year before June 1863, a fall left his horse rolling over him, bruising his spine near the kidneys; he wrote of lasting back pain, cramps, sleeplessness, lost appetite. He bought **Dingley Dell** (8 Mar 1864), the coast cottage now his museum.

**Scottish self-fashioning.** Steeped since schooldays in Scott and the border ballads (he recited *Marmion* and *The Lord of the Isles* in the dormitory after lights-out), Gordon rode in tartan racing colours (Royal Stuart or Rob Roy — sources differ) and gathered Scots friends (the Riddochs, Blackmore, G. G. McCrae). The Scottish identity reads partly as a rejection of middle-English Cheltenham; marrying the Glasgow-born Maggie reinforced it.

**Voice samples (legacy / marriage / class):**
- Financial creed (to Charley, 1857): "make up your mind to win, and if you lose shift for yourself as best you may."
- The proposal (via Woods): "Well, girl, I like your ways. You seem industrious and sensible... we will get married next week, and you shall keep home for me."
- On class, sent to the men's hut: "I am as well born as any of them, and perhaps better educated, but then they don't know that" (then reciting Burns's 'A man's a man for a' that').
- The spine injury (to his uncle, Jun 1863): "my horse rolling over me and bruising me in the spine near the kidneys... pains in the back and nasty cramps and I could not sleep."

**First poems, in secret (1864).** Gordon began publishing slowly and anonymously, mortified by exposure. His first piece, *The Feud* — a ballad keyed to Noel Paton's illustrations of the border ballad 'The Dowie Dens o' Yarrow,' under the initials "A. Lindsay" — appeared at Mount Gambier in Aug 1864 (accounts of the prompt vary: the Paton plates seen at the Mount Gambier Hotel, per Bonython; or a church bazaar, per Trainor), with authorship to be kept secret. When locals scoffed — "The idea of Long Gordon writing poetry!" — he was so stung he called in the copies and destroyed them (Trainor salvaged a few). 'Verses inspired by "My old black pipe"' (written ~1862, given to Trainor) followed in *Bell's Life in Victoria*, Oct 1864. The pattern is set early: a man who needed to write but couldn't bear to be seen doing it — which makes the stakes of *Bush Ballads* at the end all the higher.

**The horseman (Fetherstonhaugh's portrait).** Cuthbert Fetherstonhaugh, who rode with him, drew the definitive picture: over six feet, thick curly hair, strong chin, and "painfully short sighted" — a wonder he could ride at racing pace through thick timber after wild horses. "Moody, silent, reserved... some reverie locked in," an "ugly rider" who leaned so far back over a fence his head could touch the horse's rump, yet utterly unthrowable and "absolutely fearless. Possibly reckless." He sought out the brute no one else would send over the sticks; his gift was an uncanny mutual sympathy with horses, drawing "determination and 'divilment'" from them. Among his feats: winning **three steeplechases in a single day** (the blurb's headline), and the celebrated **Blue Lake leap** of 27 Jul 1864 (see scene candidates).

**Member of Parliament (1865–66).** Late in 1864 Woods — worried by Gordon's "morbid melancholy" and his complaints that his life was being wasted — persuaded the reluctant horse-breaker to stand for the South Australian seat of Victoria, hoping useful occupation might steady him. Gordon won on 1 March 1865 (378 to Stow's 367), running on the squatters' interest against the anti-squatter attorney-general Randolph Stow, amid a riotous campaign (a flag-burning Irishman, a coach-stopping, a Stow effigy snatched from the bonfire). He was, by every account, a dreadful public speaker — one "best" speech ran in full: "I know you all, and you all know me... and I will attend to them," then a Latin tag, and he sat down — but popular, fiercely independent ("he wasn't going to be at the beck and call of any of them"), and pungently anti-demagogue (invoking Jack Cade and Wat Tyler). He skewered the contest in satirical verse ('Exodus Parthenidae', 'Hippodramia'). Mostly, though, Parliament gave him a library: he moved Maggie to a rambling weatherboard house at Glenelg and spent his days not in the chamber but in the parliamentary library, reading poets, history and travel and borrowing voraciously (Aristotle, Macaulay, Derby's Homer). Here he made lifelong Scots friends — the squatter **John Riddoch** (fellow MP, later founder of the Coonawarra colony) and his brother George, and the librarian **E. G. Blackmore**, with whom he'd walk the seven miles home to Glenelg for a dawn sea-swim.

**Voice samples (Parliament):**
- Campaign plain-speaking: "I know you all, and you all know me: I know your wants as you do yourselves, and I will attend to them."
- His independence: he'd do what he honestly believed best, but "wasn't going to be at the beck and call of any of them."

**The literary turn & exit from Parliament (1865–66).** Politics bored him ("too quiet and reserved for that kind of existence," Maggie said), but it lit a real ambition. Through 1866 Gordon placed verse in the Melbourne *Australasian* and *Bell's Life* — including 'Ye Wearie Wayfarer', whose stanza became his signature. Woods noticed a new "self-assertion" and "personal vanity": Gordon was sure he'd "rise to the top of the tree in poetry," that the world "should talk about him before he died," and he resolved to resign his seat, move to **Melbourne**, get newspaper work, even try a novel. His Hansard speech (6 Jun 1865) was the legend in miniature — a dazzle of Latin and classical jokes (the squatters as Sisyphus, "the morituri"), funny and doomed, dismissed at the time as the eccentricity of a "horsey man" who'd wandered into Parliament. He resigned 10 Nov 1866 and returned to Robe; a West Australian land-and-sheep venture promptly lost him everything. Two telling artefacts of this moment: his will (3 Dec 1865), leaving all to "my dear wife Margaret"; and the careless ease of his gift — handed a friend's doggerel the night before a Great Western meeting, he said "Tear it up, old man, and I'll scribble you a lay myself," and dashed off 'Banker's Dream' on a few old envelopes (it forecast the race).

**Voice samples (the literary turn):**
- The squatters' fate (Hansard, 1865): the condemned class, "the enfants perdus, the morituri, the squatters."
- Law vs justice: "They may make it law, and... I presume it is justice. But I'll be hanged if they can make you or me call it justice."
- 'Ye Wearie Wayfarer' (his signature, later quoted by QEII in 1992 and a favourite of Diana's): "Life is mostly froth and bubble; / Two things stand like stone, / Kindness in another's trouble, / Courage in your own."

**Back to the bush, first books, and a daughter (1867).** Home from the failed West Australian venture by March 1867, Gordon gave up the Glenelg house, resettled at Mount Gambier, and resumed racing and verse. He rode strictly as a gentleman-amateur — always "Mr Gordon" in the press (professionals went by surname), never for money, never betting, though training and racing bled cash; once, having won 300 sovereigns at Ballarat, he tried to hand Maggie the chamois bag, and when she refused it had the money made into a £300 butterfly brooch she wore on grand occasions. The only surviving letter to her (Ballarat, Apr 1867) is endearingly domestic — and on its back, scribbled unawares, a working list of twenty-eight poems for the book he was assembling. On 3 May 1867, at Robe, Maggie — her health "very precarious" — bore a daughter, **Annie Lindsay Gordon**. Then, within nine days that June, his two books appeared: *Ashtaroth, a Dramatic Lyric* (10 Jun, Clarson, Massina) "by the Author of *Sea Spray and Smoke Drift*," and *Sea Spray and Smoke Drift* (19 Jun, George Robertson) "by the Author of *Ashtaroth*" — each anonymous, each pointing at the other, each 500 copies self-funded at £50. Notices ranged from warm (the Bohemian journalist Richard Birnie's *Argus* review won Gordon's lasting friendship) to withering ("it reflects great credit upon the printer, the binder, and the paper-maker"). Privately he rated *Ashtaroth* the better, though "much of it is bosh."

**Voice samples (1867):**
- On Birnie's review: "Birnie has found out beauties of which I myself was unaware."
- To Trainor, on the new book: "I will send you a volume of my poems when they are ready, but you must not lend them or you will spoil their sale."
- On heroic death (Nolan of the Light Brigade): "That man died a death the gods might envy. Let never a tear his memory stain."

**To Victoria: Ballarat and the stables (Sep–Nov 1867).** After fourteen years in South Australia, Gordon crossed to Victoria. He mortgaged Dingley Dell and the Mount Gambier land to R. Lawson for £1,500 (Sep 1867) — settling £1,000 in trust on a shadowy young woman known only as "**Marie**" near Bald Hills (Sladen claimed she bore Gordon an illegitimate daughter; the attachment is otherwise unrecorded, never mentioned in his letters) — and used the rest to lease the livery stables at Craig's Hotel, Ballarat (Nov 1867). He moved Maggie and baby Annie into a weatherboard cottage in Sturt Street, opposite Lake Wendouree, and took as partner the horseman **Harry Mount** (whose brother Lambton had shared the ruinous WA sheep run; Harry would later be entangled in the *Carl* blackbirding atrocity). The stables devoured his time and proved another money-sink; little was left for poetry — just two poems in 1868, both in **Marcus Clarke's *Colonial Monthly***, his first appearance in print alongside Clarke. Ballarat — gold-built, ~40,000 strong, third-richest town in the colony, with breweries and a stock exchange but also brothels and opium dens — was a place Clarke too knew and speculated in.

**A prodigious memory.** Gordon's freakish verbal memory is a real key to him. As a schoolboy (per Strange) he recited his punishment-lines of Virgil or Homer after one or two readings. Stockdale watched him hear ~200 lines of Hood's 'Miss Kilmansegg' read aloud once, then repeat the whole thing verbatim — and reproduce it again months later off a dozen lines' prompt. Woods saw him recite the storm-scene from *King Lear* while they shivered lost in the rain (1860), then pace a supper-room till dawn on *Childe Harold*; he carried a Latin classic in his pocket and learnt the odes of Horace in the saddle. The paradox: by his own admission he was "the worst reader" — a flat, sing-song delivery his accomplished-reader mother had despaired of — yet he loved being read to (Scott, Byron, Shelley) for hours. And at this period, Stockdale says, he "never drank or gambled" — worth holding against the opiate addiction to come.

**Ballarat: the Hunt Club, the Light Horse & the boxing (1868–69).** Gordon threw himself into Ballarat's sporting life: secretary of the **Hunt Club** (he kept the pack of harriers in a back-yard kennel — Maggie washing the hounds in hot soapsuds, a captive dingo and kangaroo in the old orchard for them to chase), and from January 1868 a member of the re-formed **Ballarat Light Horse**, drilled in his own stable-yard. Business often made him late for parade, so he'd "charge up the streets in full uniform on his mare Maud, take the fence at a flying leap, and fall in sedately" (promoted Senior Sergeant, 9 Mar 1868). The military world had always called him: destined for the army, briefly at Woolwich (a contemporary of the future 'Chinese' Gordon of Khartoum), from a family in which "twenty of us... have all been brought up for the Army." He was a formidable boxer too (taught in Cheltenham by the champion Jem Edwards), his long reach offsetting his short sight — he once knocked out a professional, Black Johnson, who'd insulted him as he mounted before a race, then got back on Cadger and won. But the fullest Ballarat portrait is **William Brazenor's**: a melancholy, silent, reckless rider who "rode to break his neck," "always seemed lost," and "had the look of a man who had lost himself," mixing "with people who were not of his class."

**Voice samples (Ballarat):**
- After being thrown when a borrowed sensitive horse baulked: "No. I'd rather a horse fell through a fence than do a thing like that."
- The grim hunt-field jest to a reckless novice ('Aniseed'): "he'll be killed to a certainty, and if he's in front of the hounds, perhaps they'll eat him; they want blooding badly."
- His black-comic nonchalance about falls (per Fetherstonhaugh): hailed on the road and asked where he's off to, "Oh, just going to give the youngster a fall or two" — and met again later in a spring cart, head bandaged, having done exactly that.

**The Ballarat catastrophes (Mar–Apr 1868).** Then everything broke at once. On **21 March 1868**, schooling a young horse, Gordon jumped a fence to cut across country; at the second jump the horse baulked, struck the top rail and fell onto him, treading on his face as it scrambled up. Harry Mount found him unconscious and had him carried home on a stretcher — five ribs and his collarbone broken, his skull fractured (by one account a scalp wound deep enough to lay two fingers in), nose and jaw broken — and Gordon told Maggie, untruthfully, that he was "not much hurt." (Accounts split on the horse: Park Low says Prince Rupert; the *Ballarat Star* and others, Necromancer, returning from training Viking.) It is the injury from which, physically, he arguably never recovered. He was still bed-bound when, at dawn on **14 April**, his ten-month-old daughter **Annie Lindsay Gordon** died of enteritis. He was "broken-hearted" — "passionately fond of her" — designed her headstone himself ("Suffer little children to come unto me"), and rode to the funeral beside the clergyman Adeney; she was buried near Lake Wendouree under a slab that does not record she was his (disinterred 1919 and reburied in his own grave at Brighton). Around the same time the **stables burned**, horses and vehicles lost. The fall was the wound he never recovered from physically; Annie's death the one he never recovered from emotionally — and his marriage, plunged into shared grief, suffered with him. With the stables making not a bare living over the £8 rent, he resolved to leave Ballarat. Four days after Annie died, the *Australasian* ran his **'Racing Ethics by the Turf-Cutter'** (18 & 25 Apr 1868) — an attack on the "carrion birds of the turf" and, with grim aptness, on the needless lethality of Australian steeplechasing: sun-baked iron ground, stiff red-gum timber, light stable-boys overpowered by hard-mouthed brutes, horses "crippled or cowed prematurely." Written just before or just after his own fence-fall, it reads as a man cataloguing the sport that was killing him by degrees.

**Foundation member of the Yorick — and meeting Clarke (1868).** Gordon is listed among the Yorick's 64 foundation members (May 1868), probably invited as an *Australasian* contributor, but in May he was still at Ballarat — entered as a "country" member, and unlikely to have been active until he moved to Melbourne that October (subscription paid 28 Feb 1869; a member to his death). The romantic claim that he stood beside Clarke at the naming is doubtful (see the founding-myth note). What is well attested is the friendship the club produced: Mackinnon records that "it was at the Yorick that Marcus Clarke first met" Gordon — "the first poet of Australia, as he was its boldest gentleman rider over a steeplechase course" — and was drawn to his "eccentric and mournful genius" by "a feeling of sympathy bordering on affection." Gordon donated an inscribed *Ashtaroth* to the club library, still not revealing he was the author.

**Madden — the feud that became a friendship (1868).** A characteristic episode. When "Actæon" (the attorney and horse-sketcher **Frank Madden**) reported a Melbourne hounds run without crediting Gordon's riding, Gordon fired an indignant letter to the *Australasian*; Actæon flayed him in reply as "the dare-devil horseman... pining for a puff in print." Stung but sporting, Gordon sought out the writer's name — not to duel but "that I may go and shake hands with him, for he has given me as good a drubbing as ever I got in my life" — walked into Madden's office ("Is your name Madden?"), and the two became close friends, riding steeplechases side by side until Gordon's death. Madden left some of the sharpest portraits of him: "a long, lean man... moody, taciturn and sometimes melancholy," who "rated horses above men" though "by no means a good judge of a horse," preferring rough bush company to "his own class." McCrae described the freakish jumping style — feet jammed home, head laid right back on the crupper — and the short sight that never stopped him.

**Another maiming, and the presentiments (Jun 1868).** A kick gave him a compound fracture in the hand so bad the doctor "said he would cut off my hand if I did not keep it quiet"; it set "with a big lump." And a darker thread surfaces — Gordon rode under presentiments of doom. More than once he tried to keep George Riddoch beside him to stay away from a steeplechase he dreaded (once riding ninety miles to Riddoch's station to avoid one); when Riddoch couldn't hold him, he rode anyway and had "one of his worst falls," his foreboding fulfilled. He courted the very falls he feared.

**Melbourne, the Esslemont hope, and a confession of despair (Oct 1868).** With the Ballarat stables gone (£350 sunk in rent) and Maggie sailed to South Australia (25 Sep), Gordon moved to Melbourne in October, staying with his friend **Robert Power** at Myrnong in Toorak and training Power's horses. Two things converge in his great letter to John Riddoch (6 Oct 1868). One is hope: a small legacy has come in, and his uncle Hamilton Gordon writes that Adam is the nearest heir to an entailed Scottish estate, **Esslemont** in Aberdeenshire — though Gordon suspects "a flaw in the entail," and adds, tellingly, that even winning it would be "of no use to me beyond my lifetime, and that is very uncertain." The other is despair, stated plainly: since his "heavy fall" he has "taken to drink" against "a constant pain in my head and back," grown so low that "if I had a strong sleeping-draught near me, I am afraid I might take it. I have carried one that I should never awake from." He is sick of racing ("I would like never to see a horse again"), and half-courts madness ("If I could only persuade myself that I am a little mad, I might do something of that sort"). Against all this stands Maggie: when he gave up after losing the hunt cup on Maud, she rallied him ("Don't give in like that, old man... no one else can ride the horse"), so he rode Cadger and won — "She has more pluck in her little finger than ever I had in my whole body." Of his new Melbourne circle he writes warmly: "Marcus Clarke, the editor, is a very nice young fellow."

**Voice samples (1868 catastrophes):**
- 'Racing Ethics': the dishonest layers are "the carrion birds of the turf... seeking for the corpses upon which they may gorge themselves to repletion."
- On the deadly fences: "Steeplechasing is of course intended to be a dangerous pastime, but the sport is scarcely enhanced by making it as dangerous as it can be made."
- To Maggie, carried in broken on a stretcher: "not much hurt."
- On his short sight in the saddle (to McCrae): "I see through a mist and never beyond the ears of the horse."
- Seeking out Madden after the drubbing: "that I may go and shake hands with him, for he has given me as good a drubbing as ever I got in my life."
- The confession (to Riddoch, 6 Oct 1868): "if I had a strong sleeping-draught near me, I am afraid I might take it. I have carried one that I should never awake from."
- On Maggie's courage: "She has more pluck in her little finger than ever I had in my whole body."

**Appearance, silence, and the bone that explained him (1868–69).** Accounts of Gordon disagree even on his face: McCrae insisted on a full russet beard, "a kind of Esau," and dismissed the only photograph — a clean-shaven Adelaide "sun-picture" — as "a fearful libel" with "no expression in it whatever"; Maggie, though, swore he was clean-shaven, with at most "short side-boards" (no reliable likeness survives — a gift and a problem for a novelist). McCrae's Gordon was a silent man who would sit beside a friend an hour without a word, never seen to sing or dance, who tore up sheets of manuscript over a single wrong word, and who — "like Kendall" — preferred the strict tête-à-tête. Rallied once on his abstemious habits in that hard-drinking circle, Gordon answered without words: he took McCrae's hand and laid a finger in "a long deep hollow in the bone" of his skull — a fracture from a fall — and McCrae "shuddered all over." The damaged head was the key to the man.

**Riding for Baker: the Babbler year (1868–69).** With the stables gone and writing paying little, steeplechasing became Gordon's living-by-proxy: barred as a gentleman amateur from riding for money, he could share a stake in a horse. His chief support was **Major Baker**, whose Babbler he rode to win the Hunt Club Cup at Flemington (Oct 1868) and the Ballarat steeplechase (Dec), then third in the Grand National (Jan 1869) — Standish and other backers pressing winnings on him. (It was on Baker's **Prince Rupert** that Gordon would have his last bad fall, March 1870.) He also rode Major Robins's Banker, immortalised in 'Banker's Dream.' Of the Clarke friendship one letter survives — a brisk Yorick supper-summons ("Dear Clarke, Scott's Hotel, not later than 9.30 sharp... Yours, A. Lindsay Gordon"), kept, Mackinnon says, "sacredly" by Clarke.

**The three-win day, and the peak (Oct–Nov 1868).** On Saturday 10 October 1868, at the Melbourne Hunt Club meet at Flemington, Gordon performed his legendary feat — riding the winners of three of the five races: the Hunt Club Cup on Babbler, the Metropolitan Steeplechase on Viking, and the Selling Steeplechase on Cadger. (Reports differ on whether Babbler fell or merely "blundered on to his nose" at a fence; either way Gordon recovered his seat "very cleverly.") The triple sealed his fame as one of the country's foremost steeplechase riders — and a month later he added the VRC Steeplechase on Viking (7 Nov), a critic marvelling that "nowhere in the world could such a sight be seen." It was the high-water mark of his riding life, performed in the depths of his despair. A small, sad coda: Cadger, his horse of many years, was sold at auction afterwards for £40.

**The 17 November letter: off the opiates, "ride for money," and the horse-box (Nov 1868).** A second long letter to Riddoch shows Gordon steadier but bleak. He is off the drugs — "I never touch opiates in any shape now" (confirming he had been using them) — drinking only "one glass of grog" at bedtime, exercising, sleeping. But the self-knowledge is merciless: "all my pluck and spirits are, I know, purely animal. I never had any moral courage." He had bungled the money even when he didn't bungle the riding (selling his Viking share for £75 rather than keeping it, fluffing the bets — "I've swallowed too many camels now to strain at a gnat"), and had crossed a line that mattered to a gentleman: "I ride for money now," he admits, begging Riddoch to find him any honest work so he might "swear against ever going near a racecourse again." His withdrawal shows in a haunting image: on Cup day he stayed away, and after his own steeplechase "locked myself in one of the empty horse-boxes in the saddling paddock and smoked a pipe while the other races were being run." The only ride he had enjoyed since his fall was the hunt where Maggie rode beside him. A Sladen fragment adds the death-note: "If I made a little money I should be quite contented to leave this 'gay and festive scene,' which I find awfully wearisome." On **Esslemont** he was now sure of his claim in law — the brothers, the unmarried childless heir, the old Scotch entail — yet sure too, against reason, that "I do not ever think I shall get this property."

**Likenesses (Lyttleton).** Among the few contemporary images of Gordon are the steeplechase paintings of **Thomas Lyttleton** — police superintendent, horse-painter and fellow Yorick man — who caught Gordon on Viking "topping" the last fence at the 1868 VRC Spring Steeplechase, "the peculiar style of Mr Gordon's seat... happily caught." His verse would later spawn a whole cult of illustration (Streeton, Conder, McCubbin, Lindsay, a 1926 memorial volume, a postage stamp) — "the Gordon craze."

**Voice samples (17 Nov 1868):**
- The self-diagnosis: "all my pluck and spirits are, I know, purely animal. I never had any moral courage."
- The comedown: "I ride for money now."
- The death-note: "If I made a little money I should be quite contented to leave this 'gay and festive scene,' which I find awfully wearisome."

**Ringleader or recluse? The Yorick Gordon (1868–69).** Accounts of Gordon at the Yorick split sharply. The club's own history makes him a high-spirited ringleader — "as much a ringleader as anybody else. When he played, he played hard" — and recalls how, "in an access of jovial feelings he pitched Marcus up to the ceiling and caught him coming down." But most who knew him stress the gloom: Turner found him "reticent and downcast... not quite so depressing as poor Kendall"; the solicitor James Moloney thought him "a very uninteresting man" with "no small talk" and a streak of "snobbishness," "English of the English"; Riddoch and Tenison Woods agreed he was "naturally reticent and depressed," locked "within himself," talking freely only of poetry. (Wilding floats a modern reading — that the "snobbishness" plus the freak memory might today suggest Asperger's; a speculation, not a diagnosis.) The truth is probably both: a shy depressive who could "let himself go" when the glass went round and the talk turned to horses or verse.

**'A Song of Autumn' at Myrnong (1868).** Staying with the Powers at Toorak, the shy, child-loving Gordon made a playmate of their five-year-old, Maud — carrying her on his shoulder round the garden in "grave converse." One spring afternoon, after the child moralised about plucked flowers dying (but they die unplucked too, she said, when the scorching comes), Gordon fell into a reverie and wrote 'A Song of Autumn' on a scrap of paper. Written in the Australian spring yet set in a northern-hemisphere autumn — "the burnt-up banks are yellow and sad" — it answers the child with his own mortality: "You may gather again, my dear — / But I go where the last year's lost leaves go / At the falling of the year." Six months after Annie's death, the death-note in his verse was only deepening. (Elgar would set it to music in 1892, at the prompting of Gordon's old Worcester love Jane Bridges.)

**The Brighton swim: a suicide attempt (late 1868).** The clearest act — not just word — of his death-drift. Gordon told Bob Power that he had "started off swimming from near Brighton into the bay fully determined not to return" — out and out, meaning to drown. Far from shore and nearly spent, the cold salt water and the effort "divested him of his morbid wish to die"; he turned and fought back to the beach, "quite spent and exhausted, but himself again." A literal rehearsal of the end — and a grim echo of his own sea-poem 'The Swimmer.' He was restless to escape altogether: "awfully sick of the life I have been leading, and the society that I have not been able to escape from," he wrote, recalling that even his West Australian venture had been an attempt "to escape from all my sporting associates, and begin a new life in the bush."

**The barracks, Maggie's return, and a win (Nov–Dec 1868).** Gordon left the Powers' for **Major Baker**'s quarters at the St Kilda Road barracks — the military world Woolwich had once promised him — training Babbler and boxing. (When an officer sketched him on Cadger and he feared they would not give him the drawing, he lay down on the floor with it and "suddenly rolled himself out of the door and bolted.") At the end of November Maggie came back from her two months in South Australia — Gordon haunting Queen's Wharf all afternoon, missing her, finding her at last at Scott's Hotel — and the couple took quiet lodgings at North Brighton. On 5 December he rode Baker's Babbler to win the Ballarat Handicap Steeplechase (£100 to him). He had "now decided to pursue" the **Esslemont** claim.

**The 1868 poems, and a champion in print.** Gordon's only two published poems that year went into Clarke's *Colonial Monthly*: a translation of Musset's 'The Three Friends' (three friends love one woman and all die — duel, poison, suicide — while the woman lives) and 'Doubtful Dreams.' The same December issue carried an unsigned essay, 'Australian literature no. 1' (possibly Clarke's own), singling Gordon out as, after Kendall, "the most Australian of our literary aspirants," and predicting "the day when it will be a boast to have discovered his genius in 1868." Then, oddly, nothing more of his appeared there for over a year — until 'The Sick Stockrider' in January 1870 — though he was quietly writing the poems of his last book, *Bush Ballads and Galloping Rhymes*.

**Voice sample:** the escape-wish — "I am awfully sick of the life I have been leading, and the society that I have not been able to escape from."

**The poet at work: Yallum and 'The Sick Stockrider' (early 1869).** Gordon rode some 200 miles down to John Riddoch's grand Yallum Park near Penola — climbing a gnarled gum tree by the old house to be alone, and composing there (Riddoch says) 'The Sick Stockrider.' His method was famous and strange: he wrote in the saddle, mumbling verses to himself for miles, reciting Byron, throwing a leg over the pommel to rest the paper — "he seemed to write because he could not help it" (Trainor). For Miss Riddoch's children he dashed off a St Valentine poem for their aunt **Mary Lord** ("Fresh flowers in a basket"), scribbled on the journey — possibly the verses a "young man" (perhaps Clarke) begged to publish. (Riding home on Maud at Ballarat, he ran down a miner's boy at the finish, breaking his leg; Gordon visited him in hospital and gave him "what money I could afford" — careless and humane at once.)

**Brighton: books, swimming, and the masterpiece (1869).** Back with Maggie, the Gordons took rooms from William Kelly, gardener to the great liberal **George Higinbotham**, at Brighton near the sea. Higinbotham lent the "bookless man" freely from his library — Browning and Swinburne above all ("Steeped in Swinburne and bewildered with Browning," Wilde would say) — and for the first time in years Gordon read widely again; his own collection had shrunk to "half a dozen battered volumes" including "a volume of my own verses which I can't get rid of." He swam every morning, summer or winter, half a mile out "regardless of sharks"; warned once that he'd gone too far to get back, he said that "if death came without his actually seeking it, he at least would have no cause for complaint" — the very temper of his sea-poem 'The Swimmer.' And it was at Brighton, writing every evening, that he composed most of the poems of his last and best book, *Bush Ballads and Galloping Rhymes* — his masterpiece made in his decline. Maggie traced the melancholy plainly: "a succession of bad falls, his baby's death, and the loss of his property."

**Voice samples (Brighton, 1869):**
- The swimmer's fatalism: "if death came without his actually seeking it, he at least would have no cause for complaint."
- The cripple letter: "I'm getting such an infernal old cripple that I shan't be able to stand anything soon."
- 'De Te': "No man may shirk the allotted work / The deed to do, the death to die."

**Living by the pen — and "hoped for a fall" (early 1869).** Gordon tried to turn writing into a living. The sporting press wanted him: Hammersley of the *Australasian* slipped cheques "quietly into his hand with every regard for his feelings" (Gordon was too proud to ask), and the chief, Evans, offered him a staff post as racing reporter. Gordon refused it — knowing the job would keep him among racing men and hotels, and that "a weakness for stimulants was growing upon him," with "a tendency to use opiates in order to sleep at night." "He distrusted himself." He stayed scrupulously straight as a rider even as punters backed the man over the horse — and let slip to Riddoch the line that undoes everything: the public "would not be so eager to [follow my mounts] if they knew how often I hoped for a fall." Hammersley caught him whole: proud, bush-clad but unmistakably a gentleman, forever quoting Latin, clutching his Esslemont law papers "terribly in earnest," with a "wild eye" that "seemed to look into one's very soul" — "the strangest, most weird, mysterious man I ever saw."

**Voice sample:** the death-wish in the saddle (via Riddoch) — the public "would not be so eager [to back my mounts] if they knew how often I hoped for a fall."

**On the staff after all (mid-1869).** Having refused the *Australasian's* formal sporting-reporter post in the spring, Gordon nonetheless told Blackmore by July that he was "regularly on the staff" — contributing not the perilous race-meeting circuit he'd feared but deliberately dull desk-work ("most of my contributions are purposely made as dry as possible... these pay best"), plus the occasional piece "thought well of" like 'How We Beat the Favourite.' His one substantial racing essay, 'The Ring and the Books' (July), is a wry, uncensorious defence of betting as just another speculation ("hovering between the borders of the turf and the confines of the fourth estate"). It paid poorly — King the paymaster "an awful old screw" — so he fell back on horses again, building "a tip top stable" to train and trade, and in August was made a steward of the Melbourne Hunt Club alongside Baker, Watson and Madden: a mark of social acceptance to set beside Clarke's Melbourne Club, and a way to stay among the horse-owners he needed.

**"I have not the self-denial now": Adelaide and a hard fall (October 1869).** In October Gordon at last took up Blackmore's standing invitation to Adelaide — and the reason he gave is its own quiet confession: "I have not the self-denial and strength of will now to take good exercise and to be abstemious by myself as I used to be... but with a 'guide, philosopher and friend' like yourself I could do this." The man who once disciplined himself now needed a friend to keep him straight. The English sporting-novelist George Whyte-Melville had just praised *Sea Spray* lavishly in print ("I know nothing more spirited... a real workman"), and Gordon asked, and was granted, leave to dedicate his coming *Bush Ballads* to him — a grim rhyme, since Whyte-Melville too would die in a hunting fall. Back from Adelaide, Gordon rode at a rain-lashed Hunt Club meeting that was "a chapter of accidents" and "had a severe fall at the far side of the course," never recovering his style that day. The falls kept coming.

**The last fall, and "the next world is no worse" (March 1870).** The end was close now, and Gordon seemed to know it. With Maggie away in Robe (her father dying) and himself "left alone," he took one more ride for cash — on Major Baker's big black Prince Rupert, 12 March 1870 — and his friends, fearing the worst, stationed themselves around the course "in case he came to grief." He did: Prince Rupert blundered and threw him, he recovered, then the horse fell again and got away. It was, he told Riddoch, "a very bad fall... worse than usual"; carried to Brighton that night, "there by myself I did not care much either way," and he lay five days unable to sleep — "I am hurt inside somewhere, I think... I don't think I shall get over this fall easily." Days later, on 21 March, he wrote Riddoch the bleakest letter of his life: begging a few shillings, hounded by a money-lender, clinging to the Esslemont hope ("If I last I shall come into that place") but unable to face "going through the court or being otherwise exposed" — and then, plainly: "had it not been for my wife I should have got out of my troubles somehow before this. I don't think the next world is worse than the present, and if I got a little more desperate I'm sure my wife would be better without me." His head was "failing" him; he could no longer always write. Only Maggie stood between him and the end — and he had already arranged that if Esslemont came, "all the back rents go to my wife."

**Bush Ballads, finished joylessly (April–May 1870).** Even now he was readying his masterpiece. He had "a small volume of verses... now ready," to be published for fifteen or twenty pounds, and told Riddoch "I really think they will succeed" — but the making had gone cold: "I have been writing again a little lately and my book is almost ready, but I write with great effort now and don't feel much interest in it." (English notice was arriving — Baily's Magazine praised *Sea Spray*, Swinburne praised Kendall's *Leaves*.) Everything hung on lasting: "I am quite sure about Esslemont now, if I can last out two or three months." *Bush Ballads and Galloping Rhymes* — the book that would make his name — was being finished by a man who no longer cared, holding on only for the inheritance that would provide for his wife.

**The Esslemont blow, and "riding for falls" (June 1870).** The inheritance was the last hope, and on 4 June it died: word came that Esslemont's class of entail had, by a recent court ruling, been barred — his claim failed, the £2500-a-year gone. "This Esslemont affair upset him more than any of his other troubles," McCrae thought; he had "almost decided on taking his passage home" and had even "had travelling trunks made to order in anticipation of this trip home" (Maggie). Now there was nothing: no estate, no escape, no means of paying debts mounted past £450 (Brighton tradesmen, the money-lender at 90 per cent, £200 to Riddoch, £75 to Massina, £30 still owing on his own book). Robertson had declined *Bush Ballads*, so he was self-publishing it at his own cost — a masterpiece finished without humour or interest. He "had no wish to continue riding, and falling, in steeplechases," yet observers saw him court the falls anyway: forcing "an inexperienced horse over all the most difficult jumps... long after the race had been won," until a watching general said flatly "he was riding for falls"; and refusing in a gale to take in sail until his companion protested he had "a strong objection" to being drowned alongside him. Walking to a cricket match shortly before the end, he told McCrae of a planned magnum opus, 'Penthesilea' — never written.

**The two poets: Gordon and Kendall (1869).** With Kendall — newly arrived — the withdrawn Gordon found a congenial fellow-poet, though the bond was real rather than deep. They shared no tastes (Kendall "loathed... the idiotic frenzy of the race-course"; Gordon lived for it) and "never became bosom friends," and proud poverty kept them out of each other's houses — but Gordon "looked beneath the odd exterior... saw an inner core of goodness," and "their common poetic enthusiasm made a partial bond." Howlett-Ross remembered them "strolling down Collins Street" together; Gordon saluted Kendall in 'From the Wreck' ("Let Kendall write legends in letters of gold," a stanza he later cut); and after Gordon's death Kendall's grief was unfeigned — "hallowed by the tenderest lights of an abiding friendship," "that royal spirit so gifted, so human, and so unfortunate." (The later myth that Gordon taught Kendall to drink is false — Gordon drank little; Kendall's was the family curse.) The warmth was real on Gordon's side: praising Kendall's 'The Hut by the Black Swamp,' he wrote that he now knew it "by heart," rated it "better as a piece of music than Swinburne's Felise," and — generous and self-deprecating — "burnt" his own poem-in-progress on reading Kendall's ("I am sick of writing").

**Reluctant fame: 'How We Beat the Favourite' (12 June 1869).** The same Saturday Horne sailed for England, Gordon's 'How We Beat the Favourite' appeared anonymously in *The Australasian* — and made him famous overnight. "Within a few days every sporting man in Melbourne knew it by heart" (Madden); it became his best-loved poem. Clarke caught the shyness behind it: "feeling much of that shame at the exercise of the higher intelligence which besets those who are known to be renowned in field sports, Gordon produced his poems shyly, scribbled them on scraps of paper, and sent them anonymously" — owning his authorship only once the couplets were on every tongue. He was, by all accounts, "one of the worst readers of his poetry" alive: at a Melbourne Hospital gathering he read it so badly that young James Moloney complained, whereupon Gordon good-naturedly handed him the poem to read aloud and made him a present of the manuscript.

**Voice sample:** Clarke on Gordon — the renowned horseman's "shame at the exercise of the higher intelligence."

**'The Sick Stockrider' (January 1870).** Composed in the gum tree at Yallum and published as the *Colonial Monthly* folded, 'The Sick Stockrider' became Gordon's — and arguably Australia's — foundational poem: W. H. Wilde marks it as the source from which "the ensuing hosts of bush ballads derived," "the moment when the literature of this country began to move in a... characteristically Australian direction." Gordon, typically, shrugged: "they made quite a stir here... I don't think much of them myself." Unable to recite his own verse, he had Madden learn and perform it for him. And he cut its final stanza on a colleague's advice — withdrawing, Sutherland thought, lines that "laid bare his own heart": the dying stockman's flat truth that "it makes no difference to the dead man underground / What the living men remember or forget," and that some, "as weak as water... to make the best of life, / Have been, to face the worst, as true as steel." Clarke's preface caught what made it real: "The writer has ridden his ride as well as written it."

**The end (24 June 1870).** *Bush Ballads and Galloping Rhymes* was published on 23 June. That day Gordon went into town: to Massina's, where he owed £75 and promised "a cheque in the morning"; among friends who gave him "friendly criticism" of the book; and he was shown, in proof, Kendall's glowing *Australasian* review, which called him a poet who had "made a rich addition to the permanent possessions of English literature" and "laid Australia under a deep and lasting obligation." He met Kendall, and the two ruined poets sat a while over drinks — by one account with seven shillings between them, which they spent. The accounts of his mood conflict: to one friend at the Yorick he seemed "unusually cheerful... spoke very hopefully of his prospects"; to Madden, who walked with him to St Kilda about four o'clock, he was "absolutely sober, but very much depressed" — a friend had just refused to lend him the £100 he needed to get to England. He asked Madden to keep the manuscript of his poems "as a present"; "I did not think when he said present he meant a memento." That night he was restless, in and out of the house. Early the next morning he rose without waking Maggie — though she half-woke at the touch of his beard as he bent to kiss her — whispered that he was going for a walk, and took his rifle. He walked into the tea-tree scrub by the Brighton beach he had swum from all year, and there, with deliberation, he shot himself. He was found at nine by a grocer searching for a stray cow; his hat lay a few yards off, set down before the end, holding his knife, his pipe, some tobacco, and a single shilling. The thing that had held him so long — "restrained by the thought of his wife," as he had more than once admitted — was, at the last, not enough. He was thirty-six.

**After (June 1870).** The inquest returned the expected verdict — death "by his own hand while of unsound mind." Dr Murray, who had attended him, testified that the steeplechase falls had fractured his skull and left his brain "much affected," prone to "delusion, and to attacks of melancholy"; "he himself has said he was mad." Maggie disputed the coarser details — that he was drunk and quarrelsome that last night: he never touched strong drink, she said; he had paced the room "quoting verses and mumbling to himself." The funeral, the next day, was small and quiet — one mourning coach, Major Baker and the Power brothers, a straggle of others over the three miles to Brighton Cemetery, where (McCrae remembered) there were "no heavy clods, only the purest of white sand." Maggie insisted, against her friends' wishes, on following him to the grave. The verdicts on why he died never settled: Massina thought it "a small financial embarrassment" — the £75 cheque he could not make good; Tenison Woods, a "morbid melancholy"; his own uncle, writing from England, "the strangeness of manner in the family." Only the week before, he had sat reading his poems aloud to the McCraes. The day after the funeral, *The Argus* reviewed *Bush Ballads* with enthusiasm.

**The afterlife (1870 onward).** The racing world farewelled its own — "Here comes Gordon," the cry as he cantered down last from the saddling-paddock (a rule he made, to be "last out"); the unmistakable throw of his body back in the saddle over a fence; and again the falls, the Prince Rupert spill when "he lay a long time stunned and bleeding... his head was then much affected." His own last published lines were elegy: "But I go where the last year's lost leaves go / At the falling of the year." Maggie went back to Robe. She packed their few possessions into the travelling trunks once made for the voyage to England — one box reserved for Gordon's riding things — and that box vanished on the journey, taking with it, perhaps, the only draft of 'Penthesilea.' She kept his whip and bridle, married a surveyor in 1873, and with her son preserved the relics that became a small industry of memory — saddle-bags (he had tried to leave Trainor the copyright of his poems, but there was none), a lock of hair, the stockwhip, the bank-book that showed he had once had means. When his Brighton house was demolished the bricks were salvaged but never rebuilt, the mayor scoffing that "a man who is behind in his rent is not worth remembering" — even as a hotel hung a plaque calling him "a shining soul with syllables of fire." The canonization was swift. Within months a subscription raised a monument over the Brighton grave — a fluted Doric column "broken off, to complete the sad picture of the poet's life, so rudely shattered by death in the day of its perfection and strength" — and *The Australasian* was already calling him "our greatest — we had almost said our only — national poet." Mrs Lauder planted a black wattle over him, taking the cue from his own 'Sick Stockrider' ("Let me slumber in the hollow where the wattle blossoms wave"), tended the grave thirty years, and sent its seed to schools across Australia; the annual pilgrimage drew four thousand by 1921. In 1934 a bust was unveiled in Poets' Corner, Westminster Abbey — Gordon the one Australian poet so honoured. The man found in the scrub with a shilling in his hat had become the national poet within a generation. Even Anthony Trollope, touring in 1872–73 and all but denying Australia had a literature, made an exception for Gordon — struck by the *Bush Ballads*' "energy" and "genius," judging 'Britomarte' alone enough "to entitle him to be called a poet." He had heard the story at Yallum Park: a young gentleman who "had not done well... fallen into a sad melancholy, and had – died." Trollope first wrote "destroyed himself"; it was softened for print.

His widow profited least of all. The copyright tangle left Maggie selling her rights to Clarson Massina for "a very small sum, much less than they were worth," the firm holding a dishonoured £70 of Gordon's against her claim — and then *Poems of the Late Adam Lindsay Gordon* (1880), carrying Clarke's preface, became a runaway bestseller: 20,000 copies in a decade, dozens of editions, the bedrock of his fame. "I have often regretted parting with the rights," Maggie said in old age, "but it is too late to trouble about that now." The man canonized as the national poet enriched his publishers and his country; his widow got a pittance.

### Henry Kendall

**A disreputable inheritance.** Kendall's line was poorer and wilder than Clarke's or Gordon's. His grandfather **Thomas Kendall** (b. Lincolnshire 1778) went out as a missionary to the Bay of Islands (1814) and did real scholarship on the Maori language — but also traded muskets to the Maori, abandoned his wife, and at forty-three set up house with the seventeen-year-old daughter of a Maori chief; suspended as "a public disgrace to the sacred ministry," he drifted to Chile, took up cedar-trading land at Ulladulla, and drowned in a shipwreck at fifty-one. Henry's father **Basil** — who "animated to a slight extent with the poetic fire" — was a forger: convicted of uttering a forged £5 bank order (1847), two years' hard labour, dead of consumption at forty-two (1852). His mother **Melinda** (surname recorded as McAllan or McNally, first name as Millinda/Metinda/Matilda), an Irish policeman's daughter, claimed descent from **Leonard McNally** — the United Irishmen's trusted advocate who was secretly a paid government informer. She loved literature, wrote verse, reputedly published a volume; Henry always credited her with his education and his bent.

**Childhood & the bush.** Born a twin (with Basil Edward), 18 Apr 1839, in a bush humpy at Kirmington near present-day Milton, NSW — though in later life he liked to shave off a couple of years and claim 1841–42. The beauty of the country south of Sydney ('Araluen', 'Illa Creek', 'Kiama') marked his first book. From 1845 the family moved north to the Clarence/Orara (Gordon Brook station); after the father's ruin and death, his mother kept a day school near Wollongong.

**The South Seas & "Mad Harry" (1855–59).** At sixteen Kendall shipped out for ~18 months as an apprentice in the South Sea Islands trade (the whaler *Waterwitch* under Capt. Lee, possibly also his uncle Joseph's brig) — a Melville-tinged interlude Clarke later romanced (Typee, Omoo, "a glimpse of Moby Dick"). Back in Sydney by 1857, an eighteen-year-old errand-boy for Biddell Brothers the confectioners, he carried trays of tarts on his head through the streets, lips working over half-made lines, eyes vacant — "There goes Mad Harry!" His first poem, 'O, Tell Me, Ye Breezes' (1859), appeared — poignantly — in a temperance journal; more followed in *The Empire* and (from 1861) the *Herald*. He signed himself "N.A.P." (Native Poet), which *The Empire* gently mocked.

**Voice samples (early):**
- The South Seas, remembered: "The wonderful islands of the South Pacific are mirrored in my memory yet."
- On *The Empire* and the literary establishment (to J. S. Moore, 1864): "How I hate that paper!... Why are they and men like Fairfax allowed to trample on the head of Genius? Some day yet I shall take my proper place in the world of letters."

**Into Sydney's literary world (1860–61).** By twenty-one Kendall had twenty-one poems in print and a foothold in literary Sydney. **J. Sheridan Moore** — a Dublin lawyer's son, ex-Benedictine and schoolmaster — became his first mentor and brought him into the circle of the Balmain bibliophile **Nicol Stenhouse** (a former friend of De Quincey, whose 4,000-volume library later went to the University): the poet-politician Daniel Deniehy, the poet-barrister James Michael, and Dr John Woolley, first principal of the University, who secured Kendall standing access to the University Library and offered him a free Arts course — declined, since he had to earn a living. With Moore superintending, Kendall published his first book, *Poems and Songs*, by subscription — the drive launched in 1861 — and it appeared in October 1862, advertised as "the boy-poet of Australia."

**London recognition & the reception of Poems and Songs (1862).** Kendall aimed beyond the colony: the *Cornhill* ignored him, but the London *Athenæum* (Sep 1862) printed three poems with his audacious covering letter — a self-educated bush poet announcing himself across "16,000 miles," his later work "too long and too Australian to be cared for by Englishmen." The *Athenæum* judged his verse "dark and sorrowful" but full of energy. *Poems and Songs* sold 369 of 500 copies before the publisher went bankrupt and Kendall lost money. The reviews marked him as the most promising — and most melancholy — of native poets: Barton called it the high-water mark of Australian verse, with no poet "drawing such dismal meanings from the external world," as if he'd been "lost in the bush" early and learned to tie horror to beauty. Kendall later annotated his own copy ruefully ("the sincerest records of dead emotions"); another carries a note by **Henry Lawson**, who bought it secondhand in 1895.

**Grafton, debt, and the day-job (1862–64).** Back in Grafton (Dec 1862–Apr 1863) he lectured ably on Shelley, Wordsworth and Burns — but a planned lecture on Australian literature collapsed in stage fright (see scene candidates). *Poems and Songs* left him, for the first time, in debt: "I never owed ten shillings before 1862... Confound all literature! The pen now burns my fingers." He copied out Michael's verse for an unpublished volume even as his real allegiance shifted to **Charles Harpur**, the older native-born poet he revered (he pointedly rededicated a poem from Michael to Harpur in 1863). Returned to Sydney, he took a clerkship in the Lands/Surveyor-General's office (1863, ~£150/yr, via Robertson and Halloran) — and weathered a strange family humiliation when his twin **Basil**, exploiting their likeness, impersonated Henry to borrow money around town, forcing a public CAUTION notice (Aug 1864). The family genius for fraud shadows him still — the forger father, now the impersonating twin, and Henry's own forged cheque still to come.

**Voice samples (1862–64):**
- The *Athenæum* appeal (Jul 1862): "I am an Australian, and a self-educated one... Don't turn from me... because I am a native of a country yet unrepresented in literature."
- On his first debt (Mar 1863): "Confound all literature! The pen now burns my fingers. I shall slowly and peaceably settle down into a mere money-grubber."

**Parkes's patronage & the Colonial Secretary's office (1866).** When **Henry Parkes** — himself a published poet — became NSW Colonial Secretary in 1866, Kendall courted him as a fellow versifier, even floating the idea of standing for Parliament himself (Parkes gently advised him to wait). He borrowed £25 from Parkes in April (and another £5 in October, unable to repay the last of the first), and in December 1866 Parkes's patronage landed him a clerkship in the Colonial Secretary's department (£250/yr). His gratitude curdled: a decade on he wrote that Parkes "transferred me, with my coppers and pawn ticket, to his office... When I took up my position at his damned desk I was a youth with Apollonian locks. When I left it I was fit for the Liverpool Asylum." Recognition kept coming (the *Athenæum* again, Feb 1866; Barton's high praise; *Sydney Punch*'s skit on the "three literary Henrys" — Parkes, Halloran, Kendall — at their desks). But a warning stood close by: in 1866 **Daniel Deniehy** drank himself to death at 37, and Kendall's elegy and letters dwell on the wasted, "transfigured" ruin of a gifted man — the braid's first drink-death, and a mirror Kendall could not yet see was his own.

**Voice samples (Parkes / Deniehy):**
- On Parkes (1878, ungrateful): "When I took up my position at his damned desk I was a youth with Apollonian locks. When I left it I was fit for the Liverpool Asylum."
- On Deniehy's relapse: "he went into the cursed drink with more infatuation than before."

**Rose (1866–67).** Around this time Kendall loved **Rose**, daughter of *Empire* publisher Samuel Bennett — the muse of 'Rose Lorraine' ("There is no passion like the first"). The romance ended, by legend, in a fit of mutual stubbornness: seeing her off at Redfern station (Mar 1867), he would only retrieve her dropped parasol if she said "please," she wouldn't say it, the train pulled away, and the rift never closed.

**The Sydney Punch brotherhood, the Dyspeptic Lectures & meeting Charlotte (1866–67).** Though a constant target of *Sydney Punch* (and likely the author of the anonymous counter-satire *The Bronze Trumpet*, 1866), Kendall joined its staff — a "Bohemian brotherhood" meeting weekly over "cognac and cigars" to plot the cartoon, a Sydney rehearsal for the Yorick clubland to come. In Apr–Jun 1867 he ran five 'Dyspeptic Lectures' whose subjects now read as grim self-prophecy: love as the opium-eater's dream that "awakens to the tortures of the damned," the miseries of marriage, and 'Concerning Debt' ("you will become prostrated — a slave"). He ground away meanwhile as a public servant, complaining to Harpur of being worked mercilessly. On 17 Sep 1867, after lecturing on "Love," he met **Charlotte Rutter**, eighteen, a doctor's daughter; struck by her attentiveness, he thought "such a good sister would make a good wife," and they soon married. His letters to "Lottie" are ardent.

**Voice samples (1867):**
- 'Dyspeptic Lecture' on love: it is the opium-eater's dream that "awakens to the tortures of the damned."
- To Charlotte: "I want to be good and clever for your sake... attain to the highest point of literary excellence for the end of pleasing you."

**Grafton and James Lionel Michael (1862–65).** In Feb 1862 Kendall left Sydney to clerk for **James Lionel Michael** — Wandsworth-born lawyer-poet, once a friend of Ruskin and Millais — who had settled in Grafton on the Clarence. Michael became his true literary education: a magnificent library at his disposal, and the man who steered him toward Shelley, Mrs Browning, Tennyson, and French (Béranger, Hugo). Kendall's widow Charlotte said he "learned more from him" than from anyone. Yet the regard curdled into ambivalence — Michael was "a smart chatterbox who has a happy knack of persuading everybody that he knows everything," his "verse feeble and his prose flippant." Tellingly, Kendall would later level the very same charge at Clarke ('A Colonial Literary Club', 1871) — a recurring pattern of devouring then disparaging his mentors (he had already "cut" the Stenhouse-circle patrons Halloran and Woolley by Sep 1862). From April 1862 his poems ran in the Clarence and Richmond Examiner alongside the Sydney papers.

**Marriage and the O'Farrell shooting (Mar 1868).** On **7 March 1868** Kendall married Charlotte at Glebe, Sydney — six months after they met, the banns dispensed with by special faculty (he 29, she 19). He married under the self-invented name **"Henry Clarence Kendall"** (baptized plain Thomas Henry; "Clarence" probably in homage to the Grafton river he'd lived beside) — a small, telling flourish of self-fashioning, though his books stayed simply "Henry Kendall." A week later, on 13 March, the **O'Farrell** assassination attempt on the Duke of Edinburgh convulsed Sydney; Kendall's patron Parkes exploited it with a (later discredited) Fenian-conspiracy theory, and Kendall — the loyalist for hire — contributed two poems to the commemorative *Prince Alfred's Wreath*.

**A hard first married year: a sick wife, a drinking mother, dying mentors (1868).** The marriage darkened fast. Kendall's heavy-drinking mother Melinda moved in with the newlyweds, and Charlotte fell ill almost at once — by 31 May he was writing, "I am afraid that I have married a chronic invalid." Then his mentors died in quick succession: Michael drowned on 28 April, and on **10 June Charles Harpur** died of consumption — "a noble ruin... scorched and wasted, as it were, by fire," Kendall wrote, ranking him above Deniehy for his "aboriginal power." His memorial poem ran in the *SMH* (7 July). Within six weeks both the men who had taught him were gone. In the same season he was looking south — his letter to Oakley (30 June) carries his first known mention of **Marcus Clarke** ("I am sorry to hear of the illness of Marcus Clarke"): Melbourne, and the braid, beckoning.

**The outsider's chill eye (the class fissure).** Alone of the three, Kendall was native-born, barely schooled, and had worked for wages — and he watched the Yorick's English public-school gentlemen with "a clear if chill eye." His 1871 memoir of the club skewers their pedigree-worship: a "leading spirit" whose chief "qualification was that he knew a gentleman who had a brother, who during the Rev. Charles Kingsley's schooldays had acted as a 'fag' to that genius." Where Clarke, Gordon and the rest carried the colony's transplanted English class system as second nature, Kendall stood just outside it — a difference that quietly shadows his place in the braid.

**The dipsomaniac mother, a prize, and a second child (1868–69).** Two forces pulled Kendall toward Melbourne. One was misery at home: his mother Melinda was a "confirmed dipsomaniac" whom, during his government years, he'd had to "keep quiet by bribing her with brandy every night"; after his marriage he tried restraint, she "became furious," Charlotte "couldn't stand her," and it helped drive him out of Sydney — she was, he said, "too insane to be responsible for her actions," and more than once turned up drunk at his work. ("As a characteristic sequel to her family history, my mother is on the spree," he wrote that December.) The other force was the loss of his patron — Parkes resigned in October 1868 and the ministry fell, dimming his Sydney prospects while he sank into debt. Two bright spots cut the gloom: on 2 January 1869 Charlotte bore a second daughter, **Lizzie (Violet) Araluen** (the delicate mother, the strong child; Brereton lent them his Gladesville cottage — "Take your fill of love. Leave the Devil to his own"); and that same month Kendall won the poetry prize in Williams's *Illustrated Australian Annual* for **'A Death in the Bush'** (as "Arakoon"), judged — with rich irony — by Horne.

**Arrival in Melbourne: the braid completes (April 1869).** Kendall resigned the public service and, "wearying of official routine," took ship for Melbourne — landing on "an ominous first of April" 1869 with four shillings and a few letters of introduction (bailiffs had hastened his exit from Sydney). Charlotte and baby Araluen followed by the SS *Dandenong* on the 18th; they took a house facing the Carlton Gardens. With this, all three principals were at last in the same city — the convergence the novel is built toward. A try at office work collapsed at once: set to balance a "deaths sheet" in the Registrar-General's statistical branch, he floundered for three days, could not make it add across, drew his pay and "never darkened that office door again" ("it only means softening of the brain or madness if further persisted in"). He committed to letters instead — and arrived in print immediately: 'Ogyges' in the *Colonial Monthly* (April, sharing the issue with Clarke's 'Pretty Dick'), the now-famous 'Bell Birds' (May), 'Galatea' and more in *The Australasian*. "He had arrived."

**The Yorick induction, and a portrait in black.** Clarke welcomed him in — "I must make you an honorary member of the Golgotha... Let us imbibe another beer, and start at once" — and led him up the "villainous ladder" to the dingy club room (the over-plentiful spittoons, the shabby den, the red-nosed sporting editor). Kendall, the native-born outsider, eyed the unliterary members drily, but used the place and joined; Clarke seconded his membership on 29 July 1869. He cut a singular figure there. Sutherland remembered "an undertaker from some melodrama dropped in among a godless set of jovial spirits" — black frock-coat, black gloves, the narrow-brimmed topper on its crown, the umbrella across his knees, never drinking, never talking, drawing "a furtive puff or two" before gathering hat and umbrella and leaving, "the subject of droll witticisms." McCrae drew him spare, pale, sad, nervous (forever clutching that umbrella), good-natured but "quick to take offence," writing all his poems with the left hand (the right half-paralysed). McCrae called him "Mr Mould"; Kendall called McCrae "Quilp."

**Voice samples (Kendall in Melbourne):**
- Arriving: he landed "on an ominous first of April... with a stock-in-trade consisting of two or three letters of introduction, and a sum in money not exceeding four shillings."
- On the office job: "it only means softening of the brain or madness if further persisted in."
- Accepting the Yorick (to Clarke's offer): "That I was a scribbler I was too vain to deny, the invitation to beer I was too modest to refuse."

**What he fled: the family that ruined him (the Neild letter, June 1869).** From the safety of the Yorick — secret from his wife, secret even from Clarke — Kendall poured out the full story of his Sydney ruin to Dr Neild on 22 June 1869. For years before his marriage he had been the sole support of his mother and sisters, handing over "every shilling" of his salary, even going without lunch to give them more. They repaid him with betrayal: his youngest sister secretly married "an unmitigated scamp" (a marriage contracted, they told him, "as a safeguard against me"), and Kendall — the nominal head of the house — was billed for the wedding fees and the bridegroom's outfit; his brother in Queensland forged his name for £45, which Kendall borrowed and paid "to save the whole family from disgrace"; the sisters "wore nothing but silks" while his wife pinched, "to keep up appearances for your sake." Worst of all, they settled the household furniture on the sister's husband, made Kendall "a mere lodger," and when he came to collect his mother and his share, "the bill of settlement... was shown to me, and I was laughed at" — leaving him "with a newly married wife and without a stick." The moneylenders closed on him "like a cancer"; as a civil servant he could not take the insolvency court; resignation and flight were his "last and only source." "I saved my life by the step I took in leaving Sydney." The poetic temperament, he reflected, "is generally a thing to play upon."

**The working writer (1869–70).** For all the begging letters, Kendall worked hard at his trade. Clarke made good a *Colonial Monthly* debt owed him from before his arrival and — when Kendall offered to write for nothing — insisted on paying him; Kendall appeared there and in the *Australian Journal* (every issue from July 1869 to March 1870) with poems and prose. Poetry could not feed a family ("no livelihood could possibly be made from their publication"), so he mined the one capital Clarke and Gordon lacked: his South Seas youth, turning out saleable adventure prose ('A Fight with a Devil Fish,' 'Sperm Whaling') from a man who had actually cruised the Pacific. His closest Melbourne friend was not Gordon but **G. G. McCrae**; at the *Colonial Monthly* office, its walls frescoed with caricatures (a "Kendall or two" over the fireplace), the office-boy "Shrimp" dubbed the umbrella-clutching, wild-haired poet "the snaky Poet," a "Son of Medusa."

**An awkward debt (July 1869).** The small world tripped him up. Neild had quietly arranged for Kendall to borrow money from — of all people — Walter Montgomery, just as Kendall was joining the anonymous *Punch* squibs mocking Montgomery's vanity. Mortified to have lampooned his own benefactor (the editor had printed his initials and altered a stanza without consent), Kendall wrote Neild a stricken apology, insisting he held Montgomery "a man of unquestionable genius" and that "in the whole course of my five years' association with the press, I have never written a line calculated to wound the feelings of anybody." A small, telling study of his thin-skinned decency — and of how tangled bohemia's debts and feuds had become.

**"Your letter has frightened me": the begging, and a fraying mind (July–August 1869).** The Rusden letters show how near the edge he was. Newly nominated to the Yorick, earning "sometimes £5 per week — at other times nothing," he wrote secretly to the parliamentary clerk G. W. Rusden begging a pound on a post-dated *Argus* order ("a stranger in a grey gloomy flinty-hearted city"), asking him to find any government berth, even shepherding — "anything is better than the hard iron destitution that I am set face to face with." Then something stranger: Rusden's reply referred to Kendall having written of "destructive news from England," which Kendall had no memory of saying. He floundered to explain it — "I never drink. In view of that fact, it is clear to me that I must have been suffering from an excitement equivalent to temporary insanity" — and signed off, shaken: "Your letter has frightened me." An early, chilling glimpse of the dissociation and breakdown that lay ahead.
