**The depth of the wretchedness (August 1869).** Within months of arriving, his condition was dire. When Parkes — owed £30 and now in trouble himself — threatened to sue, Kendall answered from the floor of despair: he had feared even to consult Parkes, whose "painfully frigid" manner "repelled and silenced me" ("you almost told me to go. I felt choked, and went"); and as for the threat of gaol, "it is out of your power to inflict blows heavier than those I have already received... If you were to put me in gaol, I should probably be happier than I am now." He withdrew his own name from the Yorick ballot — "a bitter farce on my part to seek election. I have not earned a penny for the last month" — and confessed the rest to Neild: "My health is failing fast. I have not slept three hours for the last seven days"; "I can't pay my rent. I am dunned for it. Sleep and food are things I don't care about. I hide all these things from my wife. The deception is wicked." He refused the doctor's prescription — "I intend to fight it out with open eyes" — and begged that Charlotte never learn of the charity from Montgomery and the *Argus*. The insomnia, the not-eating, the secrecy, the longing for "rest": the breakdown was already well advanced.

**Leaves from Australian Forests (September 1869).** Amid the wretchedness came his second book — fifty-eight poems from George Robertson (5s; 1,500 copies, 500 for England), dedicated to Charlotte in lines of plain gratitude ("Who, when I thought all light was out, became / A lamp of hope that put my fears to shame... Who faced for love's sole sake the life austere / That waits upon the man of letters here"). "The *Colonial Monthly* of October has put me at the top of the tree," he wrote — yet "I should prefer a steady income of some kind or other, to all sorts of literary fame." Like Gordon's *Sea Spray*, it failed commercially (Robertson lost £90; the profit-share after 700 copies never triggered) — "the feeble glow of public applause is too easily replaced by a chilling frost."

**The satirist, and "the first poet in whom Australia speaks" (autumn 1869).** Two things cut against the gloom. First, Kendall was more than the melancholy lyrist of legend: with Clarke he co-edited the satirical *Humbug*, turning out sharp topical work — a usurer's monologue ('L.S.D.-ism': "My mission in life is to make money"), the temperance broadside 'The Demon of Drink,' the title-poem flaying the "god of the guineas and dollars." Second, *Leaves from Australian Forests* won him real critical stature even as it failed to sell: the *Argus* likened his ear to Swinburne's and his eye to Keats's ("there is no twilight in his poetry... broad day or blackest night"); the *Economist* called him "a true poet"; and Oakley's *Colonial Monthly* review crowned him "the first poet in whom... Australia speaks," declaring "a great poet is rising in the land." (Oakley also tutted at the "libidinous" heat of his love poetry — "not the women of love, but of lust.") Famous and broke at once: the national poet, sleepless over the rent.

**A catalogue of despair (October 1869).** His essay 'About Some Men of Letters in Australia' reads, in the end, like a survey of his own fears: Harpur's life "so sad, so full of hopeless trouble from beginning to end," Deniehy's "vicissitudes of a really hard life," Michael's "syllables of despair" ending in suicide — the doomed roll of the Australian writer, into which he was plainly writing himself. (He still found room to call Clarke "the brilliant author of *Long Odds*.") A Yorick testimonial, got up that autumn, kept the wolf from his own door — "for a while."

**Souring with Clarke (winter 1869–70).** The Clarke partnership curdled. Kendall bridled at Clarke's *Humbug* parody mocking his drinking ('Glycera'), and grumbled in his own verse about his "bear" of an editor who paid "a note — a wretched one-pound-note — per column." When *Humbug* died, it was Kendall who wrote the cruel mock-obituary of it in the rival *Touchstone* — and then, calling on Clarke to borrow money, denied authorship to his face. He confessed the lie to James Smith: "I was weak enough to imply that I was not the writer of the objectionable verses... The prevarication was wicked, though I sinned to save Clarke's feelings." The compromised heart of the prickliest dyad: mocking the man, lying to him, and borrowing from him, all at once.

**Into the squalor (1870).** Through 1870 Kendall came apart. The family moved from house to house — Carlton, Fitzroy, Collingwood, Richmond — as "poverty, disappointment, and anxiety wrought an unutterable depression" (Sutherland): "he grew more and more unsteady, became less capable of work, and drifted rapidly into squalor," the family hidden "in a dingy lane of Richmond," the poet spending "his evenings in obscure public-houses, and his nights too often seated in some lane or right-of-way." The national poet was now drinking in taprooms and sleeping in alleys. Years later he wrote the self he had been — the man "who used down fetid lanes to slink, / And squat in tap-room corners grim, / And drown his thoughts in dregs of drink" — and the "fierce remorse" that followed, beside which "the hell the Christian fears to name / Was Heaven."

**The death of Araluen (2 February 1870).** The blow at the centre of his life fell that February: his first-born daughter Araluen — "a pocket edition of her father," Neild said — died at the Swan Street cottage of "dental fever aggravated by malnutrition," and Kendall could not afford the funeral. He had been begging five shillings for her prescription days before ("Baby is in an almost dying state"). Charlotte said "the wailing voice of the child before her death seemed to haunt him ever afterwards — she suffered so," and Kendall came to believe her death was his own fault, for failing to earn enough to keep her — a guilt that hardened, years later, into a "delusion that he was being accused of the murder of his daughter." He wrote it twice in 1879: the father "writing by the dead / To earn the undertaker's fee" ('On a Street'), and the lacerating 'Araluen' to Charlotte — "Three there were, but one has vanished. Sins of mine have made you weep; / But forgive your baby's father now that baby is asleep." It is the wound from which the rest of his ruin flows.

**Reaching for Gordon (March 1870).** As both men foundered, Kendall reached for his fellow-poet. Learning late of Gordon's bad fall — "extreme trouble... prevented me from writing" — he wrote to thank Gordon for defending him against "the scandalous lies promulgated by Shillinglaw and Clarke," and to confess his own desperation: "I have not been without sins myself but my sins have been those arising from desperation... The day baby died I had not a shilling to bury her with." When Gordon, gravely injured, did not reply, Kendall wrote again, wounded: "I feel your silence very keenly... Sincere respect for your genius is the only impulse that has led me to write to you again. Your silence looks like an undeserved slight." Neither knew how near the other was to the end. (A bright note that April: Horsley was commissioned to set a Kendall poem as the cantata for the new Town Hall's opening.)

**"Don't trust me": the loss of control (April–May 1870).** From Atherton Street, Fitzroy — the "haggard street" of his later poem, where Mrs Kendall pawned their goods under the alias "Mrs Clarence" — Kendall wrote James Smith a letter of painful self-knowledge. He had wanted Smith to *hold* his pay, "from a knowledge of my weakness in resisting, while in the possession of money, the demands of pressing creditors"; when the landlord abused him and Charlotte at the door loudly enough to draw passers-by, the ashamed Kendall handed over Smith's money for the rent. "If you do, draw the money yourself and don't trust me. I have no desire to be dishonest, but when placed in the position I have described I have no control over my impulses." It is Kendall's version of Gordon's self-distrust — a man who sees his own weakness clearly and cannot master it.

**Writing his own future: Supple (May 1870).** That month a Yorick member, the Irish-nationalist barrister **Gerald Supple**, shot at the politician G. P. Smith in Spring Street, missed, and killed a bystander. Kendall, who knew him, wrote of Supple with uncommon feeling — a "quiet, lonely man... damned with a sensitiveness that almost amounted to disease," who magnified "every little accidental slight... into a glaring injury" and went on "brooding, and fancying, and fretting, till the demon of insanity had him fairly in its grip." It reads as unconscious self-portrait: McCrae would describe Kendall in nearly the same words — "over-sensitive... apt to fancy malice where nothing but fun was intended." In Supple's doomed sensitivity Kendall was, without knowing it, describing the temperament that would help carry him to his own breakdown.

**Two grieving fathers, one image (June 1870).** Kendall's 'Elijah' (June 1870) lifted three lines, unacknowledged, from Gordon's still-unpublished 'The Road to Avernus' — lines about a dead baby's "small sweet mouth" at the mother's "yearning breast." Both poems were written out of the same wound: Gordon's grief for his daughter Annie, Kendall's for Araluen. Gordon, his poem not yet in print, "was not amused." Kendall apologised from the Yorick with disarming honesty — "the lines in 'Elijah' were copied from you in a moment when my memory was in a maze... the beauty of your verse, and its relation to my late loss, caused me to carry it away in my head" (the same unconscious absorption by which he had once taken a line from Horne's *Orion*) — and printed a public acknowledgement. He used the letter to call Gordon's coming *Bush Ballads* "the highest result of the poetic faculty yet attained in Australia." Two bereaved fathers, their dead daughters meeting in a single borrowed image: the tenderest and strangest moment of the dyad.

**The last meeting (23 June 1870).** Kendall's part in Gordon's last day was tender and terrible. His *Australasian* review of *Bush Ballads* — written that week, shown to Gordon in proof — was a ringing tribute: the poet had "made a rich addition to the permanent possessions of English literature," work that would be "read, and enjoyed long after this... generation has passed away." And it was Kendall whom Gordon met and drank with that afternoon, the two destitute poets sharing what little they had (seven shillings, by one account) in a Collins Street bar. It was the last time they saw each other. Within a day Gordon was dead — and Kendall had given him, at the very end, the recognition he most deserved and least believed in.

**The empty seat (June 1870).** Kendall did not go to the funeral. McCrae, who had saved him a free seat in the club's wagonette, got only a hurried note pleading poverty — "I am penniless" — which Kendall had begun "Dear Gordon," to the dead man, before crossing it out and writing "McCrae." McCrae did not believe the excuse: he put the absence down to shock, certain that had Kendall come he "would only... break down utterly at the grave." (A later, unsubstantiated legend turned it darker still — that Gordon had proposed a joint suicide and Kendall had drawn back; Wilding rightly calls it imaginative and unsupported.) Whatever the truth, the letter slipped to the dead man's name is the truest thing in it: Gordon's death had shaken something loose in Kendall that would not be set right.

**The elegy (2 July 1870).** What he could not do at the graveside he did on the page. His 'In Memoriam' for Gordon, published within days, is one of his finest poems and the public crowning of his friend — "A shining soul with syllables of fire, / Who sang the first great songs these lands can claim / To be their own." But its deepest charge is personal. Kendall had laid elegiac leaves on Lionel Michael's grave and on Harpur's; now Gordon too was gone, and he counted himself "the sad disciple of a shining band / Now gone... I, left alone." He closed on a Tennysonian image of himself as the last man on the mere, watching Arthur pass and straining "in vain to hear the going voice." Three mentors and friends dead in two years; the poet of the dawn already writing as a survivor among graves.

**Leaving Melbourne (Aug–Sept 1870).** Two weeks after Gordon's death Kendall resigned from the Yorick; his Melbourne period was ending, on a high note and a sour one. The high: he was "unanimously selected" to write 'Euterpe,' the ode for the opening of the new Town Hall (set by Charles Horsley) — a commission for which "Gordon, and other aspirants, failed" — performed 9 August before Mayor Amess's four thousand guests, with *The Argus* saluting the poet who had "already made his mark in Australian poetical literature." The sour: within weeks he was in another public dispute, printing a "second version" of the ode with a note implying Horsley had rejected it — which Horsley furiously and effectively rebutted, producing Kendall's own letter ("the idea was yours, and I followed it... in a docile spirit") and leaving the editor to side against him. *Touchstone* caricatured the benefit-concert wrangle with Kendall as a begging dog. It is the by-now-familiar pattern — Kendall caught out by his own words, publicly humbled — and a measure of how far his judgment and credit had frayed. He wrote to Smith for copies of his own Gordon elegy and ode: "I do not possess MSS of them, and I should hardly like to lose them."

**The collapse (October–December 1870).** Kendall and Charlotte sailed back to Sydney on the *Macedon* on 24 October, and he cut Melbourne off entirely — no more contributions, as if to erase the time. Sydney was worse. Within weeks he was arrested for uttering a forged £1 cheque to a chemist he knew; he had twopence-farthing in his pocket, told three different stories about where the cheque came from, begged the detective to destroy it, and then gave way: "I see it is no good, I am guilty." The trial laid his ruin open to the whole colony. His own family testified to what he had become — he wandered the house all night, wept "like a child," contradicted himself from one sentence to the next, did not recognise his brother-in-law, said "he would sooner be out of the world." Doctors found him shattered by opium and spirits — "his intense thirst for opium had destroyed his nervous system" — and "dangerous to permit at large." Witnesses described the delusions: a boast of a forty-line poem in Æolic Greek (impossible, and he knew his hearer knew it); undressing in company because "a cloud came suddenly between him and the light." He had tried to kill himself in the gaol. Most haunting of all, a witness reported that Kendall had spoken "with great levity of the melancholy suicide of a Mr A. L. Gordon, and said with a laugh, that 'Gordon had tricked them cleverly'" — the friend he had crowned in elegy five months before now surfacing in his unravelling mind as the one who had got out. The jury found him not guilty by reason of insanity, and he was committed to the Darlinghurst receiving house. The press he had lived by reported it all across Australia — the exposure a punishment of its own. His defender, the brilliant W. B. Dalley, whom Kendall had once praised in print, won the acquittal and later wrote of him with great tenderness: his suffering "not a vain parade of affliction, but a humble confession."

The ruin went deeper still. On Christmas Eve 1870, while he was held at Darlinghurst, a son — Frederick Clarence — was born; and Charlotte, just twenty, took the child and left him, moving back to her mother's. The public, at least, was kind: the press spoke of "compassion," noting the weird lustre of his eyes in his "pallid and emaciated" face, and his friends stood "true as steel"; in Parliament a member demanded the colony stop "punishing insanity as a crime" and move him from gaol to care. (The forgery itself had been delusion, not greed — he had come to believe he was Holt's private secretary, "with unlimited authority to draw" on him.) But the marriage was effectively over. Years later he set down the worst of it: "When I have crawled, ill and weak, to her mother's home in order to have a glimpse of her and her child, she has stood and invited her brothers to turn me off the premises."

**The asylum, and the first turn (July 1871).** From the Darlinghurst receiving house Kendall was formally committed — "consigned to a Government Lunatic Asylum" — and admitted to Gladesville on 5 July 1871: melancholia, "trouble of mind," intemperate habits and heavy opium use, "violent and threatened suicide." But the bottom, once reached, was where the slow climb began. The hospital records show him almost at once "free from all delusion, quite rational in conversation," anxious to get back to his writing "for the sake of his wife's support" — Charlotte had left him, yet he meant to keep her — and within a fortnight he was contributing to *The Empire* and *Punch*, his work bearing "all the marks of mental equilibrium." He was discharged on 29 July, after three weeks. The nadir was passed; the long, partial recovery had begun.

**The critic, and the lost years (1872–73).** For a while he wrote well and lucidly. In *The Freeman's Journal* he produced essays on Irish writers ('The Harp of Erin') and on his own contemporaries — judging Gordon, now, more coolly than in the elegy: great "in naked strength, in dash," but "not a poet in the sense that Harpur was," lacking "the creative faculty"; and ranking Clarke, as a novelist, "head and shoulders over all who have attempted storytelling... in these colonies," *His Natural Life* his best work. In 'Men of Letters in New South Wales' he set down the whole tragedy of the colonial writer — the book's own thesis in his words: the young come "full of enthusiasm... but the end invariably is disappointment and sorrow"; the lucky join the press and "become plodding, satisfied newspaper hacks"; and of the rest — "The men who are not so fortunate – God help them!" He was writing it as one of the unfortunate. The lucidity did not hold. Between April 1872 and November 1873 his life goes nearly unrecorded — "Written in the Shadow of 1872," he later marked the poems of those years; "I recollect very little of my subsequent Sydney life for my mind was unhinged nearly all the time." He sank below even Gladesville: Henry Parkes found him one Saturday "tottering," "in a state so wretched that I hardly knew him," having "been lying out the previous night in the bush," and believed "he would have perished" but for being taken in, boarded, and reclothed "from head to foot." The recovery had relapsed, and lower.

**Gladesville again (April–July 1873).** The relapse ran its course in the open and then in the asylum. Brought before the court again in April 1873 — "his mind was impaired" — Kendall was readmitted to Gladesville, this time for two months. The records are stark: "mania," cause "intemperance"; since his discharge he had "plunged deeper and deeper into debauchery"; his wife "left him some months ago... owing to his ill-treatment of her and his constant drinking"; he had "lately been in a starving condition." One delusion is unbearable in the light of Araluen: "He imagined that he was accused of murdering a child." They cut his hair "owing to its dirty condition," fed him, dosed him with chloral; he picked up flesh, brightened, was "quite well" by the end of June, discharged 7 July. Up again — until the next time.

**The Fagans, and Gosford (1873–75).** The next time was the turn that held. Friends found him a newspaper post at Grafton; the steamer called at Newcastle on the way, Kendall went ashore — and did not reboard. He drank through his money, then set out to walk back to Sydney, and at Gosford, late in 1873, was taken in by the Fagans — Peter Fagan an Irish ex-convict turned timber merchant, and his grown sons. They nursed him back, gave him work as their storekeeper and accountant, and "endured so patiently my peculiarities"; Kendall's gratitude was unbounded — "these gentlemen... can never be compensated – never be forgotten." In the glen above the farm, by a rock-pool he loved, the poetry came back: 'Mooni,' 'Narrara,' 'Names upon a Stone,' some of his finest and most settled work, written early and late while he helped on the farm by day, known to all as "Harry." On Christmas Day 1874 he and the Fagan boys chipped their initials on a stone by the pool — the "Names upon a Stone" of the poem. After the oscillation of ruin and repair, this was real recovery: peace, work, family, and verse, found at last in the care of an emancipist's household.

**Renouncing Bohemia (1874–75).** Recovery hardened into renunciation. To the Fagans he was a sober bushman — a good swimmer, a cattle-yarder, wandering the gullies "book in hand," writing his poems left-handed on scraps of paper "with scarcely an alteration." He hid his whereabouts from Charlotte and his family, and turned violently against his old life: "I haven't a scrap left of my Bohemian writings"; "I would rather turn bullock driver than go back to the ragged old Bohemian life"; "I have taken nothing stronger than tea for the last eleven months." He renounced ambition itself, in a despair that swept up the whole company of Australian poets: "Why should I bother and work out my brains for a shadow? Did Harpur acquire a reputation by his writings? Did Michael and Gordon, with all their belief in themselves? If they failed what right have I to expect success?... Give me the bovine life and let the Gods go hang!" (Given a Bible by Mrs Stenhouse, he annotated it as "about the most disappointing book I have ever read," the prophets and Gospels excepted.) The poet of the dawn, recovered, wanted only to stop being a poet — and yet kept scribbling anonymous squibs for the *Town and Country Journal*.

**The useful citizen (Gosford, 1875).** His new life put down roots. He wrote the district into verse and prose — 'Arcadia at our Gates' on the "darkly magnificent" Mooni valley (not neglecting, as Clarke had not, the slaughter of its Aboriginal people), and the poems 'Mooni,' 'Names upon a Stone' (inscribed to G. L. Fagan), and 'Narrara Creek.' He made himself useful: framing the petitions that won Gosford a post office, deeper water, and its roads, and leaving with a watch the grateful inhabitants gave him — "I must have been behaving myself properly," he noted wryly. He stayed sober ("nothing stronger than water") and still hid from his kin ("I don't want to have anything to do with either the Rutter or the Kendall mob"). To Moore he gave the truest image of his new relation to his art: "Literature has been so long my crutch that I don't think I shall ever use it as a walking stick."

**Camden Haven, and Charlotte (1876–81).** When the Gosford cedar gave out, the Fagans set up a new timber business at Camden Haven, two hundred miles up the coast, and made Kendall their storekeeper and paymaster. There, at last, the family was whole again: a house was built for him, and in July 1876 Charlotte came up from Sydney with the children. For the first time in his life he was out of debt — thanks to her careful husbanding of his "slender resources." He kept the store by day and wrote at night, his shelf down to Shelley, Tennyson, Swinburne, Gordon, Harpur; the household gathered on the wide verandah for the weekly mail. The peace was real but not unbroken: even in these "happiest years," his son Frederick remembered, "the black moods of retrospection would envelop him," his unhappy early life weighing on him, and at such times he sought "the delusive consolation of alcohol" — not a drinker by volume, but "physically unfitted to drink at all." Charlotte's own portrait is the tenderest thing in the record: the thin man who felt the cold, who gathered his ideas smoking in the winter sun, who loved the bell-birds and the green mossy spot by the river ("Lottie, is your garden always bright and green like that?"), who had her kiss every poem before he sent it out for luck, and told her "my presence made him feel so pure." The town was renamed Kendall in 1891.

**Voice samples (Camden Haven):**
- Charlotte: "He never sent away a poem without asking me to kiss that paper and it would be a success... every time I came near him he caught a new idea and that my presence made him feel so pure."
- Frederick: "Even in his final and happiest years, the black moods of retrospection would envelop him... At such times he would seek the delusive consolation of alcohol."

**The reluctant hack, and the inspired bushman (1877–80).** He kept writing — squibs and prose for the *Freeman's Journal* and *Town and Country* — but disowned it: in 'Old Manuscripts' (1877) he wrote as "one who has left the fields of literature forever," a man whose "disappointments and sufferings in the domain of letters were very intense," who now knew the day-book and ledger of trade — "prior to the days of 'penny-a-lining,' I too was 'in Arcadia.'" His *Freeman* squibs, he told Moore, were "not written by one with a passion for scribbling but rather by a poor devil who needs coin." He used pseudonyms to keep his past buried — "My past will not bear analysis and I always dread its being raked up" — a dread sharpened when his mother Melinda, seventy, was jailed two months as "an idle and disorderly person." And the drink still broke through: Holdsworth found him a "practical prisoner" in the Fagans' care, then eluded his minder for three pubs; a "log" Fagan rode out to clear from the road turned out to be Kendall, drunk on roadside rum; such "spasms," Frederick wrote, ended in "sleep and an ashamed awakening." Yet against all this stands the truest tribute he ever drew: Samuel Bennett called him an "inspired blackfellow" (in the idiom of the day) — a white poet who heard the bush "in its own language," who "has his name for every blade of grass, frond of fern and bank of moss." Where Clarke wrote the desolation from distaste, Kendall had it woven into his nature.

**Crowned at last (1879).** Then, improbably, came public triumph. Friends coaxed him back — tributes to Fairfax, to Dalley, to Bennett ("By the Cliffs of the Sea") — and in 1879 the Sydney International Exhibition made him its laureate in all but name. His cantata opened the ceremony, a chorus of 250 girls singing his lines before the assembled Governors and calling for an encore; and when the *Herald* offered a hundred guineas for an exhibition poem, open "to all the world," 250 entries deep, Kendall entered and won. The papers crowned him "the premier bard of the southern hemisphere"; the four colonial Governors signed a compliment to him; a correspondent asked, "England has its Poet Laureate, why not Australia? We have the man in Henry Kendall." The wreck the Sydney cells had known was, in his own lifetime, the acknowledged first poet of the country. That same year he loosed the savage 'Gagging Bill' on his old patron Parkes — "fell from Pericles to – Henry Parkes!" — written in four hours, biting the hand that had once pulled him from the gutter. And yet, even crowned, he held himself apart: to McCrae (he and Clarke, the two survivors of the braid, never once corresponded) he wrote simply, "I am out of the running altogether... I have not even a chance for reading." The recognition came while he lived to see it — and he received it like a man already gone elsewhere.

**The other Kendall (the late 1870s).** Frank Myers, who knew him then, insisted on a Kendall the biographers missed — not the apology-needing wreck but "another Kendall... very much disposed to take the world fighting... courageous and strong." The face was "the hardest-lined I ever saw," like Lincoln's or "the chiselled granite of Carlyle," yet "all the hell he had endured left no bale-fire in his eyes." From Camden Haven he had sent Myers a scorching letter warning him off the literary life: "Genius is worse used in the cities of Australia than ever was pearl by swine... the man in whom it lurks, as a disease, is just broken up like oyster-shell and pitched on to the middens. Keep away!" His wisdom was hard and prophetic — "An angel only comes to you to impose a task... he will swing a knotted scourge later if you do not take that obligation up" — and he turned the same iron on his own crowning: "I wrote a cantata for an exhibition, and half fulfilled some obligations in the way of boots and store bills... You may almost hope to be allowed to lick the fleshpots with scullions permanently if – you succeed!" And in that letter he named Clarke with open contempt: Clarke had told the same truth "in even plainer terms, and yet is himself crook-backed and broken-kneed with bowing to Rimmon" — compromised, debt-ridden, the bailiffs forever at his door. Kendall, Myers noted flatly, "did not like Clarke; could never even discover excellence in that good bit of genuine Australian prose, the introduction to Gordon's poems."

**Voice samples ('The Other Kendall'):**
- "Genius is worse used in the cities of Australia than ever was pearl by swine... If you have got any genius, or if any genius has got you, keep away!"
- "An angel only comes to you to impose a task... Woe be to you if fulfilment fails!"

**"Wild bleak Bohemia" (1880).** It was to N. Walter Swan — Clarke's Wimmera friend, who had just won the *Sydney Mail*'s hundred-pound novel prize — that Kendall gave the phrase that names this whole world. Swan's mention of the Yorick "called up many memories of the darkest period of my life," he wrote in January 1880. "I left Melbourne with the shadow of insanity on me... In that wild bleak Bohemia south of the Murray, I went through Gethsemane and I am only the grey shadow of the young man who commenced to write with so much enthusiasm in 1861." The letters are his deepest reckoning with what he had become: a man at one desk "from six a.m. to nine p.m.," who "cannot say that I have ten minutes leisure," who does his little verse on Sundays "fagged with the week's labour" — "I do not live in the world of letters – I am merely a lingerer beside it"; "all my verses are adumbrations. I have never had the time to write a long poem." And his coolness toward Clarke surfaces even here, in a literary judgement: *His Natural Life*, he told Swan, was a work "showing great industry and more than ordinary power; but it is not for the same shelf as Luke Miver" — ranking his friend's forgotten novel above Clarke's masterpiece. The postscript is the plainest word on the braid's broken centre: "I have not written to Clarke for years."

**Voice samples (to Swan, 1880):**
- "In that wild bleak Bohemia south of the Murray, I went through Gethsemane and I am only the grey shadow of the young man who commenced to write with so much enthusiasm in 1861."
- "I do not live in the world of letters – I am merely a lingerer beside it."

**Songs from the Mountains, and a hand held out (1880).** Encouraged, Kendall set about his first book since Melbourne — *Songs from the Mountains*, thirty-five poems, dedicated "to a mountain," paid for from his own pocket and chosen for sale: "the verses that eclectics like you will sneer at are the ones that Tom, Jack and Harry admire." ("I was born in the forests and the mountains were my sponsors.") He sought security, too: swallowing his savage 'Gagging Bill,' he made his peace with Parkes and wrote a moving plea for "a position like that of forest ranger" — "I have lived a comparatively blameless life... completed a fair term of penance"; "I will not last for ever. Time is writing sad things on my hair." Parkes, magnanimous, began to help. And — most movingly — Kendall's old hostility to Clarke fell away. He asked after the Gordon preface without rancour, and in October sent Michael Fagan to Melbourne with a note of introduction to Clarke himself: "I want you to know the bearer. He is the man who led me out of Gethsemane and set me in the sunshine." But Fagan could not see him — "Clarke was ill." Kendall wondered: "What is up with Marcus Clarke?... Perhaps he is in trouble." He was; Kendall had no idea how seriously. The two survivors of the braid reached toward each other at last, and missed.

**The uncrowned laureate, and the first illness (1880–81).** *Songs from the Mountains* came out, and very nearly sank itself: it carried a political satire, 'The Song of Ninian Melville,' so libellous that the publisher Maddock — who had never read the proofs — frantically recalled the copies already delivered when Melville threatened to sue. Kendall swapped in a new poem, the row only fed demand, and the book made its money clear. The reviews were the best of his life: Dalley praised its "genuine pathos" in the *Herald*, and *The Bulletin* gave him a three-column feature — the kind reserved for politicians — calling him "without qualification or envy... the uncrowned laureate of Australia." The Melbourne notice added the prophecy and the shadow together: he "will certainly write [a great poem] if he live long enough." Parkes, meanwhile, made good — Kendall was appointed Inspector of Forests, the country post he had begged for, and moved his family to Cundletown to wait for it, a "wonder-town" to his children after the forest clearing. Security at last, for their sake. But two things darkened the triumph. His own closing poem, 'After Many Years,' mourned the masterpiece he knew he would never write — "The perfect verses... Remain unwritten yet." And he was ill: "My illness is the result of six years of overwork," he wrote in February 1881. "At present it is out of my power to write." He inscribed a copy of the book to Peter Fagan, "one of three noble brothers who led me out of Gethsemane." The recognition was complete, the security won — and the body had begun, quietly, to fail. (Swan's photograph, he noted, now sat in the family album "between Marcus Clarke and Brunton Stephens" — the two survivors together on a page, if nowhere else.)

**Inspector of Forests (1881).** Parkes's patronage came good: in May 1881 Kendall was gazetted Inspector (or Superintendent) of State Forests, a newly created post at £500 a year — the country living he had asked for, security for the six children he now had (his son Orara was born that June, matching Clarke's six). Like Clarke eleven years before, he had to give up political and satirical journalism as the price of a government billet. The work was hard and conscientious — long rides "in all weathers" to report on the forest reserves, and reports of real ecological insight: "the destruction of forests means the destruction of arable country... the disastrous consequences must of course seriously affect society." He kept faith with the braid even on horseback: writing to the *Bulletin*, he apologised that "poor Gordon's matter has been out of my power... it will, however, be a labour of deep love to attend to it." But the riding broke him further. Through that winter in the Richmond River country he sickened; "his health was deteriorating." The security had come — and the body would not hold long enough to enjoy it.

**Mourning Clarke, and his own closing (1881–82).** Clarke's death broke something open in Kendall. The man who had disliked him, dismissed his masterpiece, refused for years to write to him, now mourned him without reservation. "The death of poor Clarke was a great blow to me," he told McCrae. "Only four months ago, I received a genial letter from him... Clarke, like the rest of us, had his failings; but at the bottom, he was all gold. I intend to do my best here for the unhappy widow." In the *Sydney Mail* he set Clarke among his dead — "Harpur lies over there in Eurobodalla... Gordon sleeps by the sea; and... all that is left of the young man, with the kind face and good heart, whom we used to call Marcus" — and in the *Bulletin* he printed a verse 'In Memoriam – Marcus Clarke': "I see the eyes that once I knew – / The eyes of Marcus Clarke... The sods have hid the faded face / Of my heroic friend." Its hardest stanza named the doom they had all shared: "The laurels in the pit were won; / He had to take the lot austere / That ever seems to wait upon / The man of letters here." The antagonistic survivor had become the mourner — the braid closing over one of its own, in grief, where it never quite closed in life. And Kendall was failing himself. "My literary career has closed," he wrote; "I am rarely well now"; "I am very tired"; and at last, in his final letter to McCrae, "I can hardly move the pen" — the same failing hand as Clarke's. His son Orara died in January 1882. Asked that March to edit Harpur's poems, he declined for ill health, but saw clearly where he was bound: "It is not at all improbable that before the year be out, I shall see Charles." He was right.

**The end (1 August 1882).** He was right to the month. The Forests post that was meant to save him killed him: the endless riding "in all weathers," through wet clothes and winter chills, broke a constitution already undermined by the tuberculosis that had taken his father, his brother, and a sister. He collapsed at Wagga in June 1882, was carried to Sydney by George Fagan — a Fagan beside him at the end, as at the rescue — and entered St Vincent's, where Charlotte tended him and shared his ward for five weeks before he was moved to the Fagans' house in Redfern for the last. He died on 1 August 1882, a single day short of a year after Clarke, aged forty-three. And his dying thought was Clarke's exactly. Clinging to life, he told Charlotte: "Lottie, I shall live to the first of the month so you will get the salary" — and he did, holding on until the date that would secure his family one more month's pay. "To the last," she wrote, "his only thought was that he had not left me and my little ones unprovided for." With his right hand resting on his face, he passed away. He had asked, in his last half-delirium, for "a drink from the sea." Charlotte's verdict was unsparing: "if he had resigned his position a few months ago he would, I am sure, be alive now." The security came, and then it killed him.

**Voice samples (the end):**
- "Lottie, I shall live to the first of the month so you will get the salary." (holding on for the family's pay)
- "Give me a drink from the sea." (in his last delirium — and he was buried by it)

**Afterward.** They buried him at Waverley, in "the most sheltered nook of that swelling upland which looks out upon the Pacific" — the sea he had asked for. But only twenty mourners came. The man the *Bulletin* had crowned "the uncrowned laureate of Australia" eighteen months before was followed to the grave by a handful: "had the dead poet been but a cricketer," Holdsworth said bitterly, "his funeral might have been a colossal one; but as he was merely a poet... he had but a scanty following." A chance passer-by, a stranger, stepped forward and showered the coffin with wildflowers. The obituaries did what obituaries do — the *Herald* set him beside Gordon as the purest Australian spirit; the *Argus*, grudging, allowed "a very mellifluous versifier"; the *Courier* found him "no man's enemy but his own." And then, slowly, the enshrinement: Charlotte and Sutherland brought out the *Poems*; Palgrave admitted 'Orara' and 'After Many Years' to the *Golden Treasury*, Quiller-Couch put 'Mooni' in the *Oxford Book of English Verse*. The neglected grave was rescued by an outsider — Louisa Lawson, newly arrived in Sydney, found it weed-grown under a sagging wreath and was "aflame with indignation," and campaigned the monument into being (then was forgotten by the committee at its unveiling). She had named her own son Henry after Kendall, "envisaging a literary destiny for him" — and Henry Lawson, the next and greater national writer, would in time be buried in the plot beside him. The founder lay neglected, then honoured, then joined by his successor, by the sea. Maggie Gordon lived to 1919, Marian Clarke to 1914, Charlotte Kendall to 1924 — the three women outlasting their poets by forty years and more.

**Michael's drowning (Apr 1868).** Seven weeks after the wedding, on **28 April 1868**, Kendall's Grafton mentor **James Lionel Michael** was found drowned in the Clarence, aged 44 — a death the inquest left open between accident, murder and suicide (a jagged, unexplained wound to the right eye). Kendall had no doubt it was suicide: a farewell letter left in the office, "syllables of despair" in verses found on the body — "I do not know where I have seen a more touching farewell to men and time." He wrote a tribute for Sheridan Moore's memorial *Life and Genius of James Lionel Michael*. Another poet gone into the water — a pattern (with Deniehy's drink-death) gathering around him.

## 4. Scene Candidates
_Documented co-presence, dialogue/action, contradictions worth dramatising._

- **Clarke vanishes on arrival (Jun 1863).** 17-year-old new arrival with £300 disappears off the *Wellesley*; Judge uncle wires the Chief Commissioner of Police; police trace him to the Criterion Hotel only to find he's blithely gone out to dine. Comic, characterful, and it puts Clarke in Standish's orbit from day one — documented in telegram/memo text.
- **The bank — Clarke as correspondence clerk, and his exit (1864). [KEY SCENE]** A genuine comic set-piece, documented from several angles:
  - *The job.* Placed via David Macarthur. Turner's portrait: Clarke loathes bookkeeping and makes ludicrous errors, but is the life of the office — burlesque ballads, lampoons of the officials, writing off Aeschylus in the original or turning a stray joke into Horatian verse; the other clerks would sooner cover his work than "see Pegasus in harness."
  - *The correspondence-clerk turn (SA Register).* He answers letters in five minutes flat, serene and happy, and leaves on the stroke of four — until it emerges he's been replying in his own style with scant regard for accuracy or business etiquette. The bank is threatened with lawsuits; the pompous manager, "red as a turkeycock" and near apoplexy, summons him to his private room daily.
  - *The exit — three rival versions to choose from.* (a) *SA Register:* the manager at last invites his resignation; "Resignation. Certainly. Just my opinion. Give me a piece of paper" — Clarke scribbles it, hands it in, takes up his hat, dusts it, and strolls out humming an operatic tune while the manager sits speechless. (b) *Mackinnon's dialogue:* framed as a leave-of-absence request, granted "Indefinitely, Mr Clarke, if you do not object!" (c) *Clarke's own ('On Business Men'):* the manager "loved me as his own brother," admired his waistcoats, handed over a quarter's salary in advance, but his "genius was evidently fettered in a bank" — out, because "I was not a Business Man."
  The competing accounts are a feature, not a problem: pick or blend. Establishes the charm-and-ruin pattern and his contempt for respectable institutions.
- **The Queensland overlanding disaster (1867).** Six men drive stock north; floods drown the cattle, horses die, fever fells the party, ~£300 each gone. The Hungarian Max Kabat dies and is buried in the bush. Clarke survives three months of it and reaches Adelaide in rags. A bleak near-death set-piece — the bush as ruin rather than romance — and the making of the world-weary 21-year-old who turns up at *The Argus*. Sourced to Clarke's own letter to Cyril Hopkins.
- **Clarke jackarooing at Swinton/Ledcourt (1864–67).** The effete English boy turned overseer: brilliant, hot-tempered, broke. After a row with John Holt he lets loose a swearing-fit that appals the station's veteran bullock-driver — "a queer medley" of classical mythology, Shakespearean archaisms and modern filth. Estranged from Joseph Holt, he walks two miles to Glenorchy most nights to drink, play cards and swap yarns at the Royal Mail (landlord W. B. Pine). Long solitary rides through the Grampians feed the melancholy of "Pretty Dick." Sourced to John Wallace's reminiscences.
- **The Collins Street chambers with Telo (1867–68). [colourful]** Clarke flings his 30 shillings a week into the ménage and stows his trunk in the back attic of Telo's three-room suite over a sewing-machine shop. The room is a death-haunted gallery — Holbein's Death stalking the revellers and tolling the church bell, Martin's "Palace of Satan," a startlingly lifelike mezzotint monk, Balzac's *Contes Drolatiques* and Doré's *Dante* on the table — the very décor of the hashish piece 'Cannabis Indica' (Feb 1868). Telo holds court in his fragmentary, finger-flashing way. A perfect bohemian-bachelor set-piece, and the room where the drug thread begins.
- **The Drummond snake-bite experiment (1 May 1868). [KEY / dialogue set-piece]** The season's most bizarre death — and Clarke is in it. Over coffee at the Café de Paris, the fearless magistrate Drummond resolves to prove the snake-showman Shires a fraud by being bitten himself "for science." Telo recoils; Clarke, asked along, spends the night vainly trying to stop him — to the casino, then to the barber's where Drummond calmly has his hair brushed, laughing off the danger all the while. Sworn to secrecy, Clarke gives up and goes into the theatre. Alone, Drummond goes through with it at Garton's Hotel: the tiger snake drawn from Shires's shirt, the bite six inches above the wrist, the snake's head cut off with Drummond's own pocket-knife to send to Halford, the £1 5s paid, the ten-minute swoon, the soda-gas inhaled. He cabs across town, wakes Professor Halford after midnight with the severed snake and a pencilled note ("Shires applied his antidote. What it is worth I shall leave you to decide"), refuses help, and dies the next evening — pupils blown, pulse failing, coughing blood. A complete, documented, dialogue-rich tragedy of reckless rationalist bravado, with Clarke as the helpless witness. (Shires arrested for murder, a snake still in his bosom at the watch house.)
- **The founding of the Yorick (9 May 1868). [KEY / setting / dialogue]** The club at the heart of the book is born over pipes and pewter in a bare rented room above the *Punch* office. Clarke hammers all night for "Golgotha — the place of skulls," his brown skull grinning on the mantel with a pipe jammed in its teeth; the company, their pewters "not empty," argue it down until Carrington lifts the skull and says "Alas, poor Yorick!" — and the name sticks. They adjourn to a midnight eating-house; next morning Walstab goes round blacking the faces of the sleepers with burnt cork. The origin scene of the novel's central institution — bohemian, funny, death-tinged from the first.
- **The skull's journey: Yorick to Hamlet to a wife (1868). [KEY / connective]** Beaten on the name, a huffy Clarke pockets his skull and disappears for days — then gives it to the actor Walter Montgomery, who lifts it for "Alas, poor Yorick" in his Hamlet at the Theatre Royal. Playing Ophelia beside him: the young Marian Dunn, whom Clarke will marry fifteen months on. A single prop ties the club's christening, the play it was named for, and Clarke's marriage — a gift to a novelist.
- **The suggestion-book wars (1868). [colourful]** Clarke as the club's gadfly secretary, filling the suggestion book with mock-outrage at the "Abomination of Desolation" eating-room and the worst dinner of "a brief but chequered career," pleading for "a clean cloth; some casual hint of tenderness in the steak, and a suspicion of mustard in the mustard pot." The "boy nuisance" in his element — light relief that also shows his bottomless appetite for an audience.
- **The Clarke–Montgomery newspaper war (Apr–May 1869). [colourful / KEY — Clarke the duellist]** A six-week public brawl in the press: the vain actor accusing Melbourne's critics of being bought; Clarke, once his friend, dismantling him letter by letter — the overdraft, the scarf-ring, the rejected Jesuit play all paraded — until he brands Montgomery "unfit for the society of gentlemen" and reaches for his solicitor. The set-piece of Clarke as forensic, merciless polemicist, honour and wit weaponised — and a window on the touchy subject of his money and his playwriting ambitions.
- **"Going to give birth to a possum" (Nissen's café). [colourful]** Montgomery, absurdly proud of the bearskin coat the Duke gave him, dozes in it on a hot night "like a she-bear"; Stiffe shouts "Wake up, Montie!" and Aspinall stage-whispers, "S'sh!... It looks as if Montie might be going to give birth to a possum at any minute." The Yorick wits at their cruel, funny best — the clubland sport that helped make Montgomery an enemy. (Clarke's own contribution: making "a noise like a collection plate" while Montgomery recited badly — never forgiven.)
- **Clarke's near-fatal fall (June 1868). [KEY / contradiction]** Days after christening the Yorick, Clarke is carried off senseless with a fractured skull — kicked in the head by his own falling horse. Three weeks unconscious; Telo nursing him "like a woman," translating Russian novels aloud at the bedside; the doctors "literally" bringing him back to life. He surfaces with a permanent dent in his skull and a memory that will fail him without warning ever after. (How it happened is itself contested — a reckless dare at a hunt, or a schooling accident in Goyder's yard.) A near-exact mirror of Gordon's fall three months before: two of the three poets nearly killed, and lastingly marked, by a horse.
- **The Spring Meeting race report and the outcry (Nov 1867). [contradiction]** Clarke's debut *Argus* splash covers the crowd, not the horses — and lands ugly anti-Semitic mockery of Jewish racegoers. An "indignant howl" from Melbourne's Jewish residents; the paper passes it off as an editorial oversight and censures but keeps him ("invaluable as a writer"). The tension to dramatise: the slurring journalist shares his rooms with, and adores, the Jewish Telo.
- **The Lalla Rookh steeplechase (Worcester, May 1852). [KEY / contradiction]** The escapade that sent Gordon to Australia — and the records are gloriously irreconcilable. One version (Mrs Lauder, from Gordon himself): forbidden by the owner to run the favourite, he took the horse anyway, rode, won, and dismounted into the arms of the owner and a waiting policeman. Another (the "accepted account" via Sladen): he'd paid £5 to ride the mare, but a creditor seized her for a £30 debt and locked the stable; Gordon broke in — and, per cousin Frances, thrashed the ostler — to ride and win. The horse is *Lalla Rookh* in legend but "Louisa" in the 1852 Steeplechase Calendar; *Berrow's Worcester Journal* (12 May 1852) reports the meeting but notes the horses ran under fictitious names and riders under assumed names, so "winners or losers" can't be stated. Disgrace or adventure? Frances Gordon insists he was never in disgrace — his father was "rather proud" of it, only sore at the cost. A perfect documentary knot: pick a version, or stage the ambiguity itself.
- **The breakfast row over Jane Bridges. [dialogue]** Gordon and his father ("the Governor") at breakfast; the father needles him about "the farmer's daughter your uncle says you've been hanging after," Gordon fires back about his sister's poor match, declares the girl "wouldn't have me, tho' I am the Honble. Capt. Gordon's son," and walks out slamming the door — whereupon the father seizes on an unpaid bill to renew the quarrel. Crisp documented dialogue; the domestic friction behind the exile.
- **The Jane Bridges farewell (Worcester, Aug 1853). [KEY romance]** Gordon calls on Jane the morning he leaves; he won't come forward or speak, then says abruptly, "I came to say good-bye." When she says they're all sorry to lose him, he answers, "If you will say one word, I won't go" — the word is "Stay." He pours out that he'll work, be whatever his father wants; he never meant to tell her he loved her, "but now he could not help it." She has to stop him: she loves another. He takes it as a sentence — "Now I know what you have told me, it is all over with me; I may as well be in Australia as anywhere else" — gives her the silver pen-holder he's been turning in his hands, asks her to write, covers her hands with kisses ("the first and last he ever gave me"), and is gone. The whole romance, she says, "began and ended in a few brief moments." A complete, tender, devastating scene with its own dialogue — and it flatly contradicts the gold-fever legend: he left lovesick and depressed, not adventurous.
- **"Mysterious Gordon" and the locked box (1853–54). [colourful]** The silent, dark new trooper nobody can place; the barracks-men's long speculation about his locked wooden box; the day it's found open, half-full of racing paraphernalia; the unmasking of a brilliant secret horseman who exercises the officers' mounts by racing them cross-country and brings them back suspiciously "heated" — "he had been a bit fretful." Blackmore's elegiac coda: "It was all good honest timber in those days. The curse of wire had not fallen upon the land." Atmospheric, and it crystallises Gordon's reserve and his one true joy.
- **Trainor's circus arrest — meeting Gordon (Penola, mid-1850s). [colourful]** Trainor, the circus's star rider, is playing the drunk in the "changeable act" when he's bundled out of the ring and seized by the neck by the sergeant; marched off to be locked up, he protests he's a performer, unbuttons his coat to flash the spangles and riding costume — and the tall trooper throws his head back and roars with laughter before walking him back to the tent and letting him go. "That was my introduction to Gordon." A moonlit, funny first meeting between two men "as opposite as the two poles" who became inseparable.
- **The police resignation (Penola, 1855). [contradiction]** Why Gordon quit the Mounted Police survives in four incompatible versions: (a) *George C. Scott* — chided for rusty stirrup-irons, Gordon snaps "I didn't join as an officers' groom," the inspector says "if you don't like what you have to do you had better resign," and he does; (b) *Trainor* — ordered to escort "a woman of disreputable character" sixty miles in custody, he resigns rather than do it; (c) *Mullaley* (aged 105 in 1924) — ordered to lock up a tipsy local woman, Gordon takes her home instead, is reprimanded, and quits to break horses; (d) *the official record* — inspector George B. Scott reports he simply left, thinking droving "more lucrative," having "conducted himself remarkably well" over ~18 months. The chivalric refusals flatter the legend; the rusty-stirrups pride is the sharper character beat; the letter is the dull truth. Pick what serves.
- **"Mad Harry," the tart-carrying poet (Sydney, c.1857–59). [colourful]** The eighteen-year-old just back from sea, errand-boy to a confectioner, balancing a tray of cakes on his head through the Sydney streets, lips moving over half-made lines, eyes vacant — passers-by turning to point and laugh, "There goes Mad Harry!" A perfect image of the poet-in-embryo amid the city's indifference; recorded via Louisa Lawson's Kendalliana hunt.
- **The South Sea Islands voyage (1855–57). [colourful]** Sixteen-year-old Kendall, eighteen months at sea as an apprentice in the island trade — whaling, "a fight with a devil fish," the wonder of the Pacific that stayed "mirrored in my memory." The romance is half-myth (Kendall muddled the ships' names himself; Clarke later gilded it with Melville), which is its own usable tension: the formative adventure that poet and admirers alike mythologised.
- **The Grafton lecture fiasco (Dec 1862). [KEY colourful]** Billed to lecture on Australian literature, Kendall walks onto the platform, glares at the audience, gasps — and flees. The chairman saves the evening with an impromptu talk on beetles, "An Hour with a Microscope." Per Victor Daley, this stage-fright rout is "practically all that the eldest generation of Grafton remembers" of "the sweetest of Australian singers." A painful, funny vignette — the lyric gift married to crippling shyness (the irony being he lectured fluently elsewhere, on Shelley and on love).
- **Basil's impersonation (1864). [colourful / double]** Kendall's identical twin Basil, trading on their likeness, spends a month impersonating Henry and borrowing money from Henry's friends — until Henry must publish a CAUTION in the *Herald* to stop the frauds. The literal double turning the poet's own name into a con, and the family's hereditary fraud surfacing once more — after the forger father, before Henry's own forged cheque.
- **The Rose parasol parting (Redfern, Mar 1867). [colourful]** Kendall sees Rose off at the station; she drops her gay parasol from the carriage window — "Pick that up, Harry!" — and he, nettled, will only do it if she says "please." She won't; he won't; the train moves off; he runs after it with the parasol, but the huff has hardened on both sides, and that is the end of them. A small, perfect, maddening scene — two "petulant persons" losing a love to pride. (Recovered by Louisa Lawson; the seed of 'Rose Lorraine'.)
- **Gordon's twin anonymous books (Jun 1867). [colourful curiosity]** Within nine days Gordon issues two volumes from two Melbourne publishers, each credited only to "the Author of" the other — *Ashtaroth* and *Sea Spray and Smoke Drift* — self-funded at £50 apiece, his name nowhere. The gentleman's anonymity (cf. Rolf Boldrewood) plus a bibliographic oddity, and the moment the bushman becomes a published poet — on borrowed money, as ever.
- **Bright meets Clarke at the Café de Paris (~1868). [KEY]** The actor Walter Montgomery sweeps the young journalist up to Bright — "Charley, my boy; you ought to know Marcus" — and Clarke, a dainty dandy of twenty-one with almost feminine beauty and a Charles-Lamb stammer, is found sipping absinthe purely "to find out" whether it really drives a man mad in a month, having already tried opium and "rather liked it." The pose is cynical, blasé, theatrically reckless — yet skin-deep. The perfect establishing scene for Clarke: the drug-curious bohemian whose affectation hides real warmth, a giant only at the desk.
- **The Free Banquet fiasco (Melbourne, Nov 1867). [colourful]** To fete the Duke of Edinburgh the city barbecues a whole bullock and twelve sheep, lays on 900 gallons of wine and 3,452 buns — and it collapses into a riot when the baked, long-waiting crowd rushes the tables. Standish, filthy and dust-blind, intercepts the Prince before he can step onto the ground (certain a crush would kill women and children), then faces the heaving mob from the dais to announce the royal no-show and is nearly thrown down before his "bobbies" haul him out. (Coroner Curtis Candler had it all from Standish that night — plus the anonymous letter warning the Duke that Standish was "a notorious gambler.") Chaos, class, and Standish at full colour.
- **The phantom review (1867–68). [contradiction]** Two versions of how Clarke lost his staff job, the same shape: he files a vivid notice of a show he didn't properly attend — and the performance (or a song) never happened as written, the artist being ill. Exposed by a rival paper, "Clarke found himself suffering from a cold still more severe," and was let go. His imagination "too active to be limited by the stern necessities of facts" — charming and fatal.
- **The hashish experiment (17 Dec 1867). [KEY]** Clarke lies on a sofa wheeled before the fire while his doctor friend doses him with cannabis indica and records the slow onset clinically — pulse climbing, thirst, the fixed brilliant eyes, the stupor — until, surfacing, Clarke announces he can dictate and, "rather reciting from a book than composing," pours out a story while drugged. A strange, dramatic set-piece: the supervised psychological experiment that becomes literature ("the doors of thought").
- **The sun-bath caught short (Carlton). [colourful comic]** Clarke, evangelist of the nude "solar wash" as a nerve cure, talks a sciatic friend into stripping off in the back orchard; the two stroll naked "discussing psychological subjects" until the friend's wife and her sister come laughing up the path. Clarke darts behind his (equally naked) friend as a shield, stammering "Those women are between us and our clothes... Hide me! Sit on me!" and bolts over the back fence. (Told by Clarke to Garnet Walch — possibly tall, gloriously characterful.)
- **The Chinese Quarter at midnight (1868). [colourful]** Guided by Fook Shing — ex-mandarin, opium addict, now detective — Clarke threads Little Bourke Street's gambling house, opium den, reeking eating house and brothel, where a girl quietly asks the detective to tell them "she was not always a Chinaman's woman." Atmospheric exposé with a real humanitarian undertow.
- **The Café de Paris / Saddling Paddock (setting). [KEY setting]** The social engine-room of the novel's Melbourne: upstairs the café where "squatters, lawyers, doctors, loafers... actors and littérateurs all mixed together" over claret and Moselle; downstairs the long bars of the "Saddling Paddock" — barmaids in tights, two hundred men smoking and drinking, drunk young clerks (Banbury of the Customs), the prostitutes, the doorway many a clerk "dated his downfall from." Drawn in full in 'A Day in Melbourne'; use it as the recurring stage for the bohemian round (with the Café Lutetia memoir for its elegiac register).
- **The Mooney's IOU bonfire. [colourful comic]** At Young & Jackson's (then "Mooney's"), where patrons' IOUs sit in a tumbler on the mantel to be settled each Friday, Clarke stands with his back to the fire charming the adoring landlord with a story — and, his pipe forever going out, nonchalantly relights it nine times with IOUs plucked from the tumbler, burning nine men's debts while Mooney, entranced, notices nothing. Come Friday the accounts are mysteriously, hilariously short. (Via "Wattle B.," *Bulletin*.)
- **The Colonial Monthly office (setting / Yorick precursor). [setting]** Clarke's editorial "den" in lower Elizabeth Street, walls pencilled by McCrae with battle-pieces and topical caricatures — Walstab on a rearing charger ("ye gods! What a policeman!"), Shillinglaw "in Liquidation" tipping his gallon measure — the imp messenger "Shrimp" ferrying a great tin of beer back from the Duke of Rothesay across the road at every lunch hour. The first real gathering-place of Clarke's circle, and the working rehearsal for the Yorick Club: useful as the room where the braid's print-convergence becomes a social one.
- **The Tenison Woods midnight talk (Old Lake Hawdon, early 1860s). [KEY]** The brooding bush horse-breaker and the scientist-priest sit up past midnight over their favourite authors, home and schooldays, the antiquity of man. Woods tells how Newman turned the whole tenor of his life; Gordon, who half-wishes he'd gone into the army, cuts through the metaphysics — "Look here Father, what does it matter. Old or young it all comes to eat, drink and be merry for tomorrow you die." Both lighthearted by turns and suddenly grave, "as though their thoughts were far away"; Stockdale, the witness, felt that "in each life the past held a shadow." Two haunted, gifted men in a lamplit hut — quiet and resonant.
- **The Blue Lake plunge (Mount Gambier, early 1860s). [colourful]** Dawn after dawn Gordon takes the steepest, most dangerous descent to the Blue Lake, strips, and plunges into near-freezing blue-black water, staying in till his flesh goes "goosey" and his teeth chatter — maintaining all the while that it's "glorious" — while the prudent Stockdale watches from the safe path. At Robe he swims out until he's lost among the waves. "In everything this man did he was different from other people... a law unto himself."
- **Gordon's memory feats (SA, late 1850s–early 1860s). [colourful]** A wealth of documented instances to choose from: hearing ~200 lines of Hood's 'Miss Kilmansegg' once and reciting them back word-perfect (and again months on); reciting the *King Lear* storm-scene to Woods while the two shiver lost and rainsoaked under a tree, waiting for the moon; pacing a station supper-room till dawn on *Childe Harold*; learning Horace's odes in the saddle. The paradox to play: a freak reciter who was, by his own account, a wretched flat "singing" reader. A vivid way to dramatise his inwardness and his soaked-in literariness amid the stockyards.
- **Gordon's unromantic proposal (Robe, 1862). [KEY]** No courtship to speak of: leaving one morning, Gordon simply tells the industrious hotel girl he's been watching — "Well, girl, I like your ways... I will take a cottage at Robe, and we will get married next week, and you shall keep home for me." She consents; they marry days later; he gives her a gold watch and calls her "girl" forever after. Told flatly by Gordon to Woods ("no romance about my love-making") and corroborated against the false nursing legend by Maggie and Peter Low. Bleakly funny and revealing — the reticent romantic who proposes like a man hiring a housekeeper.
- **The class slights (early 1860s). [colourful]** Sent to the men's hut instead of the homestead parlour because he's "only" a horse-breaker, Gordon recites Burns's 'A man's a man for a' that' and remarks, "I am as well born as any of them, and perhaps better educated, but then they don't know that." Disqualified from a Lady's Purse race as not a "gentleman rider" (it's won by a squatter's son lately a publican), he watches the ladies pointedly strip the prize-bag of its best contents in protest — and comments "very cynically." The gentleman-in-exile chafing against colonial caste.
- **The "lady by the fire" (Mount Gambier, early 1860s). [KEY / colourful]** The vicar's daughter (later Mrs N. A. Lord) recalls Gordon riding his grey mare Modesty to her father's house to talk Tennyson, Browning and Whyte-Melville novels in his "strange, deep, monotonous voice," eyes burning, almost never meeting hers. Charmed, she takes to watching him school Ingleside over stiff timber by the Blue Lake; he rides up between jumps for a few minutes' talk of horses and poetry — until he asks, "Does your mother know you come to see me jump?", learns she'd be horrified, and gently ends it: "It is the first time I have touched a lady's hand for many a day – my own fault... you don't know the world. I do; don't come again." She walks home and "the country looked strangely misty." A tender, melancholy near-romance — and her verdict ("hopelessly sad... a vein of despairing sorrow in almost all his poems") is a key to the whole man.
- **The Feud — ridiculed into ashes (1864). [colourful]** Gordon's first poem, printed anonymously; when Mount Gambier sneers, "The idea of Long Gordon writing poetry!", he recalls and destroys the copies, salvaging only a few (Trainor keeps some). A sharp, sad beat: the secret poet humiliated out of his own work — foreshadowing how much will ride on *Bush Ballads* at the end.
- **Gordon's famous Blue Lake leap (Mount Gambier, 27 Jul 1864). [KEY / contradiction]** Riding above the Blue Lake the day after the Border Handicap, Gordon — lagging behind, dismounting as if to loosen a girth — suddenly puts his horse (Red Lancer) over the roadside fence onto a narrow strip of ground above a sheer ~100-foot drop to the water, rides along the ledge, and jumps back to the road "almost in a stand." Asked why, he shrugs: "when a man wears a green jacket... he is supposed to do something more than somebody else." The accounts famously conflict — Fetherstonhaugh and Trainor tell it vividly (Trainor claiming he saw it; rivals insist he was too far off), N. A. Lord makes it a riposte to Learmonth's taunt "There, that licks you" and disputes the direction (lake→road, not road→lake), Maggie (not present) calls it a "pounding" exhibition, and only Bradshaw Young clearly saw the second jump. Witnessed or not, it became legend (even Ripley's "Believe It or Not," 1969). The definitive expression of Gordon's cold-blooded, death-adjacent daring — stage it as the documentary knot it is.
- **Gordon and Blackmore in the library (Adelaide, 1865). [colourful]** Gordon wanders into the parliamentary library and asks the librarian for a Horace — he has the first line of an ode stuck in his head and can't get further. Blackmore, who knew Book I by heart, finishes it for him; the shared Horace seals a friendship. Gordon then claims kinship (all the Aberdeen Gordons being one family — and "Adam" the modern "Edom" of the old ballad) and, learning Blackmore rides, proposes they walk the seven miles to Glenelg for a sea-swim. A warm, bookish vignette of the poet-politician who preferred the library to the House.
- **Gordon's flying-leap muster (Ballarat Light Horse, 1868). [colourful]** Late for parade again, Gordon comes charging up the street in full uniform on his mare Maud, clears the tall picket-fence at a flying leap, and falls in "sedately," to the delight of the troop and the gathered crowd. The reckless horseman as comic spectacle — and the man playing soldier in the military life he was denied.
- **The Black Johnson knockout (Mount Gambier races). [colourful]** Foot already in the stirrup, colours on, Gordon is insulted by a professional pug; he hands off the horse, peers shortsightedly through the crowd until he finds the man, thrashes him, remounts, and wins the race. The gentleman-amateur's cold, complete competence — pluck, reach, and no fuss.
- **Gordon and Madden: the drubbing and the handshake (1868). [colourful / dialogue]** Nettled at being left out of a printed hunt report, Gordon picks a public newspaper fight with the pseudonymous "Actæon" — and is comprehensively out-written ("the dare-devil horseman pining for a puff in print"). Then the turn that makes the scene: he hunts down the man's name, walks into the office and asks "Is your name Madden?" — not to demand satisfaction but to shake the hand that gave him "as good a drubbing as ever I got in my life." Vanity, wit and sportsmanship in one beat; the start of a friendship that lasts to his death.
- **The presentiment fulfilled (Gordon & Riddoch). [colourful]** Dreading a steeplechase he's been pressed to ride, Gordon tries to anchor himself to George Riddoch's company to keep clear of it — rides ninety miles to Riddoch's station to avoid it — then overrides his host, goes back, rides, and takes one of his worst falls. The foreboding comes true; he half-engineers it. A small, eerie study of a man riding toward the thing he fears.
- **Gordon's letter of despair (6 Oct 1868). [KEY]** Alone in the emptied Ballarat house after seeing Maggie off — "I felt sure I should never see her again" — Gordon writes to Riddoch the most naked statement of his death-drift: drinking since the fall against constant head and back pain, low enough that "if I had a strong sleeping-draught near me, I am afraid I might take it. I have carried one that I should never awake from." Sick of racing, half-wishing himself mad. The clearest pre-1870 evidence of the suicide to come — and, folded into the same letter, the Esslemont inheritance hope and a warm word for Clarke. A whole tragedy in one document.
- **Maggie and Cadger: "Don't give in" (1868). [dialogue]** Beaten and broke, Gordon has thrown in the towel — won't ride Cadger in the selling steeplechase, "no use." Maggie won't let him quit: "you've gone too far to back out now, and no one else can ride the horse... every shilling is of consequence to us now." So he rides, and wins, and Viking takes the hurdle too. The wife's steel against the husband's despair — "She has more pluck in her little finger than ever I had in my whole body."
- **The Watson reconciliation (the hunting field). [KEY / dialogue / braid co-presence]** Gordon and the irascible hunt-master George Watson are bitter enemies — Gordon once knelt on Watson's chest throttling him, Watson nearly clubbed him with a riding-crop. Then Maggie is thrown and knocked senseless in the field; Watson dismounts first, strips off his coat for a pillow, sends a boy for water, and tends her. Gordon, half-dazed from his own fall, staggers up — and **Marcus Clarke, who is there, explains what has happened** and tries to steady him. Gordon kneels: "Maggie! Maggie! Are you hurt?" — "Adam dear, it's scarcely anything." He touches the wet coat: "Whose coat?" — "Mr Watson's, sir." A beat; then the two enemies' hands clasp "with a joyful sound." A near-perfect scene: documented co-presence of Gordon and Clarke, a feud healed through Maggie, dialogue ready-made.
- **The hollow in the bone (McCrae). [colourful / KEY]** Teased about not drinking in a circle that drank hard, Gordon doesn't argue — he takes his friend's hand and presses a finger into the deep dent a fall left in his skull, and McCrae shudders. A wordless, chilling way to carry the head-injury, the pain, and the death-drift in a single gesture.
- **Gordon's three wins in a day (10 Oct 1868). [KEY]** Sick of racing and half-broken, Gordon nonetheless rides the winners of three of five events at the Melbourne Hunt Club meet — Babbler, Viking, Cadger — in his straw-and-black, forcing the refusing Babbler over at the third try and recovering from a near-fall "very cleverly." His legendary feat and the peak of his riding fame, achieved in despair. (Afterwards, faithful old Cadger goes under the hammer for £40.)
- **Riding with "Reckless": Clarke restrains Gordon (Nov 1868). [KEY / co-presence / death-theme]** Clarke rides out with Gordon and two of his admirers; all day Gordon hurls himself at impossible timber, and at a bluestone-faced St Kilda drain he wheels the little mare Maud to "have a go" — "it was all Marcus Clarke could do to induce him not to make the attempt." Clarke's own account (as "P.P.") is the braid's clearest contemporary witness to the death-wish: a friend physically holding Gordon back from a fence that would have killed him, and naming what he sees — a man who'd "ride against Death on the pale horse" for six pounds and a start.
- **Gordon alone in the horse-box (Cup day, Nov 1868). [colourful / KEY — depression]** Sick of the whole "gay and festive scene," Gordon stays away from the course on Cup day; after his own steeplechase he shuts himself into an empty horse-box in the saddling paddock and smokes a pipe by himself while the other races are run — and is "chaffed a good deal" for it afterwards. A small, eloquent picture of his depression: the champion rider hiding from the sport that made him famous.
- **'A Song of Autumn': Gordon and the child (Myrnong, 1868). [KEY]** The tall, shy poet carries little Maud Power on his shoulder round the Toorak garden, holding "grave converse." She asks for flowers and frets, child-fashion, that picked blossoms die — but so do they unpicked, when the scorching comes. Gordon goes quiet, and on a scrap of paper writes 'A Song of Autumn,' a spring-written autumn poem that answers her with his own death: "you may gather again, my dear — but I go where the last year's lost leaves go." Tenderness, a child, and mortality in one scene, six months after his daughter's grave.
- **The Brighton swim (a suicide attempt, late 1868). [KEY]** Gordon walks into the bay near Brighton and swims straight out, "fully determined not to return." Far out and almost finished, the salt and cold and sheer animal effort burn off the "morbid wish to die"; he turns and claws his way back, reaching the sand "quite spent and exhausted, but himself again." A literal, documented rehearsal of the death — the man who will shoot himself in 1870 first tries to drown himself in 1868, and the body's will to live overrides him. (Pair with his sea-poem 'The Swimmer.')
- **The stolen caricature (the barracks, late 1868). [colourful]** An officer sketches Gordon on Cadger; Gordon, delighted and afraid the officers won't part with it, lies down on the floor with the drawing, then "suddenly rolled himself out of the door and bolted." A flash of the boyish, jovial Gordon — the one who tossed Clarke to the ceiling — against the gloom.
- **The swimmer who wouldn't seek death (Brighton, 1869). [KEY]** Every morning, winter or summer, Gordon walks down to the bay and swims out — half a mile, "regardless of sharks," past the point of easy return. Remonstrated with for nearly drowning himself, he answers that "if death came without his actually seeking it, he at least would have no cause for complaint." Not the open attempt of the earlier Brighton swim, but its quieter cousin: a man holding the door open for death every day, in the sea of his own 'Swimmer.'
- **The ride to Brighton: Clarke, Montgomery, Gordon (1869). [KEY / co-presence]** A wild, joyous afternoon: Clarke, the actor Walter Montgomery and Gordon ride down across Elwood Swamp to Brighton — Clarke pouring out "romantic rhetoric... on all subjects in heaven and earth," Montgomery declaiming Shakespeare, and Gordon on a pony so small his legs nearly touch the ground, racing it at every jumpable obstacle and darting back to fire off Latin tags — the jaunt ending "in the wee sma' oors" at Gordon's house. Documented co-presence of Clarke and Gordon (with Montgomery), and the rare sound of the braid at play.
- **The boy on the course (Ballarat, 12 Feb 1869). [colourful / humane]** At the finish, a miner's boy darts across and is knocked down by Gordon's mare, his leg broken; Gordon, who'd drunk too much and could "hardly move" the next day, visits him in hospital and gives what money he can. The plucky boy says he'd happily be run down again. A small study of Gordon's carelessness and his decency in one stroke.
- **"How often I hoped for a fall" (1869). [KEY]** Riddoch remembers Gordon, the most scrupulously straight rider on the turf, watching the crowd pour their money onto his mounts — and smiling "sadly" as he says they would not be so eager "if they knew how often I hoped for a fall." The death-wish stated flat, in the middle of his triumphs: the champion steeplechaser secretly hoping each ride will kill him. Perhaps the single most chilling line in the whole record — and pure gold for the page.
- **Horne crowns his own line (Jan 1869). [KEY / colourful / contradiction]** Judging the *Australian Annual* poetry prize blind, the grand old man R. H. Horne hands it to "Arakoon" (Kendall) for 'A Death in the Bush,' praising it as worthy of Wordsworth — never noticing that its third line ("Dry flaws wherein had crept and nestled rot") is lifted word-for-word from his own *Orion*. Kendall had absorbed it so completely he believed it his; Horne didn't recognise it; only McCrae spotted the theft, and "was not cruel enough to enlighten either of them." A perfect literary comedy of unconscious borrowing — and a sidelight on how a young colonial poet's reading became his own voice. (Kendall, needing the guineas badly, entered chiefly for the money.)
- **The Yorick pranksters (1868–69). [colourful]** The club's wild coterie — Gordon a "ringleader," Clarke "always ready for mischief," Telo "one of the most outrageous" — roam Melbourne by night lifting gilt hatter's signs with Telo's New Caledonian war-clubs, and besiege the drama critic Neild (after a bad review of Telo's play) by stripping his door-knocker and hanging his house with a gilt fish, a pawnbroker's sign and an undertaker's board. Upper-class English horseplay transplanted — useful light against the death-notes, and a documented romp with Gordon, Clarke and Telo together.
- **Clarke inducts Kendall into the Yorick (1869). [KEY / dialogue / braid]** Soon after Kendall lands, Clarke sweeps him up — "I must make you an honorary member of the Golgotha... Let us imbibe another beer, and start at once" — and walks him up the villainous dark ladder into the dingy, spittoon-strewn club.
