The documented first meeting of Clarke and Kendall, and the moment the third strand of the braid is woven in: the English wit welcoming the native poet into the clubhouse, beer in hand.
- **The deaths-sheet that broke him (1869). [colourful]** Kendall's three-day agony over a statistical "deaths sheet" he can add down but never across — until he pockets his pay, takes his hat, and walks out forever, declaring office work "softening of the brain or madness if further persisted in." The poet's comic, final unfitness for the wage-slave world, and his all-or-nothing wager on literature.
- **The Colonial Monthly office: the braid's clubhouse (1869). [setting]** In the bluestone den near the foot of Elizabeth Street, Clarke rallies his contributors — Gordon, Kendall, McCrae, Telo, Horne, Shillinglaw — amid the charcoal caricatures frescoing the walls; Kendall looks in, takes "his share in those little cheering wafts of Bacchus," and finds himself caricatured among them. The one room where the whole braid and its circle are documented together — a natural hub alongside the Yorick.
- **The secret confession from the Yorick (22 June 1869). [KEY / interiority]** Ill, hounded by creditors, newly arrived and not yet earning, Kendall sits in the Yorick — the one place he feels safe — and writes to the near-stranger Neild the whole humiliating history of his family's betrayal: the silks bought on his credit, the forged £45, the furniture signed away, the laughter when he came for his mother. He begs "three or four pounds on my pen." A postscript: "Mrs Kendall knows nothing of this note." A proud, broken man unburdening to a stranger because he can tell no one close — and choosing the club, not his home, in which to do it.
- **"Am I not the editor of the Police Gazette!" (the Yorick, c.1869). [colourful]** When the Chief Commissioner of Police, Captain Standish, puts his name up for the literary Yorick, astonished members send a deputation to ask his qualifications; he answers "with military promptitude" — "Gentlemen of the Yorick, am I not the editor of the *Police Gazette*!" — and is duly elected. The club's literary purity dissolving into clubbable Melbourne respectability, played for comedy, with the dissolute police chief joining the poets.
- **Gordon's overnight fame — and the worst reading of it (June 1869). [KEY / colourful]** 'How We Beat the Favourite' appears anonymously the very day Horne sails, and within days every sporting man in Melbourne knows it by heart; the shy steeplechaser, who scribbled his verses on scraps and hid behind anonymity, is suddenly famous. At the Melbourne Hospital he reads it aloud so badly that a cheeky young Moloney complains — and Gordon, unoffended, hands him the poem to read and gives him the manuscript. The reluctant poet at the moment of fame, self-effacing to the last.
- **The review that planted His Natural Life (1869). [contradiction]** Hyndman, reviewing *Long Odds* anonymously, praises the book but tells Clarke the truth — he is writing about an England he has never seen — and to write his own country instead. The advice that would become Clarke's masterpiece, delivered as a critical sting; sharpened by Clarke's private joke that the chapters reviewers admired were Walstab's, "the ones I never wrote."
- **Gordon damns his horse to hell (Hunt Club, June 1869). [colourful]** Gordon's mount Gaylad, cannoned by a refuser, jumps short and hangs on the fence; Gordon dismounts "leisurely enough," expresses "some very warm wishes as to his horse's future welfare in the lower tropical regions," and hauls him over. A flash of the dry, profane, unflappable horseman — comic relief and pure character.
- **The bridegroom goes looking for lodgings (22 July 1869). [colourful]** Clarke marries Marian Dunn at St Peter's before a tiny party — and the moment the knot is tied, leaves his new wife with her parents and walks off to hunt up rooms to put her in. Witnesses thought it perfectly characteristic. The absent-minded, improvising, faintly comic Clarke in one gesture — and, with Montgomery sailing for Adelaide the day before, a wedding shadowed by the rivalry that fuelled the press war.
- **"Your letter has frightened me" (the Yorick, August 1869). [KEY / interiority]** Kendall, destitute and ill, writes Rusden a begging note from the club — and is then unnerved to learn he'd apparently written something he cannot remember, about "destructive news from England." Insisting "I never drink," he can only call it "an excitement equivalent to temporary insanity," and ends, frightened of his own mind: "Your letter has frightened me." The first crack in the surface — the breakdown years foreshadowed in a single bewildered letter.
- **"Happier in gaol than I am now" (August 1869). [KEY / interiority]** Threatened with prison over a debt, Kendall writes Parkes not in fear but from somewhere beneath it — there is no blow left that could land, he says; gaol would be a rest, "I should probably be happier than I am now." To Neild the same weeks: "I hide all these things from my wife. The deception is wicked." Sleepless, starving, hiding charity from Charlotte, refusing the doctor — a man going under, told in his own unbearable clarity.
- **Gordon burns his poem over Kendall's (1869). [colourful / co-presence]** Reading Kendall's 'The Hut by the Black Swamp,' Gordon is so struck he learns it "by heart," rates it above his beloved Swinburne, and burns his own half-finished 'Camp'd by the Creek' rather than compete — "I am sick of writing." The generous, self-doubting side of the champion poet, and the warmest documented moment of the Gordon–Kendall friendship.
- **The drunkards write the temperance number (Humbug, Sept 1869). [KEY / contradiction]** Two hard-drinking, debt-ridden young men sit down to make the anti-drink issue of their satirical paper: Clarke, soon to owe £150 for wines and spirits, writes "We are a nation of Drunkards... King Nobbler rules over us," and Kendall — whose own tippling is already being whispered about, in *Humbug*'s own pages — supplies 'The Demon of Drink.' Satire as confession; the humbug they flay is partly their own. Built-in irony and real pathos.
- **The Humbug Thursdays (1869). [setting / co-work]** The weekly gathering of contributors — Clarke, Kendall, Moloney, Neild, Bright, Carrington — to pick a theme, hand the artist his cartoon, and "enjoy a pleasant time generally," in the *Punch* tradition. The braid's prickliest pair, Clarke and Kendall, actually working side by side: a natural ensemble scene of the bohemia at its productive, convivial best.
- **"I have not the self-denial now" (October 1869). [KEY / death-arc]** Gordon explains to Blackmore why he must come to Adelaide: alone, he can no longer make himself exercise or stay off the drink as he once did — he needs a friend at his elbow to manage it. The proud, self-disciplined horseman openly admitting he can no longer govern himself. Pair it with "the man who distrusted himself": the lucid, losing struggle against his own dissolution.
- **'The Sick Stockrider' and the stanza he cut (January 1870). [KEY]** Gordon's masterpiece appears as the *Colonial Monthly* dies — and he quietly removes its last verse, the one that "laid bare his own heart": the dying stockman shrugging that it makes "no difference to the dead man underground / What the living men remember or forget." The founding poem of Australian literature, published by a man who "didn't think much of them" and who had Madden recite them because he could not bear to read his own. The cut stanza is the whole tragedy in miniature — the death-haunted heart, withdrawn from view.
- **Clarke buries Humbug with a joke about his own DTs (January 1870). [KEY / colourful-dark]** Clarke's farewell to his dead magazine is dazzling and devastating at once: he was hired for "habitual melancholy and an incurable tendency to spirituous liquors," and tried to become the drunkard a comic editor must be, "and brought on five useless fits of delirium tremens." Comedy as confession — the wittiest man in Melbourne diagnosing his own ruin in print, and counting his "nine deadly enemies."
- **Kendall lies to Clarke's face (January 1870). [KEY / contradiction]** Kendall writes the savage *Touchstone* obituary mocking Clarke's dead *Humbug* — then calls on Clarke to borrow money and denies he wrote it. "The prevarication was wicked, though I sinned to save Clarke's feelings." Everything tangled in one scene: the resentment, the dependence, the shame, the genuine wish not to wound — the Clarke–Kendall friendship at its most compromised and human.
- **The madman in the cell, Port Arthur (26 January 1870). [KEY / setting]** Clarke, gathering the convict horror that will become *His Natural Life*, is shown a man transported for poaching at thirteen and now mad — "a gibbering animal" who pokes a "nail-bitten hairy finger, like the toe of an ape" through the peep-hole to try to put out a visitor's eye; "he'd best be dead," says the warder. The leaden sky, the Island of the Dead, the shame of "unmanly curiosity" — the raw, sickening core of Clarke's masterpiece, witnessed first-hand.
- **Locked in a room to write his masterpiece (1870–71). [KEY / colourful]** Chronically, gloriously late with *His Natural Life*, Clarke is padded out in bigger type, then replaced one month by a printed apology — until Massina simply locks him in a room with a pipe and a key to extract the next instalment. The chaotic genius producing the great Australian novel under literal lock and key: comedy and greatness in one image.
- **Kendall sleeps in the lane (1870). [KEY / colourful-dark]** The man just crowned "the first poet in whom Australia speaks" now drinks his evenings away in obscure public-houses and passes his nights "seated in some lane or right-of-way," his family hidden in a Richmond back-alley. Stature and ruin in the same body — the colony's national poet in the gutter, months after the acclaim.
- **The father writes beside the dead child (February 1870). [KEY]** Araluen Kendall dies at three of dental fever aggravated by malnutrition, and her father — too poor to bury her — sits "writing by the dead / To earn the undertaker's fee." Charlotte said the child's wailing haunted him to the end; he came to believe he had killed her by failing to provide. The most harrowing image in the whole story: the freshly-crowned national poet earning his daughter's coffin at her deathbed.
- **The suggestion-book solidarity (Yorick, 8 February 1870). [KEY / co-presence]** Six days after Araluen's death, someone scrawls four mocking exclamation marks beside the broke Clarke's name on the Yorick's list of subscription defaulters. Gordon writes in the suggestion book that whoever did it "ought to be kick'd," and signs his name; Kendall, in his grief, signs below him; others pile on. All three of the braid bound together on one page — Gordon defending Clarke, the grieving Kendall at his side. A rare, documented moment of the three closing ranks.
- **Clarke's brutal letters (February 1870). [KEY / contradiction]** While Kendall's child lies dead at home, Clarke — provoked by Shillinglaw's lie and Kendall's tangled debts, but knowing the circumstances — sends "two letters that were brutal," coldly demanding an account. The generous "loyal hub" at his hardest; proof the friendships were real enough to wound, and that Clarke's charm had a cold underside.
- **The last ride: friends stationed to catch him (12 March 1870). [KEY / death-arc]** Broke and needing pocket-money, Gordon takes one more race on Prince Rupert — and his friends, knowing how he rides and how he is, post themselves around the course "in case he came to grief." He does: thrown, recovered, thrown again. Carried to Brighton, "there by myself I did not care much either way." The death-wish and the daily danger meeting at last; the friends' dread is the whole arc in one image.
- **"My wife would be better without me" (21 March 1870). [KEY / interiority]** In the bleakest letter of his life, Gordon tells Riddoch the truth: only Maggie keeps him here — "had it not been for my wife I should have got out of my troubles somehow before this... I don't think the next world is worse than the present, and if I got a little more desperate I'm sure my wife would be better without me." His head failing, the Esslemont hope already arranged to fall to her after his death. The explicit threshold of the suicide, weeks away.
- **Kendall writes to the dying Gordon (March 1870). [KEY]** Both poets broken in the same weeks — Kendall just buried his daughter, Gordon hurt "inside somewhere" — Kendall reaches out, thanks Gordon for defending him, confesses "the day baby died I had not a shilling to bury her with," and, met with silence, writes again: "Your silence looks like an undeserved slight." Neither knows the other is near the end. Two ruined men missing each other in the dark.
- **The braid in one issue (The Australasian, 7 May 1870). [KEY / co-presence]** On a single Saturday the three appear together in the one paper: Gordon's 'Wolf and Hound,' Kendall's 'A Cruise Among the South Sea Islands,' and Clarke's 'The Last of Macquarie Harbour.' Less a scene than a fact for the novelist — the braid printed side by side at the very moment two of them are coming apart. The convergence made literal on a page.
- **The Supple shooting (Spring Street, 17 May 1870). [colourful-dark]** A Yorick member, the Irish-nationalist barrister Supple — driven half-mad by another man's anti-Irish articles — empties a revolver at the politician G. P. Smith near Parliament House, misses him, and kills a publican who rushes him. Sensational violence at the door of the bohemia; and, in Kendall's tender account of Supple's ruin-by-sensitivity, a mirror held unknowingly to himself.
- **The Esslemont news, and the travelling trunks (4 June 1870). [KEY]** Word comes from Scotland: the entail is barred, the estate lost, the £2500 a year gone. Gordon had been so sure he'd already had travelling trunks made to take Maggie home to "this beautiful house." Now there is no house, no escape, no way to pay his debts — and, McCrae thought, this blow "upset him more than any of his other troubles." The hope whose collapse stands immediately before the suicide; the trunks are the cruellest stage-property in the whole story.
- **"He was riding for falls" (June 1870). [KEY / death-arc]** Onlookers watch Gordon force a green horse over the hardest jumps on the Ballarat course long after the race is lost, until a general beside the rail says plainly, "he was riding for falls"; another day, in a gale on Hobson's Bay, he refuses to take in sail until his companion objects to being drowned with him. The death-courting now seen and named by others — the steeplechaser openly inviting the fall he confessed he hoped for.
- **Two dead daughters in one line (June 1870). [KEY / co-presence]** Kendall borrows, unawares, three lines from Gordon's unpublished elegy for his dead child and folds them into his own elegy for Araluen; Gordon bridles, Kendall apologises with aching candour — "my memory was in a maze... its relation to my late loss" — and makes public amends. Two grieving fathers whose dead girls meet in a single image of a small mouth at the breast. The dyad's deepest and most painful intimacy.
- **Two future masters fight for a clerkship (June 1870). [colourful / irony]** Clarke and T. A. Browne — soon to be famous as the author of *Robbery Under Arms* — both apply for the same modest Public Library post; Barry backs Clarke, who wins. The two men who would write the colony's great novels, competing for a librarian's wage: a wry emblem of what the literary life paid in Australia, where even the winner takes a government billet to survive.
- **The library as a loafers' lounge (the Public Library). [setting]** Clarke's own earlier sketch of his future workplace: the half-finished British-Museum façade, the morocco-and-gilt shelves — and, since admission is free, "the idle, drunken and dirty loafers who infest the town," one snoring "with his head pillowed on a copy of *Eothen*." The grand temple of colonial learning doubling as a doss-house — the perfect ironic setting for the writing of *His Natural Life*. (Barry, its founder and Clarke's patron, was no friend of novels: asked if it held many, he said "very few indeed," and was "thankful" the few were being stolen and "will not be replaced.")
- **The hat on the library steps (1870s). [colourful]** The dandified judge Barry, "starched" in his shining bell-topper, meets his casual librarian Clarke ambling up the steps in white flannel and a battered cabbage-tree hat (made for him at Pentridge), and delivers a freezing little rebuke — "I scarcely think your hat... is exactly suited to the position you occupy in connection with this establishment, Mr Clarke" — with "just a glimmer of a smile." Patron and protégé, hauteur and irreverence, in one exchange; the convict-made hat its own quiet joke beside the author of *His Natural Life*.
- **The last drink: Gordon and Kendall (23 June 1870). [KEY / co-presence / death-arc]** The afternoon before Gordon's death, the two poets meet in Collins Street and turn into a hotel bar — by one telling with seven shillings between them, which they spend. Two ruined men, one carrying a glowing review of the other, sharing a last drink neither knows is the last. The tenderest dyad's final scene; the novel's most natural place to let them simply talk.
- **The morning he kissed her goodbye (24 June 1870). [KEY / death-arc]** Gordon rises before dawn; Maggie half-wakes at the touch of his beard as he bends to kiss her; he whispers he is going for a walk, and takes his rifle. Hours later the doctor and a friend come to the door, and she runs out crying "Where is Gordon?" The whole tragedy in two gestures — the gentle, deceiving kiss and the wife's cry — with the death itself kept, mercifully, offstage.
- **The hat with one shilling (Brighton scrub, 24 June 1870). [KEY / death-arc]** Before the end, Gordon sets down his hat a few yards off, and in it his knife, his pipe, some tobacco — and a single shilling: the whole worldly estate of the man Australia would soon call its national poet. Found by a grocer looking for a stray cow. The smallest, most devastating still-life in the story.
- **The letter to the dead man (June 1870). [KEY]** Too shattered (or too broke, he says) to follow Gordon to the grave, Kendall scrawls his excuse — "I am penniless" — and begins it, without thinking, "Dear Gordon." Then he crosses out the dead poet's name and writes "McCrae." One slip of the pen holds the whole weight of his grief: he is still writing to a man a day in the ground. The seat in the funeral wagonette goes empty.
- **White sand at Brighton (25 June 1870). [KEY / death-arc]** A single mourning coach, a straggle of friends over three miles, Maggie following against her friends' wishes — and at the grave, McCrae remembered, "no heavy clods, only the purest of white sand." The quiet, small burial of the man Australia would within a year canonise as its national poet; the next day's paper carries a glowing review of the book he died owing for.
- **The rescue a few hours too late (24 June 1870). [KEY]** At Yallum Park, the Riddoch brothers talk over Gordon's ruin; George says, "Don't you think I ought to ask Gordon up? It is not safe for him to be in Melbourne after this." John answers that Gordon has promised to come soon. As they speak, Gordon is already dead in the Brighton scrub. The help that was coming, arriving too late by hours.
- **Clarke at the piano (1870s). [KEY]** Years after Gordon's death, in low spirits, Clarke sits at the piano and hums 'The Sick Stockrider' to his own accompaniment — "I should live the same life over, if I had to live again" — the tears running down his face. The warmest friendship in the braid, still mourned at the keyboard a decade on. A scene that can recur: a private rite of grief threaded through Clarke's later chapters.
- **Maggie packs the England trunks (June 1870). [KEY]** The widow fills the travelling trunks Gordon had made for the trip home to Esslemont that never came — now carrying their poor possessions to Robe instead, one box set aside for his riding things. That box is lost on the voyage, and with it, perhaps, the unwritten 'Penthesilea.' The luggage of a dream repurposed for a widowhood; the last cruelty of the Esslemont hope.
- **"I am guilty" (Sydney, November 1870). [KEY]** Back in Sydney barely a month, Kendall passes a forged £1 cheque to a chemist he knows, with twopence-farthing to his name; arrested, he tells three different stories, begs the detective to tear the cheque up, and then gives way: "I see it is no good, I am guilty." The poet of the dawn, a forger in the dock — the lowest point of the fall the novel has tracked from the laurel down.
- **"Gordon had tricked them cleverly" (1870). [KEY / interiority]** At Kendall's trial a witness recalls him speaking of Gordon's suicide "with great levity," laughing that "Gordon had tricked them cleverly." Five months after writing the finest elegy of his life for Gordon, the friend surfaces in his broken mind as the one who escaped — survivor's guilt turned to a graveside joke. The dead haunting the living, in a single unbearable line.
- **The family in the witness box (December 1870). [KEY]** One after another, Kendall's own relatives stand up in open court to swear that he is mad — the night-wandering, the weeping like a child, the not-knowing-them, the "he would sooner be out of the world." Love performing the public unmaking of him, to save him from gaol. The intimate humiliation of a breakdown made evidence.
- **Turned from the door (Sydney, 1871 onward). [KEY]** Ill and broke, Kendall crawls to his mother-in-law's house for a glimpse of Charlotte and the baby — and Charlotte stands in the doorway and calls her brothers to put him off the premises. The poet of the dawn, begging at a door for sight of his own child. The loneliest image of his ruin.
- **Clarke at the flogging (Melbourne gaol, February 1871). [KEY]** Half-way through writing *His Natural Life*, Clarke stands among the press and the "usual loafers" in the gaol yard and watches a man take fifty lashes in silence, "a horrible twitching and trembling in the muscles of his arms." The novelist gathering the raw matter of his convict book — and, beneath it, a man birched twice a week as a boy, looking at the thing that scarred him and would scar Kirkland. Research and memory in one scene.
- **Dinner at Clarke's (May 1871). [co-presence / interiority]** Castieau walks home from the Yorick with Clarke and stays to dinner near the university. Marian — the actress the theatre-public adored, now "the domesticated wife and mother... very quiet" — appears "only at dinner and at the end of the evening," then withdraws; the two men talk on alone. A quiet, telling tableau of the Clarke marriage: the brilliant, sociable husband and the withdrawn wife, the unhappiness he was pouring, unseen, into his stories.
- **The dinner at the Café de Paris (c. 1872). [colourful]** Clarke, meaning to spend a modest half-crown on a chop and ale, is accosted by a brilliant, ruined old barrister fresh from the Benevolent Asylum who hasn't eaten all day — and who, handed the menu, orders soup, a cutlet, sweets, cheese, claret and a cigar, melting Clarke's last sovereign. As Clarke leaves, the old man tells another diner he feels "absolutely wretched" to be "indebted for my dinner to that little cad who has just gone out." Clarke's reckless generosity and the bohemia's biting ingratitude in one anecdote.
- **Duffy's verdict (1872). [KEY]** The old Irish revolutionary reads the convict serial the young secretary has pressed on him, and gives it back marked and frank: powerful, original — and too painful, too long, its hero motiveless, the Ballarat chapters to be "mercilessly expunged." Clarke takes the "douche-bath" and goes home to rebuild the plot. The making of the masterpiece shown as an act of revision: criticism received, and used.
- **The killing of Rufus Dawes (a midnight walk, 1872). [KEY]** Clarke and the solicitor Jim Moloney walk from Lonsdale Street up past the gaol and out the Sydney road under a full moon, arguing the fate of Rufus Dawes; Clarke pleads to let his hero live — "let him have a day or two of happiness" — and Moloney presses him down, "he must die," until Clarke finds the death (Dawes drowned saving Sylvia). They wake Dr Pat near dawn: "Marcus and I have killed Rufus Dawes," and "the three murderers sat down to beer." The making of the masterpiece as a friend talking an author into the necessary grief.
- **Walstab arrested at the Yorick (21 August 1872). [colourful-dark]** Castieau, the gaol governor, is called out of the club room to find his friend the novelist Walstab under arrest for non-payment of his wife's maintenance — and a few hours later receives him at the gaol as a prisoner, quietly ordering him clean bedding. The bohemia's debt-ruin at its bleakest: one Yorick man jailing another, with a small mercy. The thin line, for all of them, between the club and the cell.
- **"Had it out with God and my soul" (a mountain peak, January 1873). [KEY / interiority]** Up-country and drowning in debt, his marriage wrecked and his wife's sister on his mind, Clarke tethers his horse below the summit, climbs to the highest peak, and lies in the scrub till dark wrestling his conscience — then comes down and writes to Rose to renounce her: he loves her, but owes "a duty to my wife, my children, my own honour." The dispossessed dandy alone on a mountain, choosing duty over the one warmth left to him, and naming his whole life "stone instead of bread."
- **'Holiday Peak': the life not lived (January 1873). [KEY]** In a story written from that same mountain stay, Clarke dreams the alternative — the lost fortune restored, Australia never reached, his schoolfellow Gerard Hopkins a famous painter showing him round the life they might both have had — before he wakes to "a poor devil banished to Bush Land, tied neck and heels in debt." An opium-dream of un-dispossession; the counterfactual the whole of Clarke's exile-psychology yearns toward. (The real Gerard was meanwhile a hidden Jesuit poet; neither schoolfellow knew the other's true vocation.)
- **McCrae the starving pavement artist (1873). [colourful]** Owed a guinea for the cover design of *Holiday Peak* and kept waiting, George McCrae dashes off a caricature of himself as a ragged pavement artist being moved on by a gendarme, the flagstones chalked "I AM STARVING & OTHER TALES BY MARCUS CLARKE" and "IMPECUNIOSUS SUM." The bohemia's chronic brokeness turned, as ever, into a joke between friends — and a wry comment on what Clarke's own books earned.
- **Trollope hears of Gordon at Yallum Park (May 1872). [death-arc]** At Riddoch's station the visiting Trollope is told of the dead poet — "a young Scotch gentleman who had gone out young, but had not done well... fallen into a sad melancholy, and had – died" — and reads the *Bush Ballads* with growing conviction of "the man's genius." The English novelist discovering Gordon two years dead, and tactfully veiling the suicide ("destroyed himself," he first wrote) even as he crowns him a poet. Recognition arriving, again, only after the grave.
- **Off the steamer at Newcastle (1873). [KEY / death-arc]** Friends have arranged Kendall a fresh start — a newspaper at Grafton — and put him on the steamer; it calls at Newcastle, he goes ashore with the other passengers, and is not among those who reboard. He drinks through his money and sets out to walk the long road back to Sydney on foot. The self-sabotage of the fresh start, and the walk that, by accident, carries him toward the Fagans and his rescue.
- **Names upon a stone (Gosford glen, Christmas Day 1874). [KEY / setting]** Recovered, at peace, working the Fagans' farm and known simply as "Harry," Kendall walks up the valley with the Fagan boys to the rock-pool in the glen, and they chip their initials into the stone by the water. Out of it comes 'Names upon a Stone,' one of his loveliest poems. The poet of the dawn restored to himself in a fern-grown glen — the calm centre after the long storm, and the source of his late, settled verse.
- **The Yorick out at elbows (1874). [setting]** The club that began as the bright hub of the braid has dimmed: Clarke deserts it for the Iphigenia café after nine o'clock, and Castieau notes it is now "studiously avoided by any but hard up people" — "to be a 'Yorick' means to be out at elbows" — before resigning it himself for the cheaper Athenæum. "In grasping at the shadow I have lost the substance." The Yorick years visibly ending; the bohemia thinning out.
- **The library under the hammer (Melbourne Athenæum, 8 August 1874). [KEY / setting]** The bankrupt Clarke's own books — 580 of them, Shakespeare and Balzac and Dumas, and the rare criminal trials and colonial pamphlets out of which he quarried *His Natural Life* — are auctioned off in the small hall opposite the Argus office, and fetch £250. The librarian losing his library; the bibliophile watching his shelves dispersed; and, the very same day, the trustees asking him to resign. The collapse of the safe ground, staged as a sale.
- **The farthing damages (the Harwood trial, August 1874). [colourful]** Sued for a libel on a theatre manager, Clarke stands in Barry's court refusing on principle to admit he wrote the anonymous notice — and is caught having told Harwood the same lie to his face ("Then you tell a lie to shield yourself," needles the prosecutor; Higinbotham defends the journalist's code). The case dissolves into farce as the manager is cross-examined on whether the Duvallis sisters' dancing is "modest." The jury finds for the plaintiff — and awards one farthing. Clarke's bohemian-press world in a courtroom: the principle, the lie, the smut, and the contemptuous coin.
- **Blackballed from his own club (September 1874). [KEY]** The Yorick, short of money, votes on readmitting nine lapsed members — and passes all of them but one. The single man blackballed is Marcus Clarke: the founder who named the club after Yorick's skull, served as its first secretary, and made it the hub of the bohemia, now refused re-entry by it. Bankrupt and tainted by the Harwood lie, the Elf is shut out of the cave he built. The bohemia turning on the man who gathered it.
- **Kendall the bushman (Gosford, 1874). [interiority]** The wreck the Sydney cells knew is gone: here is a sober man in a borrowed family, swimming, yarding cattle, wandering the gullies with a book, writing left-handed on scraps of paper — and wanting nothing to do with letters or fame. "I would rather turn bullock driver than go back to the ragged old Bohemian life," he writes; "let the Gods go hang." The survivor who lives by unmaking himself as a poet — a quiet, redemptive counter-image to everything Melbourne did to him.
- **Clarke arraigned in Parliament (9 June 1875). [colourful / dialogue]** A member rises to attack the government's hiring of Clarke to write the schools' *History of Australia* — naming him a scurrilous insolvent "kicked off the Melbourne press," a man paying "not 1d. in the £," his "whole career" disreputable — while the Speaker tries to rein him in and the minister defends Clarke as the colony's best-qualified writer. The bankrupt bohemian's name dragged through the legislature; his shame and his talent argued over his head in public. The price of the fall, made official.
- **The happy ending refused (December 1874). [interiority]** London's Bentley will publish *His Natural Life* only if the convict hero is "saved and returned home." Clarke obeys — writes the extra chapter, lets Dawes live and rescue the daughter — but begs that nothing else be touched, and the protest holds: the printed Bentley book keeps the drowning. The artist made to soften his masterpiece, complying under duress, and the tragedy surviving anyway. "No return" outlasting the market that demanded a return.
- **The poet of our desolation (September 1875). [KEY / interiority]** At his cottage desk, writing the essay that will name a national literature, Clarke ranks the living and the dead: Kendall "the chief," Gordon "now gone to his rest" and "genuine," himself unnamed. He coins "Weird Melancholy" and "the Land of the Dawning," and ends on "the poet of our desolation" — leaving open which of the three he means. The prose master crowning his poets and half-crowning himself; the founding critical act of the literature the braid was inventing, written by the one of them still standing.
- **A fellow of infinite jest (Melbourne Cemetery, August 1875). [colourful-dark / setting]** The comic actor John Dunn — Clarke's father-in-law, who gave his daughter the Yorick skull's first owner — is buried after dropping dead in the street, with Marcus among the pall-bearers and the once-warring Harwood beside him. On the stone: Hamlet's words for Yorick, "He was a fellow of infinite jest." The skull that named the bohemia's club, set now over a real grave in the family that married into Clarke's.
- **The reluctant marksman (Camden Haven, c.1877). [colourful]** Kendall — humane, who made "quite a scene" when a man burned a black snake alive — takes a gun from a fellow, fires once at a magpie flying high overhead, and drops it stone dead. He insists it was an accident, is thereafter reckoned the crack shot of the district, and refuses ever to fire again: "They will never get me to try again, as I would probably lose my reputation as a marksman." The gentlest possible joke from the man a rifle had figured so darkly for — a flash of the recovered Kendall's wry, settled humour.
- **Dinner at the Birdcage (January 1876). [setting / colourful]** Clarke moves through Butters's club opposite the Argus office in full glory — Fijian waiters, a forest of palms and ferns, turtle and venison and figs steeped in snow — perfectly at home among Lyster, Moloney, old Dick Birnie, the whole bohemian-clubland cast, getting "exactly what he wanted and without any fuss," and dropping his mordant refrain: "If you want to prove that life is not worth living, persist." The fleshpots Clarke loved, rendered in full; the city-creature in his element.
- **Clarke hates the bush (Lyster's, near Fern Tree Gully, 1876). [KEY / contradiction]** Out at the edge of the great Amygdalina forest, the man who wrote "the heritage of desolation" will not walk into it. He has "not the faintest perception of the glory of the forest," prefers "one smell of the fleshpots," dismisses the Grampians and the Wimmera with "Mount Zero and the rest; very nice; yes." The author of "Weird Melancholy" loathing the actual bush — his definitive Australian landscape a townsman's gothic projection, not a naturalist's love. A central, dramatisable contradiction: the inventor of the national aesthetic had no feeling for the country itself.
- **The Prince of Wales up all night (India, winter 1875–76). [colourful]** Touring India, the Prince of Wales is given *His Natural Life*, sits up late into one night to finish it, is "greatly struck," and says its author "ought to be in London." The convict novel written by a bankrupt blackballed from his own Melbourne club, keeping a future king from his sleep on the other side of the world — the masterpiece's reach against the smallness of its maker's circumstances. (His cousin Sir Andrew, who had saved him from eviction, likely put it in the Prince's hands.)
- **"A kind of cousin of mine" (the two Gordons). [colourful]** General Gordon of Khartoum — schoolfellow of the poet at Woolwich — believed Adam Lindsay a relation, and inscribed a gift copy of his *Poems* "From Charley to Andrew. He was a kind of cousin of mine," handing it to Sir Andrew Clarke, Marcus's cousin. The small-world web tightening: the doomed poet, the martyr-general, and the two Clarkes, all threaded through one book — possibly the very edition Marcus prefaced.
- **"I am considered well off, am always in debt" (the Cyril letter, January 1877). [interiority]** Clarke writes his oldest friend a self-portrait of comfort built on sand — the seaside house, the servants, the declined offers to go home — and lands on the line that is his whole life in nine words: "I am considered well off, am always in debt and contrive to live pleasantly." He half-jokes that he may never return to England, recalls "that vanished youth of ours in the Highgate fields," and ends on a flash of real need: "How did you like His Un-Natural Life? I mean *really* you know?" The charm, the denial, the homesickness, and the naked wish to be told the book was good — all in one letter.
- **The net withdrawn (1877). [KEY / death-arc]** Sir Andrew Clarke, whose power of attorney let Marcus weather the 1874 bankruptcy in a Brighton villa, does not renew it. The safety-net that made the dispossessed gentleman a gentleman still is quietly pulled; Clarke leaves Brighton for St Kilda, and the moneylenders he had kept at arm's length move back in. The hinge of the final descent — the moment Mackinnon points to as the start of the road that "wrought him to death before his time."
- **The inspired bushman (Camden Haven, c.1878). [KEY / contradiction]** Old Samuel Bennett, who knew him, describes Kendall as a man with "his name for every blade of grass, frond of fern and bank of moss" — a poet to whom the bush spoke "in its own language," an "inspired blackfellow" in the period's phrase. Set this beside Clarke at Lyster's, refusing to walk into the forest: the two poets of the braid are opposites at the root. One wrote the great Australian bush and could not love it; the other was so woven into the country he could barely be told from it. The national landscape was founded by the man who hated it and inhabited by the man who vanished into it.
- **Dire necessity (a Collins Street hotel, late 1870s). [colourful]** Clarke takes the young Victor Daley to dinner, then looks up with "amazed blue-grey luminous eyes" and says, "I have no money!" Undefeated, he borrows a sovereign from George Ashton on "dire necessity" — and, the coin in hand, forgets the dinner and orders champagne. When Ashton walks in to find them drinking his loan, Clarke only grins: "Dire necessity. Join us." The charm and the improvidence that would kill him, distilled into one glass.
- **The cantata at the Exhibition (Sydney, 17 September 1879). [KEY / setting]** Henry Kendall's words open the Sydney International Exhibition: Giorza conducts, a chorus of 250 girls sings his lines before the assembled colonial Governors, and the hall calls for an encore. Weeks later he wins the hundred-guinea prize poem against 250 entries from across the world, and the papers name him "the premier bard of the southern hemisphere." The man found starving in the bush, who renounced literature and would rather have driven bullocks, crowned the first poet of the country — in his own lifetime, before a cheering crowd. The recovery's full vindication, and the recognition Gordon and Clarke would only get from the grave.
- **The bird in hand refused (December 1878). [death-arc / irony]** Offered the secure Parliamentary Librarianship, with Barry himself urging acceptance, Clarke turns it down — betting everything on the Chief Librarianship he believes will fall to him when old Sheffield retires. He is, Mackinnon says, "doomed to disappointment." The dispossessed gentleman gambling away his last solid footing on a promotion that will never come; the fatal overconfidence of a man who always believed himself "capable of understanding and accomplishing all things."
- **The granite face (Sydney, c.1880). [interiority]** Frank Myers meets Kendall come up from Camden Haven — coat "green with wear, and split down the back," the hardest-lined face Myers ever saw, "like the chiselled granite of Carlyle," yet the eyes "clear and kindly always." Not the broken man of the biographies but a defiant one, full of iron wisdom and contempt for his own laurels: "You may almost hope to be allowed to lick the fleshpots with scullions permanently if — you succeed!" The other Kendall — hard, unbowed, prophetic — the version the apology-writers never saw.
- **"Hush! prohibited" (the Academy of Music, January 1880). [colourful]** Berry's government bans Clarke's localized *The Happy Land* for caricaturing the ministry; the company plays the English version instead, and every time a censored political jab is cut, an actor says "Hush! prohibited" — and the packed house roars. Detectives take notes; the manager goes into hiding; the satire lands harder for being gagged. Clarke, the hidden adapter, mocking the very radicals he wrote leaders for — and making enemies he cannot afford.
- **"In that wild bleak Bohemia" (Camden Haven, January 1880). [KEY / interiority]** Writing to his friend Swan, Kendall looks back from his timber-store desk on the Melbourne years and finds the phrase for them: "In that wild bleak Bohemia south of the Murray, I went through Gethsemane." He calls himself "the grey shadow of the young man" who began in such hope in 1861, "a lingerer beside" the world of letters who never had time to write a long poem — and signs off that he has "not written to Clarke for years." The premier bard, crowned the year before, taking his own measure as a survivor who lost the thing he survived for. The book's title, and its sorrow, in one letter.
- **Barry's deadly courtesy (the Library office, 1880). [colourful / dialogue]** Sir Redmond Barry, president of the trustees — himself a man who kept a mistress in open defiance of the same punctilious society — appears in Clarke's office "with a somewhat clouded countenance" to deliver, in elaborately spaced pauses, a masterclass in patrician reproof: one needs "some thousands a year of a private income" before one flippantly attacks a bishop "among so punctilious a community. Good evening, Mr Clarke." The establishment closing ranks against the freethinker, with exquisite politeness — and the warning that Clarke, who has no private income at all, is the last man who can afford his own honesty.
- **"And didn't I go!" (the Waxman anecdote). [colourful]** Aaron Waxman, the moneylender opposite the Library, is so proud of his famous debtor that he rhapsodizes over Clarke's Gordon preface and invites him to his parties — then is left lamenting that Clarke, having borrowed at fifty per cent and accepted every invitation, never repaid him. "Look at all I have done for you," he complains; "I lent you money and asked you to my parties." "Yes," Clarke replies, unmoved — "and didn't I go!" The charm that disarmed even his creditors, and the improvidence that was burying him.
- **The hand held out, and missed (October 1880). [KEY / co-presence]** His old dislike of Clarke dissolved, Kendall sends Michael Fagan — "the man who led me out of Gethsemane and set me in the sunshine" — to Melbourne with a note introducing him to Clarke. But Clarke, ill and hounded by bailiffs, keeps out of Fagan's way; the letter is received, the meeting dodged. "Perhaps he is in trouble," Kendall writes, not knowing Clarke is months from death. The braid's two survivors, parted for a decade, reach toward each other at the last possible moment — and the door does not open. The reunion the whole story seems to want, withheld.
- **Shining at the ball (1880). [colourful-dark]** Half-blind with ophthalmia, eight stone, the bailiffs literally in his house, Clarke talks his newspaper out of a £10 note to "exorcize" them — then forgets the bailiffs entirely and spends the night at the Governor's ball, "where he shone." The charm performing over the ruin, the gentleman dazzling a ballroom while the men of business wait in his parlour to carry off his furniture. The whole of Clarke's doomed gallantry in one night.
- **The sweet smile at the French play (early 1881). [colourful-dark]** Marcus Clarke is at the theatre when word arrives that the trustees have passed him over for the librarianship he gambled everything on. He "shrugged his shoulders, and smiled sweetly," and lets the officer beside him pass round a witty rhyme he has just made of his rivals' names. Epigrammatic gallantry over catastrophe — the man who has just lost his last hope answering it with a joke, in public, without a flicker. The same doomed charm as the Governor's ball, now at the moment of defeat.
- **Sneering and serving (1880–81). [contradiction]** In the same season that his contempt for "Superior Persons" and "noble balderdash" is destroying his last chance at the librarianship, Clarke is quietly "taking an infinite deal of trouble to spread the fame of Adam Lindsay Gordon or Henry Kendall." The man wrecking his own prospects with his pen uses the same pen, tirelessly, to build the reputations of his two fellow poets — one dead, one estranged. Self-destruction and loyalty in the same hand: the truest, strangest note of Clarke's character at the end.
- **"Uncle John from the East" (St Kilda, 1881). [colourful]** A bailiff arrives to seize the Clarkes' furniture, and Marian comes home to find a stranger awkwardly entertaining her children. Clarke's response is pure performance: "Where is my festive Cuss? I'll settle him!" — then he sweeps into the kitchen, claps the bailiff on the back as "Uncle John from the East," installs him at the dinner table, plies him with wine, and by evening has him "convulsed with laughter" over cigars. The man stays days, chops wood, and leaves swearing he'd go to prison himself sooner than see Marcus suffer. The legendary charm at its purest — disarming even the instrument of his ruin — though the tale may be half-borrowed from Bulwer Lytton, which is itself perfectly Clarke.
- **The genial letter (1881). [co-presence]** After a decade of silence and Kendall's open dislike, the thaw completes — not in a room but on paper. Kendall sends Clarke his *Songs from the Mountains*; Clarke answers with a warm *Leader* tribute and "a genial letter"; Kendall reads it, moved, and tells McCrae of it. The two survivors of the braid, who never once met in these years and never would, exchange kind words at the very end. The reconciliation the Fagan visit missed, achieved at last in ink — a closing of the circle that is also, because they are both dying, a farewell.
- **The coin-toss (Queen's Birthday, 1881). [KEY / colourful-dark / interiority]** Knowing himself dying, Clarke walks with a friend near the Yarra Bend asylum and turns his ruin into one last black joke: "Which shall it be – the mad asylum or the pauper grave? ... head, grave; tail, asylum." The florin falls tail — the asylum, the very fate that had taken men around him — and he overrules the coin: "No gibbering idiot shall I e'er be; rather the gleeful gallows tree." The dispossessed gentleman facing madness, pauperdom and death with the gallows wit intact, choosing the joke over the fear to the last. The whole of his courage and his doom in a single tossed coin.
- **The writer dies writing (St Kilda, 2 August 1881). [KEY / death-arc]** In a near-bare cottage, everything sold, his wife nursing him and at first no money even for medicine, Marcus Clarke loses his speech and beckons for pencil and paper. He moves his hand across the blank sheets as if writing — faster and faster as his mind wanders — trying, McCrae was sure, to turn out "something that was to bring a little coin for his wife and children." The pen-man dying with the pen-motion still in his hand, working to the last to provide. Then the beautiful eyes open wide, gaze far off, and close. He was thirty-five. The single most devastating image in the book.
- **Kendall's elegy (September 1881). [KEY]** The poet who disliked Clarke, dismissed his masterpiece, and would not write to him for a decade, now mourns him in print without reserve — "all that is left of the young man, with the kind face and good heart, whom we used to call Marcus" — and prints a verse 'In Memoriam' that names the shared doom: "the lot austere / That ever seems to wait upon / The man of letters here." The braid mourning its own, the antagonism dissolved in grief, each of the three in turn elegized by another — Clarke for Gordon, now Kendall for Clarke. The reconciliation that failed in life, completed over the grave.
- **Waxman's wreath (1881). [colourful-dark / irony]** A subscription fund is raised for Clarke's destitute widow and six children, and climbs past a thousand pounds — three times the debt to Aaron Waxman that had bankrupted and helped kill him. The single largest donation, ten pounds, comes from Waxman himself. The moneylender who pressed Clarke to the grave heading the charity list for his victim's family. The whole bitter economy of Clarke's life in one line of a ledger.
- **"So you will get the salary" (St Vincent's, August 1882). [KEY / death-arc]** Dying of consumption in a hospital ward, Henry Kendall clings to life by an act of will toward a date: "Lottie, I shall live to the first of the month so you will get the salary." And he does — holding on until the 1st, so his wife will be owed one more month's government pay, then dying with his right hand resting on his face. "To the last his only thought was that he had not left me and my little ones unprovided for." The exact mirror of Clarke's deathbed a year before — the hand moving over blank paper to earn a little coin. Both founders died, a year apart, doing the one thing they could still do for their families: Clarke pretending to write, Kendall pretending to live, both for the money their work would bring. The deepest rhyme in the book.
- **Twenty mourners (Waverley, 3 August 1882). [KEY / setting / death-arc]** The "uncrowned laureate of Australia," crowned by the *Bulletin* barely eighteen months earlier, is followed to his grave above the Pacific by twenty people. "Had he been but a cricketer," one mourner says, "his funeral might have been a colossal one; but as he was merely a poet... he had but a scanty following." A stranger, a chance visitor, steps from nowhere to scatter wildflowers on the coffin. The cruel anticlimax of the one recognition-in-life the book allowed: the laurels of 1880 and the scanty grave of 1882, eighteen months apart. The nation that would build its poetry on him could find twenty citizens to bury him.
- **Louisa Lawson at the grave (1884). [colourful / KEY]** Two years on, a newly arrived woman walks out to Waverley to "worship at Kendall's grave" and finds it weed-grown under a sagging wreath and a little wooden cross — and is "aflame with indignation." Louisa Lawson campaigns a monument into being almost single-handed, then is forgotten by the committee at its unveiling. She had already named her son Henry after the dead poet, "envisaging a literary destiny for him" — and Henry Lawson, who became the national voice Kendall never quite was, would be buried in the plot beside him in 1922. The torch passing in a graveyard: the founder rescued from neglect by the mother of his successor, the two writers laid side by side above the sea.
- **The Turkish bath (Russell Street, September 1871). [colourful / co-presence]** Clarke, "an old hand," walks the novice Castieau through the whole ritual of the Russell Street baths — the fig-leaf calico, the oven-hot rooms, the "naked sprites" who soap and pummel and scrape, the plunge from warm to cold, the couches after. A rare, relaxed glimpse of the sociable Clarke at leisure with a friend, and a vivid set-piece of clubbable 1870s Melbourne — the warm, performing Clarke before the private grief surfaces.
- **"She wished she had never married me" (November 1871). [KEY / interiority]** Clarke writes the line in his red leather notebook and shuts it. Two years married to the actress he'd courted against a rival, and it has already failed. One sentence, recorded and hidden, that opens the whole guarded interior beneath the public dazzle — and the private source of the unhappy marriages all through his fiction.
- **Parkes finds Kendall in the bush (Sydney, 1872–73). [KEY]** Henry Parkes — future five-time premier — opens his door one Saturday to a man "tottering... so wretched that I hardly knew him," who has slept out in the bush and "could hardly stand for debility." Parkes takes him in, boards him, reclothes him head to foot, and is sure he "would have perished" otherwise. The poet of the dawn picked up off the ground by a statesman; the lowest physical point of the long fall.
- **"My name's Rickeybockey" (a Sydney cell). [colourful-dark]** Kendall has gone missing; Holdsworth, who has often paid his Water Police Court fines, leaves the cashier's desk to look — and hears, from a cell, Kendall's voice answering to a name out of a novel: "My name's Rickeybockey." Derangement and bookishness fused in one half-comic, wholly sad moment — the literary mind still quoting from the floor of a cell.
- **Gordon carried home: "not much hurt" (21 Mar 1868). [KEY / contradiction]** Maggie watches a buggy pass with an injured man inside, not knowing it is her husband; hours later four men carry Gordon in on a stretcher — skull fractured, ribs and collarbone broken, face trodden — and he tells her he is "not much hurt." The stoic understatement of a man who courts these falls. (The rival horse-names, Prince Rupert / Necromancer, are the kind of uncertainty that clings to him — keep both.)
- **Annie's death and the unmarked grave (14 Apr 1868). [KEY]** Still bed-bound from the fall, Gordon loses his infant daughter at dawn; he designs her headstone, rides to the funeral beside the parson Adeney, and buries her under a slab that hides she was his. The grief that breaks him and his marriage both — the emotional fault-line running straight to 1870.
- **Michael in the Clarence (28 Apr 1868). [KEY / contradiction]** Kendall's mentor found drowned, the eye-wound unexplained, the colony split on accident–murder–suicide — and Kendall certain, from the farewell letter and the despairing verses, that the man chose the river. A poet's self-destruction witnessed up close by the poet who will half-follow him.

## 5. Supporting Figures — dossiers
_Recurring non-principals who get substantial text treatment. Modern scholars are logged as sources, not here._

### Captain Frederick Standish
Chief Commissioner of Police, Victoria, 1858–1880; the connective figure of the Melbourne sporting/police/clubland world, friend of Clarke's cousin Andrew (RMA Woolwich, 1840 intake) and of Clarke's "people at home."
- **Backstory:** b. 1824, younger son of an old Lancashire Catholic family; Royal Artillery (2nd lt 1843), aide at Dublin Castle. Fled England 1852 under the alias **Francis C. Selwyn** to escape gambling debts; on the Victorian goldfields he prospected unsuccessfully and ran a ginger-beer stand fronting sly grog.
- **Rise:** lifted from a desperate low ebb by Andrew Clarke's patronage (Asst Commissioner, Bendigo) → Protector of the Chinese (1855) → Gold Fields Warden, Sandhurst (1857) → Chief Commissioner of Police (Aug 1858). Catholic-born but turned Freemason at Bendigo; Provincial Grand Master of Victoria, 1861.
- **Man of pleasure:** gambler and racing man; reputedly received a daily report naming who had spent the night in the better brothels; a suppressed inquiry heard he once gave a dinner with naked women seated on black-velvet chairs to set off their skin. Sadleir: gifted but too much "a man of pleasure" to take the office seriously.
- **Racing:** foundation VRC member; per his obituary the Melbourne Cup "was entirely his idea." The **Standish Handicap** is still run.
- **Link to Clarke:** dined repeatedly with Judge Clarke and Marcus (Dec 1865, Mar 1866); offered Marcus a mounted-police commission (declined). Clarke caricatured him as "Captain L'Encrier" (French *encrier* = inkwell = a *standish*) in 'A Day in Melbourne' (1865).

### Alfred Telo
Clarke's first literary friend in Melbourne and a lifelong companion; the two shared chambers above a Collins Street sewing-machine shop when Clarke arrived as a 30s-a-week reporter. A magnificent mystery man, generally taken to be Jewish.
- **Origins (contested):** by one account born at Archangel, Russia, son of a wealthy merchant who later moved to Liverpool; an embassy attaché, then a lieutenant in a Prussian Guards regiment, invalided out after a fall shattered his thigh — dead-lame for life, with a markedly distinctive gait. Master of many European languages including Russian; reticent about his own past.
- **The legends:** Russian spy, diplomatic agent, miner, courier, husband of a countess — "the wildest legends were afloat concerning the man of mystery."
- **Manner:** stroked his beard, winked, played a fantastic flashing-finger morra (*digitos micantes*) in the air while you hung on his reply; never began a sentence without apologising for its end; delivered anecdotes in disordered fragments to be reassembled "like a Chinese puzzle" — "Look here – of course – the Queen of Spain – I don't mean to say – as I told Cortchakoff... but, however."
- **Use:** a ready-made eccentric for the Melbourne chapters, and the living counter-evidence to the anti-Semitism charge from the Spring Meeting report.

### Dr Robert Lewins
Materialist freethinker (Tyndall/Huxley school), ex staff-surgeon major from the New Zealand wars; befriended the 21-year-old Clarke at Ledcourt (early 1867) and, back in Melbourne, talked Argus proprietor Lachlan Mackinnon into hiring him. The man who opened the door to Clarke's literary career.

### William Trainor
American circus rider, Gordon's most intimate South Australian friend — and his most devoted. They met at Penola in the mid-1850s when Trainor, playing the drunk in the circus "changeable act," was hauled off by the police; the trooper who finally let him go, laughing, was Gordon. They took up horse-breaking together, and Gordon gave him the manuscript of 'Verses inspired by "My Old Black Pipe"' (which names him). The devotion outlived Gordon: Trainor named his firstborn **Adam Lindsay Gordon Trainor** and bought the grave next to Gordon's at Brighton so he could lie beside him (his 1917 death notice still read "intimate friend of the late Adam Lindsay Gordon"). A measure of the loyalty Gordon inspired — Charley Walker likewise named a son Lindsay, and Gordon named a horse Walker.

### Edward "Ned" Bright
One of Gordon's first intimate SA friends and, per Humphris & Sladen, the original of **Ned in 'The Sick Stockrider'** — Gordon's best-loved poem. Of a poetry-loving bush family (brother John wrote *Wattle Blossoms and Wild Flowers*; sister Mrs E. A. Lauder is a recurring witness). Bright wrote his own elegy, 'In Affectionate Memory of Adam Lindsay Gordon'.

### Father Julian Tenison Woods
Born to Irish parents in London, 1832, into a literary family (father a Middle Temple barrister long on *The Times*; brother N. A. Woods a Crimea correspondent alongside Russell). A Passionist novice (1850), he taught at a Toulon naval college, came to Australia in 1855, and was ordained in 1857; for a decade (1857–66) he ministered the vast, half-desert Penola district — where he befriended Gordon and met **Mary MacKillop**, with whom he later co-founded the Sisters of St Joseph. A scientist and historian too: geology articles (from 1857, in the institute Andrew Clarke founded), *Geological Observations in South Australia* (1862), and a *History of the Discovery and Exploration of Australia* (1865) that Clarke later quoted. He shared Gordon's freakish memory and love of poetry; their riding companionship across the runs is among the best-documented friendships of Gordon's SA years. (Woods first saw him in the Lake Hawdon stockyard, sitting a bucking colt until the girths broke and he landed on his feet, then talking poetry on the verandah till late.) The "historic night" talk and the storm/*King Lear* recitation are set-pieces.

### Margaret "Maggie" Park (Gordon)
Glasgow-born (22 Mar 1845), brought to SA as an infant; daughter of Alexander Park, baker turned station hand. She was working at McQueen's Criterion Hotel, Robe, when Gordon — racehorses stabled nearby — grew so infatuated he moved to be close to her; they married 20 Oct 1862. Tiny (~5 ft) and very young (17–18, though the certificate said 21), a fine horsewoman but, by Woods's and her own account, no intellectual companion — the thrifty housekeeper Gordon had asked for, addressed by him ever after as "girl." She firmly debunked the romantic nursing-after-a-fall legend. Key women's-perspective source via her 1912 *Advertiser* interview (and, remarried to Peter Low, the Park Low papers). [Their infant daughter's death is coming — a central tragedy.]

### Mary MacKillop
Eighteen-year-old governess at Penola (from 1860) who, hearing Woods preach on the parish's neglected children, offered herself to his cause; together they built a Penola school (1861) and the **Sisters of St Joseph** (1866), an order vowed to poverty and equality. MacKillop always credited Woods as the true founder. Canonised in 2010 as Australia's first saint — the Gordon/Penola world's brush with a national-historical figure.

### Charles Harpur
The first significant native-born Australian poet, a generation older than Kendall — "a son of the forests," whose verse Kendall thought "saturated with the strange fitful music of waste and broken-up places." Kendall's mentor by correspondence for six years (from 1862); they met only about six months before Harpur's death. Harpur gave him a poetic lineage and a lonely kinship ("If you knew how lonely I am here..."). Kendall's 1863 rededication of a poem from Michael to Harpur marks where his deepest allegiance lay. He died of consumption on 10 June 1868 — "a noble ruin," Kendall's elegy called him — the second of Kendall's mentors to die within six weeks.

### Henry Parkes
Warwickshire-born (1815) ivory-turner who came to NSW in 1839, founded (and lost) *The Empire*, and rose to be premier of NSW five times and "the father of Federation." A published poet himself (*Stolen Moments*, *Murmurs of the Stream*) — which gave Kendall his opening. Parkes lent him money, took him to his country seat, and placed him in the Colonial Secretary's office (1866). The patronage was real, the gratitude thin (the "Apollonian locks... Liverpool Asylum" jibe). Recurs as Kendall's patron — and later appoints him Inspector of Forests.

### Charlotte Rutter (Kendall)
Manchester-born doctor's daughter, eighteen when she met Kendall after his 1867 "Love" lecture; he always called her "Lottie." Devoted to him through the ruin ahead — "very few women were loved as I was by him; although he had that failing he was the essence of kindness and gentleness to me always." The early daughter's death, his alcoholic collapse and his disappearance all lie in their future. A central women's-perspective source (her 1882 letters to Sutherland).

### Walter Montgomery
English actor, grand-seigneur in manner, who introduced Clarke to Charles Bright in Melbourne. A blurb headliner for his end: on leaving Australia he shot his horse, and two days after marrying, shot himself. Embodies the theatrical glamour-and-doom of the Melbourne stage world Clarke moved in.

### Sir Redmond Barry
Irish-born (Ballyclough Anglican gentry, with the West Indian and military connections Clarke and Gordon also had), Trinity-educated Supreme Court judge and "patron spirit" of the Melbourne Club's "fast set" — a rakish dandy (he turned out for a duel "as if attending a Vice-Regal levee") who kept a mistress, Louisa Barrow, and four children he never married. Founder-figure of Melbourne's cultural institutions (Public Library, University, Gallery) and Clarke's chief patron: he secured Clarke the library secretaryship and watched over him with a mix of parental regard and hauteur (the cabbage-tree-hat rebuke). A blurb headliner for his end: he sentenced **Ned Kelly** to death and died twelve days after Kelly was hanged (1880). The eminent, rakish establishment Clarke moved among.

### Charles Gavan Duffy
Irish nationalist, writer and politician — founder of the *Young Ireland* journal *The Nation*, tried five times for treason, who emigrated in 1855 and held high Victorian office (his 1862 Land Act, gutted by the squatters). A Library trustee from 1870, he became Clarke's friend and patron, reading the *His Natural Life* serial in manuscript and giving frank notes; the novel in book form is dedicated to him. His real importance to the braid is as a model: the man who made *The Nation* "an appeal to an old bardic people in passionate popular verse" stands behind Clarke's and Kendall's own projects of a national literature.

### The Melbourne Club "fast set" (Aspinall, Ireland & co.)
The rakish wits around Barry: **Butler Cole Aspinall** (Eureka-rebels' defence counsel, "the wittiest man," favourite diner-out — died back in England insane and almost penniless, 1875) and **R. D. Ireland** (Galway orator who "gained and spent four large fortunes" — "I have made £140,000 in fees... and there is not one of these blessed banks that would today honour my cheque for £40"), with Standish, the coroner Curtis Candler, and "Spicy Andrew" Clarke. The flâneur-gourmet-turf-and-anecdote world ("our lot") of glamorous genteel ruin that Clarke joined.

### George Arthur Walstab
Clarke's closest collaborator on the *Colonial Monthly* and a vivid figure in his own right: West Indian planter's son, French-coup veteran, two years in the Victorian Mounted Police, then a wound in the Indian Mutiny that troubled him for life. "A soldier in his heart," he became a journalist faute de mieux — "the pen of a Thackeray with a double portion of the indolence of Coleridge" (Kendall) — and kept the magazine running when Clarke's riding accident laid him up. Bankrupt 1870. His office fresco summed him up: himself on a rearing charger, "Yes, it is true, gentlemen, that I was once a policeman... but then... ye gods! What a policeman!!!"

### John Joseph Shillinglaw ("Pennylex")
London-born ex–water-police inspector turned man of many boards (navigation, health, viticulture) and freelance writer; nautical in speech, a "robustious" singer of chanteys, devoted to Clarke and Moloney. Co-editor of the *Colonial Monthly* and probably the man who took it over from Clarke in 1869. Appears across Clarke's writing punned as "Pennylex." His office self-caricature: a man tipping a gallon measure, captioned "J. J. S. in Liquidation."

### George Gordon McCrae
Poet, artist and civil servant (deputy registrar-general), son of the diarist Georgiana McCrae (illegitimate daughter of the fifth Duke of Gordon). A regular *Colonial Monthly* contributor and lifelong friend of Clarke's, he frescoed the editorial den and, as one of the circle's longest survivors (d. 1927), became its key memoirist — his papers and conversation feeding son Hugh McCrae's 'The Yorick' articles and *My Father and My Father's Friends*. A principal source for the clubland world.

### Patrick Moloney
Irish-born (1843), one of the first two University of Melbourne medical graduates (1867); resident medical officer and later honorary physician at the Melbourne Hospital, and a *Punch*/*Australasian* contributor. Clarke's close friend — he gave Clarke the skull that nearly named the Yorick "Golgotha," and it was Moloney who christened the decade "the Golden Age of Australian Literature." A big, genial, classically handsome wit (McCrae's portrait) who later made a fortune as a surgeon, retired to London, and "died of want of occupation." Blackballed on first application to the Yorick, later admitted (with his solicitor brother James).

### Frank Madden ("Actæon")
Collins Street attorney and keen horse-sketcher (later MLA, Speaker of the Victorian parliament 1904–17, knighted 1911). His witty newspaper drubbing of Gordon over a hunt report — and Gordon's sporting response — began a close friendship; they rode steeplechases together until Gordon's death, and Madden left some of the sharpest first-hand portraits of him (the horse-breaker manner, the moody silences, the racing-cap tilt he sketched at Flemington).

### Major Thomas Durand Baker
Cheltenham-bred career soldier (Crimea, Indian Mutiny, the Maori wars; later a general, knighted after Kandahar), Deputy Assistant Adjutant-General for Australia and NZ from 1866. Gordon's chief racing patron and friend — it was on Baker's Babbler that Gordon won his famous 1868–69 chases, and on Baker's Prince Rupert that he had his last bad fall (March 1870).

### George Watson
Irish-born Master of Fox Hounds, founder of the Melbourne Hunt Club (1853) and lessee of Kirk's Horse Bazaar; "one of the best known and most popular men of his time," and famously irascible. Gordon's "old enemy" turned friend — their violent feud was healed in the hunting field when Watson tended the injured Maggie (Clarke present). A vivid figure of the Melbourne horse world.

### Thomas Lyttleton
Tasmanian-born police superintendent (a Standish appointee and dining companion, reckoned "one of the wildest, most dissipated men"), and a gifted painter of horses — Yorick, Melbourne Club and Athenæum member, foundation member of the Victorian Academy of Arts. His steeplechase paintings of Gordon (notably the 1868 VRC Spring Steeplechase on Viking) are among the rare contemporary likenesses of the poet. He was also the officer who, with another, arrested Shires after Drummond's snake-death — a small knot tying two of the era's stories together.

### Marian Dunn (Clarke)
The actress Clarke married on 22 July 1869 — Marian O'Donoghue, daughter of the Irish comedian John Dunn, a stage success in Australia and New Zealand and Montgomery's Ophelia (to whom Clarke had given the Yorick skull). Both she and Clarke were twenty-three. She gave up the touring career Montgomery pressed on her to marry "the rising journalist," and after Clarke's early death helped Hamilton Mackinnon assemble his Memorial Volume. Her loveliness — preserved in photographs — and her profession may have answered something in the motherless Clarke, whose own mother had been a beautiful woman "on the stage."

### George Higinbotham
Dublin-born barrister, liberal titan of Victorian politics — most liberal editor of *The Argus* (1856–59), Attorney-General, champion of secular education and land reform, who called the squatters "the wealthy lower orders." Like Gordon and Clarke, said to have "married beneath him"; a heavy smoker, unostentatiously kind. At Brighton (1869) he lent Gordon — the gardener's lodger — the run of his library, reopening the poet's reading (Browning, Swinburne) and later advising him on the Esslemont claim.

### Dr James Murray
Gordon's Brighton doctor, who lent him Swinburne — and a thoroughly sinister figure: he dosed asylum patients with morphia to free his weekends, presided drunk over a fatal leg of the Leichhardt search, and would later lead the notorious *Carl* blackbirding expedition. One of the darker men in Gordon's orbit (another thread to the blackbirding world that touched Harry Mount).

### Gerald Henry Supple
Cork-born Irish-nationalist journalist and barrister (a contributor to Duffy's *Nation*, author of a history of the Anglo-Norman invasion of Ireland) and Yorick foundation member. In May 1870, his "monomania" over Ireland inflamed by G. P. Smith's anti-Irish articles, he shot at Smith in Spring Street, missed, and killed a bystander. Kendall, who knew and pitied him, drew him as a man destroyed by a diseased sensitivity to slights — a portrait that doubles, eerily, as a description of Kendall himself. At his trial Higinbotham pleaded insanity; Supple, unrepentant ("he had done a good service to society"), played the martyr, was sentenced to death, then reprieved on appeal — the Melbourne bar rising "almost to a man" to defend him.

### John Buckley Castieau
Governor of Melbourne gaol ("the Hotel Castieau," Birnie called it) and a Yorick member from mid-1870, whose diary is one of the richest sources for the club's daily life — the cribbage and Bezique, the papers, the hard-up members (Shillinglaw selling his lithographs before leaving) — and for literary-legal Melbourne at large. He noted Gordon's death ("blew his brains out... this suicide has created much sensation") and, "in consideration of Supple's blindness," led the condemned man out of the dock.

### William Bede Dalley
The most brilliant Australian-born advocate of his day (barrister, parliamentarian, briefly a minister), whom Kendall had praised in 'About Some Men of Letters.' He defended Kendall free in the 1870 Sydney forgery trial, winning the insanity acquittal that spared him gaol, and a dozen years later wrote of the poet's confessional verse with rare compassion — his suffering "a humble confession," not a parade. The friend who caught Kendall at the bottom.

### Richard Henry (Hengist) Horne
The grand old man of colonial letters: in England a friend of Elizabeth Barrett and Dickens, author of the farthing epic *Orion* (which Poe called nearly "the very noblest" poem of the age); in Australia (from 1852) a gold-escort trooper, magistrate, planter of the Tabilk vineyard, and mines registrar in "the Siberia of the gold fields" — vain, guitar-playing, self-renamed "Hengist." Now leaving after seventeen years. He feuded with Clarke (who parodied him as the Mounted-Police poet "Horsa Hengist" in *His Natural Life* and probably drew his barbed portrait of Clarke as "the Hermit"), and judged Kendall winner of the 1869 *Annual* prize — missing his own pilfered line. A third poet-turned-policeman, and the braid's memento of faded literary glory.

### William Murray Drummond
Barra-born ex-army officer, police magistrate and goldfields warden, Melbourne Club member, and the *Colonial Monthly*'s likely principal financial backer — a man, the *Argus* said, "who scarcely knew what danger was." His death defines him: to disprove the snake-showman Shires, he had himself bitten by a tiger snake "for science" (1 May 1868) and died within a day, taking his magazine backing into the grave with him. Clarke's friend, and the subject of Clarke's helpless eyewitness account.

## 6. Thematic Notes

- **The Australasian as the braid's shared venue.** The *Argus*'s literary weekly — "the chief literary authority in Australia," "very much the clubman's paper" — is where all three converge in print: Gordon and Kendall from 1866, Clarke from 1867. A documented common ground before and alongside the Yorick Club.
- **Balzac as self-fulfilling template.** Clarke's idol *and* warning: he reads Balzac's life as "one long struggle with debt and difficulty," the finest work "written with his life-blood" — then lives it out. Seeds the debt/ruin theme and his own two bankruptcies.
- **Death-haunted aesthetic / the drug thread.** The Holbein-and-Satan décor of the Telo chambers, recorded inside 'Cannabis Indica,' marks the start of Clarke's hashish experiments and the morbid strain running through his Melbourne writing.
- **Two poets to the Mounted Police.** Gordon, like Clarke, was steered toward the Mounted Police — "devilish good pay, a horse, three suits of regimentals." Gordon took the berth; Clarke declined. The force is a recurring conduit for the literary set and an early structural rhyme between the two men.
- **Gordon's death-wish, pre-loaded.** Before he even sails, the 1870 suicide is foreshadowed: his father "would sooner see me in my grave," and Gordon half-agrees. Carry the thread the whole way.
- **Inherited madness, twice over.** Clarke's father dies insane; Gordon's mother lives out a melancholic exile. Mental affliction shadows both principals from childhood — a shared inheritance worth weighting.
- **Gordon the self-mythologiser vs the record.** The cherished legends — letters thrown overboard, gold fever, the aristocrat who chose the ranks — are contradicted by the documents (he used his introductions, left lovesick not gold-struck, actually wanted an inspectorship). Decide how much of the myth to honour: Gordon helped build it, and it may be truer to his self-image than the facts are.
- **Inherited ruin, three generations (Kendall).** Henry's arc is half pre-written in his blood: a gifted grandfather ruined by scandal and disgrace, a forger father dead young and broke, a mother of shifting name and informer ancestry. Decline runs in the line.
- **The forgery rhyme.** Basil Kendall's forged £5 order (two years' hard labour) directly foreshadows Henry's own later forged cheque — a father-and-son crime to weight when the Sydney collapse comes.
- **The temperance-journal debut.** Kendall's first published poem appears in a Band of Hope temperance organ — bitterly ironic given the alcoholism that will define his "darkest period." A small, sharp detail to plant early.
- **Twin reticence (Gordon & Clarke).** Both were "morbidly reticent" about their origins — Maggie and Frank Madden on Gordon ("very difficult to get him to speak about himself"), Mackinnon on Clarke's silence about his childhood. A shared guardedness worth braiding.
- **The inheritance squandered.** Gordon's ~£7,000 legacy (1861) evaporates in reckless generosity and racing losses — the debt theme made literal, and a rhyme with Clarke's coming bankruptcies.
- **Gordon's deepening melancholy.** By the mid-1860s Woods is openly "apprehensive" about Gordon's "morbid melancholy" — the complaints of a wasted life, the solitary walks and rides, the hours sitting alone by the sea. Parliament was partly a prescription for it. Track this darkening straight to 1870. By Ballarat (1868–69) Brazenor sees it complete: a silent, reckless rider who "rode to break his neck" and "had the look of a man who had lost himself" — the death-courting recklessness and the depression now one.
- **The braid converges on Melbourne (from 1866).** Gordon's resolve to quit Parliament, move to Melbourne and live by the pen is the structural hinge. Clarke joins the *Argus*/*Australasian* in 1867; Gordon and Kendall are already in the *Australasian*; all three are about to be in one city and one club. The Yorick years are now in view — weight the narrative toward this gathering.
- **Deniehy: the first drink-death, and Kendall's mirror.** Deniehy's alcoholic ruin (d. 1866, aged 37), which Kendall elegised, prefigures Kendall's own collapse — and the wider intoxication pattern the blurb promises (Kendall's drink, Gordon's and Kendall's opiates, Clarke's hashish).
- **Gordon's romance of heroic death.** Long before 1870 he idealises the glorious end — Burke and Wills (he wished he'd ridden with them), Nolan of the Light Brigade ("a death the gods might envy"). The death-glamour suffuses his poetry and prefigures the suicide.
- **Kendall's Dyspeptic Lectures as self-prophecy.** In 1867 he lectures, half in jest, on the opium-eater's reaction, the miseries of marriage, and the slavery of debt — every one of which will define his own ruin. An uncanny detail to plant early.
- **Two Bohemian rehearsals before Yorick.** Kendall's *Sydney Punch* "brotherhood" (cognac, cigars, weekly cartoon talk) and Clarke's Telo chambers both prefigure the Yorick Club — the clubland sociability that will bind the braid.
- **Gordon and Clarke first share a page (1868).** Gordon, now in Victoria and short of time for verse, puts his only two 1868 poems in Clarke's *Colonial Monthly* — the braid's first print contact, before they ever meet at the Yorick.
- **Clarke the drug-experimenter.** From the first he treats intoxicants as research — absinthe "to find out" if it maddens, opium "rather liked," soon hashish ('Cannabis Indica').
