The blasé curiosity that completes the braid's intoxication pattern (Kendall's drink, Gordon's opiates, Clarke's hashish/absinthe).
- **Heads "knocked about."** Clarke blamed his stammer on a childhood horse-kick to the head; Gordon's falls and sunstroke battered his. Two men literally damaged by horses — a small, grim rhyme.
- **The Duke's visit: a near-convergence (Nov–Dec 1867).** Clarke covers the royal celebrations in Melbourne as Q; Gordon hosts the Duke at Ballarat (arranging his horses, riding his steeplechase, even buying his carriage and pair). The braid's two ends brush the same event months before the men meet.
- **Upper vs lower Bohemia: Clarke's self-warning.** His 'Lower Bohemia' (1869) splits the bohemian life into the Bohemia of choice (supper-parties, the next play, always hope) and the destitute Bohemia "from which there was no return." Wilding reads him writing his own fate — the slide his bankruptcies would later trace. A frame for the whole novel's view of the bohemian life.
- **The keynote: "we did not lead virtuous lives."** Clarke's 'Café Lutetia' memoir — the pawned ring, the Peacock and Screechowl, the gaslit round — is the book's epigraph and the novel's emotional keynote: the bohemian life recalled as joy, the tragic cost running underneath. Build the Yorick years toward it.
- **Clarke's humanitarian eye.** Amid the comedy his journalism keeps turning to the era's victims — the deceived emigrant women forced into prostitution, the Chinese-quarter poor, the lower-Bohemia destitute. The compassion that complicates the dandy pose.
- **Marked bodies: arms and skulls.** The three are physically broken in rhyming ways. Two bear maimed arms — Clarke's left shortened by childhood bone disease, Kendall's right crushed by a door (each adapting: the pocketed coat, the left-handed writing). And two are nearly killed and permanently marked by horse-kicks to the head within three months of 1868 — Gordon (21 Mar) and Clarke (June), both skulls already "knocked about," both men subtly changed after. Damage is the braid's shared signature.
- **Kendall's mentors keep dying.** Michael drowned (28 Apr 1868), Harpur dead of consumption (10 Jun) six weeks later — both the men who taught him gone in a season, each eulogised as a "noble ruin" or a touching farewell. With Deniehy's earlier drink-death, a pattern of lost, wasted elders closes around Kendall — the fate he half-senses coming for himself.
- **Gordon's presentiments: riding toward the fall.** Gordon raced under explicit forebodings — trying to keep George Riddoch beside him to avoid dreaded steeplechases, then riding anyway and falling, "his presentiment right." With the death-wish, the cold-water plunges and the "rode to break his neck" recklessness, this superstitious courting of doom is the clearest sign of where he is heading: a man drawn to the very danger he fears.
- **Gordon's death-drift, in his own hand.** The 6 Oct 1868 letter is the pivot of the death arc: drink begun after the head-injury, chronic pain, a carried sleeping-draught "I should never awake from," the flirtation with madness. Everything from here bends toward 1870. Pair it with the **Esslemont** inheritance — the Scottish estate he's the "nearest heir" to — as the fragile hope whose eventual collapse will sit right before his suicide.
- **Gordon and Clarke: the deep kinship.** Wilding spells out why they took to each other: both upper-middle-class with military traditions, both raised in England then torn to Australia as boys (Gordon 20, Clarke 17), both effectively motherless — Clarke's mother dead when he was three and all but unknown to him, Gordon's alive but absent and volatile (knife- and poker-throwing, forever "away for her health"). Two déclassé, half-orphaned gentlemen recognising each other; the friendship the Yorick produced has roots far below the clubroom.
- **The braid assembles socially (late 1868).** Beyond the print convergence, the men now meet in the flesh — Gordon dropping into the Colonial Monthly office for "yarns" (a shrine "not only of the Muses but also of Bacchus"), deepening his intimacy with Clarke, McCrae, Telo and Shillinglaw at the Yorick, and coming to know Kendall "at a later date." Note the stagger again: Gordon arrives socially in late 1868, Kendall only in 1869.
- **The marked skull, made visible.** Gordon's head-fracture is not just biography — it's a gesture he uses, pressing a friend's finger into the hollow in his bone. Tie it to the drinking-since-the-fall, the constant head/back pain, and the marked-bodies motif (he and Clarke both horse-struck in the skull). The damage is something he carries — and shows.
- **A documented braid moment (the Watson scene).** The hunting-field reconciliation puts Gordon and Clarke provably in the same field at the same time — rare hard evidence of co-presence, and emotionally charged (Clarke steadying his dazed friend over the hurt Maggie). Worth weighting as a real braid scene rather than an inferred one.
- **Clarke's "Reckless": the death-wish witnessed.** Clarke's 1868 'Pig-Skin' sketch is the single best contemporary evidence of Gordon's suicidal recklessness — not retrospective legend but a friend's eyewitness, Clarke in the saddle restraining him. It doubles as hard co-presence (the two riding out together) and as the death-theme stated by a man who loved him: Gordon would "ride against Death... for six pounds and a bit of a start." It anchors the Clarke–Gordon friendship and the road to 1870 at once.
- **Gordon's opiates — and the "ride for money" fall.** The 17 Nov letter confirms Gordon had been on opiates (now renounced) and that he had crossed from gentleman-amateur to riding "for money" — a quiet social descent that shamed him. With the bedtime grog and the chronic headaches "I am getting used to," it fills the intoxication thread for the second of the three (Clarke's hashish, Kendall's laudanum, now Gordon's opiates) and deepens the déclassé note.
- **"Leave this gay and festive scene."** Another plain death-wish in his own hand — wearied of life, content to go if only solvent. Read alongside the 6 Oct sleeping-draught and the horse-box withdrawal: a man narrating his own approach to the end through the back half of 1868. (And his afterlife answers it — the painters, memorial volumes and stamp of "the Gordon craze" turning the broke, despairing man into the national poet on the schoolroom wall; a possible coda.)
- **The Yorick's high-jinks — and the class fissure.** The club's "frivolities of 1868–69" (sign-stealing, knocker-wrenching, besieging Neild) are upper-middle-class English public-school horseplay, transplanted: Clarke, Gordon, Haddon, Walstab and the rest came out of England and its private schools. Native-born, barely-schooled, wage-working Kendall stood outside that world and saw its pedigree-snobbery "with a clear if chill eye." The braid is real, but a class seam runs through it — two transplanted gentlemen and one colonial outsider.
- **Gordon: ringleader and recluse.** The sources can't agree whether Gordon was a hard-playing ringleader (tossing Clarke to the ceiling) or a locked-in depressive who talked only of poetry. Hold both: the man could "let himself go" with the glass and the horses, then vanish into silence and gloom. A productive contradiction for characterisation (Wilding's tentative Asperger's gloss is one modern key, no more).
- **The swim: a rehearsal of the death.** Gordon's Brighton swim is the hinge of the whole death arc — not a written wish but an act: he tried to drown himself in late 1868 and his body refused. It rhymes with the sleeping-draught he carried, the recklessness Clarke witnessed, and his own sea-and-swimming verse ('The Swimmer'). When 1870 comes, it is the second attempt, not the first.
- **'The Three Friends' — an accidental prophecy.** Gordon's 1868 Musset translation — three friends loving one woman, all dead by duel, poison and suicide — sits strangely beside both his own coming suicide and the braid itself (three writers, three early deaths). A possible epigraph or motif; at minimum, an eerie note to register.
- **Clarke champions Gordon in print.** The Colonial Monthly essay (likely Clarke's) crowning Gordon "the most Australian" after Kendall and predicting it would be "a boast to have discovered his genius" shows the friendship working publicly — Clarke the editor using his magazine to build Gordon's reputation, a year before 'The Sick Stockrider.'
- **The poet-policeman, yet again — and Horne.** Add Horne to the running rhyme (Gordon and Kendall both ex-Mounted-Police, Walstab too): Horne rode in the gold escort, and Clarke immortalised him as the trooper-poet "Horsa Hengist" in *His Natural Life*. The colony keeps turning its poets into policemen. Horne is also the braid's memento of literary failure — the once-famous man who came for gold, planted vines, and sails home broke and vain: a fate hanging over all three.
- **Kendall's family curse: the drinking strain made flesh.** The dipsomaniac mother — bribed nightly with brandy, raging under restraint, turning up drunk at his work, "too insane to be responsible" — is the living face of the three-generation ruin already in his blood (the gun-running grandfather, the forger father). It darkens the marriage from the start and helps push him to Melbourne — and it is the addiction he will not himself escape.
- **The sea-death he wouldn't quite seek.** After the Brighton swim's open attempt, Gordon's daily half-mile swims "regardless of sharks" become a standing wager with death — "if death came without his actually seeking it... no cause for complaint." It is the passive face of the death-wish, and the exact mood of 'The Swimmer.' The water keeps offering him the end.
- **The masterpiece written in the dark.** *Bush Ballads and Galloping Rhymes* — 'The Sick Stockrider' and the rest — was composed through 1869 in his depression, at Yallum's gum tree and the Brighton cottage. The renewed writing looks like recovery, but the verse is fatalist to its core ("the death to die"). Hold the irony: his best work and his lowest year are the same year.
- **The braid at play (a documented co-presence).** The Clarke–Montgomery–Gordon ride to Brighton is hard evidence of the conviviality the novel needs — Clarke's torrent of talk, Montgomery's Shakespeare, Gordon's Latin and his absurd little pony — a joyous counterweight to the death-notes, and proof the friendships were lived, not just inferred.
- **"Hoped for a fall": the death-wish in the saddle.** Of all the death-notes, Gordon's confession to Riddoch is the starkest — the celebrated rider, backed by the public, privately hoping to fall. It binds the racing, the recklessness Clarke witnessed, the swims and the sleeping-draught into one: every steeplechase was a held-open door. The keystone quote of his arc.
- **The man who distrusted himself.** Gordon refused the *Australasian* staff job because it would feed the drink and the opiates "growing upon him" — a rare, clear-eyed act of self-protection from a man usually bent on his own destruction. The tragedy is sharper for it: he could see the dissolution coming and still couldn't stop it.
- **The Clarke–Montgomery–Marian triangle.** Clarke and the actor Montgomery — friends, skull-giver and skull-getter, linked through the Yorick's naming — fell out "for some foolish reason," and the timing points at Marian Dunn, who left the stage as Clarke's bride-to-be. A human-scale rivalry threading through the theatrical world the braid moved in (and Montgomery's own end is one of the blurb's shocks).
- **Clarke the polemicist: wit as a weapon.** The Montgomery war shows the other edge of Clarke's brilliance — the merciless satirist the Hermit warned of, who "could not always praise" and turned a wounded friendship into a public dismantling. The same gift that made him magnetic made him enemies; it's of a piece with the phantom-review sackings and the suggestion-book jabs. A useful dark counter-note to his charm.
- **The braid completes (April 1869).** With Kendall's arrival, all three principals are finally in Melbourne at once — the convergence the whole book is built toward. From here the story can run on real co-presence rather than parallel lives.
- **The three dyads (not one friendship but three).** Clarke–Gordon: the warmest and best-documented (the rides, the Watson field, "Reckless," deep temperamental kinship). Gordon–Kendall: two reticent poets with a real but limited bond — no shared tastes, proud poverty keeping them from each other's homes, "never bosom friends"; Kendall's tenderest words about Gordon are his elegy *after* the death, so grief may have deepened it in memory. Clarke–Kendall: the prickliest — the dazzling English flâneur and the gloomy native wage-slave poet, admiration shot through with class-resentment ("Perks"). And note the correction: Kendall's *closest* Melbourne friend was neither principal but G. G. McCrae. Stage the braid as asymmetrical, partial, alive.
- **The torch passes (June 1869).** On a single Saturday the old grand man Horne sails home (to a cold reception) while Gordon's 'How We Beat the Favourite' carries him into fame. "'Tis always morning somewhere in the world," Horne had written; the line lands as the colony's literary generation turns over — the farthing-poet out, the steeplechaser-poet in.
- **Hyndman's seed; the Walstab irony deepens.** *His Natural Life* is born from a stranger's review telling Clarke to write his own country — and Clarke's bitter joke that critics praised the ghost-written chapters ("the ones I never wrote") braids his masterpiece's origin back to the accident that nearly killed him.
- **The déclassé marriages (Clarke and Gordon).** Both men "lost caste" and married "beneath them" — and both married *out* of England, back toward their own roots: Gordon the Scot wed a Scottish girl, Clarke (Irish blood, north-of-Ireland Cromwellian stock) an Irish one. Both lived on evaporating inheritances built on others' subjection — Gordon's faded aristocracy, Clarke's family wealth from the now-emancipated slave plantations of the West Indies. Two "detribalized" Englishmen reaching for an older identity through their wives: a deep, exact parallel to anchor the Clarke–Gordon kinship.
- **The actress and the lost mother.** Clarke's mother, dead when he was a child and half-mythologised ever since, had been a lovely woman "on the stage"; in marrying the lovely actress Marian, the motherless man may have been reaching back toward her. Tie this to the Bulwer-Lytton mythologising and the dispossessed-gentleman psychology already in his dossier.
- **Kendall's fraying mind (August 1869).** The Rusden "temporary insanity" letter — a lapse he can't remember, a fright at his own mind, written only months after arriving — is the first documented sign of the dissociation and collapse to come. Plant it early; the breakdown doesn't arrive from nowhere.
- **Kendall going under (August 1869).** The despair is now physical and total — no sleep, no food, secret charity, a wife kept in the dark, a longing for "rest" and even gaol. Read against the Rusden lapse, it is the breakdown arriving in real time, barely months after the braid completed. The novel's brightest convergence and Kendall's darkest descent are the same season.
- **Three poets, three failed books — one publisher.** George Robertson published all three principals (Gordon's *Sea Spray*, 1867; Clarke's *Peripatetic Philosopher* and Kendall's *Leaves from Australian Forests*, 1869) — and the poetry lost money every time (Kendall's a £90 loss; Gordon's needed the author's own subsidy). "The feeble glow of public applause is too easily replaced by a chilling frost." The shared publisher is a real braid-thread; so is the shared commercial failure — the colony could make them famous but not solvent.
- **The drink-satire irony.** *Humbug*'s temperance crusade ('The Curse of the Country,' 'The Demon of Drink') is written by two men sinking into drink and debt — Clarke's £150 spirits bill, Kendall's increasingly public tippling. The hypocrisy ("humbug") they denounced was nearest to home; play it straight, not as cynicism but as men who saw their own ruin clearly and joked at it.
- **The prickliest dyad, working together.** For all the class-friction of "Perks," Clarke and Kendall actually *collaborated* — co-editing *Humbug*, week by week. The Clarke–Kendall relationship isn't only resentment; it's also genuine partnership and Clarke's repeated practical help. Hold both.
- **Kendall: "the first poet in whom Australia speaks."** The 1869 reviews mark the moment the native-born outsider is recognised as the national poet — exactly as he is most destitute. The gap between literary stature and material ruin is his defining tension, and the colony's verdict on what it paid its poets.
- **Gordon's lucid decline.** Gordon names his own deterioration repeatedly now — "I have not the self-denial... to be abstemious by myself as I used to be" — the same clear sight as "the man who distrusted himself" and "how often I hoped for a fall." He is not blind to his ruin; he watches it come. And the dedication of *Bush Ballads* to Whyte-Melville, who would himself die in a hunting fall, is one of those accidental rhymes the record keeps handing the novelist.
- **'The Sick Stockrider': the braid's literary apex.** As the bohemia's two magazines die, Gordon's masterpiece — the founding poem of Australian bush balladry — comes into the world. The shared venue (the *Colonial Monthly*) ends carrying it, beside a Clarke story: the braid's print-home expires with its single greatest work. A natural hinge for the novel.
- **The cut stanza: the heart withdrawn.** Gordon removes the verse that bared his death-longing ("no difference to the dead man underground"). Of a piece with the anonymity, the shyness, "I don't think much of them myself" — he keeps showing the world his ride and hiding his heart. The novel can restore the stanza the man couldn't bear to print.
- **The bohemian moment closes (January 1870).** *Humbug* and the *Colonial Monthly* both die in one month, less than a year after the braid completed. The shared print-world that bound the three is already gone; what remains is the friendships, the falls, and the debts. From here the story turns toward the endings.
- **The compromised dyad.** The Clarke–Kendall relationship reaches its tangled nadir: Clarke parodying Kendall's drinking, Kendall mocking Clarke's dead magazine, lying about it, and borrowing from him in the same breath — "I sinned to save Clarke's feelings." Not a clean friendship or a clean enmity, but the real, soiled thing. Play it without resolving it.
- **His Natural Life: the seed realised.** Hyndman told Clarke to write his own country; a year later Clarke stands at Port Arthur gathering the convict horror that becomes the great Australian novel. The masterpiece is born of the same humanitarian eye that walked the Chinese Quarter and Lower Bohemia — Clarke the witness to the colony's buried cruelty, turning "conditions he dared not commit to print" into fiction. The book grows out of the bohemia's collapse: the magazines die, and from their wreckage comes the one work that outlasts them all.
- **Kendall's collapse: the gutter beneath the laurel.** Within a year of being hailed the national poet, Kendall is drinking in taprooms and sleeping in lanes, his family hidden in a Richmond alley. The starkest statement of the book's cruellest theme — that the colony could crown its poets and let them starve. Hold the two images together: "the first poet in whom Australia speaks," asleep in a right-of-way.
- **Araluen: the nadir.** The death of the malnourished child is the emotional floor of the whole book — the national poet too poor to feed or bury his daughter, writing her coffin-money at her corpse, and carrying the guilt into delusion. Everything in Kendall's later collapse traces back here. Stage it as the hinge of his tragedy.
- **The suggestion-book: the braid on one page.** The 8 February entry is the best-documented instance of all three closing ranks — Gordon's defence of Clarke, Kendall's co-signature, days after Araluen. Shared poverty and shared loyalty in one gesture; a scene where the three are literally bound in a single record.
- **The loyal hub complicated.** Set against his real generosity, Clarke's "brutal" letters to a grieving, penniless Kendall show the cold edge of the man — the same caustic pen that made "nine deadly enemies" turned, briefly, on a friend. Don't sand it down; Clarke was loyal *and* capable of cruelty.
- **The misguided project.** The whole dream of living as a writer in Melbourne was, as Wilding judges, misguided — Clarke "barely managed to survive," and for poets like Gordon and Kendall "there was little hope." The colony could crown them and could not keep them; Kendall's inflated later CV is the wishful gloss on a defeat.
- **Gordon's death now stated outright.** The 21 March letter crosses the line the earlier ones approached: not "hoped for a fall" but "if I got a little more desperate I'm sure my wife would be better without me." Only Maggie holds him; his head is failing; he has arranged the Esslemont rents to fall to her. The suicide is now weeks away and fully foreseen in his own hand — the death-arc's penultimate step.
- **Esslemont: the hope that comes too late.** The inheritance he might finally win is, he says, "too late in the day to do me any good" — and he wants it only so the back-rents can provide for Maggie when he is gone. The estate becomes not a rescue but a bequest; its real function in the story is to let him die having provided.
- **Two friends sinking together (March 1870).** Gordon hurt "inside somewhere" and Kendall fresh from burying Araluen reach for each other through the Shillinglaw mess — and miss, Gordon too injured to answer, Kendall wounded by the silence. The tenderest dyad at its most poignant: two ruined poets in the dark, neither knowing how close the other is to the end.
- **The braid printed together (7 May 1870).** All three share one issue of *The Australasian* — a literal, datable co-presence to set beside the Yorick and the *Colonial Monthly* office. Useful precisely because it lands as the convergence is ending: the three names on one page even as Gordon nears death and Kendall nears collapse.
- **Two men watching themselves fail.** Kendall's "draw the money yourself and don't trust me... I have no control over my impulses" is the exact twin of Gordon's "the man who distrusted himself." Both poets see their own weakness with total clarity and cannot govern it — the shared, lucid helplessness that makes both arcs tragic rather than merely sad.
- **Kendall writes his own future (Supple).** In pitying the over-sensitive Supple — slights magnified to injuries, "brooding, and fancying, and fretting, till the demon of insanity had him... in its grip" — Kendall unknowingly diagnoses himself (McCrae's Kendall, almost word for word). Plant it as foreshadowing: the temperament that will carry Kendall to his own breakdown, named in another man's ruin.
- **Esslemont: the last hope dies.** On 4 June the inheritance fails — the estate barred, the income gone, the travelling trunks useless — and with it the only future Gordon could imagine. McCrae thought it hurt him worse than anything; it falls immediately before the suicide. Structurally it is the final blow: the hope whose collapse releases the end.
- **"Riding for falls," now witnessed.** What Gordon confessed privately ("how often I hoped for a fall") others now see plainly — a general naming it at the rail, a friend refusing to drown beside him. The death-wish has become visible behaviour; the novel can show it from outside as well as in.
- **The grieving fathers' poems.** The plagiarism dispute is really the deepest point of the Gordon–Kendall bond: their dead daughters (Annie, Araluen) meet in one image, Gordon's unpublished grief absorbed unawares into Kendall's. Shared loss bleeding literally into shared verse — handle it not as theft but as kinship.
- **The unwritten magnum opus.** 'Penthesilea,' the "horsey" epic Gordon described on the way to a cricket match weeks before his death, was never written — the future amputated mid-sentence, a small sharp emblem of everything the suicide cut off.
- **Clarke alone finds safe ground.** While Gordon drowns in debt and Kendall sleeps in lanes, Clarke lands the one thing that saves him — a secure government post with light hours and a great library on tap. It is the decisive divergence of the three fates: the prose-writer survives the freelance trap that kills the poets. The post is also what makes *His Natural Life* possible — leisure, salary, and the convict records on the shelves. The price, half-joking, is real: "I have sold my birthright of free speech."
- **The Irish patrons and the national-literature project.** Behind the braid stand two Irish patrons — Barry and Duffy — and behind Duffy stands *The Nation*, his Young Ireland journal that built a literature by "appealing to an old bardic people in passionate popular verse." Clarke and Kendall were pursuing the colonial version: Clarke boosting "the beginnings of a national school of Australian poetry," Kendall later writing his 'Harp of Erin' essays on Duffy's Irish poets. The dream of a national literature the three were trying to found has an Irish template — apt, given Clarke's and Kendall's own Irish blood.
- **The death foreseen arrives (24 June 1870).** Everything the file has tracked converges: the falls, the swims, "how often I hoped for a fall," "the man who distrusted himself," "my wife would be better without me," the Esslemont collapse, the refused £100. The suicide is not a shock but an arrival — the end of an arc the reader has watched approach for the length of the book. The one restraint, Maggie ("restrained by the thought of his wife"), finally gives way. Weight this most heavily; it is the gravitational centre.
- **The cruellest timing.** Gordon kills himself the day after his masterpiece is published and Kendall's review crowns him a permanent figure of English literature. The recognition he had hidden from all his life arrives — and a day later he is dead in the scrub with a shilling in his hat. The gap between the laurel and the man is the book's deepest irony, here at its widest. (Even *The Argus* found the timing made "his suicide so soon afterwards the more strange.")
- **The contested last day.** Cheerful to one friend, despondent to another; sober (Madden) or drinking (Sutherland); the manuscript a "present" that proved a memento. The record itself cannot agree how he seemed — a gift to the novelist: the unknowable interior of a man who had decided, behind an ordinary face. Choose, or hold the ambiguity.
- **No one was surprised.** The reactions read like a chorus that saw it coming: Tenison Woods ("a morbid melancholy growing more upon him"), Hammersley, Madden, the family's own "strangeness of manner." The suicide confirmed rather than astonished — exactly how the file has framed the whole arc: a death foreseen, arriving on time. The one true shock was Kendall's.
- **The cause that never settles.** Debt (Massina's "small financial embarrassment," the £75 cheque)? The brain "much affected" by the falls (Murray)? Esslemont, despair, hereditary melancholy? The inquest fixed "unsound mind" and the witnesses never agreed — the same irresolution as the contested last day. Useful: the novel need not, and historically cannot, deliver a single cause. Let the reasons crowd and contradict.
- **Gordon's death cracks Kendall.** He cannot face the grave; he writes to the dead man by name. From here Kendall's own descent steepens — the loss of Gordon folding into the loss of Araluen, the drink, the fraying mind already glimpsed in the Supple portrait. The funeral absence is the hinge into Kendall's collapse.
- **The braid mourns its first death.** Gordon's death is the braid's first, and it marks each survivor differently: Kendall crowns him in the finest elegy of his life and counts himself "left alone, the sad disciple of a shining band now gone"; Clarke, wordless in public, mourns him at the piano for a decade. The convergence that took a book to assemble begins, here, to come apart — and the two who remain are the two least able to bear it.
- **The trunks.** Track the travelling trunks as a motif: made in hope for the voyage to Esslemont; useless when the inheritance failed; then packed by the widow to carry his possessions — and his lost manuscript — to Robe. One object that holds the whole shape of the ending: the dreamed escape, the failure, the death, the scattering of what little he left.
- **The braid begins to dissolve (mid-1870).** The convergence that took the whole book to assemble starts coming apart within weeks: Gordon dead in June, Kendall resigning the Yorick in July and gone from Melbourne by autumn. The three-way co-presence is over almost as soon as it was complete; what remains is two men, apart, declining. The Yorick years close here.
- **Kendall's fraying credit.** The Euterpe squabble is one more instance of a recurring failure: Kendall, under pressure, makes a claim his own letters disprove (as with the Smith money, the plagiarism, the Westminster spat), and is publicly caught and shamed. Not villainy but disintegration — the honesty and judgment of a man coming apart. Track it as a worsening symptom, not a series of isolated quarrels.
- **Canonized in the scrub's despite.** The gap the book has tracked between the laurel and the man reaches its furthest point after death: the poet found with a shilling in his hat is, within months, "our only national poet" under a broken marble column, and within a generation the only Australian in Poets' Corner. The instant, posthumous canonization of a man the colony let die broke is Gordon's final, bitterest irony — and a fact the novel can close on.
- **Gordon as the archetype (Lawson's echo).** A generation later Henry Lawson, with Gordon in mind, told young Australian writers to flee the country or else "shoot himself carefully with the aid of a looking-glass." Gordon's suicide became the defining myth of the doomed Australian writer — the curse the book has watched fall on him and Kendall, passed down as a grim national inheritance. A closing forward-echo: the death meant something beyond itself.
- **Kendall's nadir — the fates rhyme.** Gordon's breakdown ended in the Brighton scrub; Kendall's ends, five months later, in the Sydney dock and the Darlinghurst receiving house. The same forces — debt, drink, a broken nervous system, the wish "to be out of the world," the attempts on his own life — drive both poets to the edge; one goes over, one is dragged back by a jury and a friend. Set the two collapses as deliberate mirrors: the divergence of the three fates is also, here, a near-convergence of two.
- **The dead haunting the living.** "Gordon had tricked them cleverly," Kendall laughed in his madness — the friend he had just crowned in elegy reappearing as the one who got out clean. Survivor's guilt, derangement, and a terrible envy in one line. Gordon's death is not behind Kendall; it is inside his collapse.
- **The press as punishment.** The trial was reported across Australia — "a punishment in itself for someone trying to make a living from that same press." The machine that had carried Kendall's poems now carried his disgrace: the double edge of the literary life the whole book describes, the press that makes the colonial writer able to unmake him in public.
- **Kendall utterly alone.** The collapse takes the last things: his liberty (Darlinghurst), his name (the press), and now his family — Charlotte gone with the newborn, her brothers set to turn him from the door. Where Gordon kept Maggie to the end, Kendall loses everyone while living. The cruelty of survival: he is left to inhabit the ruin Gordon escaped.
- **The convict novel as autobiography.** *His Natural Life* is built partly from Clarke's own body and history — the floggings at Highgate, the bank-clerk past and the "effeminate" self-description folded into Kirkland, the gaol-yard flogging watched first-hand. The great Australian novel of convict suffering has a private taproot: Clarke writing his own remembered pain into a system he never endured. Worth surfacing whenever HNL is in play.
- **Kendall turns the corner (mid-1871).** Gladesville is the floor of the fall: from there the records show him rational within days, writing within a fortnight, discharged within three weeks, still resolved to support the wife who had left him. The recovery is real but partial, and it sets the shape of his remaining years — not the clean death Gordon found but a long, grinding climb back, again and again. The survivor's harder road.
- **Clarke's life in his stories.** As *His Natural Life* drew on his flogged body, the Bullocktown and later tales drew on his unhappy marriage — "his life is in his short stories," and almost no one saw it. The dazzling public wit was encoding a private grief in plain sight. A way into the gap between the performing Clarke and the suffering one: read the fiction as confession.
- **Clarke's marriage fails — confirmed.** The notebook line, "Marian said she wished she had never married me," is the hard documentary proof of what the stories encode and Castieau glimpsed. The dispossessed-gentleman's romance with the lost-mother/actress-wife has soured within two years. It gives his remaining decade its quiet undertow: the dazzling public man carrying a failed marriage and a notebook he doesn't show.
- **The suicides multiply.** Walter Montgomery — Clarke's rival for Marian — married an actress in London and shot himself two days later; Clarke wrote him a generous obituary. Set beside Gordon's death and Kendall's attempts, the pattern hardens: the bohemia is ringed with self-destruction, and Clarke keeps writing the death-notices of men he knew. A recurring shadow to keep in the weave, even where it touches the braid only glancingly.
- **The colonial writer's doom, in Kendall's own words.** 'Men of Letters in New South Wales' is the thesis of the whole book stated by one of its subjects: the young writer arrives full of hope, finds no leisured class to buy his work, and ends in "disappointment and sorrow"; the lucky become "newspaper hacks," and the rest — "God help them!" That Kendall wrote this as he was sinking into the bush and the cells gives it terrible authority. A candidate epigraph, or a load-bearing speech: the diagnosis delivered from inside the disease.
- **The relapse — recovery is not a line.** Gladesville looked like a turn upward; instead Kendall sank lower, into the unrecorded "shadow of 1872," sleeping rough, "unhinged," saved from death by Parkes. The survivor's arc is not redemption but oscillation — down, up, further down — harder to dramatise and truer than a clean recovery. Hold the pattern: Kendall will rise and fall more than once before the end.
- **Duffy reshapes the masterpiece.** *His Natural Life* became great partly by being cut. Duffy's frank critique — the motiveless martyr, the over-long Ballarat digression "mercilessly expunged," the unrelieved pain — named the faults Clarke then worked to mend between the 1870–72 serial and the 1874 book. The Library gave Clarke the records; the flogging gave him the body; Duffy gave him the edit. Treat the novel's composition as a collaboration of pressures, not a lone act of genius.
- **Clarke the debt-bohemian.** The Cave of Adullam — the club of the distressed, the indebted, the discontented, "Honour! No Frills!" — is Clarke's truest milieu and a warning. He gives away his last sovereign, bounces cheques on friends, lives a windfall ahead of the bailiff. The man who "found safe ground" at the Library is, underneath, one of the Adullamites — which is just how the security will fail him in the end.
- **The masterpiece made by committee.** *His Natural Life*'s greatness was a collaboration of pressures: the Library's records, Clarke's flogged body, Duffy's cuts, Shillinglaw's "mother's honour" motive, Moloney's insistence that Dawes must die. Clarke supplied the genius of construction, but the novel was argued, edited, and pruned into shape by his friends. A truer and more dramatic account of authorship than solitary inspiration — and a gift to a novelist staging the writing of it.
- **Clarke's deepening misery.** Behind the public dazzle, 1873 is Clarke's dark year: the marriage failed (he blames himself — "mine the fault"), an anguished half-affair with his wife's sister renounced on a mountaintop, the debts mounting past saving, and his ambitions, by his own account, "defeated... stone instead of bread." The man who found safe ground is privately as ruined as the poets — only quieter about it, and still performing. The gap between the wit and the wreck is now at its widest.
- **The Hopkins counterpoint.** Two schoolfellows — Marcus Clarke and Gerard Manley Hopkins — became two of the era's significant writers, and neither knew the other had. Clarke dreamed Gerard a painter; Gerard, a hidden poet, likely never knew Clarke was famous. It doubles Clarke's "life not lived" theme with a real, poignant mirror — the road not taken made flesh in a friend who took a stranger one. A quiet motif for the Clarke chapters.
- **Kendall's relapse, confirmed.** A second Gladesville, longer than the first; the wife gone, the body starving, the mind delusional — "accused of murdering a child," Araluen's ghost in clinical prose — then, again, recovery. The oscillation is now the established rhythm of his survival: ruin and repair, ruin and repair. Where Gordon's line ended, Kendall's keeps sawing up and down.
- **Trollope's imprimatur.** The most famous novelist of the age, touring and all but denying Australia had any literature, names exactly two of its writers — Marcus Clarke and Adam Lindsay Gordon. The braid's prose master and its poet, singled out together by the visiting eminence: external confirmation that these were the two who mattered, and a validation of the book's own focus. (Kendall, tellingly, goes unmentioned — recognition is uneven even among the three.)
- **Clarke and the genocide.** The Port Arthur articles show Clarke telling a truth the colony preferred buried: the 1830 "war of extermination" of Tasmania's Aboriginal people. He met the denials head-on, and a later editor quietly cut the passage. It situates *His Natural Life* in its full historical darkness — not only convict suffering but native destruction — and marks Clarke as willing to name the country's founding crime. The genocide is the shadow behind the convict romance.
- **Kendall's recovery, at last real.** The Fagans are where the oscillation finally turns up and stays: an emancipist's family who nurse him, employ him, and give him the peace to write 'Mooni' and 'Names upon a Stone.' After the asylums and the bush and the cells, the rescue that holds — and it comes not from the literary world but from a kind Irish ex-convict and his sons. The survivor reaches solid ground in the least literary place of all.
- **The Yorick years end.** The club that opened the story as the braid's bright centre is, by 1874, "out at elbows," deserted for a café, resigned by its steadier members. Gordon dead, Kendall gone north, Clarke decamped to the *Herald* and the Iphigenia — the physical hub has dissolved. Mark it as the close of the Yorick era: the structural heart stops beating here.
- **Clarke radicalized — and tilting.** The convict records that made *His Natural Life* also turned Clarke leftward, and his "impending insolvency" sharpened the edge into the Balzacian contempt of 'The Wicked World.' The word insolvency now sits in the record beside the £400 salary: the man on safe ground is quietly sliding toward the ruin the Cave of Adullam foretold.
- **The masterpiece arrives, undervalued.** *His Natural Life* — the great Australian novel, finished after years and reshaped by every pressure the book has tracked — appears in 1874 to modest sales and a colonial shrug; it took an English lord's praise a decade on to make it sell at home. The recognition-too-late motif that hangs over Gordon hangs here too: the work outlasts the lifetime's indifference. Clarke's triumph and his ruin arrive in the same season.
- **The safe ground collapses — but he's caught.** Three months after the masterpiece, the insolvency the Cave of Adullam foretold: Clarke goes down with the indebted and discontented after all. But where Gordon had no net and Kendall only the Fagans, Clarke has a cousin who is a colonial governor — power of attorney, a Brighton house, the fall absorbed. The class advantage that runs through his whole story saves him one more time: the dispossessed gentleman is still a gentleman.
- **No return.** The single sharpest revision: in the serial Dawes goes home to England; in the book he drowns. Clarke cut the return — for his hero, and, the dedication to the twice-gaoled Duffy hints, for himself. By 1874, insolvent and alienated from English establishment values, Clarke too had quietly accepted there was no going back. The novel's ending is his own.
- **Kendall renounces literature.** Recovered, Kendall turns on the whole literary life that nearly killed him — sober, hidden, anti-ambition, ready to "turn bullock driver." His despair is also a verdict on the book's whole subject: Harpur, Michael, Gordon "all failed," so why write "for a shadow"? The survivor's peace is bought by quitting the game the others died playing. (And yet he keeps scribbling squibs — the renunciation is never quite total.)
- **Clarke cast out.** The Yorick blackball is the bitter inversion of the founding: the man who gave the club its name and its skull is the one it refuses to re-admit, bankrupt and shamed. The bohemia the braid created turns on its own. Where the early chapters show Clarke gathering the company, this shows the company closing the door — a sharp marker of how far the golden years have curdled.
- **The masterpiece resists.** Pressed by his English publisher to give *His Natural Life* a happy ending — the hero saved, returned home — Clarke caved and wrote the chapter, yet the tragic drowning survived into print regardless. The market wanted a return; the book refused one. The "no return" theme tested and vindicated: the work's bleak integrity outlasts both the colony's indifference and London's commercial softening. Worth noting how nearly the great ending was lost — and wasn't.
- **Clarke names the aesthetic and crowns the braid.** The 'Country Leisure' essay is the convergence's intellectual capstone: the prose master writes the founding statement of Australian literature — "Weird Melancholy," "the Land of the Dawning" — and in the same breath ranks his two friends, dead Gordon and distant Kendall, as its chief poets. The braid theorized from inside: one of the three defines the national art and consecrates the other two within it. The closing phrase, "the poet of our desolation," hangs unassigned over all three — the role each of them, in his way, was trying to fill. This is the critical centre the novel can build toward and quote from.
- **The masterpiece will outlive him.** Hyndman's verdict — that *His Natural Life* would "live by sheer force of its terror-inspiring delineations long after his other work is forgotten" — is the prophecy that came true, spoken while Clarke was still alive and sliding. The recognition-too-late motif again, but inverted into foresight: the one imperishable thing is already made, even as the man who made it goes under. Pair it with Gordon's posthumous canonization — both men's true reputations arrive sidelong, in spite of their lives.
- **Clarke's bush was a projection.** The Myers revelation cuts straight across the 'Country Leisure' manifesto: the man who defined the Australian landscape as "Weird Melancholy" actually hated the bush, never entered the forest willingly, and preferred the fleshpots of clubland. His desolation was a literary construction — a city gothic projected onto a country he found merely uncomfortable. This doesn't diminish the achievement; it sharpens it. The founding image of the Australian bush was made by imagination and distaste, not love — its own kind of story, and a rich contradiction to set against Kendall, who actually lived in and loved the glen. The theorist of the desolation and the poet of it were opposite kinds of men.
- **No return — now by choice.** The *Daily Telegraph* offered Clarke the way back: England, the metropolis, "journalism of the highest order," the largest readership in the world. He declined. Where the early Clarke was exiled to Australia and the *His Natural Life* revision cut Dawes's homecoming, the 1875 Clarke turns down a real homecoming of his own. The dispossessed gentleman has, in some sense, chosen his dispossession — Australia is home now, fleshpots and all, even for a man who cannot love its bush.
- **Kendall's shadowed peace.** Camden Haven is the fullest the recovery gets: Charlotte restored, the children round him, out of debt at last, a town that will bear his name. And still the black moods come, even in the "happiest years" — the dead daughter, the wasted youth, never wholly gone. The peace is real and the wound is permanent, both at once. That doubleness — happy and haunted — is the truest note for his late chapters, and a quiet rebuke to any tidy redemption arc.
- **The preface as founding text.** Clarke's introduction to Gordon's poems is where the braid's literary project becomes permanent: one of the three writes the criticism that founds Australian literary nationalism, fixes the bush as "Weird Melancholy," and consecrates a second of the three as its poet — all bound into the book that became the era's bestseller. The convergence didn't merely happen; it produced its own canon-making document, authored from within. That the bush-evocation was (per Myers) imagined rather than loved is folded in here, not against it: Hopkins's defence — impression, not survey — is the truer account of how the founding image was actually made.
- **Even the triumph enriches others.** Gordon's *Poems* sell 20,000 in a decade and make him the national poet; *His Natural Life* keeps a prince from sleep and will sell past 46,000. And the two who made them get almost nothing in life — Maggie sells the copyright for a pittance and regrets it forever; Clarke takes £50 while the £978 comes to his widow after his death. The recognition-too-late motif hardens into something colder: the work's success is real, vast, and consistently routed away from the people who bled for it. Triumph and dispossession in the same ledger.
- **The net withdrawn — the slide begins.** 1874's lesson was that Clarke fell but was caught; 1877 is the year the catch is let go. Sir Andrew withdraws the power of attorney, the Brighton villa is exchanged for St Kilda, the usurers return. The class advantage that saved him where the poets had no net was always a loan, not a property — and now it's called in. From here Clarke's line runs the way Gordon's and Kendall's once did: downward, toward debt and an early death. The divergence of the three fates begins, quietly, to converge again.
- **The death foreshadowed, in his friends' own words.** Two of the people closest to the record name the cause of Clarke's coming death while he is still alive and joking: Cyril Hopkins — "his debts and worries eventually killed him" — and Mackinnon — the usurers "wrought him to death before his time." The end is legible four years out, in the debts and the carelessness, the way Gordon's was in the falls and the melancholy. Clarke's death, like Gordon's, will be an arrival, not a shock — and the file should let that pressure build rather than spring it.
- **The inverse poets: Kendall heard the bush, Clarke hated it.** This chunk completes the contrast the Myers anecdote opened. Clarke, who wrote the founding evocation of the Australian landscape, loathed the actual bush and would not enter the forest; Kendall, in the same years, is "an inspired blackfellow" with a name for every fern and moss-bank, the country woven into his nature. The two poets of the braid are opposites at the root — the landscape myth authored by the man without feeling for it, embodied by the man saturated in it. A structural gift: the theorist and the native, side by side, neither complete without the other.
- **Clarke's very red radicalism — and the splinter in it.** Clarke's late politics are genuinely radical: a hatred of "Shoddocracy," a Balzacian conviction that great fortunes are forgotten crimes, the lone honest voice on Black Wednesday asking why only the well-salaried get mourned. But the same friends who record it see the self-wound: his keenest contempt is for the "laissez faire Bohemianism which was the cause of all his troubles." Clarke attacks the money-world that is destroying him while practising the improvidence that hands him to it. The radicalism and the ruin share a root — and he half-knew it. (All three principals, notably, despised the political-business world: Gordon fled parliament, Kendall mocked the brass-not-gold politician, Clarke called business legalized theft.)
- **Kendall crowned — in life.** Kendall's Exhibition triumph is the great exception to the recognition-too-late law that governs the others. Gordon was canonized after his suicide; Clarke's money and fame came after his death; but Kendall, the wreck of the cells, is hailed "premier bard of the southern hemisphere" while alive and present, before a cheering hall. The survivor gets the living vindication the dead were denied. It is the strongest argument for his recovery as a real second act — and the quiet cost is that he no longer wants it: "out of the running," he takes the crown like a man already gone elsewhere. Recognition arrives exactly when he has stopped needing it.
- **The divergence inverts, completely.** Hold 1879 in a single frame: Kendall, who renounced literature and lives by a ledger in a coastal store, is crowned the nation's first poet before the Governors; Clarke, who reached the safe ground the poets never had, is declining secure posts, reprimanded by his trustees, tracking his debts to the moneylenders. The poet rises into public glory as the prose master sinks toward ruin. The book's central reversal is now total — and the cruelty is in the timing, the two lines crossing in the same year.
- **The two survivors never met.** A small, resonant fact: Kendall and Clarke, the pair who outlived Gordon, never corresponded — George McCrae wrote to each, but they did not write to each other. The braid's three strands had a true centre only while Gordon lived and the Yorick held; afterwards the two survivors ran parallel and apart, north and south, rising and falling, with no direct thread between them. The convergence was real and brief; the dispersal was permanent.
- **The braid's antagonistic survivors.** Sharper still: Kendall and Clarke did not merely run parallel — Kendall actively disliked Clarke, dismissed even the celebrated Gordon preface, and named him in a letter as a compromised debtor "bowing to Rimmon." The two men who carried the braid past Gordon's death were, privately, at odds: the puritan-recovered poet despising the bohemian sell-out, the bohemian never knowing or caring. This deepens "the two survivors never met" into something harder — they were temperamentally opposed, and the convergence the book centres on held only in the brief years when Gordon and the Yorick bound them. The frame should resist any sentimental reunion: the braid was a moment, not a friendship.
- **The colonial desolation — genius stifled.** Myers gives the book's grimmest thesis in plain prose: in that Australia there was "no literary or artistic companionship of any sort whatever," the press paid nothing for verse, and "a good deal of genius was stifled... it made a worse wreck of those lives than any [drink or drug] habit." This is the shared doom beneath all three stories — not personal weakness but a country with no place for what they were. Hold it as the structural ground: the braid is three men trying to be artists where there was no use for artists, broken differently by the same lack.
- **Clarke alienates everyone.** In one year Clarke needles the Church into suppressing a magazine and mocks his own radical ministry into banning a play. Conservatives, the devout, and his own allies — estranged in turn, just as the debts close in and the patrons he needs grow fewer. The self-wounding pattern Turner saw (the Bohemianism "that was the cause of all his troubles") now extends to his politics and his faith-fights: Clarke cannot stop antagonizing the very people who might keep him afloat. The radical's integrity and the self-saboteur's recklessness wear the same face.
- **The title, and the verdict.** Kendall's phrase to Swan — "that wild bleak Bohemia south of the Murray," where "I went through Gethsemane" — names not just his own ordeal but the book's whole subject, and gives it its title. It is the survivor's verdict on the convergence: the Melbourne years the early chapters render as bright and companionable were, from inside one of the three, a wilderness and a passion. The bleakness is not hindsight imposed on the story; it is how one of the men who lived it named it, at the end. Worth treating as the book's organizing judgement — the gold of the Yorick years and the Gethsemane underneath, the same ground seen by different lights.
- **The Bulletin builds the legend.** As the three men fade or fail, the magazine that will canonize them launches: *The Bulletin* (1880), whose editors Archibald and Stephens — "the grand originals of Australian literature" — make a point of paying tribute to the generation before them, running the memoirs, anecdotes and reprints that fix Clarke, Gordon and Kendall as founders. The legend is assembled, characteristically, by the next generation and largely after death — the recognition-too-late motif institutionalized. The men were broken by a country with no place for artists; that place was being built just too late for them, and would enshrine them as its origin.
- **The reconciliation that comes too late — but does come.** The book's deepest near-miss is also, finally, a near-reach that connects. In person it failed: Kendall sent Fagan with a warm note in October 1880, and Clarke, ill and hounded, dodged the meeting. But by post it completed — Kendall sent his book, Clarke wrote a generous *Leader* tribute and "a genial letter," and the poet who had despised the novelist exchanged kind words with him at the end. So the door is not quite shut: the two survivors who had not corresponded for a decade reconcile in ink in their final months, while never once meeting. That is the truer, stranger shape — not a slammed door and not a deathbed embrace, but a warmth achieved only on paper, only at the very end, between two men already dying. Keep both halves: the missed meeting and the late, real thaw.
- **"Life was no longer dawning."** Clarke's failing — the lethargic brain, the work grown impossibly heavy, Mackinnon's "ominous signs of an early break-up" — arrives with the same grim legibility as Gordon's end and the same cruel timing as Kendall's illness. His 1873 cantata *At the Dawning* is staged the very spring his own dawn is over. The death the file has tracked toward for chapters is now weeks or months off, and the man meets it still performing the old charm (the bailiff become "Uncle John"), the gallantry outlasting the body. As with all three, the ending was visible long before it came; the only question left was the form it would take.
- **The writer dies writing.** Clarke's death gives the book its most unbearable image and its truest one: the man of words, speech gone, moving his hand over blank paper to the very end — not for art but, as McCrae understood, to "bring a little coin for his wife and children." Everything the file has tracked is in that gesture: the fluency that was his gift and his curse, the lifelong scramble for money, the love of family under the improvidence, the pen as the only tool he ever had. He died doing the thing he did all his life, and failing at the thing he always failed at — earning enough. Let the image stand without gloss; it carries the whole life.
- **"Everything came in time, but came too late."** McCrae's line over the deathbed is the epitaph for all three. The medicine came too late for Clarke; the recognition came too late for Gordon; the laurels and the secure post came to Kendall just as his body failed. Across the braid, the help, the money, the honour always arrive — and always a beat after they could have saved anything. It is the book's deepest and coldest law, stated plainly at last by a grieving friend: not that the world ignored them, but that it gave them what they needed always one moment too late to use.
- **The death foreseen arrives — again.** As with Gordon, Clarke's end was visible long before it came — the eight-stone frame, the failing brain, the coin-toss naming asylum and grave. When it came it was, the *Argus* admitted, a "shock of surprise" only to those who had not been watching; to the man himself it was a wager whose outcome he already knew. Two of the three deaths now share this shape: not sudden catastrophe but the arrival of something long legible. Give Clarke's death the treatment Gordon's got — not a twist, but a culmination the reader has been dreading for chapters. The braid's law of timing holds even here: the obituaries that ignored him in life ran long the morning after.
- **The braid mourns its own.** Each of the three is elegized, in the end, by another: Clarke wrote the preface that consecrated Gordon and the tribute that crowned Kendall; Kendall now writes the 'In Memoriam' for Clarke. The mourning completes what the lives could not — the antagonism between the survivors dissolves into Kendall's grief, "he was all gold," "my heroic friend." It is the truest form the braid finally takes: not a friendship sustained but a series of elegies, the living writing the dead into the literature they were all trying to found. The convergence is consummated in mourning — and Kendall's elegy names the law plainly: "the lot austere that ever seems to wait upon the man of letters here."
- **Canonized too late.** Clarke's death triggers exactly the recognition his life was denied: the masterpiece becomes *For the Term of His Natural Life*, the national classic, adapted and filmed and reprinted for a century, the money flooding to a widow selling his books from a stall. Hyndman's prophecy — that the novel would outlive all his other work — comes true on schedule, and the "everything came too late" law reaches its fullest statement: the country built its literature on a man it let die destitute, and enshrined him the moment he could no longer be helped. Gordon canonized after the rifle, Clarke after the worn-out liver — the pattern is now a system.
- **The third death approaches.** Kendall's decline accelerates in the wake of Clarke's — "my literary career has closed," "I can hardly move the pen," the same failing hand — and he reads his own end without flinching: "before the year be out, I shall see Charles." His child Orara is dead; his health is gone; the laurels and the secure post he won in 1881 are already slipping from a dying man. The braid's last strand is unravelling, and Kendall, the one who got the living recognition, is following the other two down within a year of his crowning. The book is closing on its third grave.
- **Both founders died providing.** The deepest convergence the book contains is not in the Yorick or the bohemianism but on the two deathbeds, a year apart. Clarke, speech gone, moved his hand over blank paper to "bring a little coin for his wife and children." Kendall, consumption-wasted, clung to life by will until the first of the month "so you will get the salary." Each man, dying, did the only thing he could still do for his family — and each did it through the work, the writing and the post, that had used them both up. The braid's truest knot is this: two men who spent their lives failing to provide, spending their deaths trying to. Whatever else the novel does, it should let these two scenes stand together; they are the same scene.
- **Crowned, then buried by twenty.** Kendall's was the one death the recognition-too-late law seemed to spare — he was crowned "uncrowned laureate" in life, given the secure post, vindicated. And then he was followed to the grave by twenty people, "merely a poet," while the country that would anthologize him in the *Golden Treasury* and the *Oxford Book* looked the other way. Even the exception bends back to the rule: the laurels of 1880 could not fill the grave of 1882. Recognition came in life, but it was thin and brief and could not save him; the real enshrinement, as for Gordon and Clarke, waited for the man to be safely dead.
- **The torch passes.** The book closes not on an ending but on a handing-on. Louisa Lawson rescues Kendall's neglected grave and names her son Henry after him; Henry Lawson becomes the national writer the three founders were reaching toward, and is buried in the plot beside Kendall, by the sea. The literature they gave their lives and their solvency to found outlived them and found its next voice — literally over Kendall's bones. The three of them died poor, scattered, two of them cold toward each other; but the thing they were braiding survived, and the proof is a second grave dug alongside the first. There is a case for ending the book here: not with the deaths, but with the inheritance.
- **The braid, complete.** All three are now in the ground — Gordon by the Brighton sea in 1870, Clarke in Melbourne in 1881, Kendall by the Pacific in 1882, the survivors dying a year apart almost to the day. All three were recognized fully only after death; all three left widows who outlived them by decades (Maggie to 1919, Marian to 1914, Charlotte to 1924). The convergence the whole book traces reaches its final form here: not three men in a club, but three graves and a literature. The Yorick years were the braid's life; the deaths, a year apart, are its completion. What the novel has been building is now whole, and what it is finally about stands clear — how a country got a literature, and what it cost the three men who gave it one.
- **The vise closes on Clarke.** Everything converges on him in 1880: the failing body (ophthalmia, measles, eight stone), the pressing debts (Waxman, the bailiffs), the unfinished work, the Library gamble come due — and, at the exact wrong moment, the death or departure of every patron who might have saved the appointment (Duffy gone, Barry and Lyster dead in one November week). The 1877 withdrawal of the net was the first turn; this is the closing. The dispossessed gentleman has run out of the protectors that made him a gentleman still, and gambled away his fallback, just as his body begins to give. The death now imminent will be, like Gordon's, the arrival of something long visible.
- **Both survivors decline together.** With Clarke's collapse and now Kendall's illness — "the result of six years of overwork... out of my power to write" — the two surviving strands of the braid begin to fail their bodies at the same moment, in the same year. The reversal that crowned Kendall and ruined Clarke is overtaken by a deeper convergence: mortality. Gordon went first and alone; now the other two start down together, north and south, the poet with his laurels and the novelist with his debts, both worn out before forty. The braid that dispersed in fortune reunites in decline.
- **Loyal to the braid, to the end.** The redeeming paradox of Clarke's last act: even as his sneering pen destroys his own prospects, that same pen never stops promoting Gordon and Kendall. He is generous to the two poets — one dead, one who disliked him — at the exact time he is least able to afford generosity to himself. The convergence the book centres on outlives the friendships inside it: Clarke serves the braid as an idea, a cause, a literature, long after the men have scattered and cooled. It is the closest thing to nobility in his ruin.
- **The vindication shadowed.** Kendall gets everything he sought in 1880–81 — the laureate's acclaim, the *Bulletin* feature, the secure Forests post, the children provided for — and it arrives stamped with mortality: "he will write a great poem if he live long enough," and he knows the great poem "remains unwritten yet," and his body is already failing. The recovery's reward and the recovery's end arrive together. As with Gordon's reviews and Clarke's prophecy, the recognition lands just as the time to use it runs out.
- **Don't blame Gordon for Kendall's drink.** A myth to refuse (Hamilton-Grey's): that Gordon led the sober Kendall into drink. Gordon drank little; Kendall's drinking was hereditary — the dipsomaniac mother, the family curse — and predated their friendship. Frederick Kendall: "Of course it was in his family."
- **Kendall's family: the engine of his ruin.** Beyond the dipsomaniac mother lies the whole clan — the betraying sisters, the forging brother, the furniture signed away — who used the dutiful eldest son as a "thing to play upon" until he fled the colony to survive. His martyrdom as provider, and his bitterness at it, are the bedrock of his melancholy and his money-terror. The Neild letter is the key document; stage it as the wound he carries into Melbourne.
- **The Yorick goes conventional.** Even as the braid completes, the club at its heart is already softening — rules relaxed, professionals and princes and police chiefs admitted, Clarke himself growing "jaundiced." The pure bohemian moment is brief; the novel can play the convergence against the institution's drift toward respectability.
- **Women who rode.** Hunting was one arena where women met men as equals — the fearless "Diana" of the age; Maggie, Mrs Connolly, Mrs Robert Power, "no fence would stop them." A way into the women's-perspective thread, and a counter to the men-only Yorick.
- **The "Pentridge" joke that came true.** Clarke's 1868 gag about researching prison life ("Two Months at Pentridge") idly anticipates *His Natural Life* — the convict epic that would consume him two years on. A neat irony to plant early in the writer's arc.
- **Clarke the dispossessed gentleman.** Beneath the bohemian dazzle, a Tory patrician who lost his Foreign-Office future to his father's ruin and never forgave the colony its "money makes the gentleman" vulgarity — the chip that drives both his snobbery and his reckless extravagance. Note the irony: the one principal who "did not drink hard" still bankrupts himself twice.
- **The company of glamorous ruin.** Clarke's clubland "fast set" — Aspinall dead insane and penniless, Ireland's four fortunes made and gone, the witty diners-out of a "dashingly reckless" age — models the very arc he's on. The Melbourne Club is less a seducer (Turner's right) than a mirror: brilliant gentlemen spending themselves into nothing.
- **"Death has been busy among us."** Within a few weeks of 1868 the braid is ringed by death: Gordon's near-fatal fall and baby Annie; Kendall's mentor Michael drowned; Clarke's friend Drummond dead of a snake-bite dare; the actor Vincent thrown from his horse. Clarke names the mood (16 May): men "wonder who is to be the next." A natural dark centre of gravity for this stretch — and Drummond's rationalist bravado-unto-death rhymes with Gordon's "rode to break his neck" and Clarke's own drug self-experiments: the era's fatal habit of testing things on one's own body.
- **The Yorick Club: the braid's structural heart — and the founding myth.** The Yorick (founded May 1868) is where the three finally share a room, and the novel's natural spine. But beware the romantic tableau the club's own histories paint — Clarke, Gordon and Kendall together at the naming. The record staggers them: Clarke founds and runs it in 1868; Gordon is a "country" foundation member who only becomes active after moving to Melbourne in October 1868; Kendall doesn't arrive until April 1869 (Clarke seconds his membership that July). The braid is real but assembled by degrees — a truer, slower convergence than the legend. Decide consciously whether to honour the myth or the chronology.
- **A bachelor-literature haven.** The Yorick was emphatically men-only — "the great years of bachelor literature," a refuge "secure from the invasions of womankind," even for the married Gordon and Kendall. The women of the story stand outside its door — worth weighing against the women's-perspective sources.
- **The Ned Kelly threads gather.** Two Yorick-world men bracket Kelly's end: Sir Redmond Barry (who sentences him) and Howard Bindon (whom Clarke proposed for the Yorick, later Kelly's defence counsel). A quiet foreshadow of the colony's most famous trial, two clubmen on opposite sides.
- **The braid converges in one magazine.** Before the three men share a club or a room, they share the *Colonial Monthly* (1867–68): Clarke its editor and chief fiction-writer, Kendall publishing verse and prose in it, Gordon reviewed and (1868) printed in it. The print-convergence precedes and enables the social one — a natural structural hinge for the novel.
- **Shared origins: West Indian planter money.** Clarke's and Gordon's grandfathers, Walstab's father, McCrae's great-grandfather were all West Indies planters — slave-economy fortunes, mostly lost, underwriting this circle of déclassé gentlemen. A quiet common root beneath the bohemian poverty.
- **Police-to-letters, again.** Walstab (Mounted Police → journalist, "what a policeman!") and Shillinglaw (water police → editor) join Gordon (trooper → poet) and Kendall's near-miss: the colonial constabulary keeps turning into the colonial literary world. A recurring, slightly absurd rhyme worth playing.
- **The deadly turf, self-diagnosed.** In 'Racing Ethics' (Apr 1868) Gordon anatomises the very lethality — iron ground, stiff timber, reckless schooling — that has just half-killed him and will frame his end. The man writes the coroner's brief for his own kind of death, days after his daughter's.
- **Annie's death pre-loads the suicide.** Wilding marks the fall as Gordon's physical ruin and Annie's death as the emotional one he "never recovered from." Hold this as the hinge: the 1870 suicide is unimaginable without April 1868 — and his marriage never recovers either.
- **Poets into the water.** Michael's drowning (suicide, Kendall was sure) joins Deniehy's drink-death as a mirror held up to Kendall — and rhymes forward with Gordon's self-killing. The braid is ringed by self-destroyed writers.

## 7. Narrative structure — a recommendation

_A structural proposal for the novel, argued against the alternatives. Drawn from the whole analysis; revise freely once the Parts 3–8 outline is back in hand._

**The structural problem, stated honestly.** The premise is a braid of three, but the documentary record shows three strands that are truly woven together for only about eighteen months. All three principals share Melbourne — and the Yorick — only from Kendall's arrival in April 1869 to his departure in October 1870, and Gordon's suicide (June 1870) cuts even that short. Before it lie three separate approach-stories; after it, a decade-long divergence in which the two survivors barely interact and actively dislike each other. Gordon, the most romantic figure, dies a third of the way through the timespan. The deepest emotional material — the two deathbeds, the reconciliation-by-post — sits at the very end. A naïve chronological telling therefore courts three dangers: a premise that frays a third of the way in, a slack two-hander middle in which nothing converges, and a climax stranded in the final pages. The structure has to solve all three at once.

**The recommendation, in one line.** A chronological spine, with the Yorick convergence as the centre of gravity; Gordon's death as the Act II hinge that teaches the reader the book's law; the long divergence cross-cut as an inverting seesaw rather than two parallel lines; the two deaths delivered as the braid reconverging in mortality; and the close on the inheritance — Lawson's grave beside Kendall — not on the third deathbed.

**Open inside the braid, not before it.** Begin in the Yorick smoking-room in the one window when all three are present — the mahogany skull on the mantel with a pipe in its teeth, Clarke holding court, Gordon reticent in a corner, Kendall newly off the boat and starving — so the reader meets the premise as a living thing before the strands pull apart. Then drop back to bring each man to that room: Clarke the dispossessed gentleman shipped out at sixteen; Gordon the trooper-turned-steeplechaser drinking against his falls; Kendall the three-generation ruin carrying his family's disgrace. It is a convergence-first hook on a chronological spine — the braid promised in the first pages, then earned.

**Make Clarke the connective tissue, but rotate.** Clarke is the impresario of the braid: he founds the Yorick, seconds Kendall's membership, writes the preface that canonises Gordon and the tribute that crowns Kendall, and keeps promoting both even as his own pen ruins him. He is the natural centre of consciousness — the one who knows and touches everyone. But he dies before Kendall, so the book cannot live solely in his head. Recommend a rotating close-third, anchored on whoever's arc is ascendant, with Clarke as the recurring hub; and consider a framing observer — George McCrae, who knew all three and wrote the deathbed letters, and who outlives them — to open and close the book and supply its documentary voice.

**Let Gordon's death end Act II — as the template, not a tragedy spent early.** Gordon dies first, and his end teaches the reader the law that will govern the other two: the death foreseen (the falls, the Brighton swim, the confession to Riddoch that he hoped to fall); recognition arriving the morning after the rifle (the *Argus* praising *Bush Ballads* the day after the burial); canonisation-too-late (the bust in Poets' Corner, 1934). Once the reader has watched Gordon's end arrive as a culmination rather than a shock, every later sign of Clarke's and Kendall's decline reads as the same machine turning. Gordon is the book's Rosetta stone — spend him at the hinge, not at the close.

**Cross-cut the divergence as an inversion, not a parallel.** The decade 1870–1880 is the danger zone: two men who never meet. The engine that saves it is the inversion the record documents — as Clarke sinks (insolvency, the withdrawn safety net in 1877, the lost librarianship, the failing body), Kendall rises (rescue by the Fagans, sobriety, the secure Forests post, the crown of "uncrowned laureate"). Cut between them on the seesaw — the prose-master ruined in the same years the poet is crowned — and the absence of contact becomes the point: two founders of one literature moving in opposite directions, neither knowing the other is bound for the same grave. The dramatic irony, the reader knowing both will be dead within a year of each other, turns non-interaction into suspense.

**Deliver the deaths as reconvergence.** The two deathbeds are the same scene a year apart — Clarke's hand moving over blank paper to bring "a little coin for his wife and children," Kendall holding to the first of the month so Charlotte will get the salary — both men dying to provide, each through the work that had used him up. Structure them as a matched pair, close together, so the rhyme lands as design rather than coincidence. Precede them with the reconciliation-by-post — Kendall's book sent, Clarke's generous tribute and "genial letter" returned — so the braid is shown re-weaving in ink just before it is cut. Resist the deathbed meeting the record denies: the warmth achieved only on paper, only at the very end, between two men already dying, is truer and stranger than any reunion.

**End on the inheritance.** Close not on Kendall's grave but just past it — on Louisa Lawson finding that grave weed-grown under a sagging wreath, campaigning the monument into being (and being forgotten by the committee at its unveiling), and having already named her son Henry after the dead poet; and on Henry Lawson buried, in time, in the plot beside him. The literature the three bankrupted themselves to found outlived them and found its next voice — over Kendall's bones. End on the second grave dug alongside the first: both the cost, and the thing the cost bought.

**Why not the alternatives.**

- *The triptych — three self-contained movements, one per man.* Tempting, because it accepts that the three do not interact. But it surrenders the premise: the braid's whole claim is co-presence and mutual consequence, and three linked novellas would read as a collection — losing the cross-cut inversion that is the divergence's only engine.
- *The retrospective frame — open at the deathbeds and ask how they got there.* It leads with the strongest material, but it spends the deaths in the first chapter and spoils the slow-arriving dread that is the book's chief effect. The deaths must be culminations the reader has been taught to fear, not a riddle's opening premise.
- *Naïve linear chronology — start at the beginning, run to the end.* The default, and the trap: it buries the convergence in the middle, lets Gordon's death deflate the back half, and strands the climax in the final pages. The four moves above — the in-the-braid opening, Gordon-as-hinge, the cross-cut inversion, the matched deathbeds — are precisely what keep a chronological book from sagging.

**The load-bearing set-pieces, by movement** (all detailed in §4):

- *Approach & convergence.* The Yorick naming (the skull; "Alas, poor Yorick!"); Gordon's three-win day and Clarke's "Reckless" sketch; Kendall's starving arrival ("an ominous first of April"); all three in one issue of *The Australasian* (7 May 1870).
- *The hinge.* Gordon's last day in town (the refused £100, the last drink with Kendall, the manuscript "present") and the rifle in the Brighton scrub; the *Argus* review the day after the burial.
- *The divergence (cross-cut).* Kendall's rescue by the Fagans set against Clarke's blackball from the Yorick he founded; the coin-toss near the asylum; Kendall crowned "uncrowned laureate" against Clarke passed over for the librarianship.
- *Reconvergence.* The genial letter; the writer dying writing; "so you will get the salary"; the twenty mourners.
- *Inheritance.* Louisa Lawson at the grave; Lawson laid down beside Kendall.
